### Westland
  
noun.  
See: also West Coast  
[NOTES: see also petrel, pine]  
> **JOHN ROCHFORT** While I was chaining, I was surprised ... by one of the hands announcing the discovery of gold, an event ... which must have a powerful influence on the future prospects of this long neglected west-land. [ed. fn The first known occasion on which the term `Westland' was used.] *(Nov 8, 1858,Journal)* [NOTES: 1858 Nov 8 John Rochfort Journal in .1947 Reed Story of Canterbury 251]  
> **MUELLER** His Honour the Superintendent has ... been pleased to appoint you an Assistant Surveyor & Mining Surveyor at Westland, ... *(May 2, 1866,My Dear Bannie,164,1958)*  
> **MAY** `West Coast' continued to be used in the 1860's, but after 1864 the official names, `West Canterbury' and `Nelson South West', had some popular currency. Then a further complication was introduced. In 1859 John Rochfort had had coined the name `West land', a shortened form of `Westmoreland', the name he used on a map showing his West Coast explorations. After August 1865 the form `Westland' was resurrected and was used both for electoral and judicial districts which covered part or whole of the West Coast. Popularly, however, `Westland' was used chiefly for West Canterbury; the remainder of Canterbury Province was sometimes called `Eastland'. West Canterbury became the County of Westland in 1868 and the Province of Westland five years later. The Nelson South-West Goldfield kept this official name until 1876 when the provinces were abolished. *(1967,West Coast Gold Rushes,14,1962)*  
> **MAY** `West Coast' remains the popular term for the strip of country on the wrong side of the Southern Alps, though recently New Zealand geographers have adopted `Westland' for the whole West Coast. Whatever its merits, this term is unsatisfactory. It has some popular currency south of Greymouth, and it may acquire popularity through its teaching in the schools; but at an indefinable point north of Greymouth one is likely to be told, `This isn't Westland, this is the Buller'. A region itself so distinctive is also marked by strong local feeling. In 1960 `Westland' celebrated its centenary; `the Buller' did not. *(1967,West Coast Gold Rushes,15)*

### Westland black petrel
  
noun.  
> **FALLA et al.** WESTLAND BLACK PETREL <i>Procellaria westlandica</i> *(1966,Birds of New Zealand,46)*  
> <i>Procellaria westlandica</i> Falla, 1046 <i>Westland Black Petrel </i> *(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,26,1980)*  
> **LOCKLEY** The Westland black petrel, <i>Procellaria westlandica</i>, equally dark but larger (51 cm long) than the black petrel (43 cm long) of Great and Little Barrier Islands, is a winter breeder in forested country isolated deep within coastal ranges of the South Island. *(1980,House Above the Sea,194)*  
> <i>Westland Black Petrel Procellaria westlandica </i>FALLA, 1946. ... DISTRIBUTION Breeds <i>only on west coast of the South Island, south of Punakaiki River. </i> *(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,91)*  
> **DENNIS** Outstanding and unique along the Punakaiki coastline is the large mainland colony of burrowing Westland black petrels. *(1981,Paparoas Guide,180)*

### Westland black petrel
  
noun. a large black petrel inhabiting Fiordland  
See: also black petrel  
> The rare Westland black petrel breeds only on the hills of the central West Coast, just south of Punakaiki. *(Aug 8, 2003,Capital Times,4)*  
> **LOCKLEY** There were some petrels I had never seen before - ... the large all-black Westland petrel whose only known resting place is a remote rainy valley in Fiordland.... *(1980,Home: A New Zealander's Adventure,36)*  
> Fascinated by birds, Menteath had often watched the soaring flight of the rare Westland black petrels, <i>Procellaria westlandica</i>, as they swept in from the sea at dusk each day. *(Nov 1992,North & South,7)*

### Westland mudfish
  
noun.  
> **SPEIGHT** The species of <i>Galaxias</i> may broadly be divided into the small, sea-running whitebait minnows (<i>Galaxias attenuatus</i>), the larger native `trout' (<i>G. fasciatus</i> and allied species), which probably breed inland, and an aberrant species of medium size (<i>G. borrowsii</i>) which approaches the Westland mudfish (<i>Neochanna apoda</i>) in form. *(1927,Natural History of Canterbury,196)*

### Westland pine
  
noun. <i>Dacrydium colensoi</i>, manoao, silver pine, yellow pine  
See: also Westland silver pine  
[NOTES: [1919 July 29] Ver.(WB). = Vernacular names used by settlers supplied by W.Best, Otaki (And) <i>Dacrydium colensoi</i> Westland pine>19?? NZTJ 71/2]  
> [Native name] Manoao .. [Settlers' name] Westland pine .. [Family] Coniferae *(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,120)* [NOTES: 1886 T.Kirk in 1902 Settler's Handbook of NZ 120]  
> **KIRK** <i>Dacrydium Colensoi</i> Westland pine *(1889,Forest Flora)* [NOTES: (And)]  
> Silver or Westland Pine, Manoao, (D. Westlandium). *(1889,Canterbury (NZ): its resources and progress,52)*  
> Pine, Westland .. Dacrydium Westlandicum .. Timber of a yellowish tint, straight, tough, elastic; ... <i>Ibid.</i> 62 Head piece, Westland pine. *(1890,PWD Catalogue of Timbers,13)*  
> **MATTHEWS** <i>Dacrydium Colensoi</i> Westland pine *(1905,Tree Culture in NZ)* [NOTES: (And)]  
> **COCKAYNE** ... the Westland pine (<i>Dacrydium Colensoi</i> ), the <i>D. Westlandium</i> of the `Forest Flora' [Kirk], and the yellow-pine (<i>S. intermedium</i>). *(1910,NZ Plants,41)*  
> <i>Dacrydium colensoi</i> Westland pine *(1911,TrNZI,43)* [NOTES: (And)]  
> the Westland pine (<i>Dacrydium Colensoi</i>), which is not uncommon along the west coast of the South Island, is found in the North Island at a few widely separated spots ... *(1911,TrNZI,43/202)*

### Westland quintinia
  
noun.  
> The other illustrations include the yellow kowhai, the different varieties of rata and the pohutukawa, the New Zealand rubus, the geum, two varieties of quintinia or native lilac, the elegant tawari, the weiumanuia, the drosera arcturi, or "Alpine sundew." [sic] the beautiful and too little appreciated manuka or ti-tree, four varieties of myrtle, and the maire tauhake. *(May 11, 1889,Te Aroha News,3)*  
> The Westland silver-pine, yellow-silver pine and quintinia, although not peculiar to Westland, are more abundant there than in any other part of the country ... *(May 1, 1907,Progress,255)*  
> **DENNIS** Most prominent among the broadleaf species  here [in Westland] are toro (<i>Myrsine salicina</i>), Westland quintinia (<i>Q. acutifolia</i>) ... stinkwood  (<i>C. foetidissima,?i>) ... and pate (<i>Schefflera digitata</i>) with hutu (<i>Ascarina</i> [158]<i> lucida</i>) .. *(1981,Paparoas Guide,157)*

### Westland silver pine
  
noun.  
[NOTES: <i>Manoao colensoi</i> aka manoao,bog pine, silver pine, yellow pine, etc; previously <i>Dacrydium colensoi</i>]  
> <i>Dacrydium Colensoi</i> Westland silver pine *(1908,AJHR,C-14)* [NOTES: (And) Cockayne Report on a Botanical Survey of Waipoua Kauri Forest]  
> Westland silver-pine. yellow silver-pine, and quintinia, although not peculiar to Westland, are more abundant there than in any other part of the colony; while the southern rata, which extends to Stewart Island, is especially plentiful in the Tautuku Forest, ... *(1984,NZ Official Yearbook,330)* [NOTES: (T. Kirk)]  
> On the table-land between the edges of the forest and the base of Ngaruhoe large patches of good pines occurred, mixed with fine kaikawaka, totara, Westland silver pine, broad leaf, &c. . . *(Jan 18, 1886,Wanganui Herald,2)*  
> From a private letter from Westland, the New Zealand Herald learns that during the examination of the Westland orests by Captain Campbell Walker and Mr. T. Kirk, F.L.S., those gentlemen have discovered a valuable new timber tree, a kind of <i>Dacrydium</i> related to the manaoa [sic]of the North Island.   It has been largely exported to Melbourne under the name of Westland silver pine, and is highly valued for its great durability. *(Aug 7, 1877,West Coast Times,2)*

### Westlander
  
noun.  
> **HEINZ** I saw a ghastly array of departed and altogether forgotten West Coasters on this stormy night off Paringa. In the morning I found that the Something which conjured up these ghastly visions, taking the shape of many Westlanders in spirit land, was the steward's towel hanging on the cabin door. *(1974,Bright Fine Gold,125)* [NOTES: 1887 H. Richardson Rae Pencillings by Land and Sea in 1974 Heinz Bright Fine Gold 125]

### Westport
  
noun. Westport coal  
[NOTES: (?obsolesc)]  
> **WILLIAMS & REEVE** And foreign ships should never go/ Without a good supply of `Westport'. *(1891,Double Harness,45)*

### Westport weed
  
noun. <i>Cotula dioica </i>  
>  *(Jan 1946,Journal of NZ Inst. Hort.,15(2):11)*  
> <i>Westport weed [=] cotula dioica [s] </i> *(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,85)*

### westyism
  
noun.  
See: also Westieism  
> "the dirty finger of westyism creeping ever closer to the heart of the city". *(May 1997,Metro,8)*

### wet
  
adjective. inexperienced, simple; weak

### wet
  
adjective.  
[NOTES: `declare them wet' - of sheep; sense of wet from weather etc, as opp to in milk]  
> That no shearer be compelled to shear wet sheep or cancered sheep. *(Aug 17, 1904,Otago Witness,21)*  
> <i>declare them wet: </i>When there is the question of whether the sheep are too wet to shear, a vote is taken after each man shears two sheep. If more vote wet than dry they `declare them wet', and no-one shears. If the vote is even or in favour of the dry the shearing continues. *(1989,NZ English Newsletter,3/23)* [NOTES: (Wallace Shearing Glossary)]  
> That if the sheep were wet the defendant does not think it was part of the plaintiff's duty to shear them, nor to pick out dry sheep from the pens in order to proceed with the work. *(Jan 25, 1912,Marlborough Express,3)*

### wet
  
noun. a gaffe, faux-pas; a blue  
[NOTES: ca1951 Wellington (H.S. Gajadhar) (of a glaring error) How's this for a wet?]

### wet
  
noun. a beer  
> `Right-oh!' said Bill, `bring him in [to the bar] and we'll give him a wet.' *(Jul 10, 1919,Quick March,21)*  
> **BEATTIE** ... Pat and his brother Dan,/ Who greeted me with smiles,/ `Come and have a wet, old man.' *(1956,Pioneer Recollections,55)* [NOTES: 1947-1948 Beattie Pioneer Recollections (1956) 55]  
> **HOGAN** One day six bushmen .../ Called in [to the pub] to have a wet. *(1962,Billy-Can Ballads,5)*  
> **SLATTER** And the proper form and ceremony after having a few wets was to have a binder and they stumbled by instinct to the hash house ... *(1968,Pagan Game, The,165)*

### wet
  
adjective. of sheep, in milk either pregnant or suckling  
[NOTES: ca1952 Auckland farmers speak of wet sheep, meaning they're in milk.]  
> After the dinner hour the pens were refilled with sheep which were wet. *(Feb 27, 1890,Otago Witness,19)*

### wet
  
adjective.  
[NOTES: re liquor licensing]  
> [Hdg] Ashburton asks for "WetHotels". *(Aug 6, 1945,NZEF Times,IV/215 : 8)*

### wet area
  
noun. area in which alcoholic liquor was permitted to be sold  
[NOTES: Jim Cameron writes: NSOD gives the general sense, but the words (wet area) and several compounds have considerable historic significance in NZ from the 1890s almost up to the the present. DNZE does not record these - <i>wet/dry area, district, vote; go wet, stay dry</i>. NZPOD has no entry for wet in this sense but does for dry.]  
> **STEAD** Established by plebiscite at election time in New Zealand there were 'wet' areas where pubs could be built and liquor sold, and 'dry' areas where they could not. In the case of Henderson the divider between wet and dry was the local railway line, and Mr Assid Corban, a Lebanese who had planted vines and was making wine, found his house and sheds on the dry side of the tracks. This problem he solved by buying tom the railways a very small earth-floored concrete shelter, only a few yards from his house but across the tracks on the wet side. From there he began selling wine in flagons to passing motorists. *(1999,Talking about O'Dwyer,19)*  
> Men were pushed out of the suburbs and into "wet" areas, and the six o'clock swill. *(Sep 19, 1987,Listener (New Zealand Listener),31)*

### wet as a shag
  
phrase.  
> **SLATTER** I'd like to see them wet as a shag with bent aching back washing down carcases ... *(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,67)*  
> **TURNER** There is simile: `as mad as a snake', `as bald as a bandicoot' (an embroidery on the English `bald as a coot'), `as happy as larry', `rough as bags' (which I know better in the variant `rough as sacks'), `as wet as a shag'. *(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,115)*  
> **WICKHAM** Both were as "wet as shags" after their tussle with the would-be suicide and the waves. *(1897,Casual Ramblings,99)*  
> **BREMER** One of these said "Wet as a shag, Eric Mason." *(1983,Port Craig,29)*

### wet bus ticket
  
noun. [informal] especially in the phrase a slap on the wrist (or hand) with a wet bus ticket: a merely token rebuke or punishment  
> The Onehunga probation officer, who belittled the warning as a "slap over the wrist with a wet bus ticket", is now defying departmental instructions not to speak out on the issue. *(Jul 1, 2005,Dominion Post,A4)*  
> Everyone, Frank argued, would agree with Middleton getting "a slap with a wet bus ticket". *(Mar 3, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),40)*  
> Marshall was cited for an alleged stomp on Kelleher's hand but given a slap over the hand with a wet bus ticket. *(Jul 13, 2001,Evening Post,20)*  
> The New Zealand Institute of Architects (NZIA) and the Architects Education and Registration Board (AERB) are separately constituted but overlapping bodies established under the 1963 Architects Act... Perhaps because expulsion from the NZIA is the architectural equivalent of a slap on the wrist with a wet bus ticket, the institute prefers to pass all serious complaints.. on to the AERB. Notes *(Feb 1997,North & South,91)*  
> All this threatens to turn Bollard from orchestra conductor into something more akin to a blind knight waving a wet bus ticket at an unseen enemy. *(Jan 22, 2005,Listener (New Zealand Listener),15)*  
> You'd think the board of Southern Cross would have taken some notice of the slap it got from the commission's wet bus ticket, and at least not sail so close to the wind next time. *(Sep 27, 2003,Dominion Post,C2)*  
> The trustees rely on the consultants, the consultants want to keep sweet with the fund managers so apply wet bus-ticket discipline and the fund managers invest to satisfy the consultant's simplified checklist of criteria. *(Jan 24, 2004,Dominion Post,C2)*  
> Grimshaw barely gave Knox a slap on the wrist with a wet bus-ticket, just a coyly moist tut-tut about her ultra-violet prose. *(Apr 19, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),8)*  
> I emailed an American sports writer this week to se if he could explain the apparent lack of anything other than a wet bus ticket to persecute drug cheats... To avoid these unseemly confrontations cluttering the team during the season, the leagues and the players' associations come up with the wet bus ticket penalties and try to avoid the lawsuits and ensuing fallout to ensure both sponsors and team money men keep the tills ticking over. *(Nov 5, 2003,Capital Times,18)*

### wet canteen
  
noun.  
> [Heard in the canteen - no! not the wetone.] *(Aug 30, 1916,Chronicles NZEF,I/1 : 10)*

### wet claim
  
noun.  
[NOTES: aka wet-claim; cf dredging claim, beach claim, etc.]  
> I do not understand, then, how in justice wet claims, worked by hand labor, should not be on a footing, so far as dimensions go, with those that are to be worked by machinery in prospective. *(Mar 4, 1862,Otago Daily Times,4)*  
> Under the heading "wet claims," the Warden has the power, upon receipt of a requisition signed by not less than three-fourths of the claimholders affected, to declare the "wet rules" applicable to any particular district, and in like manner the Warden can suspend the operations of those regulations, upon receiving a similarly signed memorial. *(Oct 3, 1873,Grey River Argus,2)*  
> Of course, theirs was a "wet claim," while ours was too dry, because we had to carry our wash-dirt to the stream, when we got any. *(May 13, 1911,Hawera & Normanby Star,9)*

### Wet Coaster
  
noun.  
> Billie's a We't Coaster. *(Nov 15, 1916,Chronicles NZEF,I/6 : 134)*

### wet diggings
  
noun.  
See: cf. dry diggings  
[NOTES: goldmining]  
> **HOCHSTETTER** The conglomerates accumulated on the slopes of the mountains are the proper field for the "dry diggings", while from the gravel and sand streams the gold is obtained by "wet diggings". *(1867,New Zealand (Hochstetter),104)*  
> Work in that country in wet diggings was not over pleasant, as old New Zealand diggers will well understand;  up to the middle all the day in water, and generally in a dripping state. *(Jul 30, 1881,Tuapeka Times,1)*  
> **VON HAAST** He explained to his readers that there were two kinds of diggings, the `wet' and the `dry'. The first were in the bed of the river, the water of which had often to be diverted in order that the diggers might wash the gold taken out. If these diggings offer a larger profit to those who strike the right spot, they have the disadvantage, that the necessary labour is hard and unhealthy, for the diggers must often stand breast-high in the cold water from the mountains. ... The dry diggings, which generally require more capital and strength, are to be found mostly on the heights or in the higher diluvium. The water must often be brought long distances in sluices. The earth containing the gold is thrown into the sluices, in which a double floor with numerous bored openings lie. *(1948,Julius Von Haast)* [NOTES: [c1860 <i>Weiner Zeitung (Vienna) Nos 57-65 in German] in 1948 Von Haast Sir Julius Von Haast 35 Weiner Zeitung</i>, 1860]]  
> **Richardson** &#65279;In the winter, the same has been saidconcerning the summer, - "See how the wetdiggings will pay in the summer time." *(1862,Sketch of Otago,48)*

### wet dry
  
noun. a ewe that has conceived a lamb and has lost it at, or prior to, birth [rarely through abandonment.]  
> J of Ag Dec 463 .. increasing lambing percentage by using recommended techniques such as culling dry-dry and wet-dry ewes and shearing two-tooths before tupping. *(1964)*  
> NZ Farmer July 22. 17 Wet-dries are no better than dry-dries; in fact they make more work. They'll be wet-dries again next year if they can. *(1971)*  
> NZ Farmer June 1. 22 Last season they recorded 7.2% ewe deaths over winter and lambing, wet dries were 7.8% dry dry 1.7% and 15% lamb deaths. *(1994)*  
> KERR High Times in the High Country 41 'Get the dry dries, the wet dries and the late wets' .. I asked Nimmo to translate. The first were fat barren ewes destined for harder territory, the second were those who had lost their lambs destined for harder territory .. but I figured 'late wets' were late lambers who'd want a wee spell before their lambs were docked. *(2000)*  
> Country-Wide June 17 The following year, 3340 ewes docked with lambs but there were 728 wet-dries in the flock. *(2001)*  
> Dominion Nov 8. 6 Store sheep (700): Mixed-age wet-dry ewes $75 .. . [wet-dry] *(2001)*

### wet ewe
  
noun. an in-lamb ewe, or one newly lambed.  
> J of Ag Dec 14 Shearing dry ewes then on to wet ewes. *(1916)*  
> J of Ag April 20. 221 Wet ewes will require watching carefully .. Shearing of wet ewes will start this month. *(1922)*  
> MCKENNY Sheep-Farming, Fencing 14 Another good plan is to shear all wethers, hoggets, and dry ews first and to defer, somewhat, the shearing of the wet ewes. Rough weather causes wet ewes to dry up in the milk to a great extent. *(1927)*

### wet ewe
  
noun.  
> When shearing wet ewes, the shed-managermay alter the smokes and extend thehours. *(1906,Awards etc.,VII/625)*

### wet house
  
noun.  
[NOTES: Google search Feb 2006: also used in, in order of frequency, UK, Canada, Austral, S Africa.]  
> [She] suggested a wet house/hostel accommodation be provided where alcohol consumption is monitored and access to health care workers and security is readily avilable... *(Jan 24, 2006,Capital Times,3)*  
> [he] says a wet house will not "magically make street drinking go away" but it will mean there is a home for them to return to. *(Feb 15, 2006,Capital Times,5)*

### wet one's shamrock
  
phrase. to celebrate (? originally St Patrick's Day), a var. of <i>wet one's whistle </i>  
> **GREY** It was time for [the Irishmen] to depart days before they could `wet their shamrock' on the date facetiously known as the seventeeth of Ireland. *(1879,His Island Home,32)*

### wet or dry
  
phrase. an early workers' expression indicating that wages should be paid whether the weather is wet (and no work can be done) or dry  
> It was paid ill or well, wet or dry, and was earned with a trifling amount of labour. *(Nov 25, 1853,Daily Southern Cross,3)*  
> The men are insisting on the present rates to be paid wet or dry. *(Apr 16, 1918,Hawera & Normanby Star,8)*  
> The price is now lower [in a wage claim] and may be called a pound a day wet or dry. *(1865,Evening Post,40)*  
> **D'AVIGDOR** Surveying work for two guineas a week, wet or dry, which is an important qualification ... *(1888,Antipodean Notes,226)*  
> **MONEY** We received 10s. per day, `wet or dry,' <i>i.e.</i>, whether we were working or not, and provisions at Government prices. *(1871,Knocking About in NZ,69)*

### wet powder
  
noun.  
> **GRADY** <i>Wet powder</i> - Something all whalers try to avoid. On occasions, the harpoon practically fell out of the gun when the powder was wet. Keeping your powder dry was a must that applied particularly to whaling from small fast boats that were subjected to spray, waves and wet weather. *(1982,Perano Whalers,228)* [NOTES: [glossary]]

### wet shearing
  
noun. removing wool from ''wet'' ewes, either pre-lamb or just after lambing.  
> SQUIRE A to Z Glossary of Wool Terms 41 Wet shearing - Shearing of the wet ewes i.e. ewes with lambs at foot and 'in milk' or whose lambs have not long been weaned. *(1991)*

### wet sheep
  
noun. ewes in lamb, or capable of lambing  
> **WHEELER** The first round of dry sheep (wethers, hoggets and rams) and second round of wet sheep (ewes and lambs) each require seven days' shearing, ... *(1973,Historic Sheep Stations NI,70)*

### wet sheep
  
noun.  
> He had had 12 year's experience, and had never been asked to shear wet sheep. *(Feb 27, 1890,Otago Witness,19)*  
> **MEEK** WET SHEEP: When it has been decided that the sheep are too wet to shear. *(1952,Station Days in Maoriland,111)*  
> **SCRYMGEOUR** Conferences would be held, a slight glistening on the fibre of the fleece, when sunlight decreed `wet sheep' and then the shearers would declare a break, whilst the `loppies', paid wet or fine, would be put on varying jobs to fill in the time until the Lords and Masters of the situation agreed to work. *(1960,Memories of Maoriland,49)*

### wet stock
  
noun. breeding stock.  
> J of Ag April 16. 349 The best means of ensuring adequate supplies of good-quality wet-stock feed during July and August is by closing large areas of pasture during May. It is good practice to hold ewes tight this month and use the pasture saved for early lambing ewes ... *(1956)*

### wet stock country
  
noun.  
> **LEVY** Aerial topdressing also makes possible, on relatively clean country, the translation of dry stock country to wet stock country by its promo-[259]tion of richer growth ... *(1951,Grasslands of NZ,258,1970)*

### wet stock farming
  
noun. stock-breeding or dairy farming.  
> Connell & Hadfield Agriculture 3 There was ushered in the change from 'dry' stock farming to 'wet' stock farming, a change that has continued and widened in scope up to the present time..New Zealand's success in this 'wet' stock farming is largely due to its mild winters and ample rainfall ideal for pasture growth.. *(1961)*  
> ibid 4 The first export of frozen meat from Dunedin. This resulted in New Zealand farmers changing from mainly 'dry' stock farming to mainly 'wet' stock farming. *(1961)*

### wet wool
  
noun.  
> If wet wool were pressed it would take heat and rot on shipboard. *(Feb 27, 1890,Otago Daily Times,20)*

### weta
  
noun. a large fearsome-looking insect of the cricket family, with long spiny legs.  
[NOTES: Maori weta[e-macron]] <i>Deinacrida</i> spp esp. <i>D. megacephala</i>, a native cricket often thought to be of frightening appearance, jimmy nipper, sawyer, scorpion, taipo, wood-devil (see also alpine-, cave-, rock-, tree-weta) pron. usually [wEt ] (?from spg cf weka), and often (?North Island) [weit ] (from Maori)]  
> **IHIMAERA & LONG (ed.)** We local kids used to get up to all kinds like.. leaving dead wetas beside the seat in the Ladies. *(1982,Into the World of Light,80)*  
> I caught a glimpse of Peter, head down, slaving over a barbecue as a weta tentatively peeked out of the bushes, perhaps hoping for a scallop or two. *(Mar 25, 2000,Dominion,23)*  
> The weta is one of the common denizens of bush and scrub-clad localities in New Zealand. *(Jan 25, 1905,Otago Witness,42)*  
> New Zealand may be regarded as the home of the weta, as more species are found here than in any other country, and most of the species have many individuals. *(May 14, 1915,Hawera & Normanby Star,8)*  
> There had been a terrible calamity:  the wetas' trees had been chopped down. *(Feb 9, 1924,Evening Post,15)*  
> **HOCHSTETTER** The oddest one of the New Zealand insects is among the Orthopterae the Weta. *(1867,New Zealand (Hochstetter),170)*  
> **LEE & KENDALL** W|eta,<i> s</i>. An insect so called. W|eta punga; ditto. *(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,227)*  
> **DIEFFENBACH** Weta - an insect so called. *(1843,New Zealand (various authors/titles),2/396)*  
> **WILLIAMS** The notable Weta about the size of a Cockroach when full grown, has long forked legs, bites severely if molested, leaving a troublesome wound. *(1844,NZ Journal,112,1956)*  
> **TAYLOR** Weta - a large insect. *(1848,Leaf from Natural History)*  
> **HURSTHOUSE** The weta, a suspicious-looking, scorpion-like creature, apparently replete with `high concocted venom', but perfectly harmless. *(1857,New Zealand (various authors/titles),1/123)*  
> **HAAST** The weta (Deinacrida heteracantha), belonging to the order Orthoptera, is another inhabitant of the forests or manuka groves; it is a very ugly looking animal, and often of a large size. *(1861,Report on Topographical Exploration of Nelson,145)*  
> We have, too, one of the ugliest-looking creatures that I have ever seen. It is called `weta', and is of tawny scorpion-like colour, with long antennae and great eyes, and nasty squashy-looking body, with (I think) six legs. *(1863,First Year in Canterbury Settlement,ch ix 141 [OED ix 441])*  
> Would you be good enough to tell me.. whether the 'weta,' the brown scorpion-like insect found in the Auckland province is poisonous or not? *(Dec 24, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,15)*  
> **BULLER** The <i>Weta</i> is a forest cricket, three inches long. *(1878,Forty Years in NZ,499)*  
> **HAY** [Hay possibly in error for <i>huhu</i>] The loathsome larva of the weta, a large white grub, is speared on a stick, toasted at the fire, and eaten with a silent rapture that my pen could only feebly pourtray [sic]. <i>Ibid.</i> 2/229 The Weta (<i>Deinacrida heteracantha</i>) is a creature of the locust form living in dead-wood. *(1882,Brighter Britain,2/151)*  
> Not a sound was heard in that lonely forest, except at long intervals the sharp noise produced by the <i>weta. </i> *(1888,TrNZI,21/41)* [NOTES: (J. Adams) [Morris]]  
> **BULLER** Rahui informed us that this [manuka grove] was a favourite resort of the Huia when feeding on the weta or tree-cricket <i>(Deinacrida thoracica). </i> *(1888,Birds of New Zealand,1/12)*  
> **BARLOW** Another insect very commonly found in soft wood tree [sic] is called by the natives the `Weta,' but by vulgar little boys `The Jimmy Nipper.' *(1888,Kaipara and Waitemata Echo,187)*  
> Not a sound was heard in the lonely forest, except at long intervals the sharp noise produced by the <i>weta ... </i> *(1889,TrNZI,21/41)*  
> Its beak is more suitable for grubbing after ... larvae ... such as the grub of the Weta of the  Natives <i>(Deinacrida) ... </i> *(1891,NZ Journal of Science,1/199)*  
> Captain Fairchild collected ... [on Bounty Island] specimens of one of those wingless locusts called weta by the Maoris - by which name they are known to all New-Zealanders. live together in considerable numbers. *(1895,TrNZI,27/174)*  
> Here they [<i>Stenopelmatidae</i>] are generally known by their Maori name of <i>weta.</i> ... Most of them are solitary, but the cave-wetas live together in considerable numbers. *(1897,TrNZI,29/208)* [NOTES: (Hutton)]  
> **MORRIS** <i>Weta, n.</i> Maori name for a New Zealand insect - a huge, ugly grasshopper, <i>Deinacrida megacephala</i>, called by bushmen the <i>Sawyer. </i> *(1898,Austral English,507)*  
> **DOBSON** Insects of all kinds were making weird noises, the cicadas would set up a shrill trill, and the wetas made a loud creaking sound, as they called to each other from time to time. *(1930,Reminiscences,150)*  
> <i>Note on</i> Hemideina megacephala <i>Buller</i>. - These remarkable insects (the taipo, or weta, of the Maori) are generally said to feed only on vegetation. *(1932,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,13/46)*  
> There are at least 30 species of wetas in New Zealand. *(Feb 4, 1933,Auckland Star)* [NOTES: (A.T.Bycroft's column: `Wetas, or Wingless Crickets')]  
> <i>we[e]ta</i>, an insect (Deinacrida megacephala), brown-banded, the male huge-headed, nocturnal insect, quite innocuous but much disliked through through drink. *(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/160)* [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]  
> <i>weta:</i> large wingless locust *(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,176)*  
> **SARGESON** (gloss.) weta: insect like a large grasshopper. *(1949,I Saw in My Dream,273,1974 ed. Rhodes)*  
> <i> weta n</i> -s [Maori] : any of various large wingless long-horned insects (family Stenopelmatidae) of New Zealand; esp : a large clumsy insect (<i>Deinacrida heteracantha</i>) measuring four inches in length *(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,2598)*  
> **KEENE** <i>weta</i>: large fearsome-looking insect; its body grows to 2 in. in length, and its antennae up to 4 in. Cave and giant <i>weta</i> are even bigger. *(1963,O Te Raki,195)*  
> **SARGESON** weta: a fearsome-looking bush insect without wings usually two or three inches long. `To strike an enormous colony in the bush (hundreds and hundreds) will give you bad dreams.' (F.S.) *(1964,Collected Stories,304)*  
> <i>weta ... n</i>. N.Z. large ugly grasshopper [Maori] *(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),21)*  
> **McLINTOCK** WETAS (<i>Deinacrida </i>spp., <i>Pachyrhamma</i> spp., <i>Hemideina </i> spp., etc.). These wingless orthopteran insects are well represented in New Zealand and are found in the forest and in caves. ... In New Zealand the Maori name `weta' is popularly applied to insects of the Order Orthoptera, which belong to two families, the Henicidae of Karny 1937, and the Rhaphidophoridae of Kirby 1883. *(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/636)*  
> <i>weta n.</i> New Zealand large ugly grasshopper [Maori] *(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),628)*  
> **BAXTER** And if in Paremata you/ Should find a weta in your shoe,/ Ugly , hard-shelled, with snapping jaws,/... Don't hit it with a shovel - No,/ Christen it JIM and let it go. *(1979,Collected Poems,429,1968)*  
> **BAXTER** weta <i>bush insect </i> *(1982,Selected Poems,190)*  
> <i>weta n</i>. NZ large wingless long-horned grasshopper that bores wood at grub stage. [M] *(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,877)* [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 544]]  
> **ANDERSON** There are four kinds [of wetas]: the <i>bush-weta, </i> the <i>cave-weta</i>, smaller in body with very long legs and feelers - the rock-weta found under rocks on mountain sides, <i>ground-weta</i>, as small as a cricket and found buried under the soil. ... The male has a huge horny mandibled head, the female a long stout ovipositor which has been stigmatised as a sting and earned for the insect the undeserved names, scorpion, and herself and her mate wood-devil and taipo. *(1942,Maori Place Names,452)*  
> The tyrannical father.. devours everyone and everything "like a weta sucking fruit from the inside". *(Aug 24, 1992,Evening Post,18)*  
> The baby wetas, strong-jawed like their parents, gnawed the young wood and are their way into the centre of the tree. *(Feb 5, 1924,Otago Witness,71)*  
> People squish weta and they shouldn't. They are beautiful creatures... *(Nov 24, 2004,Capital Times,6)*  
> With the help of his French-speaking pathologist sidekick Marie L'Espirit du Mort.. the Captain sets out to track doewn the mysterious Singing Weta. *(Dec 28, 1996,Listener (New Zealand Listener),128)*  
> After Kala is killed by marauding weta hordes, Jane narrowly escapes the same fate, scooped up by Trazan as he swings through the trees with the greatest of ease... Jane returns to the Wairarapa where she is devoured by rampant weta)... *(Jun 5, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),65)*

### weta
  
noun.

### weta
  
noun. a contraction of PUTAPUTAWETA <i>Carpodetus serratus </i>  
See: putaputaweta, marbleleaf  
[NOTES: plant]  
> ) (And) <i>Carpodetus serratus </i> *(1916,NZ Association of Nurserymen, Trade Register)*

### weta condominium
  
noun. a viewable weta nest in the Karori wildlife sanctuary  
See: weta motel  
[NOTES: -]  
> Nigel Young, our guide, says kids love the old goldmine (where they can explore in hard hats), the weta condominium, and the birdsong grove. *(Sep 20, 2005,NZ Herald,D8)*

### weta motel
  
noun. an artificial  weta refuge  
See: weta condominium  
> Weta motels.. are untreated hollow wooden blocks simulating cavities found in mature trees; and wooden discs.. simulate fallen logs found in mature native forests. These refuges are used by weta, ground beetles, spiders, snails, slugs, flatworms, and even skinks. *(Nov 2002,Forest & Bird,306/42)*  
> ..the tours are particularly popular with families who relish the chance to see tuatara basking in the sun, reintroduced kakariki chattering like canaries, weta congregating in their tree trunk "motels"... *(May 2004,Footnotes,No. 21/4)*

### wetapunga
  
noun.  
See: also weta  
> The insect fauna is varied, with its most distinctive representative, the wetapunga - a giant armour-plated cricket with crayfish-like legs - now found nowhere else in New Zealand. *(Jan 2002,NZ Geographic,16)*  
> **BULLER** Judge Munro informs me that ... he found in [a morepork's] stomach a specimen of the weta-punga, or tree-cricket (<i>Deinacrida heteracantha</i>), with a body as large as a magnum-bonum plum; ... *(1888,Birds of New Zealand,1/195)*  
> [The Maoris] distinguish it [<i>Deinacrida heteracantha</i>] as the Wetapunga. *(1895,TrNZI,27/145)*  
> This species [Deinacrida heteracantha] is the weta-punga of the Maoris. *(1897,TrNZI,29/213)*  
> The first evidences of that ... insect, <i>Deinacrida heteracantha (weta punga</i> of the Maori), were seen on a rock used by the harrier as a table. *(1925,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,8/16)* [NOTES: (Hamilton Poor Knights)]

### wetawetangu
  
noun.  
> **BULLER** SPATULA {RHYNCAPSIS} VARIEGATA. (NEW-ZEALAND SHOVELLER.) ... <i>Native name</i>. Tete, {Pateke,} Pu*taitai, Kuriwhengi, {Kuruwhengu, Papaungungu,} Kahoho, and Wetawetangu; `Spoon-bill Duck' of the colonists. *(1873,Birds of New Zealand,252,1888 2nd edn 2/269)*

### wetback
  
noun.  
[NOTES: wet-back, wet back; incl attrib]  
> **BAKER** A wetback for hot water. *(2000,Kokopu Dreams,35)*  
> A free-standing Metro woodburner with a wetback has been a great success at the heart of the house. *(Feb 8, 2003,Dominion Post,F16)*  
> **Philippa Swan** Erica had thought this some sort of spider, but no, apparently a wetback was something to do with the fireplace... *(2006,Six Pack, The,53)*  
> **LELAND** <i>wetback</i>: ... A wetback is a set of pipes or a <i>hot water drum attached to</i> the back or bottom of a <i>fire</i>place or solid fuel stove. *(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,111)*  
> **McCAULEY** In case the wet-back explodes, being empty of water and the fire still hot with embers, she gets the kids to carry out the water-catching pots from inside while she stands on a chair and empties them into the top of the tank *(1986,Then Again,78)*  
> COPPER Wetback Grate, as new. *(Oct 18, 1940,Evening Post,1)*  
> **OWEN & PERKINS1** The stove had a wetback and a hot water system. *(1986,Speaking for Ourselves,130)*  
> Do they call it a wetback because the water runs down the inside of the airing cupboard?" our son asked the plumber who came to do things to the flashing. We wouldn't let him ask why they called it flashing. *(Jul 22, 1989,Listener (New Zealand Listener),32)*

### wet-ball tramper
  
noun.  
[NOTES: (Wellington trampers' nonce) ca1951 NCC Small <i>Wet-ball trampers</i> are those who plough through anything - not minding the discomfort of mud and water: <i>dry-ball trampers</i> are the sybaritic type who mince through muck.]

### wet-bird
  
noun. rain-bird, shearwater, ? laughing jackass, hakoakoa  
> **HUTTON** 125. PUFFINUS GAVIUS. Forst. Rain-bird or Wet-bird. Shearwater. Laughing Jackass(?). Hakoakoa. <i>P. opisthomelas</i>. COUES. <i>P. assimilis.</i> HUTTON *(1871,Birds of New Zealand,45)*

### wetboot man
  
noun.  
> **McGILL** <i> wetboot man </i>bureaucrat, as identified within the outdoor ranks of New Zealand forestry and wildlife staff. *(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,122)*

### wet-chucker-in
  
noun.  
> **BREMNER** A description of an unidentified sheepstation in 1881 recorded that a twenty-two man gang ... put through [the sheep-wash] 1250 sheep in a ... day. The `yarder-up' kept the pen nearest the wash full of sheep. Two `dry-chuckers-in' forced sheep through a swing door where they fell four feet into a 20-foot wooden trough full of hot soapy water. ... The trough was divided into four partitions with a `crutcher' at each. Four times each sheep was soaped, its back scrubbed with a crutch. ... At the end of the trough three `wet-chuckers-in' hauled frightened sheep out and sent them down three slides to the cold-water rinsing wells. Each well had two spouts of cold water and four hands called `spouters' who tilted the sheep, parting the wool ... *(1985,Woolscours of NZ,15)* [NOTES: [1881] 1985 Bremner Woolscours of NZ 15]

### wether
  
noun.  
[NOTES: see also wedder]  
> These wethers you mentioned, were they 'wet' wethers or 'dry' wethers? *(May 11, 1912,NZ Free Lance,14)*  
> There perhaps 30 metres away stood an big old merino-cross wether, carrying, at the least, a double fleece, the dirty grey colour of which explained my missing his presence among the jumbled summit rocks. *(Sep 2000,North & South,138)*  
> 'Quarter-back wethers' sold for 10s each. *(Jul 12, 1890,NZ Observer,9)*  
> **OGONOWSKA-COATES** <i>Ages of sheep.</i> Although there are many variations, the following names are usually used: A sheep is a <i>lamb</i> until weaned. After weaning a lamb is called a<i> hogget</i> until shorn. A male lamb is a<i> ram lamb</i> until castrated, then a <i>wether</i>. A female lamb is a <i>ewe lamb</i>. After shearing, a ewe or ram lamb is usually called a <i>shorn hogget </i>until cutting the first two teeth. It is then called a<i> two-tooth.</i> Some use the term two-tooth as soon as the lamb is shorn. Next year the sheep is a <i>four-tooth,</i> then a <i>six-tooth</i> and then an<i> eight-tooth </i>or<i> full-mouthed</i> sheep. It is full-mouthed as long as the teeth remain perfect but then becomes broken-mouthed and gummy. *(1987,Boards, Blades & Barebellies,94)*  
> At that time I thought a wether was a little bit of a climate. *(Aug 12, 2001,Sunday Star Times,C4)*  
> **McGEE** Like how to tell the difference between wet and dry ewes. It's not just the wether, is it? *(1981,Foreskin's Lament,33)*

### wether block
  
noun. a part of a station used for grazing of non-breeding stock [requriting little attention.]  
> HENDERSON Open Country Calling 50 I had gone out alone on skis to look for sheep on the Burnt Face, our old wether block soon to be retired from grazing by the Forest Service. *(1969)*  
> NEWTON In the Wake of the Axe 7 In earlier days Orongorongo was run in with Tora Station and was then used solely as a wether block. *(1972)*  
> KERR High Times in the High Country 196 When Ian Anderson bought Ben Ledi he commandeered us to muster his wether block. *(2000)*

### wether country
  
noun. [Rural] outlying grazing land for non-breeding sheep  where little attention is given.  
> Rutherford used Benmore as wether country for Dalethorpe ... *(1930,BLANK ENTRY,241)* [NOTES: in ACLAND (1951) Early Canterbury Runs]  
> Half-bred ewe and wether country. - On this grade there is a considerable area of rough country suitable only for wethers. *(Feb 20, 1937,NZ Journal of Agriculture,100)*  
> **NEWTON** This block is the first day's muster on the wether country. *(1971,Ten Thousand Dogs,15)*  
> **GORDON** There are three different classes of country. Ewe and hogget country is fairly safe all year round, winter wether country is safe for that time of the year and summer wether country is safe only in the warmer months, when there's little chance of heavy snow. *(1987,People, Places & Paddocks,46)*  
> **HALL** It occurred when we were mustering the Mt Burke wether country between Lakes Wanaka and Hawea. *(1987,Shepherd's Year, A,70)*

### wether paddock
  
noun. a fenced grazing area for non-breeding sheep where little attention is required.  
> TAIT Farms & Stations of NZ III 42 Sherwood was a portion of the wether paddock of Lavington. *(1961)*

### wether run
  
noun. [Rural] a grazing property for non-breeding stock where little attention is required.  
> **MCLEOD** It was used mainly as a wether run and the ewes belonging to Studholme and McAlpine were run on Craigieburn and Grasmere across the river. *(1970,Many a Glorious Morning,38)*  
> **GORDON** .. with a lot of the due north country and without the 2000 ha of good sunny hill country up the Ahuriri, this would basically be a wether run. *(1987,People, Places & Paddocks,46)*

### wether station
  
noun. a property separate from a main run where non-breeding stock like wethers are kept, with little attention.  
> in ACLAND (1951) Early Canterbury Runs 254 Macfarlane used Whiterock as a wether station and took the wethers on to Coldstream where he fattened them for the coast. *(1930)*  
> HAWKINS Behind the Waimakariri 103 Whiterock then became almost entirely a wether station, the wethers being raised there and fattened at Coldstream for the butchers or for export to the Westland goldfields. *(1957)*

### wet-timer
  
noun.  
> **McGILL** They even put on a special train [on the Ross-Hokitika line], the 868, for the wet-timers, those coalminers who came up half an hour early because they worked in the wet. *(1985,G'day Country,148)*  
> **CHURCHMAN & HURST** The early afternoon train back to Greymouth was know as the 'wet-timer', as miners working in wet parts of the mine worked a shorter shift. *(1990,Railways of NZ,196)*

### wet-tracker
  
noun. a racehorse that prefers wet conditions  
> [Hdg] Wet-tracker tag irks Les... The Trentham trainer is growing tired of claims that his stable star Reg is merely a wet tracker. *(May 16, 2003,Dominion Post RaceForm,20)*  
> After she won her first four in wet stuff, the press thought she was just a wet-tracker. *(Sep 3, 2000,Sunday Star Times,B14)*  
> She's never been and [sic] out-and-out wet-tracker but she's always had a good record in it. *(Sep 3, 2001,Dominion,21)*  
> Peole thought she was only a wet tracker after she won in a bog one day at Te Rapa, but she goes in both [heavy and firm]. *(Dec 7, 2003,Sunday Star Times,B16)*

### we-we
  
noun.  
See: wiwi

### weweia
  
noun.  
> When harassed by enemies in their kaingas at Waikare-Moana they would retreat here and occupy the numerous islands in the lake, drawing their supplies probably from the surrounding forest, for the diminutive maehe is the only fish found in these waters, though wild fowl were formerly numerous, including the whio, maka, weweia and kaha, the latter a large bird which nested in the branches training into the water on the shore line. *(Aug 1, 1898,Auckland  Star,6)*  
> **BULLER** PODICEPS RUFIPECTUS. (NEW-ZEALAND DABCHICK.) ... <i> Native names.-</i> Weweia, Totokipio {, Taihoropi (Hokianga), and Taratimoho (Waikato)}. *(1873,Birds of New Zealand,350,1888 2nd edn 2/280)*  
> **FALLA et al.** DABCHICK <i>Podiceps rufopectus Local names</i>: Diver, Weweia. *(1966,Birds of New Zealand,28)*  
> <i>16. Podiceps rufopectus Gray, 1843 New Zealand Dabchick (Weweia)</i> North Island ... *(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,18,1980)*  
> <i>New Zealand Dabchick Podiceps rufopectus </i> GRAY, 1843 ... OTHER NAMES: Weweia. ... Once the New Zealand dabchick bred in the South Island; *(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,54)*

### whaakaro
  
noun. thoughts  
See: whakaro  
> We are intelligent enough to put forward our whakaaro [thoughts]. *(May 3, 2004,Salient,52)*

### whaariki
  
noun.  
See: whariki  
> Phebe Eruera, of Ngati Tuwharetoa, had a very old whaariki that had been given to her by her grandmother. *(Nov 22, 2005,Whakatane Beacon,4)*

### whack
  
verb. to fall (trees); bushwhack  
> **TURNER** Only eighteen months ago I was whacking some bush on a siding. *(1922,Happy Wanderer,7)*

### whack into
  
verb.  
> However, I whack into anything that offers, and continue to eke out an existence, so don't imagine I'm going to give up yet. *(Apr 12, 1909,Ohinemuri Gazette,3)*  
> The action of the railway authorities in cutting out the dining car on the Rotorua express between Auckland and Mercer and Putararu and Morrinsville is a cruel blow to the cheap feeders who eschew two shilling lunches and make up for the omission by whacking into the generous afternoon and morning teas provided for the modest sum of sixpence. *(Jul 31, 1915,Observer (Auckland),16)*  
> .. and at mid-day Ifeed in town and whack into a plate of soup and the old steak and kidney with the hot gravy. *(Jun 21, 1924,NZ Truth,4)*  
> **SARGESON** whack into (food): attack vigorously. *(1964,Collected Stories,304)* [NOTES: nf P8: 1964 Pearson Glossary]

### whack up
  
verb. knock up (scones, a loaf, etc.), to make and bake (at short notice)  
> She's dying her dresses and sweetly confesses they'll look pretty well when they're inside out;  she's putting up pickles to save a few nickels, she's whacking up cabbage to make into kraut. *(Apr 1, 1913,Northern Advocate,2)*  
> **CRUMP** Now pass me pants and I'll whack up a loaf that your mother'd be proud of. *(1961,Hang on a Minute, Mate,51)*

### whacked
  
adjective. beaten  
> **MASTERS** We got lots of pigs, but try as I did I couldn't get Jim to go in with the holding dogs and stick one, not even a sucker. He reckoned a man needed experience before coming at that sort of thing, though how he reckoned to get it by standing away back and just watching, had me whacked. [175] Just how he knew pigs were up there, or how they had managed to get there, seeing as, according to him, men never had, had me whacked - ... *(1960,Back-Country Tales,85, 175)*

### whacker
  
noun. a bushwhacker  
> **DEVANNY** She flashed Taipo a glance of temper and went over to the other whackers. *(1930,Bushman Burke,288)*

### whacker
  
noun. a piece of wood  
[NOTES: (obs seaman's) -->ca1930's Lyttelton p.c. W. Martin a piece of wood, usually 2x2 or 4x2 (?for fighting scabs with)]

### whacker
  
noun. a mechanical earth or road compactor  
> Two spaceman-like figures driving road compactors (whackers) along the grass verge ... A quarter of a mile ahead of the whacker operators was a square white van packed with electronic equipment ... *(1987,Victoria University Review,3)*

### whacker
  
noun. masturbator  
> **McGILL** <i>whacker</i> wanker or masturbator, used as term of abuse, affection or affectionate abuse; or dismissive of a fool or idiot; from `whack off', to masturbate, not the first antipodean or American variant of an English word for coition. *(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,122)*

### whacko
  
exclamation.  
[NOTES: I cannot remember hearing it before 1941 and then from schoolboys at St Patrick's College, Silverstream, often in the extended form whacko-the-diddle-o! It was not used in Havelock in the 1930s. In 1953 a US Fullbright exchange student thought it `peculiarly and humorously New Zealand'. -->1951 14 M 10 Wellington H.S. 18 WHACKO (WACKO) GEORGE -->1951 14 M 10 Wellington H.S. 18 WHACKO -->1951 14 M 14 St Bede's, Chch 18 WHACKO (WACKO) [M14 6] -->1953 15 M A25 Thames DHS 18 W(H)ACKO [delight; good things M15-16 20] --->1960 16C F B2 Hawera Tech, C. 18 WHACKO -->1960 16C F B5 Wellington Girls C. 18 WHACKO -->1960 17C F B10 Napier Girls H.S. 18 WHACKO ->1984 16 F E7 Pakuranga Coll. 4 WHACKO [something liked F16 3]]  
> **MACKENZIE** We still find some time for our girlfriends and wives,/ `Whacko' when three sixty hourly arrives,/ The Cat boats will not fly tonight! *(1982,WAAF Book,54)* [NOTES: 1943-1944 `CAT BOATS' in 1982 Mackenzie WAAF Book 54]  
> **BENNETT** The exclamation `good!' has become `goodo!', on the analogy of `righto!', and there is even `whacko!', used to express pleasant anticipation (from such phrases as `whacking good time'). *(1943,English as it is spoken in NZ,91)*  
> **BRUNO** Chips let his [mills bomb] go ... Whacko! *(1944,Desert Daze,75)*  
> **SEDDON** I've just begun being a soldier. Wa.a.c.o! [pun on WAAC, Women's Auxiliary Army Corps] *(1945,Whims of a Waaf,2)*  
> **HENDERSON** Whackoh! You ninety-mile snipers, you'll do us. <i>Ibid.</i>139 ` ... oh Christ...[sic] oh whackoh! Oh boy, see that one! That'll show the greasy bastards.' `Oh you beautiful blue orchids, oh you beauties, you'll do us.' Cruuuuump! <i>Ibid.</I14 Packets and packets of whacking great slabs of chocolate. Whacko! *(1945,Gunner Inglorious,3)*  
> (twice) I would include among New Zealand English words: whacko *(May 6, 1952,VUC First Year English Class)*  
> In 1941 there was something mysteriously comradely among artillerymen at Wingatui in greeting one another Whacko! Girls caught on, and the cry became faintly suggestive of sexual expectation. *(Sep 1952,Landfall,23/211)* [NOTES: (Pearson `Fretful Sleepers')]  
> **SLATTER** ... I wave to him rather apologetically. He winks back and sings out, `Whacko, old boy.' *(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,200)*  
> **MUIR** `This time next week I'll be at Surfers. Wacko.' *(1960,Word for Word,203)*  
> **JOSEPH**  *(1960,I'll Soldier No More,271)*  
> **ASHTON-WARNER**  *(1960,Incense to Idols,129)*  
> **HARVEY** Whacko! Christmas Eve. This was the night we were going to make our fortunes cleaning shoes for the Yanks.<i> ibid</i> 37 `Want to come for a look?' When I said, `Whacko, yes!' he said, `Well, you've got to be quiet ...' *(1954,Any Old Dollars Mister?,16)*  
> **McCARTHY** The next day the rain was still belting down and again we said `whacko', no work today. *(1973,Listen...!,142)*  
> ... when the New Zealand Times rang to say he had scooped the Timesword jackpot of $2500. `Whacko,' said Dave, `that makes my day.' *(Aug 18, 1985,NZ Times,1)*  
> **KEINZLY** `Wacko for his old woman. She won't have to duck every time she walks past him.' *(1960,Tangahano,124)*

### whacko the diddle-oh
  
phrase. good, OK  
[NOTES: 1970s B.Manhire University of Otago also 1940s (DEH)]

### whacks
  
noun. corporal punishment, the cane  
[NOTES: 1951 16+ M 26 Wellington H.S. 23 WHACKS also Silverstream 1940s]

### Whaddarya?
  
exclamation. What are you?  
> I dread the next time I have to pretend polite tolerance when some witless parent shouts "Wodd are yer?" at a son or daughter who shows signs of turning the other cheek. *(Feb 10, 2002,Sunday Star Times,C6)* [NOTES: Frank Haden]  
> We don't need to buy those women's mags anymore to keep up with the latest Hollywood gossip. Whadarya? *(Nov 27, 2002,Dominion Post,B6)*  
> At 80mg, you've reached the giggling, who-gives-a-stuff level, the 'I've always noticed I drive better when I'm drunk' stage, the point at which you ask yourself "Wodareyer? A big girl's blouse?" when you have twinges of befufddled conscience as you start fumbling the key into the lock. *(Dec 14, 2003,Sunday Star Times,C2)*  
> If anyone accuses you of being a computer nerd smile sweetly and holler "Whaddaya!". *(Oct 1999,North & South,29)*  
> [Hdg] Whadarya? *(Nov 13, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),62)*  
> Colin Meads didn't need any blindfolds or fancy "man management"; he made do with a sheep under each arm and a  decent-sized King Country hill. Fun? "Whaddarya?", as they said in <i>Foreskin's Lament</i>. *(Feb 20, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),5)*  
> "Q. What's the difference between a man and a condom? A. Condoms have changed; they're no longer thick and insensitive." Not laughing? Whaddarya? *(Dec 18, 1999,Dominion,22)*  
> One night my cousin came up dishing out the beer and he said, 'Want one?' and I said no and he said, 'What are ya?' *(Oct 19, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),33)*  
> Whaddarya? A Te Papa-ite or a traditional museum buff? *(Apr 2000,North & South,48)*  
> These poems are at their weakest when they are either self-consciously striving to establish Turner's black singlet paddock-cred ("cobbers" talk about the "footie" and revile the smooth talkers from "the Big Smoke", etc), or openly lament the metrosexualisation of the New Zealand bloke: "but oh, real Kiwi jokers, Whaddarya / even Valentine's Day has caught on like the pox". *(Jun 4, 2005,Listener (New Zealand Listener),42)*  
> The show.. searches for some kind of explanation as to why the game [sc. rugby] has meant so much to us, a response to the question barked across a frosty training field in <i>Foreskin's Lament</i>: "Whaddarya?" *(Jun 15, 1996,Listener (New Zealand Listener),24)*  
> [Hdg] WoddAREyer, a big girl's blouse?.. It wasn't the trendy dance drug Fantasy that caused last weekend's death of yet another young male chance-taker. It was the dread cry WoddAREyer.. The authorities should instead think about the central role played by WoddAREyer, the chorus of ridicule so feared by young males... Under the threat of WoddAREyer, young men won't back off from taking an unnecessary risk. *(May 6, 2001,Sunday Star Times,C6)*  
> "Garn, woddare yer? Can't yer take a joke, mate?" is the least you can expect when you stand exposed, cringing in the spotlight of supposed affability disguising unspoken contempt for a loser. *(May 12, 2002,Sunday Star Times,C9)*  
> "Each individual comes under group pressure to assert their acceptance of the group values by joining in anti-women behavour," Phillips says. To abstain from such behaviour invites endless ribbing, accusations that you're a poofter or effeminate." It's the "whaddarya?" mentality, he says, citing the famous line from Greg McGee's play <i>Foreskin's Lament.</i>" *(Feb 22, 2004,Sunday Star Times,A8)*  
> The play was a lament for the innocence of a lost small-town rugby culture, nowhere more powerful than in the concluding (and very theatrical) "lament" - a long monologue wherein Foreskin poured out his misery, his inability to cope with his lost sense of self. The final words were the cry "Whaddarya? Whaddarya? Whaddarya?", as eloquent an interrogation of our notions of masculinity as New Zealand literature has produced.... Maybe the original play (and especially its conclusion) was just too theatrical to be filmed. Or maybe somebody should ask Greg McGee if he's an insightful commentator on New Zealamnd mores, or a purveyor of bland entertainments of "international" (that is, nowhere) appeal. Whaddarya? *(Nov 30, 2003,NZ Catholic,17)* [NOTES: Graeme Evans]  
> Groups of leering young men would saunter up to us and yell, 'Whaddaya, a pack of poofters?', or 'You're just a pack of girls', or even 'You're not real Kiwis, are you?' *(Sep 19, 1987,Listener (New Zealand Listener),29)*  
> [Hdg] Whaddarya?... I've gone cold turkey twice. It's bloody hard. But don't expect any sympathy from me. If I can do it, so can you. Whaddarya? *(Jun 2, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),38)*  
> Whaddarya? - a question that has entered our cultural lexicon. A signifier of an unsavoury side of New Zealand male behaviour on and off the footy field. It was the catchcry of a young man called Foreskin in Greg McGee's play <i>Foreskin's lament</i>. *(Jul 31, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),37)*  
> **McGEE** I'm hanging up my boots - whaddarya? / Kicking for touch - whaddarya? / Chucking it in - whaddarya? Kicking it in the guts / shimmying away on the blind-side / just ... couldn't ... hack it... / Whaddarya? / Whaddarya? / Whaddarya? ... *(1981,Foreskin's Lament,96)*  
> It's a virtuoso piece of language that directly contrasts with that of his foulmouthed peers, and "whaddarya?" still packs a whacking great theatrical punch. *(Aug 21, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),42)*  
> **McGILL** <i>whaddarya?</i> rhetorically questioned contempt; ref. Greg McGee's play <i>Foreskin's Lament. </i> *(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,122)*  
> Sure, they'd have preferred better weather but the idea of not going? Oh no. It's, well, whadare ya? Geroff. Of course we're going. *(Jul 19, 2004,Dominion Post,B4)*  
> Even my carefully thought-out argument of "What are ya?" failed to have any effect. *(Jul 21, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),46)*

### whaheen, waiheine
  
noun.  
See: wahine

### whai
  
noun. [Maori] any of various skates and rays  
See: also whai kuku, whai repo  
> **LEE & KENDALL** Wai, <i>s</i>. ... a fish so called; *(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,221)*  
> **ANDERSEN** <i>whai</i>, a fish (Trygon thalassia and other spp.), sting-ray. *(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/160)*

### whai
  
noun.  
> **PARKINSON** A Vocabulary of the Language of New Zealand. [...] Egooree, <i>A dog. </i>Teyka, <i>Fish.</i> Hewhai[a-grave], <i>A skate. </i>Eraperape, <i>A fish called Chimaera. </i> Hepaooa, <i>Ear-shells </i>. Hekohooa[a-grave], <i>Small ear-shells.</i> [...] Heanoohe[e1-grave]<i>, Fern root.</i> [...] Hewai, <i>Water.</i> [...] Tochee, <i>A hatchet, or adze </i>. Hecacahoo, <i>A garment.</i> *(1770,Journal,127)*  
> **COLENSO** One pretty little species [of the genus Raia, Linn., fn], I once saw, had a very long filiform cylindrical and smooth tail. These fish are called 'Wai [sic], by the natives. [ed.fn Whipray, <i>Myliobatis </i>sp.] *(1840,Excursion in the Northern Island of NZ)* [NOTES: 1840 Colenso Excursion in the Northern Island in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 50]  
> **SHERRIN** SKATE (<i>Raja nasuta </i>). ... [90] in one of his papers on `The Dunedin Fish Supply,' says what may be taken as of general application: `The skate is not often brought to market, but is not scarce, and as a food fish has few superiors; was fifteen days in the market against thirteen last year.' It is called by the Maoris wae. *(1886,Handbook Fishes of NZ,89)*  
> **PHILLIPPS** RAJA NASUTA Muller[u-dieresis] and Henle. <i>Skate; Whai </i>. ... Distribution: New Zealand coasts (rarer in the south). *(Jun 1921,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,iv/123)*  
> **PHILLIPPS** <i>Raja nasuta </i> Mueller and Henle. SKATE; WHAI. *(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,10,1971)*  
> 5.<i>Raja nasuta </i>Mueller and Henle. Skate. Maori: Whai *(1929,TrNZI,61/140)* [NOTES: (Young)]  
> <i>Raja nasuta </i> Mueller and Henle. Skate. <i>Whai.</i> *(1938,TrRSNZ,68/401)* [NOTES: 1938 Graham Fishes of Otago Harbour in TrRSNZ 68/401]  
> **BEATTIE** The above list of fish to be caught along the Canterbury coast has some notable omissions, as hoka (red cod) ... Others omitted are the tarakihi, or terakihi, the moki, the komutumutu (trevalli), the puhaiwhakarua (red-jacket), koiro (conger eel), tuere (blind eel), whai (skate), ... *(1945,Maori Place-Names of Canterbury,62)*  
> **ANDERSEN**  *(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,160)*  
> **POWELL** SKATE <i>(Raja nasuta)</i>, Whai of the Maoris .... *(1947,Native Animals of NZ,63)*  
> **DOOGUE & MORELAND** ROUGH SKATE ... <i>Other names: Raja australis </i>; whai (Maori) *(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,193)*

### whai
  
noun.  
> **WILLIAMS** The whole of the long range of the New Zealand Coast is abundantly supplied with beautiful fish. Of this variety of 78 species I will name a part, viz. ... Wai [Whai], Stingeree; ... They are up to 10 centimetres long and have powerful spined hind legs. *(1843,NZ Journal,40,1956)*  
> **WILLIAMS** The whole of the long range of the New Zealand Coast is abundantly supplied with beautiful fish. Of this variety of 78 species I will name a part, viz. ... Wai [Whai], Stingeree; ... They are up to 10 centimetres long and have powerful spined hind legs. *(1843,NZ Journal,40,1956)*  
> **TAYLOR** Fam. <i>Raiae Raia nasuta, (whai,)</i> the rays attain a very large size, feet in diameter; the stingy ray, so called from its having a barbed spear beneath the tail, with which it inflicts very severe wounds, often proving fatal; it abounds in all the shallow bays. *(1855,Te Ika a Maui,627)*  
> **BEST** The whai, or sting-ray, was taken by means of a hardwood spear,... *(1929,Fishing Methods,43)*  
> **ANDERSEN** <i>whai</i>, a fish (Trygon thalassia and other spp.), sting-ray. [<i>Glossary</i> 120 whai skate; whai repo - stingray, stingaree] *(Jun 1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/160)*  
> **DOOGUE & MORELAND** SHORTTAIL STINGRAY ... <i>Other names: Dasyatis brevicaudatus</i>; stingray, stingaree; whai, pakaurua, oru, roha (Maori). *(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,192)*  
> **WILSON** STINGRAY, SHORTTAIL <i>Dasyatis brevicaudatus:</i> ... Also known in New Zealand by the Maori name whai, pakaurua, oru and rohu. *(1981,Fisherman's Bible,201)*

### whai
  
noun. a game  
> They do some of the Maori games, onecalled the Cat's Cradle, or "Whai" inMaori. *(Sep 21, 1927,NZ Tablet,39)*

### whai kuku
  
noun.  
> **BEST** The smaller brown sting-ray is called whai kuku in the Otaki district, and the larger black one whai repo *(1929,Fishing Methods,48)*

### whai repo
  
noun.  
See: electric ray  
> **HECTOR** ... the formidable Stingaree, or Wairepo of the Maoris (<i> Trigon thalassia </i> ), which is greatly prized as food among the natives, ... *(1872,Edible Fishes,121)*  
> **SHERRIN** WAIREPO (<i> Trygon thalassia </i> ). ... To this family of fishes also belongs the formidable stingaree, or wairepo of the Maoris (<i> Trygon thalassia </i> ), which is greatly prized as food among the Natives, and in some parts of the coast attains to the most enormous size. *(1886,Handbook Fishes of NZ,103)*  
> **MORRIS** <i> Wairepo, n. </i> Maori name for the fish called <i> </i> Sting-ray <i> . *(1898,Austral English,493)*  
> **PHILLIPPS** <i> Narcobatus fairchildi </i>  (Hutton). ELECTRIC RAY; WHAI REPO. *(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,10,1971)*  
> **BEST** The smaller brown sting-ray is called whai kuku in the Otaki district, and the larger black one whai repo. *(1929,Fishing Methods,48)*  
> SOUTHERN NUMBFISH, OR WHAI REPO. The Southern numbfish was first described as new by Hutton in 1872, ... <i>Narcobatus fairchildi </i>  (Hutton). SOUTHERN NUMBFISH, OR WHAI REPO. ... The species is also called `torpedo' or `electric ray.' Hutton states that the Natives (presumably of Napier) call it whai-ngenge. <i>Ibid.</i> 105 <i>Dasyatis brevicaudatus </i> Hutton. WHAI REPO; STING-RAY, OR STINGAREE. *(1929,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,11/98)* [NOTES: (Phillipps)]  
> **GRAHAM** STING RAY or STINGAREE (WHAI REPO) <i> Bathytoshia brevicaudata </i>  (Hutton) ... The name Stingaree is a corruption of Sting Ray - a lackadaisical habit we have with some of the vernacular names of the fauna and flora of New Zealand. *(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,100)*  
> <i> Wairepo n.</i>  in New Zealand the string-ray [Maori]. *(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),628)*  
> <i> wairepo ... n.</i>  in N.Z., string-ray [Maori]. *(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),21)*

### whaihanga
  
noun. [Maori] architecture  
> A three-year degree programme covering five different aspects of Maori art including: Weaving (raranga), Carving (whakairo), Painting (te tuhi whakaniko), Architecture (whaihanga), and Body Art (ta moko). *(Dec 2, 2005,Whakatane Beacon,10)*

### whaikorero
  
noun. [Maori] speech-making, oratory  
See: -  
> After the whaikorero I found Bill sitting around the back on a form. *(Dec 1998,Mana,7)*  
> Wairaka marae invites Ngati Awa whanui to participate in the wananga: Kaupapa - Whaikorero and Karanga. *(Aug 31, 2005,Whakatane Beacon,15)* [NOTES: -]  
> On most Marae, the Whaikorero ususally takes place outside on the marae atea (courtyard), and not inside tthe meeting house. *(Feb 4, 2006,NZ Herald,Canvas)*  
> She did not want to contribute any skills or knowledge to the front-row role such as whaikorero or karakia, or display institutional leadership. *(Oct 31, 2005,NZ Herald,B6)*  
> While men had traditionally held the speaking (whaikorero or whaikupu) role, "it saddens me ... that they have stuck rigidly to that rule". *(Nov 18, 2005,NZ Herald,A6)*  
> He said, 'Get in front, boy, you're doing the whaikorero'. *(Jul 29, 2003,Dominion Post,B5)*  
> That sets a whaikorero (conversation) for the whole house. *(Aug 10, 2005,NZ Herald,B6)*  
> I'm not referring to the proper whai korero. *(Jun 28, 1986,Listener (New Zealand Listener),20)*  
> **IHIMAERA** The child followed the pathway that her gaze had taken, to where a Maori elder, the first to stand to greet the ope, was preparing to begin his whaikorero. *(1986,Matriarch, The,75)*  
> WANANGA .. kaupapa - Whaikorero and Karanga... *(Aug 19, 2005,Whakatane Beacon,41)*

### whaikupu
  
noun. [Maori] speaking, speech-making  
See: whaikorero  
> While men had traditionally held the speaking (whaikorero or whaikupu) role, "it saddens me ... that they have stuck rigidly to that rule". *(Nov 18, 2005,NZ Herald,A6)*

### Whaingaroan
  
adjective. Geol. classification  
> Early Odontoceti, especially the Squalodontoidea or shark-toothed dolphins, are well represented - two forms are known from the Whaingaroan, while others occur in the Duntroonian and Waitakian. *(Apr 1977,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,43/21)*  
> These collections are especially important as this sequence is the type of the Runangan Stage, and also contains the Whaingaroan hypostratotype. *(Mar 1986,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,71/45)*  
> The only vertebrate so far described from rocks of the Te Kuiti Group of south-west Auck- [sic]  have been a single broken penguin bone of Whaingaroan age from Motutara Peninsula, Kawhia (Marples and Fleming, 1963). *(Dec 1970,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,29/1)*  
> The northern three quarters of the peninsular is underlain by undeformed, northeast-dipping, massive to crudely bedded glauconitic sandstone (Ruatangata Sandstone, Te Kuiti Group, Runangan-early Whaingaroan) known from drillholes to be over 129 m and possibly up to 300 m thick  (Fig. 2; Ferrar 1925 p. 43). *(Aug 1989,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,85/19)*

noun.

### whaka blonde
  
noun. [derogatory] [f. ironical application to the Maori guides at the Whaka(rewarewa) tourist attraction at Rotorua] a Maori female; cf. WAIOURU BLONDE  
[NOTES: ca1950 Robert Barraclough p.c. a North Island term 1983 p.c. John Egan, applied to Rotorua guides 1953 14 M A17 Thames DHS 28 WAKA BLONDE (WOCKA BLONDE)]  
> <i>whaka blonde</i>: bleached/dyed hair on a Maori *(Feb 1981,Avondale College Slang Words in Use)* [NOTES: (Goldie Brown Collection)]  
> **McGILL** <i>waka blonde</i> a Maori woman; offensive. *(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,121)*

### whakaaro
  
noun. [Maori] thinking, opinion  
[NOTES: also whakaro q.v.]  
> Among our roopu were people who were very whakama but stood up to express their experience and whakaaro on the whole day's events. *(Oct 2006,Pu Kaea,4)*  
> Senior Support Officer. Whakaaro Matauranga/Team Up  The Whakaaaro Matauranga team's key role is to support the implementation of Whakaaaro Matauranga - Think Learning, a Maori education initiative. *(Sep 27, 2006,NZ Herald,E9)*

### whakaheke
  
noun.  
> **EDWARDS** Tuna are better cooked on a wire rackover embers, very nice this way,especially the whakaheke, the silver-bellied ones. *(1992,Mihipeka,39)*

### whakahihi
  
adjective. boastful  
> He says many of the Maori authorities prefer to remain low profile. One reason is the traditional Maori aversion to being whakahihi, or boastful. *(May 12, 2002,Sunday Star Times,C1)*

### whakairo
  
noun. [Maori] carving  
[NOTES: [Ma. <b>whakairo (tia -ngia)</b> carve] cf. P.M. Ryan Dict. Modern Maori (1995) 300.]  
> An ancient whakairo or carving, probably a canoe prow piece, has been found in a cave on the western shore of Lake Taupo. *(Oct 26, 1987,Evening Post,9)*  
> As well as more traditional Maori art pursuits like whakairo (carving) and ta moko (tattoo), the open day featured... *(Jul 13, 2005,Whakatane Beacon,9)*  
> A three-year degree programme covering five different aspects of Maori art including: Weaving (raranga), Carving (whakairo), Painting (te tuhi whakaniko), Architecture (whaihanga), and Body Art (ta moko). *(Dec 2, 2005,Whakatane Beacon,10)*  
> Te Pouhono Courses in Art  Kowhaiwhai, Whakairo, Raranga and more. *(Dec 2, 2005,Whakatane Beacon,10)*  
> His experience with timber and teaching prpeared him well for his future path as one of the 20th century's great carvers, scholars and teachers of whakairo. *(2009,Mana,86/8)*  
> These 100-hour courses help you learn new techniques in kowhaiwhai, raranga, whakairo, taniko and kakahu. *(Nov 17, 2006,Whakatane Beacon,13)*  
> Great tools the Maori man had for his carving these days, tools for his new <i>whakairo</i>, but there you are, a man had to eat. *(1984,NZ Short Stories III,111)* [NOTES: 1980 Grace (Journey) in 1984 NZ Short Stories 111]

### whakaiti
  
noun. humility and a willingness to not take yourself too seriously  
> .. the term whakaiti, which means humility and and a willingness to not take yourself too seriously - a quality necessary for durability. *(Dec 14, 2004,Dominion Post,B5)*

### whakaka
  
noun. <i>Carmichaelia</i> nf elsewhere Williams unless derived from Williams 81 <i>Ka[a] (i) ... whakaka[a] ... Inflame, incite</i> [?the reddish colour of native broom]  
[NOTES: is this a dialectal variant of or a slip for <i>Ma[a]kaka </i>(Williams 168 3.)]  
> **FEATON** C[ARMICHAELIA] AUSTRALIS. (<i>Brown</i>.) ... It is called `Whakaka' by the Maoris.<i>Ibid.</i> 178 Whakaka [Maori name] .. Broom [Settlers' name] .. Carmichaelia Australis .. Shrub *(1889,Art Album NZ Flora,109)*

### whakakai
  
noun. ear pendant  
> **COWAN** Taking the dead warrior's gun and <i> whakakai </i> pendant of <i>tangiwai</i> greenstone as trophies, Mair hurried back to the scene of the fight. *(1922,NZ Wars, The,2/158,1983)*  
> **JENKINS** On another occasion she found a beautiful greenstone <i>tiki, whakakai</i> (ear pendants), and a greenstone <i>mere. </i> *(1970,NZ Mysteries,101)*

### whakama
  
noun. shaming  
> He says the tradition of shaming - "whakama, we call it" - a sort of outing of violent men, coupled with a healing process of marae justice, is no longer working. *(Jan 23, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),31)*

### whakama
  
adjective. [Maori] embarrassed, ashamed; shy  
> In his quiet whakama way he tells how he and his wife had together decided that the family came first. *(Mar 14, 1987,Listener (New Zealand Listener),21)*  
> Dad was whakama about not being able to speak Maori. *(Dec 1998,Mana,36)*  
> A lot of them are whakama because they've been brought up in a Pakeha world. *(Dec 1996,Mana,No. 14 : 26)*  
> Were there times when you were whakama about the situation? *(Nov 12, 2003,Radio NZ / National Radio)*  
> People were so excited that they even got up to recite their whakapapa without being whakama. *(Oct 2006,Pu Kaea,4)*

### whakama
  
noun. [Maori] sense of shame, embarrassment  
> He's generated some awhangawhanga in his time and he's copped to his whakama. *(Aug 12, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),15)*  
> Yet ... there is a tension, what we call whakamaa." No, it doesn't mean shamefaced, not exactly. "More of a sense that, '<i>Crikey</i>, we made a mistake.'" *(Sep 25, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),28)*  
> The concept and meaning of whakamaa are difficult to explain concisely in English. However, the word is generally translated to mean one of these three things shy, embarrassed, or ashamed... Whakamaa most often occurs for Maori people, either as individuals or as members of a group, when they are placed in a situation where they perceive themselves to be at a disadvantage and thus to have a lower status. Whakamaa, therefore, has a lot to do with mana, which is most often translated by Maori people to mean power or authority... Third, utu or making amends requires restoring a person's or group's mana. Metge concludes that whakamaa will continue until a person or group is re-integrated with and placed in their proper position within a community. *(Mar 7, 1987,Listener (New Zealand Listener),37)*  
> My feeling of whakama [shame] was so great that I wasn't able to refer to it for 15 years. *(Jun 8, 1996,Listener (New Zealand Listener),38)*  
> He shifts into galvanising-leader mode, exhorting membership drives, slamming recent government publicity "about-turns" on Maori policy, dismissing the planned 2005 hui tamata (Maori economic summit) as little more than a government publicity stunt, stressing the need to validate Maori knowledge and "take New Zealand with us. We have to do as we preach; we have to help them get over the whakama [embarassment] of saying 'Papatoetoe' [correctly] in front of their friends ... We will Maori-ise New Zealand. ..." *(Dec 2004,North & South,66)*  
> My mother and my grandparents were the disciplinarians, as were whakama and consideration for whanau, not for the individual. *(Jul 29, 1991,Listener (New Zealand Listener),10)*  
> It's hard to imagine him.. burdened with a great whakama - a sense of shame. *(Jan 1992,North & South,120)*  
> The authority accepted that the plaintiff had suffered whakama (awkwardness) as a result of the protest issue and the issue of her manager's remarks and the other conduct complained of. *(Oct 19, 2005,Dominion Post,C5)*  
> She is the very picture of whakamaa: too shy to give her name, and terrified of being called before the cameras. *(Aug 22, 1992,Listener (New Zealand Listener),16)*  
> She says she's never been whakama to ask questions or to ask her friends, family and teachers for support.  "You've got to stand up, speak up and be proud! I wouldn't be where I am today without the help of others." *(2007,Te Mana  Taiohi,29)*  
> There is no more powerful punishment in Maori society than whakama, shame. It is known that people have willed themselves to death because the finger of shame is pointed at them. *(May 14, 1990,Listener (New Zealand Listener),11)*

### whakamanamana
  
noun. the seeking of mana  
> ..two terms from the Maori leadership lexicon were never far from earshot. First, the term whakamanamana, which describes posturing and glory-seeking - not a good idea if you seek to be durable. *(Dec 14, 2004,Dominion Post,B5)*

### whakamara
  
noun. [Maori] preserved puha and onion  
[NOTES: whakamara: marinate (P.M. Ryan Dict. Modern Maori (1995) 302)]
