> Tuku, a native speaker of Maori, was a whangai brought up by his kuia and koroua in Ngaruawahia and was groomed by his kaumatua for big things in Tainui. *(Jun 1996,Mana,25)*  
> My grandfather's young brother and his wife took me in as their whangai. *(Oct 1998,Mana,No. 24: 37)*  
> Because he was a whangai, adopted. *(Aug 14, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),33)*  
> She and her husband Morrie had no children of their own, but brought up many whangai over the years. *(Dec 1996,Mana,No. 14 : 14)*

### whangi
  
noun. (spg uncertain) the joker in a pack of cards (there seem no Maori antecedents in Williams; possibly from a <i>whanger</i> something large or excellent of it kind; or possibly from an exclam. <i>whang</i> said when playing the joker (cf <i>whammy!</i>)  
[NOTES: ca1940 W.J.C. `common in Southland']

### whanui
  
adjective. [Maori] wide, broad (added to iwi names to denote an association or grouping of iwi within the broader designation) ???  
> Wairaka Marae invites Ngati Awa whanui to participate in their wananga. *(Sep 30, 2005,Whakatane Beacon,43)*  
> Te Arawa whanui and interested persons are invited to attend. *(Oct 6, 2006,Whakatane Beacon,22)*  
> The 14,500 claimants come from four tribes -- Te Atiawa, Ngati Ruanui, Taranaki, and Ngati Tama -- collectively known as the Taranaki whanui. *(Sep 29, 2005,Dominion Post,A9)*

### whao
  
noun. [Maori] a chisel  
> [They] must have heard of Nails which they call <i>Whow</i>, the name of a tool among them generally of bone which they use as a chissel in making holes etc. *(Feb 1770,Cook's Journals,1/250,1955)*  
> **COLLINS** E-whow, <i>A chizzel, nail, or iron. </i> *(1804,English Colony in NSW,561)*  
> **SAVAGE** Whou .. A carving tool, small chisel. *(1807,New Zealand (various authors/titles),76)*  
> **LEE & KENDALL** W|ao, s. A nail, a chisel, &c. *(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,225)*  
> **TREGEAR** Long narrow axes were used as chisels (<i>whao</i>) or gouges, and, being lashed long-wise to a handle, were struck with a piece of wood as a mallet. *(1904,Maori Race, The,320)*

### whapuka
  
noun.  
See: whapuku, hapuka

### whapuku
  
noun.  
See: hapuku  
> **KEENE** <i> whapuku:</i> species of<i> hapuka</i> (alternative name). *(1963,O Te Raki,195)*  
> [index to common names 283-293] whapuku 1281, 1313 *(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography)*

### wharaeki
  
noun.  
See: wharariki

### wharangi
  
noun. [Maori]  
[NOTES: aka warangi]  
> **BULLER** The <i>Ririwa.. Warangi</i> (Rutaceae). *(1878,Forty Years in NZ,502)*  
> In their midst, young wharangi, five-finger, karaka and kohekohe, lovingly placed and tended by the Oku Street Reserve Group and others, are drawing a deep breath for the rush of spring. *(Sep 2004,Branch Out,No. 14/6)*  
> The common karaka, tupaki (or tutu), warangi, pisonia, and many other New Zealand trees are plentiful on the island. *(Sep 9, 1887,Timaru Herald,3)*  
> The bush wanderer can also be assured of a supply of chewing gum from the resin of plants such as wharangi (Meliocope ternata), kauri, and the sugary gum of young manuka... *(Apr 30, 2006,Dominion Post,E12)*  
> <i>Melicope ternata, Forst. (Wharangi.) The gum of this tree is chewed by the Maoris for foul breath. </i> *(1905,TrNZI,37/114)*

### wharangi
  
noun.

### wharangi'd
  
adjective.  
> **R. MASON** `used chiefly in the North Island' <i>wharangi'd:</i> Poisoned by <i>Brachyglottis repanda,</i> rangiora, or wharangi, used of stock poisoned through eating WHARANGI leaves, shoots, etc. (cf TUTUED) *(Jun 7, 1952,Letter)*

### wharangipiro
  
noun.  
[NOTES: aka wharanagi-pirou, wharangi-piro, etc]  
> NAMES, USES, AND PROPERTIES OF NEW ZEALAND TREES .. Wharangipiro Cabinet work, in which satin wood is used. *(Mar 7, 1846,New Zealand Spectator & Cook's Strait Guardian,4)*  
> On the tabernacle, the base which supports the cross is surrounded by columns formed of a rare specimen of a native wood, wharangipiro, presented to Father Luck by Mr S. Wilson, Ngaruawahia. *(Feb 17, 1893,NZ Tablet,20)*  
> Olearia furfuracea (Wharangipiro). *(Mar 2, 1917,Ohinemuri Gazette,4)*  
> **HOOKER** Eurybia <i>Cunninghamii,</i> Hook. fil. ... Nat. name, `Wharangi-piro', Middle Island, <i>Lyall. </i> *(1853,Flora NZ,I Flowering Plants 117)*  
> **HOOKER** Wharangipiro, Middle Island, <i>Lyall. Olearia Cunninghamii. </i> *(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),769)*  
> Wharangipiro. Olearia Cunninghami, Hook. f. *(1869,TrNZI,1/III/44)* [NOTES: (Buchanan Botany Otago)]  
> **CHEESEMAN** <i>O[learia] furfuracea, Hook.f</i>. ... A much branched shrub or small tree 8-20 ft. high; NORTH ISLAND: ... <i>Wharangipiro; Akepiro. </i> *(1906,Manual NZ Flora,284)*  
> **LAING & BLACKWELL** <i>Olearia furfuracea (The Akepiro)</i> ... A bush plant. Maori names, <i>Wharangipiro, Akepiro. </i> *(1940,Plants of NZ,444)*  
> **HOOKER** Wharangi-pirou, <i>Col[enso]. Melicope ternata. </i> *(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),769)*

### wharangipiro
  
noun.

### wharangi-tawhito
  
noun.  
> **CHEESEMAN** <i>B[rachyglottis] repanda, Forst. ... Pukapuka; Wharangi-tawhito. </i> *(1906,Manual NZ Flora,367)*

### wharanui
  
noun. [Maori; Rural] a variety of the farmed native flax, Phormium tenax.  
> The fibre of the Wharanui, the most common flax in the Manawatu is not strong. *(Aug 22, 1903,Farmers' Union Advocate,1)*

### wharariki
  
noun. a variety of flax considered inferior for manufacturing  
> There are two species: Phorium tenax, also know as harakeke, whararahi or swamp flax; and phorium cookianum, also known as wharariki or mountain flax. ... Harakeke can be differentiated from wharariki by its larger size and seed pods. *(Apr 22, 2006,Dominion Post,Indulgence (supplement) / 20)*  
> Try <i>Phormium cookianum</i>, wharakiki or mountain flax, the smaller green flax we plant lots of in our park. *(Oct 2007,North & South,97)* [NOTES: wharakiki sic in source? (misspelt if so)]  
> Flax which will not bear scraping, but yielding, by my process, a very soft material.   It is the mountain flax, called by the natives, <i>Wharariki</i>. *(Oct 16, 1860,Colonist (Nelson),2)*  
> There are but two varieties of flax growing on the flat land about Otaki, Manawatu &c., called by the natives Harakeke and Wharariki. *(Jan 21, 1871,Star, The,2)*  
> **HOOKER** Wharaeki. <i>Phormium Colensoi. </i> *(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),79)*  
> **LINDSAY** <i>P. Colensoi</i> is apparently what the Maoris designate `Whakariki, (syns. `Wharariki' - `Wharaeki') according to the late Bishop Williams ... and which he defines variously as `a <i>stunted kind</i> of Flax plant' ... *(1868,Contributions to NZ Botany,78)*  
> **CHEESEMAN** <i> P[hormium] Cookianum, Le Jolis</i> ... Much smaller and less rigid than <i>P.tenax. ... Wharariki. </i> *(1906,Manual NZ Flora,716)*  
> <i>wharariki,</i> a plant (Phormiun Cookianum) an inferior Maori flax. *(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/160)* [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]  
> **BROOKER et al.** <i>Phormium cookianum</i> Le Jolis Common name: <i>Mountain flax </i> Maori name: <i>Wharariki ... </i> Many authors have used <i>P. colensoi </i>but this name is antedated by <i>P. cookianum. </i> *(1981,NZ Medicinal Plants,35)*  
> **WILSON** <i>Phormium cookianum </i>MOUNTAIN FLAX ...  Wharariki (= <i>P. colensoi</i>). *(1982,Stewart Island Plants,300)*

### wharauroa
  
noun.  
> <i>wharauroa,</i> a shortening of pipiwharauroa, which <i>see. </i> *(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/160)* [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

### wharawhara
  
noun.  
> A [Astelia] banksii (wharawhara, coastal astelia) is a slightly smaller plant with relatively narrow green and silver leaves. *(Apr 7, 2007,Dominion Post Indulgence,17)*  
> Of epiphytes ... the commonest and most noticeable are forms of wharawhara (<i>Astelia</i>) ... *(1911,TrNZI,43/198)*

### whare
  
noun. [Maori] 1.a small house used by pioneer farmers as a home, boundary hut or out-station (fern-tree, manuka, nikau, ponga, slab, sod etc.) or a small hut for the single man on a farm or station (occasionally portable). 2.  a communal living quarters or bunkhouse for single men on a station, occ. latterly where each man has his own bedroom or whare in an adjoining row.  
[NOTES: see also stinkie, brownie]  
> Oct 20 Letter in NZ Journal (1850) April 6 79 We have begun to clear the bush, and have erected what the Maories call a warrie on the land. *(1849)*  
> Hawke's Bay Herald & Ahuriri Advocate April 10. 2 Sheep run for sale. A good dwelling house and two whares. *(1858)*  
> Russell Diary in HILL (1990) Between the Rivers 135 Paul Kawhia making a raupo whare for 35 pounds, my first house at Tuna Nui. *(1860)*  
> BELL In Shadow of Bush 2 The slab whare will have given place to the weatherboard cottage. *(1899)*  
> Hawera Star Nov 2. 1 Wanted to sell: furnished whare on wheels, practically new. Owner desirous of going to the front. *(1915)*  
> SILK History of Puhoi 26 The Provincial Government had erected two nikau whares each twenty feet by ten ... *(1923)*  
> Dairy Exporter April 29. 75 I live in a little whare a short distance away from the house. *(1932)*  
> BARRER Brave Days 97 A toi whare which housed them was burnt down and everything was lost .. *(1939)*  
> **BARRER** In 1866, the toi whare was left behind, for a fine twelve-roomed, two-storied house was built .. *(1939,Brave Days: Pioneer Women of New Zealand,102)*  
> ibid.  238 It was in virgin bush, except about an acre, where they built a slab whare, roofed with shingles. *(1939)*  
> DEANS Pioneers on Port Cooper Plains 113 The seven teamsters were camped out in portable whares, with a cook to provide their meals, moving round from paddock to paddock as the work progressed. *(1964)*  
> J of Ag Jan 45 Other buildings (all of recent concrete block construction) include a 2-stand woolshed, fuel house, workshop, garage and a whare. *(1965)*  
> KEENE Mimiwhangata Station 19 Jamie, who was employed on the estate doing scrub-cutting, bush-felling and drainage work, lived in a nikau whare on the property. *(1978)*  
> NEWTON Five Hundred Horses 21 As we were working some miles from the homestead we camped on the job, in a portable whare, and did for ourselves.   2. *(1978)*  
> HOBHOUSE Letter Nov 4 The necessary features of a station are .. a whare for the 'hands' fitted with bunks for the shearers who are too numerous to be bedded. *(1859)*  
> NZ Farmer 465 The rest of the musterers, who had gone down to the whare, finding we did not return, turned out about the middle of the night to look for us. *(1891)*  
> MCKEE WRIGHT Station Ballads 59 I sigh for the good old days in the station whare again. *(1898)*  
> NZ Farmer March 234. 5,000 acres, men's whare ... *(1907)*  
> NZ Farmer March 367 The whare system has been a curse as long as I can trace it. In Canada the whare is called the bunk-house. The man in the whare loses something of himself when he settles there. *(1918)*  
> NZ Farmer May 237 If I see a man turn into the whare for his dinner after a hard day without feeding his dogs first, I know he isn't going to last long on this job. *(1932)*  
> WOODHOUSE George Rhodes of the Levels 126 The station hands slept in long, narrow whares, with three tiers of bunks along the walls, a dining table down the centre and a fireplace at one end. *(1937)*  
> STRONACH Musterer on Molesworth 8 The whare was big, with bunks all round the walls and a fireplace at each end. *(1953)*  
> SHARPE Country Occasions 130 That night, when the station cook served us some stew and potatoes in the men's whare ... *(1962)*  
> LOUGHNAN Glossary 44 Hut: the building which houses the station hands. In the North Island more perhaps than in the South Island, it will answer to the title of whare, which is the Maori word for a house. *(1981)*  
> THORNTON NZ Heritage of Farm Buildings 176 .. the men's hut where single station hands lived was more frequently referred to as the men's quarters but sometimes, especially in the North Island, it was called the whare. *(1986)*  
> GORDON People, Places and Paddocks 77 An important part of the Glenburn experience comes from living in the 'monastic' whare. [warrie; station whare] *(1987)*

### whare
  
noun.  
[NOTES: A hut,cottage, or small shelter; often as separate accomodation for farm hands. wari, warri, warry, worry, whari, wharre, and many more varaiants]  
> **CHAPMAN** The whare consists of one large room with a bunk in each corner - a standing table between each pair of bunks and one large clay chimney. *(1847,Letters to His Father, 1843-1851,00)* [NOTES: Quoted in Miller; Early Victorian NZ, p. 140]  
> Most parents had grown up and worked in the country, where whares / shacks / baches were commonplace. *(Oct 19, 1996,Listener (New Zealand Listener),26)*  
> **IRELAND** The 'whare' in which the family lived - with its interior walls coated with a paste made from a mixture of flour, water and alum on which were slapped thick layers of newspapers and pretty pictures cut from calenders and magazines - stood abandoned before the relentless advance of the regenerating bush. *(1998,Under the Bridge & Over the Moon,14)*  
> **GIBBONS** There was a big garden near the house.. a pathway to the paddock beyond, then Mark Walker's whare... The whare was a large room they had built for him. *(1989,Recollections (various authors/titles),39)*  
> **PHILLIPS** The property, which included a house and, for the stablehands, annexes (colloquially known as whares), was owned by a veteran trainer... *(2000,First Past the Post,28)* [NOTES: Jim Knight "I want to be Up there " <i>Grenville</i> 1977]  
> **PHILLIPS** I looked  in at the whare door and sniffed the familiar smell of tobacco smoke and grey blankets... When he had finished we went into the whare at the end of the stables. *(2000,First Past the Post,65)* [NOTES: A P Gaskell "All Part of the Game" in 1996 <i>Some other Country</i>]  
> Coates came up with the idea after the three men lived in a whare together on the island in the 1970s. *(Oct 17, 2001,Capital Times,8)*  
> My mother was 14 when she started school. That's when the local school was opened in a whare that belonged to one of the mill-hands. *(Nov 19, 1988,Listener (New Zealand Listener),49)*  
> In those days we lived on in all the glory of our ti-tree whares. *(Aug 7, 1890,Otago Witness,36)*  
> We went up to the Hutt. and purchased a tois-tois whare, where we took up temporary abode *(Aug 21, 1890,Otago Witness,32)*  
> **ANDERSON** A well-worn track leads up the gentle rise behind the house, past the garage and stables to the single men's whares and the shearing quarters. *(1999,Long Hot Summer,140)*  
> A native named Paora Tewura, aged 60, was burned to death in a whare at Waipiro Bay. *(Jan 20, 1919,Northern Advocate,2)*  
> ABOUT two o'clock this afternoon, a large raupo whare at the foot of the Karaka was entirely destroyed by fire, with the household furniture it contained. *(May 5, 1870,Daily Southern Cross,3)*  
> The first-named seems to be the proper day, for then it was that the flag was first hoisted and saluted where Wellington now stands, but where then only Col Wakefield was, in his raupo whare. *(Dec 7, 1889,Marlborough Express,2)*  
> They took it into a small bark wharre;  the beast was quarted [sic] with the skin on;  we are all our own butchers, and are accustomed to skin beasts;  It is not so good with the skin left on. *(Sep 3, 1861,Wellington Independent,5)*  
> Although scant, the evidence is there: the half-loop in the adjacent Waipaoa River; scattered whare slabs of heart totara... Notes *(Oct 4, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),28)*  
> **McALLISTER** After some delay the tent was restored, as the 'whare' because it was given wooden walls and iron roof. *(1976,Old Taranaki,65)*  
> **OWEN & PERKINS** The Maoris at that time lived in whares, and they were just like the meetinghouse scaled down, with raupo walls and thatch, and an earthen floor covered with beautiful flax mats on which they used to sit or sleep. *(1986,Speaking for Ourselves,111)*  
> Stanton Del land lay on his mungamunga bed in his whare by the Auckland Harbour. *(1900,Auckland Weekly News,Xmas No. / 27)*  
> **DARE** [The shearers] slept in a whare (house) built especially for them, and furnished with bunks, mattresses and pillows ... *(1956,Rouseabout Jane,16)*  
> He knew the deceased, Manihere Toto, who lived in a whare built on a bank of the Porangahau, and about four yards from that stream. *(Sep 2, 1893,Bush Advocate,2)*  
> A STRONG, excellently built, weather-proof Warri, comprising a parlour, bed room (lined and floored,) and a Store with convenient fittings;  situated in a pleasant and central part of the Beach. *(Nov 7, 1840,New Zealand Gazette & Wellington Spectator,2)*  
> He accepted an invitation by the young men in their week-end whare on Wellington's sea coast to dine on stewed rabbit, mushrooms and blue cod. *(Jul 1, 1926,NZ Truth,1)*  
> In addition to the wages, "sleeping accommodation" has to be provided; in Taranaki this has been interpreted to mean "whares" but no stretchers or bunks, the men usually providing these themselves. *(Jun 23, 1911,Maoriland Worker,4)*  
> **BLAIR** He dwelt in a lonely warry. *(1889,Lay of the Old Identities, The,27)*  
> **TORLESSE** Knocked up a warre for the night and made a humu to cook our pork. *(1958,Torlesse Papers,68)* [NOTES: 1849 citation]

### whare
  
noun. The men's cookhouse.  
[NOTES: (farming)]  
> **LANCASTER** .. the eating-whare. . *(1905,Sons o' Men,4)*

### whare
  
noun. prison cell  
> Boobslang terms of Maori origin include <i>whare</i> for a cell (lit. 'house' - a cell is also referred to as one's <i>house</i>. *(Oct 1999,NZWords,2(2) : 3)*

### whare boss
  
noun. a supervisor of single shepherds and station hand accommodation on a sheep station.  
> SALISBURY I Was So Lucky 6 Apart from Peter Malone, there was only one other pakeha in the whare, Ted Cook. I guess he must have been in his early sixties and was whare boss, come storekeeper and quite often did the killing. *(1994)*

### whare boy
  
noun. a single shepherd or station hand living in single men's quarters on a station.  
> SALISBURY I Was So Lucky 5 Mrs Ken, as she was known to us whare boys, was a regal lady in the truest sense. *(1994)*  
> Dominion Nov 22 C6 If a whare boy (single shepherd) is lucky, he might gain the bottom wire of the fence (marry the farmer's daughter). *(2002)*

### whare bug
  
noun.  
> **COOPER** The annoyances [on Thames Goldfield] are, armies of mosquitoes, showers of sand flies, frequent visitations of the stinking or whare bug ..., and a deficiency ... of all vegetable food. *(Nov 6, 1867,Digger's Diary)*

### whare cook
  
noun. a station cook who prepares food for single shepherds or whare boys.  
> Listener Jan 13. 4 When the whare cook ate yeast and climbed inside the bread oven to ring for the doctor ... *(1950)*

### whare door
  
noun.  
> To the whare door she went, asking bite and sup,/And an old wahine brought goat's milk in a cup,/Thick milk in a cup. *(Dec 19, 1932,Evening Post,18)*  
> .. and another Tiopara, the only Putiki native who was gone to the war, was cut in two while standing at a whare door in Tukipapa, by the first shell which fell there. *(Jun 19, 1863,Otago Daily Times)*  
> As Remington was inside Moller and some others burst open the whare door, and the unfortunate occupant was found lying dead upon the floor slightly burned. *(Nov 4, 1897,Poverty Bay Herald,2)*  
> One evening at dusk, on returning from a rabbit shooting expedition, we found standing at the whare door a half-caste Maori. *(May 18, 1901,Star, The,4)*  
> One evening at dusk, on returning from a rabbit shooting expedition, we found standing at the whare door a half-caste Maori. *(May 18, 1901,Star, The,4)*  
> Getting no reply the Maori pushed the whare door open and saw deceased lying across the bed, fully dressed. *(Aug 26, 1914,Poverty Bay Herald,2)*  
> A string of blue cod, freshly caught, lay at the whare door. *(Jul 1, 1926,NZ Truth,1)*

### whare fire
  
noun.  
> If the notes taken by Sir George Grey and Mr. Bell, in investigating the titles to the Grey and Bell Blocks, are of equal value to the minutes here presented to us, Mr. Parris may have attained as correct results over the whare fire at nights, while investigating Teira's claim. *(Nov 12, 1863,Nelson Examiner,7)*  
> In years to come it will be pleasant to look back on the times that one has spent along with these old mates around the whare fire or in the camp. *(Jun 8, 1899,Otago Witness,57)*

### whare fund
  
noun. [Rural] a ''kitty'' or pool where single men occupying the whare on a station contribute a share for small expenses.  
> **GORDON** They hire films .. and pay for them out of the whare fund, a group purse financed by bottles that have been emptied at the end of a day's work. *(1987,People, Places & Paddocks,76)*

### whare hauhau
  
noun. [Maori] a lockup  
[NOTES: (Chatham Islands history)]  
> hey [the Hauhaus] laughed and put on a smaller pair [of handcuffs] and said you can go to the lockup now (whare hau hau). No one guarded me and I walked to the whare hau hau through crowds of them and took in all I could see in the time. *(Jul 4, 1868,Chudleigh Diary,224)*

### whare hui
  
noun. [Maori] meeting house  
> And they'll be doing so, at times, in a whare hui where everyone will gather for powhiri and poroporoaki and other occasions when the call of the family, the whanaungatanga, is important. *(Sep 24, 2005,Listener (New Zealand Listener),32)*  
> Turning left, you pass the entrance to the whare hui (meeting house) then, tucked in behind the punga fence enclosing the marae, six kaumatua flats with neat flower gardens out front. *(Dec 2004,North & South,63)*

### whare kai
  
noun. dining hall, dining room  
[NOTES: aka wharekai, whare-kai]  
> Then he's back, dragging a flax mat into the wharekai and issuing instructions to cut it into strips. *(Feb 1998,Mana,No. 20: 21)*  
> As one, they move into the wharekai (dining room) to eat at the same table and to deepen this closeness for ongoing growth in the wharenui (meeting house). *(Dec 19, 1999,NZ Catholic,7)*  
> Dogside is without a wharekai and, despite frequent sausage sizzles, money for its construction is elusive. *(May 11, 2001,Evening Post,5)*  
> The plans for the development remain unchanged and include the wharenui, a wharekai with seating for 100 people and a cultrual centre incorporating performing arts, cultural workshops, museum and archives and parts of Te Whare Wananga o Awanuiarangi. *(Nov 2, 2005,Whakatane Beacon,4)*  
> Somehow ancient kuia and a wharekai without electricity seem more deserving of a makeover than the average reality recipient. *(May 2004,Sunday (Sunday Star Times Magazine),29)*  
> Somehow ancient kuia and a wharekai without electricity seem more deserving of a makeover than the average reality recipient. *(May 2, 2004,Sunday (Sunday Star Times Magazine),29)*  
> Workmen are installing windows in the new wharekai where the visitors dine, standing in the mud, dodging rivulets of water coursing through the still unfloored building. *(Jun 4, 1983,Listener (New Zealand Listener),16)*  
> Allow me through your valuable columns to occupy a small space, in order to contradict statements made in your issue of this date concerning the fire which broke out at the rear of my shop early on Sunday morning, by which Mrs Murphy's Maori "whare kai" was destroyed. *(Dec 21, 1882,Waikato Times,2)*  
> The visitors were then conducted to the whare kai where refreshments had been prepared for them *(Oct 12, 1900,Southland Times,3)*  
> A large number of guests were present, and they were afterwards entertained at luncheon in the "whare kai." *(May 22, 1914,Poverty Bay Herald,2)*  
> .. we learnt that the whare tipuna is named after Karaitiana Takamoana and the wharekai is named after Winipere and the hapu is Ngati Hawea. *(Oct 2006,Pu Kaea,4)*  
> Photographs were taken of .. a curious long building called a whare kai, or dining room. *(Jun 23, 1899,Auckland Weekly News,1,Suppl.)*  
> **KEENE** <i>wharekai:</i> eating-house *(1963,O Te Raki,196)*  
> The star of the charming <i>Kai Time on the Road</i>, Pete Peeti gets on the road again to present a new cooking show although this time it's a decidedly larger affair, as he's in wharekai all around the country. *(Jun 3, 2006,Listener (New Zealand Listener),66)*

### whare karakia
  
noun. [Maori] chapel, church, house of prayer  
> **Michael King** Particular Maori offence was taken at the firing of a whare karakia or house of prayer in which a group of supposed non-combatants were killed... *(2003,Penguin History of New Zealand,196)*  
> The natives had, however, made an effective clearance of almost everything portable, leaving simply the cracked bell at the whare karakia, to be carried away by sacrilegeous [sic] hands, and an old axe, with a few kits and mats. *(May 18, 1864,New Zealand Spectator & Cook's Strait Guardian,3)*  
> The whare karakia was well patronised by them, and work of all descriptions was suspended. *(Jul 5, 1923,Hawera & Normanby Star,16)*  
> **WILLIAMS** Flags of various colours displayed [by Kororarika grog shops], bidding welcome to all who would enter therein; the natives referred to them as the <i>ware karakia</i> of Satan, where his followers assembled to do him honour. *(Apr 28, 1833,Early Journals of Henry Williams, The,309,1961)*  
> **TAYLOR** here they wanted to lodge me in their whare karakia which they had profaned by eating in *(Jul 10, 1843,Journal,48)*  
> **TAYLOR** ... I told them that when one day's work was sufficient to build such a large ware karakia there could be no excuse for any place being without a church ... *(Aug 24, 1844,Journal,3/11)*  
> **ANGAS** Close to Pipitea is a <i>ware karakia</i>, or chapel, belonging to the Christian natives, which is built of <i>raupo</i> and <i>tohi-tohi</i> grass, according to the native fashion. *(1847,Savage Life,1/235)*  
> **MEADE** Meanwhile the evening-bell was rung at the <i>whare-karakia</i>, and prayers were read by the native deacon. *(Dec 21, 1864,Ride Through Disturbed Districts)* [NOTES: 1864 Dec 21 Meade Ride through Disturbed Districts in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 436]

### whare kiore
  
noun. [Maori] rat house  
> "All visitors must report to the whare kiore (rat house) for inspection" *(Nov 25, 2005,Dominion Post,B8)*

### whare kitten
  
noun. a cat living in shepherds' quarters, to keep down the mice population.  
> ARTHUR Waiouru: Land of the Tussock 1935-40 21 I hear the whare kitten purring contentedly on my bed so I am gratified that someone is happy. *(1984)*

### whare kura
  
noun. [Maori] a Maori house of knowledge or learning  
> The war chief, in order to protect his protege, announced to the tribe assembled in the great meeting-house, "Whare-kura," that Bent was now his "mokopuna," and that his name henceforward would be Tu-nui-a-moa, which had been that of his (Titoko's) great-grandfather. *(Jun 13, 1916,Hawera & Normanby Star,4)*  
> "Wananga, the Maori name for esoteric scientific knowledge and ancient tradition taught in the old whare-kura, or "red houses," the tribal training colleges for chief's [sic] sons, is pure Sanskrit "vanana ..." *(Nov 13, 1926,Evening Post,15)*  
> **WILLIAMS** <i>Wharekura </i>... The building in which the <i>tohunga</i> imparted esoteric lore to his pupils. Applied sometimes simply to the common meeting house of the<i> ka[a]inga.:</i> spg also <i>whare(-)kura </i> *(1917,Dictionary of the Maori Language,490,CHECK EDITION)*  
> **FEATON** A site was chosen ..., and a pa was built, and, at the request of the Whare Kura, I drew up laws and regulations for them. *(1879,Waikato War,10)*  
> **DOMETT** The tribe's great Council-Chamber this should be,/ Their Whare-kura [a1, u-acute], Hall of sacred Red,/ For worship - justice; .../ *(1883,Ranolf and Amohia,1/197)*  
> The sons of chiefs were taught ... by the tohungas, in schoolhouses known as wharekura. *(1900,Canterbury Old & New,155?)*  
> **TREGEAR** The name Whare-kura ... was transferred on the arrival of the Maori in New Zealand to tribal buildings with something of the old [Polynesian] attributes. *(1904,Maori Race, The,376)*  
> Besides, the old tohungas, learned in their sacred school `Wharekura' the use of simples[????] for every suffering. *(Jul 1910,Kai Tiaki,104)*  
> <i>whare-kura,</i> school of learning; building where esoteric law was imparted to fit pupils: knowledge of karakia and other valuable lore was called kura, and kura also means treasure, since with the Maori, knowledge was above rubies (or their equivalent, pounamu). *(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/160)* [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]  
> **STEVENSON** The last session of a Whare-kura in the South Island was held at Moeraki in 1868. The Whare-kura was a school in which selected students were instructed in religion, ritual, tribal lore, agriculture, and the art of warfare etc. *(1947,Maori and Pakeha in North Otago,84)*  
> **BRATHWAITE** WHAREKURA House of Knowledge. *(1967,Evil Day,382)*

### whare kura
  
noun. [Maori] a Maori secondary school  
> While lobbying for a secondary school version of kura, wharekura, he wrote a detailed case for "why Maori had to regain control over kura, the need for Maori-speaking teachers or else the language will die, and the need for training for parents to run the school", offering a blueprint for producing high-quality teachers fluent in te reo. *(Dec 2004,North & South,70)*  
> He explains that though it's supposed to be immersion Maori, the wharekura has had to employ non-Maori-speaking teachers because of shortages of bilingual teachers in some subjects. *(Dec 2004,North & South,64)*  
> " ... There wasn't a rangatahi programme that catered for our kura kaupapa Maori and wharekura kids, or that showed Maori being used in various situations appropriate to the time and society in which we live." *(2002,Te Mana  Taiohi,17,Summer 2002)*  
> It is applying for wharekura (year 1 to 10) status. *(Jul 9, 2002,Dominion Post,A7)*  
> We have our Maori renaissance. We have our kohanga reo, our kura kaupapa, wharekura and wananga, and bilingual classes multiply in mainstream schools. *(Aug 11, 2005,Dominion Post,A13)*  
> The kura is in the process of applying for wharekura (secondary school, catering for students up to form seven) status. *(Apr 12, 2002,Evening Post,3)*

### whare manager
  
noun. a person who takes responsibility for the living quarters for single men on a station.  
See: whare boss  
> GORDON People, Places and Paddocks 76 The seven or eight shepherds who live in 'the quarters' lead a rather dormitory-like existence, though each has his own room and there's no housemaster. The closest they come to authority is the whare manager, appointed from within their ranks. *(1987)*

### whare manuhiri
  
noun. guest hall  
> **FINLAYSON** From afar they could see rising smoke at the back of the whare manuhiri, or guest house, where haangi fires were being kindled. <i>Ibid.</i> 142 whare manuhiri: the hall for accommodating guests at huis on the marae. *(1938,Brown Man's Burden,10,1973)*

### whare mate
  
noun. [Maori] 'death house', place where a dead person's body rests  
[NOTES: see JM Maori Words p.160 for citations]

### whare paddock
  
noun. a fenced area in which single men's quarters or bunkhouse is situated.  
> MOWAT Diary March 19 After lunch marked out roughly 1 acre in whare paddock. *(1943)*  
> SALISBURY I Was So Lucky 148 We mustered the Mata into Te Anga then brought the hoggets into the whare paddock. *(1996)*

### whare paia
  
noun. [Maori] a unit for mentally disturbed patients  
> The supporters ... arrived for a meeting ... at the whare paia Maori health unit... *(May 16, 1988,Dominion,1)*  
> A new wrangle is brewing at Carrington Hospital's whare paia unit, whose co-ordinator Titewhai Harawira, is adamant that new staff should be responsible to a Maori board expected to be formed tomorrow. *(Jun 16, 1988,Dominion,8)*  
> There were 70 people [in Oakley mental hospital]; the majority of those were crims, most were stir-crazy. ... The Whare Paia got the M3 dregs, not much improved from Savage's day *(Jun 18, 1988,Listener (New Zealand Listener),17)*

### whare paku
  
noun. [Maori] a toilet  
[NOTES: see also JM Maori Words p.160 for citation]  
> Miro felt really sick. ... He sprinted at top speed to the wharepaku... *(2001,Huia 4: Contemp. Maori Fiction,31)*

### whare paru
  
noun. [Maori] raupo whare, raupo hut  
> **WILLIAMS** In the evening natives came, too numerous for my study. Met them in the <i>Ware paru. </i> *(Nov 2, 1832,Early Journals of Henry Williams, The,262,1961)*

### whare pit
  
noun.  
> Still existing are the overgrown Maori whare pits and earthwork defences at Pukehuia, Tikitiki and Matuaha Pa garrisoned by Tuhoe during the above Ngati Kahungunu assault. *(Oct 4, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),30)*

### whare runanga
  
noun. [Maori]  
> .. Nathan informed me that "an interview would be granted, and that he would immediately parade the troops for the purpose of escorting me to his majesty's palace," distant about three hundred yards, by a winding road from the council chambers (whare runanga) where we were then sitting. *(May 9, 1862,Daily Southern Cross,5)*  
> The whare runanga, or Maori joss-house, was the great sight of the village. *(May 28, 1880,Hawke's Bay Herald,3)*  
> The nobly-proportioned Whare Runanga, forty years ago a prominent object on the banks of the Rangitikei river at Onepuhi, had been removed and re-erected near Mr Morrison's homestead, and it was owing to the completion of this great work that a suitable building was available for last night. *(Dec 10, 1910,Feilding Star,4)*  
> Some of the carvings to be incorporated in the whare runanga, he said, were similar to carvings which had been done by members of the school for the new meeting house opened at Tokomaru Bay last January. *(Oct 9, 1934,Evening Post,8)*  
> **KEENE** <i>whare-runanga: </i>meeting-house. *(1963,O Te Raki,195)*  
> The whare runanga, or meeting house, still formed a fairly large part in Maori life. *(Jul 22, 1924,Otago Witness,4)*

### whare system
  
noun. a communal whare or bunkhouse form of accommodation.  
> NZ Farmer March 367 The whare system runs on this station, as it runs on most of the places in New Zealand. The whare system has been a curse as long as I can trace it. In Canada the whare is called the bunk-house .. The whare system beat them all. *(1918)*

### whare tapu
  
noun.  
> **McCRAE** ... it was called Whari Taboo which means `a sacred house' and in which it was usual to deposit whatever was tabooed. *(1820,Journal,15,1928)*  
> **McCRAE** ... it was called Whari Taboo which means `a sacred house' and in which it was usual to deposit whatever was tabooed. *(1820,Journal,15,1928)*

### whare tipuna
  
noun. [Maori]  
> .. we learnt that the whare tipuna is named after Karaitiana Takamoana and the wharekai is named after Winipere and the hapu is Ngati Hawea. *(Oct 2006,Pu Kaea,4)*

### whare umu
  
noun. [Maori]  
[NOTES: ware umu]  
> He shewed me into the ware umu or, cooking-house, which is usually assigned for the reception of visitors ... *(May 9, 1840,New Zealand Gazette & Wellington Spectator,3)*  
> **BURTON** On a permanent marae, hangis were dug out of the floor of a cookhouse (<i>kauta</i>), which was usually a simple lean-to shelter but might be a sturdily constructed house (<i>whare umu</i>) with shuttered windows and outlets for smoke. *(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,3)*

### whare wananga
  
noun. [Maori] a house of learning  
[NOTES: aka whare-wananga]  
> The "tohunga" school or college assembled at sunset and continued in session until midnight, and was known as the "whare wananga," or "whare kure." *(Jun 21, 1904,Evening Post,5)*  
> The story begins with life in the Hikurangi Pa of the Arawas around Rotorua, famous for its Whare-wananga or School of Learning, where the tohungas instructed the chosen youth in the lore of the race, its legends and traditions. *(Apr 19, 1930,Evening Post,17)*  
> A most entertaining lecture on the Whare Wananga, or Maori school of learning, by Mr. Elsdon Best, was given before the Philosophical Society last night. *(Jul 19, 1922,Evening Post,11)*  
> **STEVENSON** The higher branches of learning, known as the Whare-wananga, included all knowledge and ceremonial for the spiritual enlightenment of mankind. *(1947,Maori and Pakeha in North Otago,84)*  
> **SINCLAIR** The superior kind of priest received an arduous training in a whare wananga, a house of learning. <i>Ibid.</i> 302  [Maori glossary] <i>whare wananga</i> House of learning some of which embraced Australia as well as New Zealand. *(1959,History of New Zealand,22)*  
> **FRANCES** `Whare wananga, house of learning,' the reporter said. `A place where the tohungas teach history and mythology, things like that.' *(1964,Johnny Rapana,167)*  
> **MASON** HONGI: Did I not offer my tongue to your whare wananga - KENDALL: University! HONGI: Did not your tohungas - KENDALL: Scholars! *(1971,Hongi)*  
> Somewhere out there in a paddock is the underground whare wananga, the sacred storehouse where tohungas carried out their secret rituals. *(Mar 31, 1984,Listener (New Zealand Listener),19)*  
> **IHIMAERA** You really have to know your stuff, the stuff that is usually taught only to the men in the whare wananga - the cut and thrust of debate. *(1986,Matriarch, The,[?25 to 28])*

### whare wananga
  
noun. [Maori] a Maori tertiary institution  
See: also wananga  
> He's chasing government funding for a whare wananga, a tertiary institution based on Maori custom. *(Dec 2004,North & South,70)*

### whare-atua
  
noun.  
> **RICHARDS** <i>LYCOPODIUM ... varium .</i>.. Nodding Club Moss. WHARE ATUA (spirit house). *(1952,Chatham Islands,17)*

### wharehahunga
  
noun.  
> **WILLIAMS** We were conducted to the <i>Ware'ha'hunga</i> and shewn a long range of baskets of food, which were intended for us. *(Jun 16, 1828,Early Journals of Henry Williams, The,135,1961)* [NOTES: [Williams seems to use this mainly in collocations ere as <i>Ware'ha'hunga </i>the house of the hahunga: /'/ represents the special quality of a northern dialectal /h/, often written /sh/]]

### wharehou
  
noun.  
See: warehou

### whare-iti
  
noun. outdoor toilet, little house  
[NOTES: (trampers') R. Mason reports (ca1950s) signs with <i>whare-iti</i> on them by several trampers' huts in the Tararuas]  
> Round the corner we found a permanent camp [in the Ruahine ranges] - two sleeping tents and a large living tent with carpets, table, deck chairs ... a radio, and a really swept-up whare-iti with chromium trimmings. *(Sep 1958,Tararua,15)*

### Wharekauri
  
noun. Maori name for the main island of the Chathams  
> Maori, who invaded in 1835 and slaughtered and enslaved the Moriori, call [the main island of the Chathams] Wharekauri, loosely meaning house of yearning. *(Jun 26, 2004,Dominion Post,E1)*

### wharemoko
  
noun. [Maori]  
> **Harris** I was five, nearly six, telling my holiday story about Ma's funeral. That I wore the pink dress that Mummy made, slept at the wharemoko under Tarauaua, or one of those tupunas anyway ... *(2008,Six Pack 3, The,73)*

### wharengarara
  
noun.  
> <i>Asplenium lucidum</i>, wharengarara. - Abundant, especially near the sea; forms large clumps on the sands in the southern part of the Colony. *(1873,TrNZI,6/52)* [NOTES: (Kirk)]

### wharengarara
  
noun. [Maori] a native berry-bearing daphne.  
> Balfour Diary Dec 25 I noticed some nice Wharengarara with the berries ripe on it. *(1875)*

### wharenui
  
noun. [Maori]  
[NOTES: aka whare nui, whare-nui]  
> **BAKER** That night in the wharenui Sean asked him why he was alone and walking north when they met. *(2000,Kokopu Dreams,47)*  
> The wharenui (meeting house) "in a lot of ways was a large landscape compressed in to [sic] a small zone. People could go in and read stories of the land and their ancestors in the whare, on the rafters describing icons and codes." *(Oct 25, 2003,Dominion Post,E16)*  
> The plans for the development remain unchanged and include the wharenui, a wharekai with seating for 100 people and a cultural centre incorporating performing arts, cultural workshops, museum and archives and parts of Te Whare Wananga o Awanuiarangi. *(Nov 2, 2005,Whakatane Beacon,4)*  
> **Grace** An office had been set up in the wharenui. A few old tables had been brought in for a computer, telephone and other office equipment. *(2001,Dogside Story,195)*  
> .. the wharenui we slept in during the duration of our stay. *(Oct 2006,Pu Kaea,4)*  
> But this is not the Whare-nui - the palace that is to be. *(Feb 5, 1858,Daily Southern Cross,3)*  
> Te Hira left the whare nui (large house) with a guard of thirty armed men. *(Feb 1, 1869,Daily Southern Cross,6)*  
> Aster [sic] the feed was over, and we had our smoke, I was summoned to head the tangi in the wharenui. *(Jul 18, 1873,Colonist (Nelson),3)*  
> In the evening he left her with her step-mother, whilst he proceeded to the wharenui to attend a Maori meeting. *(Sep 22, 1893,Poverty Bay Herald,2)*  
> Te Puea is re-establishing a marae at Ngaruawahia, a pathetic effort to carry out certain Tawhiao dreams. Her present mission was to raise funds for a whare nui. [ed fn] Meeting house. *(Feb 9, 1928,Na To Hoa Aroha,1/67)* [NOTES: Ngata]  
> Waimarie moved to greet the men at the front of the Wharenui. *(1985,Landfall,156/455)* [NOTES: Grace (Waimarie)]  
> A haangi is being held at the Quad Centre on Thursday 31st July at 12.00 noon. All tickets will be pre-sold at $5.00 each. Proceeds go to the opening of the new Wharenui. *(Jul 25, 1986,Victoria Univ. Weekly Staff Circular)*  
> He Paanui [heading] Regular meetings are now being held at Te Herenga Waka Marae for the kaiawhina (helpers) in preparation for the opening of the new wharenui on 6th December. *(Oct 31, 1986,Victoria Univ. Weekly Staff Circular,No. 813/)*  
> On Saturday 2 May I had the privilege of being present at the dawn ceremony to lift the tapu at the new Wharenui at Wellington Teachers' College. *(May 12, 1987,Karori and Western Suburbs News,13)*  
> **SCOTT** Until the royal head is set in place, aluminium frame windowys are the only feature of this unusually bare wharenui [Ngati Whatua meeting house] set in an empty paddock. *(1987,Seven Lives on Salt River,11)*  
> The gate across the road into the Nga Hau e Wha Christchurch National marae is chained and double- padlocked. Behind the palisade fence the weeds are knee high. The whare nui is boarded up ... None of its carvings and tukutuku are in place and the carvings are still tapu. The whare wananga beside it, meant to house the kohanga reo, lies uncompleted, torn building paper flapping. *(Mar 5, 1988,Listener (New Zealand Listener),16)*  
> Wood used for carving and building carries with it the spirit of the tree, the spirit of place including the name of the place, and the spirit of the carvers who create the powerful images of tribal ancestors which decorate wharenui (meeting houses). ... Anyone who has spent time in a carved house (wharenui) will know the depth of feeling the carvings evoke, for they make the wharenui a living thing. *(Nov 22, 1988,Dominion,10)*

### wharepuna
  
noun. var. of wharepuni  
> **TINNE** ... enjoying dry ... quarters at the great `whare-puna', or meeting-house, of the tribe. *(1873,Wonderland of the Antipodes,9)*

### wharepuni
  
noun. a Maori sleeping house  
[NOTES: whare-puni, whare puni Thick walled house built for warmth, as for sleeping quarters but includes general 'warm house' use.]  
> **MILLER** In the evenings, wrapped up to their eyes in European blankets crawling with vermin and seldom changed, they would hold fierce arguments in their foul <i>wharepunis</i> about the 'immaculate conception.' *(1958,Early Victorian NZ,105)*  
> **PAYTON** Tukipo..  consisted of a wharepuni and two dilapidated wharfs, inhabited by two hags and a very old man. *(1888,Round about NZ,258)*  
> Hundreds of opossum tokens- the ears and a strip of the back - were hanging on the washing lines in front of a wharepuni. This old style Maori sleeping house ...seldom used since the `old people' died ... *(1978,Manawatu Tramping Club Jubilee,196)*  
> **ADCOCK** wharepuni <i>meeting-house </i> *(1982,Contemporary NZ Poetry,143)*  
> **BAXTER** wharepuni <i>meeting-house </i> *(1982,Selected Poems,190)*  
> <i>wharepuni n. N.Z.</i> in a Maori community, a lofty carved building that is used as a guesthouse. [< Maori <i>whare</i> house + <i>puni</i> company] *(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,1340)* [NOTES: [nf. 1986 NZPOD; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 545]]  
> **Hochstetter** The hut in which we found ourselves was aso-called Wharepuni, a conversation- andsleeping-room, such as existed in formertimes in every Maori village. *(1867,New Zealand (Heaphy),358)*  
> **STEWART** He led me up the hill to where the little Maori settlement stood,.. the long low wharepuni or meeting house. *(1983,Springtime in Taranaki,215)* [NOTES: 1991 edition]  
> A relic of ancient building construction was the wharepuni, or warm house, which was still used in the Urewera on account of the cold climate. *(Jul 22, 1924,Otago Witness,4)*  
> .. and the chief in whose house I slept took the rest of my things and put them in his ware puni or hot house, that they might be safe from further depredation. *(May 16, 1840,New Zealand Gazette & Wellington Spectator,3)*  
> "At most, if not all, of the villages may now be seen comfortable weatherboard cottages, with windows and chimneys, and the wharepuni, which used to be the common sleeping-room, is now only used for meetings, or to lodge strangers in ..." *(Aug 21, 1884,Poverty Bay Herald,2)*  
> VIEWS .. Wharepuni at Parakino ... *(Jul 27, 1899,Hawera & Normanby Star,4)*  
> The last night of the tangi and the whanau gather together in the wharepuni, an embodiment of our ancestor, to share stories. *(2005,Huia 6: Contemp. Maori Fiction,58)*  
> **TAYLOR** Here we found a newly built pa named Antioch with about a doz. well built houses chiefly wharepuni, the old village seated amongst the boiling springs still remaining. *(Feb 5, 1853,Journal,8/74)*  
> **RUTHERFORD** For instance, suppose calomel administered to a Maori who exposes himself to the sudden changes of temperature of the `whare puni'. *(1949,Selected Documents,112g)* [NOTES: 1861 Nov 1 Memorandum by Ministers on Governor Grey's Proposed Institutions for the Native Race in 1949 Rutherford ed. Select Documents 112g]  
> **MOSER** ... we will look at another sort of house called a whare-puni. ... In these horrid places they sleep hudddled up together, with a roaring fire ... *(1863,Mahoe Leaves,22)*  
> **GRACE** They has been devoured with fleas and almost poisoned with tobacco smoke and the bad air of the `whare puni.' *(Oct 21, 1863,Journal)* [NOTES: 1863 Oct 21 T.S.Grace Journal in Grace Pioneer Missionary (1928) 122]  
> **MEADE** At Puke Tarata ... the inhabitants have ... built most of their dwellings in the style of a <i>whare-puni</i> described before ... *(Jan 5, 1865,Ride Through Disturbed Districts)* [NOTES: 1865 Jan 5 Meade Ride through Disturbed Districts in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 461]  
> **BAINES** In some of these old `whare puni' [sc. old pits over which a roof had been raised] trees of a considerable size are found growing. ... The `whare puni' is still used by the Maories in some out-of-the-way places. The side view at a short distance is that of a hillock, for the thatch of the roof is finally covered with earth; they are gable-ended, the roof projecting in front forming a kind of porch; there is one window and one door, both small, the latter a hand-and-knee affair as you go in or out. *(1874,Edward Crewe,230)*  
> **WALLACE**  *(1891,Rural Economy of Aust. & NZ,218)*  
> **TREGEAR** Some of the dwelling-houses were called `nestling houses' (<i>whare-puni</i>) or `warm houses' <i>(whare-mahana)</i> because in them the inmates crowded together for warmth. *(1904,Maori Race, The,277)*  
> **BOREHAM** Here and there several [Maori] families had established their domestic economy upon socialistic or cooperative principles, all living together in a <i>whare-puni</i> or larger <i>whare. </i> *(1911,Selwyn,54)*  
> Not an evening falls without these prayers ... in the gargoyled wharepuni. *(Jan 10, 1920,Quick March,13)*  
> **GUTHRIE-SMITH**  *(1926,Tutira,86)*  
> The wharepuni customs are also persistent & our pakeha friends seem quite reconciled to seeing the two sexes herded together at meetings in the whare runanga or under canvas. *(Jun 17, 1929,Na To Hoa Aroha,1/208,1986)* [NOTES: Ngata]  
> **HYDE** The work ... recalled to the New Zealanders ... that old world of different fighters - the red-ochred stockades, [111] the wharepunis, the little store-houses standing on their high stilts ... *(1936,Passport to Hell,110)*  
>  *(1953,Short Stories (various authors),265)* [NOTES: 1944 Stewart in 1953 Short Stories 265]  
> <i>whare-puni,</i> a losely-built house, thickly thatched, for sleeping in. *(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/160)* [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]  
> **REED** At the close of day he would take his precious greenstone with him to the <i>wharepuni</i> or sleeping house, rub it till he fell asleep, and pick it up again at wakeful moments. *(1947,Story of Canterbury, The,28)*  
> **BUCK**  *(1949,Coming of the Maori, The,3/373)*  
> **JENKIN** They were told ...of the reputation of the <i>wharepuni</i> (meeting house) at Matahiwi. *(1970,NZ Mysteries,108)*  
> **BAXTER** .. Up at the wharepuni/ She said, looking at new planks,.... *(1949,Collected Poems,497)* [NOTES: (1971) 1979 Baxter Collected Poems 497 .]  
> **WHEELER** Wharepuni, or sleeping house, Moawhango. *(1973,Historic Sheep Stations NI,51)*

### whare-taua
  
noun.  
> **ELDER** August 15th. - When an husband dies the widow laments and lives in a small house called the aretaua <i>(Whare-taua),</i> making mats to wrap the corpse in when taken up again. She must not wet her fingers with her spittle while making the mats, called watu-tangi-knitting - crying <i>(Whatu-tangi)</i>; the mat is called kakaho-wroimate, mat (of) tears, or bed-wet-with-tears-while-making <i>(Kakahu-roimata)</i>, a mat of sorrow; a widow of Rangeehoo (Rangihoua) made two for her departed husband and one for herself *(1934,Marsden's Lieutenants,254)* [NOTES: 1822 Aug 15 John King in Elder Marsden's Lieutenants (1934) 254]

### whare-tutae
  
noun.  
> **GRUNDY** I heard there was a [person called] Whare-tutae on the island - his rather undignified venture into the world was made in the toilet. *(1968,Who'd Marry a Doctor?,62)*

### whare-tutae
  
noun.  
> And there was now no Harold Gretton to celebrate the re-building of the whare-tutae. *(1971,VUWTC'71,41)*  
> At Tauwharenikau there's a fine whare-tutae:/ The seat gives a view of unusual beautae. *(1984,Thousand Mountains Shining,42)* [NOTES: 1984 H.W. Gretton `Pass the Roll Over' in 1984 Knox Thousand Mountains Shining 42]

### wharewaka
  
noun. a waka house  
> Crowded with Wellingtonians during last Thursday's council organised Hexstatic concert and the weekend's Hula Lagoona, it is proposed to replace the "mound" with a wharewaka by the edge of the lagoon. *(Feb 22, 2006,Capital Times,3)*  
> More than 1200 partygoers will be able to pack into the wharewaka. *(Mar 4, 2010,Dominion Post,A7)*  
> However, he does say that the wharenui and wharewaka could be combined into one building. *(Dec 14, 2006,Wellingtonian,1)*

### wharf
  
noun. a dock (an extension of sense wharf to dock or platform: cf. early pl use at Kororareka - ?from which sense `docks' developed )  
See: wharves, wharfs  
[NOTES: NZ English prefers -ves plurals for <i>wharf, roof, hoof</i>)]  
> The wharf was patrolled during the night. *(Oct 5, 1925,Evening Post,6)*  
> **WAKEFIELD** ... a frontage of about 140 feet is allotted to a Public Wharf ... *(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,95)*  
> **THOMSON** After a twenty hours' voyage [from Lyttelton] we arrived at the pier or (as they call it) wharf, of Wellington - very broad and extensive, ... convenient for shipping. *(1867,Twelve Years in Canterbury,45)*  
> **CHRISTIAN SHIRRES** ... the town of Dunedin. We walked from the Wharfe to the Grand Hotel. *(Jun 1888,Diary)* [NOTES: 1888 June Christian Shirres Diary in 1980 Neave The Land of Munros 53]  
> **MANSFIELD** The sailors put their shoulders to the gangway. A huge coil of dark rope went flying through the air and fell `thump' on the wharf *(1922,Stories,471,1984 Alpers)*  
> **HYDE** Alf was a ganger on the wharf at Lyttelton; and his son ... worked down among the cranes and wharf-trucks. *(1936,Passport to Hell,50)*  
> WHARVES, BERTHS, SHEDS, ETC. *(Dec 1, 1898,Auckland  Star,40)*  
> FLOATING BREAKWATER, WHARVES, AND RAILWAY APPROACHES. *(Jun 1, 1907,Progress,296)*  
> It had been suggested that a better site for the wharves and shipping sheds was between the Monmouth Redoubt and the Railway Cutting. *(Jul 9, 1920,Bay of Plenty Times,2)*  
> Large stores were built on Aotea Quay for use by the American authorities, and storage space was also provided off the wharves. *(Dec 26, 1945,Evening Post,3)*

### wharf horse
  
noun. a horse used for hauling produce on and off ships.  
> **DAVIN** One of the wharf horses, not properly broken, had been frightened by a shunter once and jumped clean into that water .. and the great wharf horses no longer stung him to the consciousness of his own weakness ... *(Sep 1935,Review (Otago University),49/29)*

### wharf labourer
  
noun.  
[NOTES: wharf-labourer]  
> Lawrence Gosling, a wharf labourer, who stands charged with the theft of an oilskin coat..called evidence in deference in the Magistrate's Fourth this morning. *(Jul 11, 1900,Evening Post,2)* [NOTES: Year needs checking. OED says 1890, NZDE says 1891.]  
> Messrs Whitcombe and Tombs' goods will be treated by the railway servants, the seamen, and the wharf labourers and carters as ordinary merchandise. *(Aug 7, 1890,Otago Witness,30)*  
> A wharf labourer named Leonard George Carter Huston was sentenced to fourteen days' for pillaging cargo ex-steamer Ripple. *(Mar 20, 1922,Hawera & Normanby Star,7)*  
> "Pretty cheeky, wasn't it?" commented the Magistrate, Mr. F. K. Hunt, when the police explained a charge against James Ivan Turner, aged 38, wharf labourer, of carrying on business as a bookmaker at Ellerslie on Monday. *(Dec 30, 1937,Evening Post,11)*  
> Six weeks' imprisonment for the theft of a bottle of whisky valued at 15s was imposed on James Summers, wharf labourer, 40 in the Magistrate's Court today. *(Mar 16, 1944,Evening Post,6)*  
> After the defeat of the 1980 strike the Lyttelton port employers sponsored a Lumpers and Wharf-Labourers Association. *(Jul 1952,Here & Now,2(10) : 11)*  
> Lawrence Gosling, a wharf-labourer, ... *(Jul 11, 1891,Evening Post,2)*  
> Is not this a fact ...: that the Canterbury Stevedoring Association wholly controls the Lyttelton Lumpers' and Wharf-labourers' Association? *(1952,Here & Now,11)* [NOTES: 1897 Royal Commission in 1952 July Here & Now 11]  
> A conference of shipping representativesand other employers of wharf labour anddelegates from the Wharf Labourers'Industrial Union met last night. *(Dec 29, 1899,NZ Times,7)*  
> The coat was discovered in the hold ofthe vessel on which a party of wharflabourers were working. *(Jan 14, 1905,NZ Observer,16)*  
> These "peculiar people" evidently do not "trust to the Lord" for food, which Mr. Wagtaffe [sic] earns in the usual way as a wharf-labourer. *(Apr 30, 1868,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,3)*  
> "A Wharf Labourer" writes complaining that the captain of the Columbus lent his men to the captain of the barque Galatea, to move the latter vessel into the stream, when there were many men on the wharf anxiously waiting for a job. *(Oct 19, 1877,Auckland  Star,2)*  
> The house was owned by Mr. Stacey, and until last Saturday was occupied by a wharf labourer. *(Jan 10, 1883,Evening Post,2)*  
> WHARF-LABOURERS ON STRIKE. *(Oct 20, 1911,Evening Post,7)*

noun.

noun.  
>  *(1906,NZ Truth,42)*

### wharf police
  
noun.  
> Sergeant Melville and Constables  Cleverly and M'Rae of the wharf police were quicklv on the scene. *(Oct 5, 1925,Evening Post,6)*  
> The Westport Coal Company, Mr. Briggs's employers, have placed their launch at the disposal of the missing man's relatives and accompanied by a constable from the Wharf Police Station, two sons of Mr. Briggs made an attempt yesterday to search the harbour in the vicinity of the hulk's anchorage, but choppy water caused them to turn back. *(Nov 10, 1939,Evening Post,9)*  
> He joined the Force in December, 1916, and the following February was one of the constables appointed to the Wellington wharf police when that station was then organised. *(Sep 1, 1944,Evening Post,6)*

### wharf shed
  
noun.  
> 27 Tons Picked DERWENT POTATOES, in bags, ready for shipment, and at present lying in Wharf Sheds. *(Jan 26, 1866,Southland Times,4)*  
> At Rattray street Wharf Shed.   W. L. LEES will sell by auction .. 100 bags milling wheat. *(Dec 4, 1872,Otago Daily Times,4)*  
> The Customs authorities have caused the apples by the s.s. Zealandia, from San Francisco, to be detained in the wharf shed, pending an examination by experts. *(Oct 20, 1884,Thames Star,2)*  
> Thomas Harrison, alias John O'Brien, alias Hefferman, a traveller, aged 33, who was charged with frequenting one of the wharf sheds with felonious intent, was remanded to 4th August. *(Aug 2, 1926,Evening Post,11)*  
> Four hundred cases of the bananas brought from Norfolk Island by the Maui Pomare on Wednesday remain in the wharf sheds, the consignees refusing to take delivery. *(Feb 7, 1930,Evening Post,10)*  
> **MANSFIELD** He crossed the railway line, he crept behind the wharf-sheds and along a little cinder path that threaded through a patch of rank fennel ... . *(1913,Stories,133,(1984 Alpers))*  
> **FINLAYSON** As they scurry round the end of the wharf-shed they see the steamer, the oldest ... of the small steamers tied up to this old wharf. *(1948,Tidal Creek,19,1979)*  
> That the coat was found in the hold, andnot in the wharf shed.. appears to bebeyond question. *(Jan 14, 1905,NZ Observer,16)*  
> [Heading] Wharf Sheds Should be Kept as'Vital Symbols' says Architect. *(Jun 21, 1973,Evening Post,28)*  
> The Jervois Quay wharf sheds are vitalsymbols of our beginnings as a tradingnation. *(Jun 26, 1973,Evening Post,28)*

### wharf shed
  
noun.

### wharfie
  
noun.

### wharfie
  
noun. waterside worker, watersider, a wharf labourer  
[NOTES: (cf British <i>docker, US longshoreman</i>) the colloquial wharfie is, or has been, often used in pejorative, anti-union contexts; watersider is possibly felt as a more neutral term)]  
> When Ormrod took his idea to Sid Holland, the Prime Minister was fresh from his stoush over the waterfront lockout and wasn't much interested in dealing with an enthusiast who had been secretary of the local wharfies' band for 20 year. *(Mar 9, 2000,Evening Post,7)*  
> **IRELAND** The great fight to the finish between Government and the wharfies had just started,. Their confrontation is usually referred to as the 1951 Waterfront Dispute. *(1998,Under the Bridge & Over the Moon,137)*  
> **SARGESON** ... a lot of wharfies. *(1946,That Summer,75)*  
> **GASKELL** It seems to me that the wharfies always choose the wrong way. *(1978,All Part of the Game,73)*  
> A gang of six wharfies worked steadily to finish the loading of the cargo. *(1948,Our Own Country,12)*  
> Wharfies could not be more shameless in their lack of industrial conscience. *(Dec 1952,Here & Now,14)*  
> I would include among New Zealand English words: <i>wharfie </i> *(May 6, 1952,VUC First Year English Class)*  
> **HAMILTON** Schoolboy or wharfie, he's the product of the pavement and the gutter. *(1953,Till Human Voices Wake Us,207)*  
> The captain strolled away and the wharfie ... made for the cranes and tarpaulin-covered trucks *(1977,Listener Short Stories,45)* [NOTES: 1954 Frame in 1977 Listener Short Stories 45]  
> I have been on the New Zealand docks and heard wharfies swear - it sound like a minsters' reunion at home [ sc. in U.S.] *(1954,These New Zealanders,53,1956)*  
> Lemmy turned and picked up the black beret one of the wharfies had given him .. *(1977,Listener Short Stories,44)* [NOTES: 1954 Frame `Lolly-Legs' in 1977 Listener Short Stories 44]  
> **SUTHERLAND** `No, they're wharfies,' he explained. `You'd call 'em dockers in the Old Country, I suppose. ...' *(1956,Green Kiwi,18,1960)*  
> **SLATTER** Two tarpaulins for those waggons and tying knots that would baffle the wharfies. *(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,20)*  
> **HILLIARD** `Not good enough for you, eh? A Maori harlot and a bloody wharfie and it's not good enough for them - hah!' *(1960,Maori Girl,246)*  
> **BAXTER** The dead who drink in Bellamy's/ Are glad hen school kids clap/ ... In a land where a wharfie's daughter can/ Marry some day a squatter's son. *(1979,Collected Poems,266)* [NOTES: 1963 Baxter in 1979 Collected Poems 266]  
> **BAXTER** The dead who drink in Bellamy's/ Are glad hen school kids clap/ ... In a land where a wharfie's daughter can/ Marry some day a squatter's son. *(1979,Collected Poems,266)* [NOTES: 1963 Baxter in 1979 Collected Poems 266]  
> **SARGESON** wharfies: dockers, longshoremen. *(1964,Collected Stories,304)*  
> **GRUNDY** He was earning a wharfie's wage unloading cement. *(1968,Who'd Marry a Doctor?,85)*  
> **McLAGLAN** He [81] was a `wharfie' who had read my advertisement and thought `he'd see what I was like'. *(1965,Stethoscope & Saddlebags,80)*  
> **SINCLAIR** The tactics of Barnes and Hill, in fighting for better pay and conditions for `wharfies', seemed to be non-stop aggression, ... *(1976,Nash,282)*  
> **BAXTER** In a land where a wharfie's daughter can/ Marry some day the squatter's son. cf P:<i> wharfie</i> (or<i> -y</i>) a docker: Austr.: since ca1920 Ex <i>wharf</i>-labourer. *(1979,Collected Poems,266)* [NOTES: (1963) 1979 Baxter Collected Poems 266]  
> **LELAND** <i>wharfy</i>: Wharfies are STEVEDORES *(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,112)*  
> <i>wharfie n. Austral. & N.Z. </i>a wharf labourer; docker. *(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,1340)* [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 877; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 545]]  
> **MARSHALL** The union men were the aristocracy of the shift; anyone else was a seagull, no matter how regular. The wharfies kept jobs like tally-clerk and winchman to themselves. *(1984,Day Hemingway Died, The,38)* [NOTES: 1984 Marshall The Day Hemingway Died 38 (Late Call)]  
> My mother hated him being a wharfie, especially when he worked on a slag boat and came home as black as a coalminer. *(Aug 8, 1987,Listener (New Zealand Listener),66)* [NOTES: (Sinclair `At the Point')]  
> <i>Bill aims to reform ports and wharfies </i> *(Jul 16, 1987,Dominion,2)*  
> [Hdg] "Wharfie" Walklin's Words. *(Nov 24, 1917,NZ Truth,4)*  
> **McGILL** <i> wharfie</i> waterside worker. NZA. *(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,122)*  
> **MASON** Timi and his eighty-one wharfie friends stacked away trestles, loaded up the trucks and the great doors closed at midnight. *(1969,Awatea,115,1978)* [NOTES: 1969 Mason Awatea Appendix II (1978) 115]  
> **HENDERSON** He would down his DB and shrug off the stockagent on the screen with stories about the wharfies. *(1975,Log of a Superfluous Son, The,63)*  
> **COLE** &#65279;It was some time before Andrews hadnoticed the way the senior club membersincreased their digs about the LabourGovernment and the wharfies when Joe wasaround. *(1949,It Was So Late)*  
> The average "wharfie" seems to feel no guilt when it comes to "acquiring" something on the wharves. *(Jan 29, 1947,NZ Observer,9)*  
> The wharfie made room for the ladies. *(May 12, 1938,NZ Observer,8)*  
> "If in doubt, blame the wharfies." *(Nov 14, 1945,People's Voice,4)*  
> We..lived in a state house in Gonville - surrounded by the families of wharfies, freezing workers and labourers. *(Apr 2000,North & South,130)*  
> **DAVIN** They..watched the wharfies unloading. *(1949,Roads from Home,226)*  
> Sid Holland would put those bloody wharfies and miners in their place. *(1963,Coal Flat,355)*  
> **MASON** But one of his wharfie mates had given him a ticket for his birthday. *(Aug 1978,Islands,18)*  
> The successful business man join[s] the "wharfie" for a cook-house parade and at last they have something in common to interest them - what will the menu be? *(Oct 2, 1918,Poverty Bay Herald,9)*  
> The "wharfies" desisted at the announcement "the parasites are coming". *(Jun 13, 1928,NZ Free Lance,47)*  
> The two Frontiersmen did not "go through" the "wharfies". *(May 10, 1913,NZ Observer,21)*  
> **Owen Marshall** He'd been a shearer, wharfie, dozer driver, deck hand, forklift operator and maintenance man. *(2005,Watch of Gryphons,37)*  
> **SARGESON** The pair of us went down on the wharves, and half-way a long the wharf we watched while a lot of wharfies worked on a boat that was unloading guano... The wharfies were shovelling the stuff into bags, but you could hardly see them for the dust. *(1964,Collected Stories,179)*  
> Today's orderly waterfront is a shadow of the surging docks of those days. Sixteen hundred wharfies, hired by the day, filled the rope's slings with the nation's farm exports. *(Nov 15, 2003,Dominion Post,E15)*  
> Sinclair's father lost his shipping clerk's job in the Depression, and became a "wharfie" with a big family to support on a small income. *(Jul 17, 1993,Listener (New Zealand Listener),47)*  
> A meeting of the Grey Borough Council was held in a tea-room one night last week.   Mr Butcher (mayor) occupied the chair and Crs Lawyer, Publican, Ironmonger, Jeweller, Printer, Wharfie, Stationer, and Grocer were present. *(Jun 10, 1911,Grey River Argus,5)*  
> "Welcome home to New Zealand, but not on holidays," added another.   "The wharfies must have their holidays, but never mind the man that has been fighting for them." *(Oct 24, 1945,Evening Post,15)*  
> [Hdg] Wharfies want mass protest. *(May 27, 1950,Southern Cross (Wgton),7)*  
> **MIXER** You're a wharfie. That's enough! *(1926,Transport Workers' Song Book,44)*  
> [Hdg] Wharfies v Seagulls : Scene setfor big stoush on the Aucklandwaterfront... Auckland stevedoringcompanies are limited by collectiveemployment contracts to the amount ofcasual labour they can hire and the typeof work carried out by "seagulls", asthey're known. *(Mar 24, 1995,Independent (Auckland),6)*  
> **BAILEY  & ROTH** The Milit'ry Service Board/ Sat in state ... The first case was a wharfie/ Who'd a wooden leg from birth. *(1916,Shanties,116)* [NOTES: 1918 Oct. `The Mixer' N Z. Watersider in 1967 Bailey and Roth Shanties 116]  
> **DEVANNY** Imagine Holly haranguing the wharfies from the soap-box. *(1926,Lenore Divine,46)*  
> You're a wharfie, that's enough. *(1926,Transport Workers' Song Book,43)*  
> **HYDE** The wharfies sit with backs against the tin sheds, their legs sprawled out, red handkerchiefs crusted round their heads. *(1938,Godwits Fly, The,69,1970)*  
> **BENNETT** A <i>wharfy</i> (usually <i>wharfer</i> or <i>wharf-lumper</i> in Australia) is a wharf-worker, or longshoreman. *(1943,English as it is spoken in NZ,88)*

### wharfies' special
  
noun.  
> There are a few other trains in New Zealand enjoying pet names of their own. For instance, there is the 'wharfies' special" running morning and evening between Christchurch and Lyttelton. *(Jul 18, 1928,NZ Free Lance,35)*

### wharfies' sunshine
  
noun. wet weather  
[NOTES: (cf CHATHAM ISLANDS SUNSHINE)]  
> The Golden Coast has been dulled by leaden skies and as I write is in a sodden state. Month after month of what the Wellington watersiders call `wharfies' sunshine' have checked its growth. ... At the end of the war the Golden Coast was scattered with baches where Wellington people spent summer weekends. [check xref `Golden Coast'???] *(Oct 1963,Comment,17/11)*

### wharfinger
  
noun. A person who directs cargo traffic on a wharf.  
> His father was a "wharfinger", a person who directs cargo traffic on a wharf. *(Nov 9, 1985,Listener (New Zealand Listener),54)*

### wharfs
  
noun. variant of wharves q.v.  
> **SARAH MATHEW** The situation of this place [Bay of Islands] is very beautiful at the head of a fine cove, ... Immediately on the beach stands an extensive well-built store, with wharfs and such buildings as boat houses, etc., ... *(Mar 19, 1840,Journal)* [NOTES: 1840 Mar 19 Sarah Mathew Journal in 1940 Founding of NZ 94]  
> **WAKEFIELD** [pl wharfs] [`Terms of Purchase of Land in the Settlement of Otago' agreed on by the `Otago Association' and the Company] 12. In laying out the Chief Town ... to be named `DUNEDIN', - due provision to be made for ... Baths, Wharfs, Quays, Cemeteries *(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,317)*

### wharfside
  
adjective.  
> The introduction of the new union of Wharfside Workers, and subsequent acceptance of many old hands, appears to have provided an excess of hands, and complaints are being made by some new hands that they do not get a fair share of work, and are left out. *(Jul 21, 1907,Evening Post,2)*  
> The Aotea Quay breastwork, completed in 1940, is a modern reinforced concrete structure 3680 feet long, served with a double-track railway for nearly its full length, and has provision for future wharfside cranes. *(Jul 8, 1944,Evening Post,8)*  
> **SHADBOLT** And there was the shabby wharfside milk-bar where, from a rainbow juke-box, a thin masculine voice wheedled for the return of lost love. *(1959,New Zealanders, The,130,1986)* [NOTES: (Play the Fife Lowly)]  
> The above property is to be disposed of either with or without about 45 acres of land contiguous to the town boundary, well adapted for building sites, having extensive frontages on the Governor's Bay road and the harbour, with several available and very [valuable] spots for deep wharfside premises, and an enclosed paddock, with a convenient landing place for stock, and an ample supply of building stone. *(Jul 6, 1861,Lyttelton Times,5)*

noun.  
[NOTES: wharf truck]

> Although the machine was rather cumbersome, and weighed two and half tons, yet by the excellent wharf appliances, and the skill of Mr Batt, it was soon placed all safe on one of the wharf trucks, from whence it was put in one of Bowe, Birchley and Co.'s waggons and carted to the ship. *(Mar 7, 1879,Manawatu Herald,2)*  
> Complaints reach us of persons leaving wharf-trucks and other small vehicles of a similar nature on the footpaths in some of the back streets, which is certainly very dangerous. *(Apr 11, 1883,Wanganui Herald,2)*  
> HAVING recently moved to New Premises, we have no use for the following articles, which are to be sold cheap .. 1 Wharf Truck ... *(Apr 7, 1894,Auckland  Star,8)*  
> The visit of the Amokura to the port was an occasion when the fund, I think, could have been justifiably drawn on, but the only visible hospitality shown was, apparently, free rides on the wharf trucks for the lads, which did not much diminish the fund. *(Apr 14, 1909,Bay of Plenty Times,2)*  
> The blacksmith has worked on wharf trucks, blasting tools, Maui, John Townley, beacons, coal-shoot, town wharf, slip, harbor launch, and No. 1 shed. *(Nov 24, 1913,Poverty Bay Herald,4)*  
> **HYDE** Alf was a ganger on the wharf at Lyttelton; and his son ... worked down among the cranes and wharf-trucks. *(1936,Passport to Hell,50)*

noun. watersider  
> **BENNETT** A <i>wharfy</i> (usually <i>wharfer</i> or<i> wharf-lumper</i> in Australia) is a wharf-worker, or longshoreman. *(1943,English as it is spoken in NZ,88)*

### wharfy
  
noun.  
See: wharfie

### whariki
  
verb.  
> **SELWYN** we found water, which the natives drew for us with the least possible admixture of mud, by `wariki'ing the bottom of the hole; that is, spreading the branches of trees over the mud, and letting the water filter up through the leaves. *(Dec 23, 1842,Journal)* [NOTES: 1842 Dec 23 Selwyn Journal Letter in 1847 NZ Part I (The Church in the Colonies) 90 ...]

### whariki
  
noun. a floor mat  
> Invovement ranged from ...our kaumatua escorting the Prime Minister Helen Clark to meet whanau, and the South Taranaki ropu Te Kohikohinga Raranga laying their whariki under a tree and inviting anyone and everyone to mahi harakeke beside them. *(May 2005,Kokiri Paetae,8)*  
> We dried our clothes with the aid of a good fire, and lay down to rest for the night upon native mats (whariki) which we found in the hut. *(May 9, 1862,Daily Southern Cross,5)*  
> **BAINES** ... my companion set about ... cutting fern for a `whariki', a foundation to cover the ground and on which to spread our blankets. *(1874,Edward Crewe,140)*  
> **KNIGHT** ... Father drove four stakes into the ground and Mother took out [45] sheets with which to make a kind of a roofless tent with two floor mats (whariki) on the ground. *(1975,Poyntzfield,44)* [NOTES: c1920s Eliza McKenzie in <i>Outlook</i> in 1975 Knight Poyntzfield 44-45]  
> Then, I'd roll out my threadbare Whaariki/ - to help you remember to take your boots/ off - spread an old newspaper on the floor/ and on it place a steaming pot of puha,/ kamo-kamo, riwai, brisket-on-the-bone and/ dumplings what we call: doughboys. *(Sep 7, 1985,Listener (New Zealand Listener),12)* [NOTES: (Hone Tuwhare)]  
> **BULLER** ... our Maori attendant (Rahui) had barely time to fix up our tent and collect `whariki' for bedding before thick darkness had set in. *(1888,Birds of New Zealand,1/12)*

### whario
  
noun. warehou  
[NOTES: (?Wellington local)]  
> **LYON** In the vicinity of Wellington a fish large enough for a family meal, the whario, was, at certain seasons very plentiful, ... These fish were about ten or twelve pounds in weight, and resembled a large mullet. *(1952,Faring South,131)* [NOTES: [spg whario also 77]]  
> **PARROTT** The Warehou is a southern fish becoming less plentiful in the North Island. It is known as the Trevally, and sold under this name, in Dunedin and Christchurch. It is an excellent food fish, and is always in good demand. It is also found in Australia where it is known as the `Whario' a corruption of the Maori name Wharehou, and in Tasmania it is sold as `Snogtail' Trevally. *(1957,Sea Anglers' Fishes of NZ,65)*  
> In Wellington [c1870s] at certain seasons the whario (this may have been a warehou, a mullet-like fish, about five or six kilograms in weight) was plentiful *(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,25)*

### whario
  
noun.  
See: warehou

noun.
