### wharves
  
noun. standard NZ term for docks  
> **KIRK** ... some of the oldest totara piles in our wharves and piers are equally good ... *(1875,Durability NZ Timbers,1)*  
> **KNOX** We [in Wellington] have several clubs, half a dozen banks, wharves and dry docks for shipping, three daily papers, and several weeklies ... *(1888,Boy Travellers,248)*  
> Among the shipping, by the wharves, in search of any job. *(1903,King Bill's Philosophy,24)*  
> **MANSFIELD** He walked onto the wharves, past the wool-bales, past the loungers and the loafers to the extreme end of the wharves. *(1913,Stories,133)* [NOTES: (1984 Alpers)]  
> **WILLIAMS** S.s. `Tahiti' ... anchored in the stream some two miles ... from wharves. *(1922,New Zealander's Diary,9)* [NOTES: 1915 Feb 13 Williams New Zealander's Diary (c1922) 9]  
> **MULGAN** There liners and tramp steamers ... tied up at wooden wharves, pouring in machinery ...whisky and tinned salmon ... *(1934,Spur of Morning,44)*  
> **HYDE** Or in the evening ... a one-armed man solemnly proffers a large and gleaming mackerel, caught off the edge of the Auckland wharves. <i>Ibid.</i> 51 ... pay your way on the wharves. *(1936,Passport to Hell,15)*  
> ... great floodlights mounted ... above the wharves casts ... strange patterns ... *(1948,Our Own Country,105)* [NOTES: (`Westport')]  
> **SCRYMGEOUR** ... the trappers brought [for collection for freezing] on pack-horse their `catch' [of rabbits] of the previous night, slit down the belly line, freed of inside then legged to hamstring and hock, linked in pairs hung on a roughly improvised trestle, over which hessian kept the rays of the sun from contamination. This `underground mutton' was taken to a depot, frozen, then carted to railhead, then to the wharves, and exported to Britain, where ... a ready market was always assured for New Zealand rabbit. *(1960,Memories of Maoriland,39)*

### what am
  
phrase. a children's catchphrase of delight in something first-class; `just it!' -  
[NOTES: (?obs) ca1928 R. Mason It displays the essential qualities to the full extent; it's a firstclass article of its kind: e.g. `That's a knife, what am!' = That's a <i>real</i> knife!]

### what the sheol
  
exclamation. what the hell! (rare, euphemistic), from Hebrew (the underworld)  
> **PRESHAW** These moles are sixteen shillings a pair' `Then what the sanguinary sheol are those you have outside marked four shillings!' *(1888,Banking Under Difficulties,163)*

### whata
  
noun. an elevated platform or stand, usu. for storing foodstuffs on (?or in, in roofed varieties)  
> **LEE & KENDALL** W|ata; A platform, or scaffold for stores; *(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,226)*  
> **WILLIAMS** The boys preparing a <i>Wata</i>, a high stand for [245] native food, about 16ft. long and 10 wide, 16ft from the ground, to preserve it from the English rats which are now beginning to infest all parts of the land ... *(May 16, 1832,Early Journals of Henry Williams, The,244,1961)*  
> **MARSHALL** ... watas or stages. *(1834,Narrative of Two Visits,170)*  
> **MARKHAM** [wutter] I saw the Potatoes on (Wutters) [<i> Whata </i>] for the first time; there are two sorts, one is four upright Posts, 20 to 30 feet high ... and the Potatoes are in Kits or Baskets said to average 60 lb weight. [ed fn] Markham may be using the Maori <i> kete </i>, `basket made of flax', the English `kit', meaning knapsack or valise, or a combination of both; the similarly between the two words and their respective referents has added to the New Zealand idiom a distinctive term of which this may be an early example. *(1834,New Zealand (various authors/titles),43,1963)*  
> **POLACK** The many <i> watas </i> [a's-acute], or platforms raised on trees, to protect the provisions of the people from the rapacity of the dogs and rats, were loaded with seed potatoes, ready for planting. <i>Ibid.</i> 1/167 ... he accordingly mounted a wata [a's-acute], where the provisions are always kept, and brought down a small flax basket, containing the human viand. </i>Ibid.</i>2/27 Platforms (watas[a2-acute] ...  <i>Ibid.</i> 2/33 The watta [as-acute] is a platform built upon trees, or raised on stout branches. These are solely used for the purpose of preserving the provisions ... *(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/65)*  
> **POLACK** The bones are then deposited in a new mat, and placed on a scaffold (<i> wata </i>) about four feet from the ground.<i>Ibid.</i> 1/214-215 <i> Wattas </i>[as-acute] or platforms built upon trees having stout branches as supporters, are common to every [215] village. *(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,1/77)*  
> **TAYLOR** [Word list] Wata a stage for potatoes *(Aug 1843,Journal,52)* [NOTES: 1843 Aug Taylor Journal 1842-1843 [TS:ATB] 52]  
> **SELWYN** Its [the Canterbury kainga] lofty Watas (potato stores) standing up against the sky, ... suggested the ruins of an ancient temple. <i>Ibid.</i> 70 ... lofty watas looking like ruins. *(Jan 13, 1884,Journal,NZ Part III/11)* [NOTES: 1844 Jan 13 Selwyn Journal in 1851 NZ Part III (Church in the Colonies VIII) 11]  
> **BARNICOAT** (Nelson) The absence of futtas or provision stores seemed to indicate poverty [among the local Maoris]. *(1845,Journal,186)*  
> **WAKEFIELD**  *(1845,Adventure in NZ,1/147)*  
> **ANGAS** The bones, after being well scraped and cleaned, are then deposited in a <i> whata </i>, or elevated box, somewhat resembling a provision store; ... <i>Ibid.</i> 2/34 These [kumeras] are the choicest of the New Zealanders' produce, and their <i> whatas </i>, or stores for these vegetables are frequently more carved and adorned than the houses they dwell in. *(1847,Savage Life,1/331)*  
> **MANTELL** Arrived at the Kaika, Wahinei searched the wata and among a quantity of putrid fish ... found 2 kits of potatoes of which we made a hearty meal. *(Oct 24, 1848,Diary)* [NOTES: 1848 Oct 24 Mantell Diary in 1947 Stephenson Maori and Pakeha 128]  
> **TAYLOR** ... the sacred food which in former days they placed on their watas as an offering to their gods (these food stages corresponded with the altars of other countries) ... *(May 26, 1850,Journal,7/103)*  
> **MORRIS** <i> Whata, n.</i> Maori word for a storehouse on posts or other supports, like a <i> Pataka </i> (q.v.). <i>Futtah </i> (q.v.) is a corruption, probably of Irish origin. *(1898,Austral English,508)*  
> **TREGEAR** Rough <i> whata </i> for holding firewood were also to be seen in a <i> pa. </i> *(1904,Maori Race, The,282)*  
> <i> whata n.</i> see FUTTAH *(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,12)*  
> **BEATTIE** Besides the whare (houses), there were whata (futtahs) standing there when I was a boy. They stood on two posts, and each post had a niche cut round it to stop the rats from getting up to the food stored on them. There was no roof on them, the food being covered with flax mats. *(1945,Maori Place-Names of Canterbury,33)*  
> </i> whata, a stage, often in the shape of a whare, for storing food, elevated on a high pole: approached by a ladder called arawhata (ara, way or path; whata, food-store). *(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/160)* [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]  
> <i> futtah </i>: corruption of whata, raised storehouse. <i>Ibid.</i> 176 <i> whata </i>: raised storehouse. *(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,173)*  
> <i> whata n </i>. a storehouse built on posts [Maori]. *(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),628)*  
> <i> whata ... n.</i> storehouse built on posts [Maori]. *(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),21)*  
> <i> futtah n. NZ </i> (also <i> futter </i>) = WHATA. <i>Ibid.</i> 677 <i> whata n. NZ </i> Maori food store raised on posts. [M] *(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,307)* [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]  
> <i> futtah n. NZ </i> (also <i> futter </i>) = WHATA. <i>Ibid.</i> 677 <i> whata n. NZ </i> Maori food store raised on posts. [M] *(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,307)* [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

### whata
  
noun. in esp. SI English, and in esp. early station contexts (?mainly but not exclusively Canterbury and Otago) a storehouse (often roofed) raised off the ground, usu. on poles (?as used by early explorers for provsion caches, often temporarily made) ; `a (rat-proof) storehouse on pole(s)',  
See: futtah  
Pronunciation: -  
> Spittal ... making a watta for preserving the flour on. *(May 3, 1850,Torlesse Papers,144,1958)*  
> **PAUL** You are fairly entitled to something better than a warre[e-acute], say a two-roomed hut for yourself ... and a large, strong wata, or store raised on posts to keep your provisions safe from rats. ... slabs plastered with clay are also used for walls ... One of these huts must serve you for your first year's wool-shed, with the help of a few hurdles in front, ... *(1857,Letters from Canterbury, NZ,90)*  
> **HAAST** ... a very nice and well covered whata (provision store) erected by Mr. Rochfort's native labourers. *(1861,Report on Topographical Exploration of Nelson,32)*  
> **HAMMET** ... the Darkie ..., who they had found camped at the Watti ... had eaten about 40lbs of flour besides plenty of tea and sugar. *(Jun 11, 1863,Journal)* [NOTES: 1863 June 11 Hammet Journal in 1863 Sep 1 Examiner (Nelson) in 1974 Heinz Bright Fine Gold 98]  
> **PRENTICE** Another small structure with walls and a roof thatched with tree fern and called a `whata' was used [c1850s] as a store-room on the station, this was erected on four strong piles rising six feet out of the ground and encircled with tin near the upper part to keep off the rats, these vermin sometimes proving very destructive on account of their large numbers. *(1870,Tale of NZ,29)*  
> **ELWELL** Walker ... afterwards gave him another pair of trousers from the futtah. *(1878,Boy Colonists,100)*  
> **HAAST** ... this well-beaten track, the numerous remains of whata's (provision stores), ... show that formerly a much larger population ... had peopled these interesting shores. <i>Ibid.</i> 429 Thus we obtained several portions ... of totara piles belonging to a Wata (provision store), and of spears; ... and a fork made of manuka wood. *(1879,Geology of the Provinces of Canterbury & Westland,100)*  
> **PASCOE** [futtah is used for an explorer's storehouse on the West Coast - get citation] *(1957,Mr Explorer Douglas,36)* [NOTES: c1884 Douglas in 1957 Pascoe Mr Explorer Douglas 36-37]  
> **DUNCAN** The buildings of the home-station were represented by a `futter' and a long narrow hut. - *(1888,Wakatipians,22)*  
> Our second [camp] was in George's Creek, where we built a `futtah' for provisions. *(1892,NZ Alpine Journal,1/95)*  
> **ROBERTS** These stores were called by the Europeans `futters' - but the Maori name was Whata. *(1895,Southern Districts of NZ, The)* [NOTES: 1895 Roberts Southland in 1856]  
> **HARPER** A `futtah' is a small shelter of bark and canvas, raised off the ground, in which to leave provisions and stores sheltered from the weather, wekas, and rats; the one we made here [on Copland River] was 4 ft. off the ground, with a floor 7 ft. by 4 ft., and 5 ft. from floor to roof. It was built of rata bark and saplings ... *(1896,Pioneer Work in the Alps of NZ,181)*  
> ``Futtah' is familar as `household words'. There were always rats in New Zealand--that is, since any traditions of its <i> fauna </i> existed. The original ones were good to eat. They were black and smooth in the hair as the mole of the Old Country, and were esteemed delicacies. They were always mischievous, but the Norway rat that came with the white man was worse. He began by killing and eating his aboriginal congener, and then made it more difficult than ever to keep anything eatable out of reach of his teeth. Human ingenuity, however, is superior to that of most of the lower animals, and so the `futtah' came to be - a storehouse on four posts, each of them so bevelled as to render it impossible for the cleverest rat to climb them. The same expedient is to-day in use on Stewart Island and the West Coast in fact, wherever properly constructed buildings are not available for the storage of things eatable or destructible by the rodents in question.' ... ][Futtahs are today in use on Stewart Island and the West Coast. - *(Feb 3, 1896,Southland Daily News)*  
> **MORRIS** <i> Futtah </i>, ... a settler's corruption of the Maori word <i> Whata </i> (q.v.). *(1898,Austral English,156)*  
> **BEATTIE** Every station had a `futtah', a small wooden building on stilts, a rat-proof store for provisions. *(1913,Trade Hunting,[16])*  
> ... the pioneer runholders on the Waimea Plain ofund `futtahs' (whata = storehouse) left by former Maori inhabitants. *(1921,TrNZI,52/57)* [NOTES: (Beattie)]  
> <i> futter, futter n.</i> a storehouse raised on four posts especially designed to be rat-proof. [corrupt. fr. Maori whata, a storehouse] *(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,4)*  
> **DOBSON** This good <i> whata </i> belonged to Purua, a rangatira wahine, who lived here with her servants. *(1930,Reminiscences,68)*  
> **GUTHRIE-SMITH** <i> Futtah:</i> a station store, built on high posts to keep rats and mice out. Both the design and the name are borrowed from the Maoris. Some people used to call them watties. With the increasing use of iron and concrete they are becoming rare; the last built, I think, I put up at Braemar in 1910. (PAUL, p.90, where it is spelt wata; [1898 Morris Austral-English]) *(Oct 21, 1933,Press, The,15)* [NOTES: Acland Glossary]  
> **AYSON** Picture a farm-house with a dairy and a `futtah' detached from the main building, a futtah being a store-house erected on strong wooden legs sheathed with tin to prevent marauding rats from climbing into the repository for flour ... *(1937,Thomas,11)*  
> **STUDHOLME** Most [early] stations had a futtah ... *(1940,Te Waimate,247)*  
> **ANDERSEN** The word whata often appears, particularly in Westland and Otago, as `futter' or `futtah' ... *(1942,Maori Place Names,373)*  
> **McLEOD** .. behind that [Samuel Butler's hut] again a wooden storehouse on stilts known in early times as the `Futteh'. *(1970,Glorious Morning,136)*  
> **McNEISH** He walked right under the whata. A rat scurried down. The tinfoil was peeling, Droo would have to sheathe the legs again against the rats. *(1970,Mackenzie,55)*  
> **SANSOM** A few sheep, pigs and geese supplied food for the whatas, the long legged rat-proof food safes, but there was no living to be made from the land. *(1982,In the Grip of an Island,55)*

### whata
  
noun.  
> **HARPER** While alone at the `futtah' camp I had the opportunity of seeing how quickly a Westland river can rise in heavy rain. FUTTAH. [Maori] *(1896,Pioneer Work in the Alps of NZ,184)*

### whata
  
noun. Maori storehouse raised on a post or posts  
[NOTES: see also "pataka" North Island in Maori contexts of Maori <i>whata, </i> with spg variously <i>wata, wata[a2-acute], watta[as-acute], wutter,</i> etc. indicating anglicised pron. of [wata], [w t ], but not (as whare) [w t ]]  
> They.. erected there some huts of toe-toe, including a store, or whata, which as built on piles on account of the rats. *(1988,Tales of Pioneer Women,215)* [NOTES: Writing of 1940]  
> Several of them.. have recorded details of their daily lives.. rats that necessitated the erection of futtahs(whatas). *(1988,Tales of Pioneer Women,299)* [NOTES: Writing of 1940]  
> **HULME & MORRISON** Thomas Brunner writes of the numerous whata (food storehouses) he saw at Okarito in the 1840s, but the rot and the bush got them long ago. *(1989,Homeplaces,10)*  
> The house was a small whata, or hut on stakes. *(Nov 12, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,22)*  
> <i>Wha.ta</i> (hwae..t&adot.), n. A storehouse built on four posts, which are capped with inverted cones to prevent rats from reaching the floor. The roof is usually thatched with snow grass and native flax. Called also <i>futter. </i> *(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2034)*  
> Occasionally the tubers were placed on a raised platform<i> (whata)</i> and covered with mats .. *(1903,TrNZI,35/22)*  
> **KEENE196** <i>whata:</i> food-house; also called <i>pataka. </i> *(1963,O Te Raki)*  
> **GUTHRIE-SMITH** The other buildings of the primitive homestead were an ark, 6 feet by 9 feet, a whata or store-house on piles, empty now in the station's dire extremity, ... *(1926,Tutira,150)* [NOTES: [NI]]  
> A peaceful shepherd.. named Duncan M'Causland used to take a two hundred pound bag of flour under each arm, carry them several paces to the futtah or whata (Maori), and step up the ladder withoutany trouble. *(Nov 2, 1920,Otago Witness,54)*

### whata
  
noun. [Ma.] [see futtah; watty] a farm or station food storage building, built on poles to keep out rats etc.  
> JAMIESON Old Memories 40 Then they built two primitive toi toi tents or huts and another on piles, called the whata, a place for stores. *(1916)*  
> GUTHRIE-SMITH Tutira 45 Those giants of the prime, the early station-holders, mostly housed themselves and their belongings in raupo-thatched whare and rat-proof whata. *(1921)*

### whataitai
  
noun. a taniwha (Maori mythical monster)  
> .. Paul moves past candlesticks taller than she is as she relates the story of the taniwhas Ngati and Whataitai. *(Dec 8, 2004,Capital Times,p14)*

### whataroa
  
noun. [Maori] a variety of Maori potato.  
> Lifestyle Farmer Aug/Sept 75 Our huakaroro and whataroa types - 'old yellow' and 'old pink' - are not as popular as they deserve to be. *(2001)*  
> Organic NZ May/June 23 Whataroa - waxy potato. Good flavour, producer and keeper. *(2002)*

### Whataroa virus
  
noun.  
> Mr O'Connor said he hoped mosquitos carrying the Whataroa virus, which causes fatigue, rashes and joint pain, would bite environmental protesters who wanted to perch up trees. *(May 19, 2000,Dominion,2)*

### what-ho she bumps
  
exclamation. ??  
>  *(Aug 20, 1977,Dominion,11/1-3)* [NOTES: see Catch-cries?]

### whatiffery
  
noun.  
> After 15 minutes of "whatiffery", Palavi sighs. *(Aug 21, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),32)*

### whatitiri
  
noun. ?=gumdiggers' soap  
> <i>whatitiri [s] Pomaderris phylicaefolia</i> Lodd. var. <i>ericifolia </i>(Hook.) L. B. Moore *(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,85)*

### what-o
  
adjective. busy  
> **HARVEY** Then it was what-o for the rest of the morning. This Christmas morning business was okay as far as presents go, but us kids had to peel the new potatoes .. *(1964,Any Old Dollars Mister?,55)*

### what's new in the zoo?
  
phrase. an elaboration of the exclamatory greetings <i>What's new? What's the news?</i> (CCR notes: in 1939 the expression was well established to become the title of a bit song `What's New?'  
[NOTES: ca1959 Sep 29 Wellington adolescents cf P: <i>What's new?</i> Army coll.: 1939+; also WF]

### what's that got to do with the price of eggs?
  
phrase.  
> **McGILL** <i>what's that got to do with the price of eggs? </i>challenging the relevance of some remark; Australians prefer `fish' to `eggs'. *(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,122)*

### whatu
  
noun. a weaving stitch  
> We wove slim bundles of two or three tillers side by side to form a flat mat, then, with a few rows of whatu, a quick and simple "stitch". bound these tightly together. *(Nov 2003,NZ Geographic,No. 66/40)*

### whatu taniko
  
noun. [Maori] finger-weaving  
> A perfectionist, Dame Rangimarie was considered to be the expert in the field of raranga - weaving raw flax strips into items such as kete and mats - and whatu taniko or finger-weaving fine flax fibre into garments., Whatu taniko, made without the aid of loom or tools, is Te Kanawa's forte. *(Aug 20, 2003,Dominion Post,A15)*

### whatu-tangi-knitting
  
noun.  
> **ELDER** `August 15th. - When an husband dies the widow laments and lives in a small house called the aretaua (<i>Whare-taua</i>), making mats to wrap the corpse in when taken up again. She must not wet her fingers with her spittle while making the mats, called watu-tangi-knitting - crying (<i>Whatu-tangi</i>); the mat is called kakaho-wroimate, mat (of) tears, or bed-wet-with-tears-while-making (<i>Kakahu-roimata</i>), a mat of sorrow; a widow of Rangeehoo (Rangihoua) made two for her departed husband and one for herself. *(Aug 15, 1822,Marsden's Lieutenants,254,1934)* [NOTES: 1822 Aug 15 John King in Elder Marsden's Lieutenants (1934) 254]

### whau
  
noun.  
[NOTES: attrib.]  
> 296 Lithograph of whau foliage (corkwood). *(1890,PWD Catalogue of Timbers,21)*  
> **JEFFREY** She stopped raking the leaves that were shrouding the hellebores, and began frantically pulling up handfuls of milk-weed that grew about the trunk of the whau tree. *(1964,Mairangi,30)*

### whau
  
noun. also called corkwood a small tree remarkable for its light wood. [Maori] <i>Entelea arborescens</i>, =cork(-wood)(new zealand); =cork tree; =mulberry (new zealand)  
> Whau (Entelea arborescens) - A small treeof great merit, though not greatly usedin home gardens... Maori used the wood asfloat on their fishing tackle. *(Nov 11, 1993,Evening Post,14)*  
> The lightest wood in the world, whau, grows in what Australian ecologist Tim Flannery says was the most wondrous forest in the world, filled with the most extraordinary avifauna ever to shed a feather (before the arrival of God's chosen ape and the mammals that trailed at our feet). *(Oct 11, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),34)*  
> She's equally fond of the native whau (<i>Entelea arborescens</i>), with its prickly seedcases. This fast-growing small tree bears white flowers throughout spring and early summer, followed by clusters of spiky green fruits that are a spitting image of chestnut burrs. *(Jun 1, 2003,Sunday (Sunday Star Times Magazine),21)*  
> This was the Whau or hauama of the Maoris... The striking appearance of the Whau makes it a desirable ornamental tree. *(1945,Korero (AEWS Background Bulletin),3(4) : 21)*  
> Perhaps the most remarkable thing about the whau is the lightness of its wood. *(Oct 15, 2005,Dominion Post,E13)*  
> **NICHOLAS** The trees which the natives chiefly make subservient to their purposes, are [besides the pines cowrie, totarra, towha and zarida mentioned above] ... the henow, from which they extract a black dye, the towha, a tree resembling the sycamore, the <i>river river</i>, the grain of whose wood is similar to that of the beech, a species of cork-tree, called by these people vow, a large tree named eckoha, and another named kycata, a tall and beautiful tree, ... *(1817,New Zealand (various authors/titles),2/245)*  
> **CRAIK** Among those which the natives principally make use of, are the <i>henow,</i> from which they extract a black dye, and the <i>vow,</i> a species of cork tree. *(1830,New Zealanders, The,175)*  
> **POLACK** ... vow (native cork tree) [get cit] <i>Ibid.</i> 2/399 The <i>Pongo</i>, and <i>Wou, (achroma petandria Zelandica)</i> are varieties of the corktree, when cut down an adhesive juice exudes in some quantity, and are of much service to the natives in their fishing nets. The fronds are five feet long, virent, circumference of trunk one and a half foot, covered with chaffy scales. *(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),2/131)*  
> **WILLIAMS** The Pan [whau] is rather a graceful tree, does not grow high, ... *(1844,NZ Journal,106,1956)*  
> **HOOKER** <i>Entelea arborescens</i>. ... Nat. name, `Whau', <i>Colenso</i>, and `Hauama', in the Middle Island. *(1853,Flora NZ,I Flowering Plants 32)*  
> **HOOKER** Whau, <i>Col[enso]. Entelea arborescens. </i> *(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),769)*  
> .. for floats, the light wood of the small tree Whau, or Hauama, <i>(Entelea arborescens,) was used ... </i> *(1869,TrNZI,1/iIII/35)* [NOTES: (Colenso Botany North Isd) .]  
> **BULLER** The <i>Wau</i> [sic] produces wood as light as cork; ... *(1878,Forty Years in NZ,502)*  
> **BULLER** The <i>Wau</i> [sic] produces wood as light as cork; ... *(1878,Forty Years in NZ,502)*  
> **HAY** The Whau (<i>Entelea arborescens</i>) is a small tree ... The wood is light, and the tree yields a fair substitute for cork. *(1882,Brighter Britain,2/196)*  
> The few clumps of trees consist of pohutukawa, mapou, manuka, rewarewa, akeake, whau or corkwood, pukapuka, and a few puriri, which, however, is of little value, being very scattered and ruined by fire. *(1884,TrNZI,17/419)* [NOTES: (Gold-Smith)]  
> **FEATON** ENTELEA ARBORESCENS (<i>Brown</i>.) ... The `WHAU', or `HAUAMA'.- This very beautiful small tree is peculiar to the Northern Island of New Zealand, but is not common. ... Owing to the extreme lightness [61] and bouyancy of the wood, the tree is known to the Colonists as the `Cork Tree'. <i>Ibid.</i>) 178 Whau [Maori name] .. Cork-wood [Settlers' name] .. Entelea arborescens .. Small tree *(1889,Art Album NZ Flora,60)*  
> Whau (corkwood) .. Entelea arborescens .. The lightest timber in New Zealand, weight 8lb. to 11lb. per cubic foot, being about half that of cork; ... *(1890,PWD Catalogue of Timbers,14)*  
> **MORRIS** Whau, n. Maori name for the New Zealand Cork-tree, <i>Entelea arborescens</i>, R. Br., <i>N.O. Tiliaceae. </i> *(1898,Austral English,508)*  
> **CHEESEMAN** <i>[Entelea arborescens] Whau, Hauma.</i> ... Greedily eaten by cattle and horses, and consequently fast becoming rare on the mainland ... *(1906,Manual NZ Flora,83)*  
> **LAING & BLACKWELL** <i>Entelea arborescens (The Whau)</i>. <i>Ibid.</i> 251-252 [264] The varied names given by the Maoris in different districts to this plant, are all said to spring from the same root. <i>Whau [252] signifies wrapped about, netted, - like lace</i>, and the tree is so called from the character of its inner bark. The settlers name it lace-bark, ribbon-wood, [1940 edn, no hyphens] or thousand-jacket. *(1907,Plants of NZ,244,1940, p. 256)*  
> <i>whau</i>, a tree (Entelea arborescens), cork-wood, the lightest of Newzealand timbers. *(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/160)* [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

> **McLINTOCK** Whau .. .. whau .. .. <i>Entelea arborescens </i> *(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712)*  
> <i>whau n.</i> New Zealand cork tree [Maori]. *(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),628)*

> <i>whau n. </i>NZ cork-tree. [M] *(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,877)* [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

noun. houi,  lacebark

> Other poor burning woods are puka (one of the largest-leafed trees of the bush), patata, horoeka (lancewood), houhou, rakawa, karamu, taupata, all the lacebarks (hohere, houi or whauwhi - New Zealand orange blossom), ngaio, hinau (if very dry and split thin burns better), pukatea, toro, etc. *(Dec 7, 1932,Evening Post,16)*  
> **HOOKER** Hoheria <i>Lyallii</i>, Hook. fil. ... HAB. Middle Island ... Nat. ame, `Whau-whi', <i>Lyall</i>. *(1853,Flora NZ,I Flowering Plants 31)*  
> **HOOKER** Whau-whi, <i>Col[enso]. Hoheria populnea. </i> ...Whau-whi, <i>Lyall.Plagianthus Lyallii. [=?Hoheria lyalli </i>?mountain ribbonwood] *(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),769)*  
> **1/III/35** .... for net-ropes the tough stringy bark of the Houhere, and also of the Whauwhi or Houi (<i>Hoheria populnea</i>, and of its varieties,) was plaited together *(1869,TrNZI)* [NOTES: (Colenso Botany North Island)]  
> **FEATON** HOHERIA POPULNEA (<i>A.Cunn.</i>) ... The `HOUI' or `WHAU-WHI'.- ... grows abundantly in both Islands. ... Owing to the chaste [snow-white] appearance of its flowers, it has not inaptly been termed by some the `New Zealand orange Blossom'. The inner bark is peculiar, and consists of layer upon layer of laced fibre, ...From this peculiarity it is called by the settlers the `Thousand Jacket', `lace-bark', or `Ribbon-wood'.  <i>Houi or Whau-Whi</i> Hoheria populnea. <i>Ibid.</i> 178 Whauwhi [Maori name] .. Ribbon wood [Settlers' name] .. Plagianthus Lyallii .. Small tree *(1889,Art Album NZ Flora,55)*  
> **LAING & BLACKWELL** <i>Genus Hoheria. ... Hoheria</i> is a modification of the native name.) Maori names <i>Houi, Whauwhi, Houhere. </i> *(1907,Plants of NZ,250,1940,, p. 264)*

### wheek
  
verb. to whine, grizzle  
> **DR C. CORNE** Wheek - to grizzle, whine (sulkily and/or intermittently). Usually of children. Used by C.F. Corne of Whangarei; but I use it, too, usually in `Stop (your) wheeking'. Maybe it is <i>weeking, wh</i> is an unstable phoneme for me. <i>Wheek </i>See <i>weak</i> V2 <i>veak</i> sb2 and vb Hmp Dev Corn 1) sb. A fit of ill-temper; a pasion, rage; a sudden capricious freak; a vagary; a whim. 2) v. In phr. <i>to go veaking about</i>, to be fretful <i>Queak</i> v and sb Sc also written queek. 1) v. To squeak, cry, as the young of rats and mice. 2) sb A gentle squeak, the weak, peeping cry of the young of small animals. *(Oct 14, 1943,Letter)* [NOTES: DR C. CORNE Letter (Auckland)]

### wheelie bin
  
noun. a large refuse bin on wheels  
[NOTES: I suspect wheelie-bin appears to be Oz but Can-Plan being a local trade-name is NZ. The only American words that come near I can find are trash can and garbage can but I would still suspect that it was the Americans who first put them on wheels, probably in California where they have longer drives than in New York. The Oxford Dictionary of New Words (1991) has it as "a large refuse bin on wheels; a Eurobin. The wheelie bin first appeared in the UK about 1986, but both the object and the name seem to have been used in Australia for some years before that". However, the only Oz dictionary I could find it in was Macquarie 3rd edition, no quotes, no dates but it gives as alternative names "Otto" or "Otto bin" and "Sulo" or "Sulo bin", both being trade-names a la Can-Plan. In our street where every second person is on Can-Plan, we tend now to say "The neighbour has got his can-plan out". The Yellow Pages for Wellington give a number of variants : handikarts, wheelie carts, daily karts, care carts, handcarts, wheely bin, wheelibin. **OED online: 4. Special Comb. wheelie bin orig. Austral., a large refuse bin on wheels.]  
> [Hdg] Wheelie bins go country. Rural Southlanders were requesting wheelie bins at the rate of about 20 a month... Of the 7100 ratepayers using the wheelie bin service, about 300 were not on the original routes. *(Dec 13, 2000,Southland Times,00)* [NOTES: Get pages]  
> Mr Banks began by lambasting Mrs Fletcher over the city's wheelie-bin rubbish hassles, asking how she could solve the "wheelie big" issues if she could not deal with the "wheelie little" ones. *(Oct 3, 2001,Evening Post,25)*  
> [Advt] Can Plan. For a Green Clean Wheelibin - One Call Does it All. *(1996,Telecom Yellow Pages,Wellington / 894)*  
> Police also said a green wheelie bin was missing from the family home. *(Sep 21, 1998,Dominion,1)*  
> A green wheelie bin with the number 8/39 on it was missing from the family home and .. a glory box about 1.5 m long was also missing. *(Sep 21, 1998,Evening Post,13)*  
> Police.. would not say today if her body was in a green wheelie rubbish bin found last night. *(Sep 22, 1998,Evening Post,2)*  
> [The body] was believed to be inside a green wheelie-bin police found on Monday night and sent to Auckland for tests. *(Sep 23, 1998,Dominion,1)*  
> [Hdg] Wheelie-bin ride ends in tragedy. An early morning joy ride in a wheelie-bin down the world's steepest public street ended in tragedy yesterday when the plastic container crashed into a parked trailer. *(Mar 2, 2001,Dominion,1)*  
> DO [sic] you realise that the Whakatane District Council is going to confiscate our big green wheelie bins and replace them with a much smaller receptacle? *(Feb 22, 2006,Whakatane Beacon,2)*

### whekau
  
noun. the laughing owl  
[NOTES: also wekau]  
> It was the wild cry of Whekau, the Laughing Owl. *(Dec 10, 1910,Marlborough Express,6)*  
> The laughing owl, or whekau, as it was called by the Maoris, is much larger than the morepork and has a fair amount of grayish white on the face and head. *(Sep 12, 1936,Evening Post,17)*  
> <i>Athene albifacies</i>. - Whekau, Laughing Owl. South Island, and Kaimanawa Range in North Island. *(1886,TrNZI,18/112)* [NOTES: (Martin)]  
> **ANGAS** Amongst the strange ornithological forms that occur in New Zealand is a very remarkable bird of the owl tribe, called the `wekau' by the natives. It is rather larger than the ordinary screech-owl, spotted with chestnut and black, and has long legs, and small green-coloured feet. The head is very small, and the beak like that of a hawk, thus imparting somewhat of an accipitrine character to this anomalous owl. It is found in the Middle Island, where its habits are, from its conformation, no doubt terrestrial. Its scientific name is <i>Sceloglaux albifacies. </i> *(1866,Polynesia,62)*  
> **GOULD** <i>Sceloglaux Albifacies</i>, Wekau. Another of the strange inhabitants of our antipodal country, New Zealand. An Owl it unquestionably us, but how widely does it differ from every other member of its family.' *(1869,Birds of Australia,[Supplement]:)*  
> **HUTTON** ATHENE ALBIFACIES. Gray. Laughing Owl. Laughing Jackass. Wekau. *(1871,Birds of New Zealand,2)*  
> **BULLER** SCELOGLAUX ALBIFACIES. (LAUGHING-OWL.) ... <i>Native names</i>. Whekau, Ruru-whekau, and Kakaha; `Laughing Jackass' of the colonists. <i>Ibid.</i> 24 A Whekau entered a shepherd's abode at the foot of Mount Hutt, and remained for several days perching in the roof; and on one occasion it seized a mouse which a cat had just brought in. *(1873,Birds of New Zealand,21,1888 2nd edn 1/198)*  
> <i>Athene albifacies</i>, Laughing owl (whekau). *(1885,TrNZI,18/97)* [NOTES: A. REISCHEK]  
> **MORRIS** <i>Whekau, n </i>Maori name for the bird <i>Sceloglaux albifacies,</i> Gray, a New Zealand owl, which is there called the <i>Laughing - Jackass.</i> See <i>Jackass. </i> *(1898,Austral English,509)*  
> <i>whekau n</i>. a small owl; the laughing owl. [Maori] *(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,12)*  
> **STEVENSON** The [bouquet] sachets were made from the skins of laughing owls (whekaus), birds that were once quite common in this locality, but now appear to be extinct. Occasionally their weird laughter could be overheard on dark ... nights up to about 1900 ... *(1947,Maori and Pakeha in North Otago,56)*  
> **FALLA et al.** LAUGHING OWL <i>Sceloglaux albifacies Maori name:</i> Whekau. ... According to early accounts the call from which this species received its name is heard mainly on dark nights accompanied by rain or drizzle or before rain; ... *(1966,Birds of New Zealand,184)*  
> <i>~ n. </i>New Zealand bird, the laughing owl [Maori]. *(1966,Contemp. Dict. Supplement (Whitcombe's),628)*  
> <i>whekau ... n</i>. N.Z. bird, laughing owl [Maori]. *(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),21)*  
> <i>232.b. Sceloglaux albifacies albifacies</i> (Gray, 1844) <i>Laughing Owl (Whekau) </i> *(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,61,1980)*  
> <i>Extinct birds ... Laughing Owl Sceloglaux albifacies</i> (GRAY, 1844) OTHER NAMES: <i>Whekau, hakoke (North Island),rock owl</i>e `doleful shrieks' of the laughing owl, which varied seasonally, were heard incessantly on rainy nights. Apparently it was easily kept in captivity, and reports say that it cooeyed in the evening, yelped like a young dog and mewed and chuckled. ... The most recent record of sighting was at Manapouri in 1950 ... *(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,308)*

### wheke
  
noun. a fern  
[NOTES: wheki, weki]  
> The two Dicksonias - wheke and whekeponga to the Maoris - are medium tree ferns, confined to New Zealand. *(May 11, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,15)*  
> **HOOKER** Weki, <i>Col[enso]. Dicksonia squarrosa.</i> Wheki, <i>Lindsay. Dicksonia squarrosa. </i> *(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),769)*  
> Weki. Cyathea Smithii, Hook. f. *(1869,TrNZI,1/III/51)* [NOTES: (Buchanan Botany Otago)]  
> A remarkable feature ... is the social character of the arborescent ferns, more especially the Mamaku <i>(Cyathea medullaris,</i> Swartz.), and the Weki <i>(Dicksonia squarrosa,</i> Swartz.) ... *(1870,TrNZI,2/90)* [NOTES: (Kirk)]  
> **CHEESEMAN** <i>D[icksonia] squarrosa, Swartz,</i> ... Trunk 6-20 ft. high ... <i>Weki or Wheki. </i> *(1906,Manual NZ Flora,953)*  
> Some people speak and write of them as `tree ferns' ... this is of course loose and misleading. There are fern-trees on my section up to fifty feet in height, and at least one of them - the wheki variety, is a hard-bitten fellow ... The unobservant are apt to lump all fern-trees together, as pongas, or, in the vernacular, `bungers'. *(Mar 10, 1920,Quick March,38)*  
> **COWAN** ... the slopes are black-pencilled with the stumps of the <i>wheki</i>, a fern-tree whose butt is as hard as ironbark and almost as indestructible. *(1922,NZ Wars, The,1/13)*  
> **LEVY** ... such species as lancewood, karamu, toro, ramarama, koromiko, kanono, kohuhu, kawakawa, hangehange, rangiora, five finger, patete, toru, tree daisies, broadleaf, kamahi, mahoe, tarata, karo, lacebark, wineberry, fuchsia, kowhai, with tree ferns, weki, and mamuku. Toetoe, phormium, cabbage tree, with swamp coprosma and hupiro may figure as a developmental phase on wet soils. *(1951,Grasslands of NZ,87,1970)*  
> **RICHARDS** <i>D[icksonia] squarrosa</i> ... Slender or Rough Tree-fern. WEKI. *(1952,Chatham Islands,8)*  
> **MARTIN** There are three species of <i>Dicksonia, D. squarrosa</i>, [109] known as the WHEKI or SLENDER TREE-FERN being much the most abundant. *(1961,Flora NZ,108,1929)*  
> <i>wheki [s] Dicksonia squarrosa</i> (Forst.f.) Swartz *(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,85)*  
> Dicksoniaceae <i>Dicksonia squarrosa</i> wheki *(1975,Tane,21/8)* [NOTES: (Wright Vegetation Mt Maungatawhiri)]  
> **WILSON** <i>Dicksonia squarrosa</i> WHEKII ... Rough tree fern ... Trunk dark brown or black, rough with persistent stalk bases, ... *(1982,Stewart Island Plants,420)*  
> **DAWSON** Wheki (<i>Dicksonia squarrosa</i>) has a trunk surface similar to [87] that of the ponga. *(1988,Forest Vines to Snow Tussocks,86)*  
> We have three described species of this genus, namely, D. squamosa or scaly, D. squarrosa or rough stemmed, and D. lanata or woolly.   The native names for this genus are Tuakura and Weki. *(Sep 18, 1869,Taranaki Herald,5)*

### wheki
  
noun.  
> This densely coated trunk makes Wheki ponga rat-proof and it was the building material of choice for Maori storage. *(Nov 30, 2002,Dominion Post,F15)*  
> [Wheki's] less common kin is wheki-ponga, <i>Dicksonia fibrosa</i>, which grows in upland districts of the central North island and in the South Island. It is easily recognised by its thick cuddly trunk, which is made even fatter by a dense skirt of dead fronds. *(May 2002,Forest & Bird,38)*

### wheki
  
noun. [Maori; Environment] a slender tree-fern  
> Dicksonia squarrosa, or Wheki, in contrast, has a long slim trunk, is tough enough to survive bush fires and is our most common tree fern. *(Nov 30, 2002,Dominion Post,F15)*  
> Conversely, wheki feels rough and the brown trunk is often covered with the black finger-like remains of old leaves. *(Jan 3, 2004,Dominion Post,C2)*  
> These 'dinosaur' plants are wheki and wheki-ponga, belonging to the genus <i>Dicksonia</i>, with brown stems and scratchy fronds. Wheki, <i>Dicksonia squarrosa</i>, is our commonest tree fern. *(May 2002,Forest & Bird,304/38)*  
> Today wheki <<i>Dicksonia squarrosa</i>) is used for retaining walls, and the ponga for making tree vases that are widely sold in tourist shops. ... Such is their vitality, "ponga" posts - here probably wheki, <i>Dicksonia squarrosa</i> - may sprout into a living fence, despite having been decapitated and felled. *(2001,NZ Geographic,No. 49/77)*  
> At least one tree fern, the wheki - the one most often used for garden fencing - has developed the ability to make lateral buds so it can resprout. *(Aug 25, 2001,Dominion Weekend Magazine,10)*  
> The Dicksonias include wheki 9D squarrosa) and wheki-ponga (D fibrosa). .. Wheki will put up with more exposure than others, but won't look their best in a dry, windy spot. *(Jul 29, 2006,Dominion Post Indulgence,18)*

### wheki-ponga
  
noun.  
> Two species, wheki-ponga (<i>Dicksonia fibrosa</i>) and the soft tree fern (<i>Cyathea smithii</i>), are renowned for the skirt of dead fronds that surrounds their trunks. *(2001,NZ Geographic,No. 49/72)*  
> Wheki-ponga is the easiest of the lot; its fibrous, russet coloured trunk usually wears a distinctive "skirt" of old, dead fronds or stalks. *(Jan 3, 2004,Dominion Post,C2)*  
> **HOOKER** Wekiponga, <i> Col[enso]. Dicksonia antarctica. </i> *(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),769)*  
> Sometimes ... the outside was formed of hard fibrous slabs cut from the stout red-brown fern-tree, Wekiponga[sic] <i>(Dicksonia australis);</i> ... *(1869,TrNZI,1/III/35)* [NOTES: (Colenso Botany North Isd)]  
> Cyathea medullaris , black fern ... Dicksonia <i> antartica </i>,[sic] wekiponga ... <i> Cyathea dealbata </i>, white fern ... *(1876,TrNZI,8/412)*  
> **CHEESEMAN** <i> [Dicksonia fibrosa, Col.] Weki-ponga; Kuripaka. </i> *(1906,Manual NZ Flora,953)*  
> **RICHARDS** <i> Dicksonia fibrosa </i>. This is the commonest tree-fern in the Chathams. ... Woolly Tree-fern. WEKI PONGA. *(1952,Chatham Islands,7)*  
> **MARTIN** The second species [of <i> Dicksonia </i>] known as the PUNUI or WHEKI-PONGA (<i> D. fibrosa </i>) ... *(1961,Flora NZ,109,1929)*  
> **WILSON** <i> Dicksonia fibrosa </i> WHEKII-PONGA ... Fibrous tree fern *(1982,Stewart Island Plants,424)*

### whelk
  
noun.  
> ... there are not wanting Several Sorts [of fish] that are well known; such as Mullet, Cavallas, Guard-Fish, Horse-Mackeral. Amongsyt the crustaceous Tribes we found Craw-fish, Muscles. [sic] Cockles, Scollops, Whelks, Periwinkles, ... *(1773,Cook's Journals,2/787,1961)* [NOTES: 1773 Wales Journal in Cook Journal (1961) 2/787]  
> (Queen Charlotte Sound) Of other shellfish there are ten or twelve sorts such as periwinkles, wilks, limpets, and some very beautiful sea Ears and another sort that sticks to the weeds , with some other things as sea eggs, starfish ... ed. fn Periwinkles, <i> Melarhapa </i> spp. (2), and probably <i> Zediloma </i> and <i> Melagraphia </i> spp. Whelks, <i> Cominella </i> sp. Limpets, Cellana spp. The `very beautiful sea Ears' were certainly the Paua, Haliotis iris; .. *(Feb 1777,Cook's Journals,3/808,1967)* [NOTES: 1777 Feb Anderson's Journal in Cook Journals (1967) 3/808]  
> **WILLIAMS** <i> Kakara, Thais haustrum, whelk.</i> [1957/97 <i> Lepsia haustrum </i>] *(1917,Dictionary of the Maori Language,115)*  
> [species name] whelks .. [scientific name] -- [other name(s)] general name for 4-20 different species *(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters)*  
> An immense limpet, two inches across, a few whelks, chitons, and very minute mussels were all the mollusca that I obtained, and two species of actinia or sea anemone, two species of star fish and a sea urchin, completed the list of marine life. *(Nov 10, 1863,Otago Daily Times,9)*  
> .. to believe that the hermit crab was created together with his whelk shell, is like the old theory that when the Creator made the rocks He made the fossils in them. *(Mar 10, 1876,Star, The,2)*  
> A variety of whelk is also very fond of young oysters, to get at which it bores right through the shell and sucks the fish up through the hole thus made. *(Sep 16, 1893,Bush Advocate,5)*  
> Pink pearls are found in some kinds of pink-shelled whelks. *(Apr 3, 1908,Star, The,2)*  
> Although one oyster produces about 1,000,000 oysters in a year, only a few of these survive the attacks of enemies, including whelks and limpets. *(Dec 2, 1920,Ashburton Guardian,6)*

### whelk
  
noun. Cominella adspersa  
> **POWELL28** SPECKLED WHELK (<i> Cominella adspersa </i>). Kawari of the Maoris. Abundant in the North Island, particularly on rocky ground, near mud, in harbours. *(1947,Native Animals of NZ)*  
> **McLINTOCK** WHELK, SPECKLED (<i> Cominella adspersa </i>). This and several other local species are somewhat related to the English whelk. ... [647] It was known to the Maoris as kawari. <i>Ibid.</i> 3/705-712 Speckled whelk .. .. kawari .. .. <i> Cominella adspersa </i> *(1996,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/646)*

### whenua
  
noun. the land; a native country, country (perhaps best known in the collocation tangata whenua, people native to a placed.  
[NOTES: wenua]  
> ...Ngati Whatua, who had previously uncontested standing as manawhenua across Auckland isthmus, had seen over 100,000 acres of its whenua disappear with little to show for it. *(Apr 2004,Wel-com (Wellington),5)*  
> We questioned the old man ... who said that it consisted of two <i>Wannuaes</i>, that is, two lands or Islands ... *(Jan 31, 1770,Cook's Journals,1/143,1955)*  
> **FORSTER** A Comparative Table of the Various Languages in the Isles of the South-Sea ... [English] <i>Land</i> ... [Friendly-Isles] Fannooa [o1-grave, a2-macron ... [New-Zeeland] Whennooa [o2-grave, a-macron] *(1778,Observations Made During a Voyage,284)*  
> **LEE & KENDALL** W|enua, s. The earth; *(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,227)*  
> land - fe'nwa [whenua] *(1835,Boultbee Journal,112,1986)*  
> **SHORTLAND** It was he [Maui] who is supposed to have hauled up `Whenua' or the earth from the depths of the ocean, ... *(1851,Southern Districts of NZ,155)*  
> **MASON** WERIHE ... [Our people] move from the whenua to the city: from wood to brick, from old quiet to new clamour. *(1969,Awatea,60,1978)*  
> **BAXTER** [The little flies] ... regard me as their whenua. *(1979,Collected Poems,580,1972)*  
> **HOWE** Whenua - see `tangata whenua'. Working with the land was regarded as honourable, and because of its spiritual significance the land was cared for and protected (conversation link) *(1985,Towards Taha Maori,24)*  
> MAORI VOCABULARY .. The earth   Wenua. *(Jul 25, 1843,New Zealand Colonist & Port Nicholson Advertiser,3)*

### whenua rahui/nga whenua rahui
  
noun. [Maori]  
[NOTES: Nga Whenua Rahui is a contestable fund aimed at facilitating voluntary protection of indigenous biodiversity on Maori land. www.biodiversity.govt.nz/land/nzbs/pvtland/nwr.html  And see other Google hits for whenua rahui]  
> Mr McGarvey said replanting of the 2.2 hectare whenua rahui (reserve) had been achieved not by agreement or legislation but through observing Maori tikanga and a verbal agreement with landowner George Uatuku. *(Nov 11, 2005,Whakatane Beacon,6)*  
> Although the Crown negotiated a <i>nga whenui rahui</i> protective covenant in 2003 over The Needles block..DOC has so far been unable to come up with a 'wilderness covenant' proposition acceptable to the owners... *(Mar 2006,FMC - (Federated Mountain Clubs of NZ) Bulletin,32)*

### whenuatanga
  
noun. [Maori] respect for the land  
> One [feature] is whenuatanga or respect for the land as the basic resource on which all human, animal and plant life depends. *(May 2004,Fulbright New Zealand Quarterly,10(2): 5)* [NOTES: Michael King]

### where the whips are cracking
  
phrase. in the firing line, where the going is tough  
See: -  
> **PARTRIDGE** <i>Whips are cracking, where the.</i> The front line: New Zealanders': in G.W. Ex the activity of cattle-mustering. *(1937,Dictionary of Slang,952)*  
> [Slang of the N.Z.E.F.] `To be smacked up,' ` to take one's hook,' `to be where the whips are cracking' are clear, ... *(Apr 2, 1938,Press, The,18)* [NOTES: McNab NZ Slang]  
> **SYDER & HODGETTS** In the thick of the action, where the challenge to the endeavour is at its most severe. .in 4;`Take no notice of their casual attitude. Those chaps are as game as they come. They'll be there where the whips are cracking.' *(1974,Australian & NZ English [TS],1092)*  
> **TAYLOR** Big Snowy Tate kept jumping about yelling and waving his bayonet ... `I'll be there when the whip is cracking,' he kept shouting. *(1978,Twilight Hour,46)*

### whero
  
adjective. [Maori] red  
See: -  
> **PYKE** Mr Thomas Pratt, who represents the Southern Maori District in the New Zealand Parliament, informs me that the word `ferro' in Mr Palmer's statement (p. 2) should be written `wherro' - though pronounced `fherro' - the `wh' being always pronounced as `fh' in the Maori tongue. Thus Kawhia in the North Island is pronounced `Kafhia,' and so in all instanced, The meaning of the word is `red,' or is more generally used to indicate anything brightly coloured.`Simon' - the word used by Mr Archibald - is probably a corruption of `timata,' strongly accenting the `i.' It means `heavy' - and in this connection, `heavy stones.' *(1887,History of the Early Gold Discoveries  in Otago,121,1962)*

noun.

### whiffler
  
noun.  
> The "Whiffler" has again been causing amusement in Wellington. *(Oct 12, 1885,Wairarapa Daily,2)*  
> **LYON** One [in the 1880's] who was popularly known as `The Whiffler', ... contributed ... to the gaiety of both Islands. ... He referred in Court to a `whiffler' and was promptly asked to define the term. He explained that a `whiffler' was a term used in pigeon match shooting to a young pigeon of weak flight which flew far enough over the boundary line to preclude its inclusion in the winning score. Harris from that date was dubbed `The Whiffler' ... *(1952,Faring South,112)*

### whim
  
noun.  
> WHIM.- A machine consisting of a barrel or cylinder worked by horse or water power, and used for raising ore from a mine. *(1868,Thames Miner's Guide, The,56)*  
> **McARA** THREE GOOD HORSES for hauling dirt with whim must be supplied by contractors ... [104] Permanent tram road to be laid, contractor may use Company's whim to haul all dirt but must supply horse. *(1978,Gold Mining at Waihi,103)* [NOTES: 1896 Oct 26 specifications in 1978 McAra Gold Mining at Waihi 103]  
> **REED** ... reference was made to `whims', or capstans. R. Ross describes one of these appliances operated by bullocks as recently as 1937, for hauling up logs from the bottom of a steep gully. *(1953,Story of the Kauri, The,194)*

### whim hoist
  
noun.  
> **McARA** <i>Whim hoist</i>: A hoisting device operated by a horse and used in the very early days on No 2 shaft and on the Union No 1 shaft. A large drum with the winding-rope on it was mounted vertically and equipped with a long arm to which the horse was attached. By walking in a circle the horse pulled up the bucket by rotating the drum. *(1978,Gold Mining at Waihi (Glossary),337)*

### whimbrel
  
noun.

> Native birds around the harbour are also of great interest to overseas birders, such as banded rail and marsh crake, kingfisher, grey warbler, shining cuckoo and the beautiful terns and migratory waders such as godwit and whimbrel (not "whimbrill"), which many never get a chance to see in the northern hemisphere. *(Sep 2002,NZ Geographic,No. 59/4)*

### whinge
  
verb.  
[NOTES: 1972 COD 7 `dialect or Australian' (also HNZD) 1981 Auckland Savings Bank, Queen Street (Goldie Brown Collection) <i>Winge:</i> complain (as in wingeing Pom) -Internet search Jan 2006: 95 000 Austral.; 706 NZ / 500 000.]  
> But for Sir Clive and his team to whinge on about it as if it were the crime of the century... *(Jun 28, 2005,NZ Herald,A10)*  
> `All happy in the service? ...' `Of course they're not.' `They've had plenty of time to jack themselves up by now ... No excuse for wingeing. [Glossary defines as `complaining'.] ca1960s as a ppl aj collocated with POM(S), or as a vbl n [?f. Australia] *(1946,Johnny Enzed in Italy,44)*  
> **WEBBER** `I could hazard a guess, too,' I said, `but it is late in the day to start wingeing.' *(1962,Look No Hands!,67)*  
> **DUGGAN** I've lost the high tone for the whinge of it [sc Riley's notebook]. I'm a weary as you of Riley however the hell he is. *(1970,O'Leary's Orchard,171)*  
> Nevertheless, the rugby union is wingeing that it lost a fair bit of gate takings because of the telecast. *(Sep 24, 1972,Sunday Times (NZ),42)*

### whingeing Pom
  
noun.  
> TVNZ said "Poms" was a term used affectionately by Kiwis and Australians, and it rejected the complainants' view that the word was comparable to terms such as "Chinks",  "Wogs", or "Horis". It agreed expressions such as "Pommy bastards" and "whingeing Poms" were terms of abuse. Authority chairman Sam Maling noted the Human Rights and Equal Opportunity Commission's ruling that the words "Pom" and "Pommy" were unlikely to offend of insult. *(Mar 24, 1999,Dardanelles Driveller,1)*  
> Whatever happened to the whingeing Pom?... The point is, where has he gone? Not the bloke who wrote the whingeing letter about the whingeing, the WP himself? *(Nov 21, 1999,Sunday Star Times,A11)*  
> **SHERWOOD** A wingeing pom is a person from the United Kingdom who never ceases to complain in a loud whine about aspects of this country's arrangements which displease him. *(1985,Botanist at Bay,18)*  
> **McGILL** <i>whingeing Pom</i> any British immigrant complaining about life here; a stereotype of the British immigrant. NZA. *(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,122)*  
> Rosemary McLeod's column sucks. Who needs to import "whingeing Poms" when our own "home-grown" Kiwi (with her Scottish surname) can outwhinge them all (and stir up some racial disharmony while she's at it - nice one)? *(Jun 24, 2003,Dominion,B4)*  
> **HALL** I'd always looked on immigrants as abunch of whinging Poms, but now I realisewe treated them like shit! *(1980,Prisoner of Mother England,[v])*

noun.

### whio
  
noun. [Maori] a small, blue-grey, mountain-dwelling, whistling native duck <i>Hymenolaimus malacorhynchus</i>, aka blue (mountain) duck, mountain duck, whistling duck; its whistling call  
> The regal birds fly downriver, with the male whistling its distinct "whio" call. *(Jul 5, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),29)*  
> **REES** The palmipedes are some anas proper.. and a bird of the anser genus (wio), aboutthe size of a small duck, possessed of an osseous protuberance on each pinion, bymeans of which it is enabled to climb the river bank. *(1851,GB Parliamentary Papers (Irish Univ. series),1779/28)*  
> **LEE & KENDALL** W|io, a. Whistling. *(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,227)*  
> **DIEFFENBACH** 61. <i>Malacorynchus Forsterorum, Wagl., .</i>.. He-weego of the natives of Dusky Bay. Forst. *(1843,Travels in NZ,2.198)* [NOTES: 1842 Gray's Fauna in 1843 Dieffenbach Travels in NZ 2/198]  
> **TAYLOR** Mr Maclean here [?near Hikurangi] shot a wio a curious kind of duck and a duck besides. This is a very rapid stream and difficult to cross *(Nov 8, 1845,Journal,3/166)*  
> **WAKEFIELD** High up among the mountains, the rapid streams are inhabited by the <i> Wiho, (Malacorynchus Forsterorum?)</i> a black duck with a white bill, whose cry is a shrill whistle, and the flesh of which is exceedingly fat and well-flavoured. *(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The)*  
> **BRUNNER** Shot a <i> wihu </i>, or blue duck. *(1848,Journal of an Expedition)* [NOTES: 1848 Brunner Expedition Middle Island in 1959 Taylor Early Traveller 272]  
> **MACLEAN** Pigeon, <i> weka </i>, duck, and <i>whio </i> (blue mountain duck) are included in the papa ... *(Jul 16, 1849,Diary)* [NOTES: 1849 July 16 Maclean Diary in 1940 Cowan Sir Donald Maclean 48]  
> **TAYLOR** : `Whio (<i> Hymenolaemus malacorhynchus </i>), the blue duck, is found abundantly in the mountain-streams, and in the Middle Island. It takes its name from its cry.' Our provisions here began to fail, but catching some eels and whios (blue ducks), they made a good addition to our scanty commissariat. *(1855,Te Ika a Maui,407)*  
> **HAAST** The most abundant member of the family Anatidae ... is the whio, the blue duck of the settlers (Anas malacorhyncus). *(1861,Report on Topographical Exploration of Nelson,136)*  
> HYMENOLAIMUS MALACORHYNCHUS, Gml. Whio. Blue Duck, Mountain Duck. *(1870,TrNZI,2/73)* [NOTES: (Potts Birds)]  
> **HUTTON** 101. HYMENOLAIMUS MALACORHYNCHUS. Gml. Blue, or Mountain Duck. Whio. *(1871,Birds of New Zealand,37)*  
> **BULLER** HYMENOLAEMUS MALACORHYNCUS. (BLUE DUCK.) ... <i> Native name </i>.- Wio *(1873,Birds of New Zealand,262,1888 2nd edn 2/276)*  
> **BRUNNER** [wihu] shot a wihu, or blue duck ... *(1874,Great Journey,35)*  
> <i> Hymenolaimus malacorhynchus.</i> - Whio, Blue Duck. Both Islands. *(1886,TrNZI,18/113)* [NOTES: (Martin)]  
> The ordinary note of the species is a sibilant whistle, whence it derives its native name, `Wio.' *(1893,TrNZI,25/83)* [NOTES: (Buller)]  
> **MORRIS** <i> Whio, n.</i> (originally <i> Whio-whio </i>), also <i> Wio </i>, Maori name for the New Zealand Duck, <i> Hymenolaemus malacorhynchus </i>, Gmel., called the <i> Blue-Duck </i> or <i> Mountain-Duck </i> of New Zealand. See <i> Duck </i>, Professor Parker's quotation, 1889. The bird has a whistling note. The Maori verb, <i> whio </i>, means to whistle. *(1898,Austral English,509)*  
> **COWAN** We saw little squadrons of `whio' the blue mountain duck ...; they called to each other in the whistling cry that has given them their Maori name .. *(1916,Bush Explorers,24)*  
> <i> whio, wio n.</i> a duck inhabiting mountain streams. [Maori] *(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,12)*  
> <i> whio </i>, a water-bird (Hymenolaimus malacorhynchus), blue-duck, mountain-duck, whistling duck; name echoic, like English whew-duck having a similar name for the same reason. The place-name Whiorau, means blue-ducks a-plenty. *(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/160)* [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]  
> <i> whio n </i>. a New Zealand blue duck [Maori]. *(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),628)*  
> <i> whio ... n </i>. a N.Z. blue chick [sic: error =duck] [Maori]. *(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),21)*  
> <i> 114. Hymenolaimus malacorhynchos </i> (Gmelin, 1789) <i> Blue Duck (Whio) </i> *(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,88,1980)*  
> <i>Blue Duck  Hymenolaimus malacorhynchos </i> (GMELIN, 1789) ... OTHER NAME: <i> Whio.</i> *(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,149)*  
> <i> whio n </i>. NZ blue or whistling duck. [M] *(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,879)* [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]  
> The Opuiaki forest, home to many endangered species including kokako, kiwi, red mistletoe and whio, was at risk due to high numbers of possums and rats. *(Jun 2007,Conservation Action,47)*

### whip the cat
  
phrase. to be annoyed over failure; moan, whinge; express useless regret -  
> I am engaging in the work of atonement figuratively attributed to "whipping the cat." *(Jan 11, 1871,Bruce Herald,6)*  
> "Our boys" have returned from holiday-making considerably reduced in a pecuniary sense, with long faces and empty pockets.   One or two have determined to (as it is jocularly termed) whip the cat. *(Jan 21, 1882,Observer (Auckland),298)*  
> He said that though defeated he was not beaten, and he was not the man to 'whip the cat.' *(Nov 28, 1890,Mataura Ensign,7)*  
> After his sparkling performance in the Mosgiel Handicap the owners of Bonnie Winkie must be whipping the cat over withdrawing him from the New Zealand Cup. *(Oct 21, 1922,NZ Truth,12)*  
> Phar Lap's owner may now be whipping the cat for not entering the freak three-year-old for the Sydney Cup. *(Apr 3, 1930,NZ Truth,11)*  
> It is little use "whipping the cat" now over the "spilt milk" in both places. *(Mar 14, 1942,Evening Post,6)*  
> It should be borne in mind that many of the members of the police force are married men with families, and these will have to suffer to the extent of some aGBP5 or aGBP6, in order that they may appear for a day or two in superior style, while the unmarried men will have to "whip the cat" for some time to make up the outlay. *(Mar 10, 1868,Evening Post,2)* [NOTES: sense not quite in line with headword definition]  
> If you `blew in' at the races,/ Then you `whip the flaming cat'. *(Mar 31, 1906,NZ Truth,6)*  
> **THOMPSON** You could make tenners then like winkin',/ Dough now you are vipping der cat,/ *(1909,Ballads about Business,12)*  
> **McGILL** <i>whip</i>, phr.<i> crack the whip </i>urge to greater effort, as a jockey would; NZA. - <i>fair crack of the whip</i> appeal for fair treatment; NZA. - <i>where the whips are cracking</i> the front line in battle, WI, from cattle mustering and small arms fire eliciting similar sounds. - <i>whip the cat</i> to moan or reproach yourself; make a fuss. NZA. *(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,122)*  
> But `Critic' asks the said gentleman to submit the following sentence to any school boy or a semi-intelligent adult: `The Opposition are whipping the cat because they've got Buckley's chance of pulling the leg of the farmer with an onkus [sic] freehold lurk.' *(Jun 11, 1910,NZ Truth,259/1/3)*  
> Tell him [sc a misled person] he has leave to go and whip the cat. *(1911,Triad, The,18/3/18)*  
> **PETER NEWTON** Musterers use `whip the cat' in the sense of expressing regret at some act or decision *(1952,Personal Communication)*  
> **REY BYRNE** I missed getting the flat and I whipped the cat about it for a couple of days *(1951,Personal Communication)* [NOTES: Wellington]  
> <i> whip the cat </i>(phrase) to ` cry over spilt milk.' *(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. (Aust.-NZ Suppl.),613)*  
> <i> cat, whip the (Phr.)</i> `cry over spilt milk.' *(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),5)*

### whip-behind
  
noun.  
> The boy upon foot cannot bear to see the boy who is riding.   And so it is with envy of a larger growth.   We are always crying out, "Whip behind," in the miserable hope of seing [sic] some hanger-on, more fortunate than ourselves, knocked off his perch. *(Dec 2, 1856,Daily Southern Cross,4)*  
> It is like the ragamuffins in the street who cry out "Whip behind!" directly they see one of their comrades who has got a lift. *(Aug 19, 1861,Bruce Herald,3)*  
> The Guy was placed on the hind-step of the 'bus with his tall hat nodding gracefully, so as to be seen through the glass of the doorway.   "Whip behind!" yelled the urchins, and whip behind it was. *(Nov 20, 1877,Nelson Evening Mail,2)*  
> The vehicle had passed Wills Street before the appeal was heard, and the way the boy scattered on the stoppage of the lorry showed that he had received a lesson more lasting than the old-fashioned "whip behind." *(Apr 20, 1920,Ashburton Guardian,4)*  
> Ho! driver, whip behind! *(Jun 11, 1885,Bay of Plenty Times,2)*  
> 'Whip behind!' yelled some wretched urchins. *(Feb 3, 1894,Observer (Auckland),7)*  
> "WHIP BEHIND" ON THE MAIL COACH. *(Apr 20, 1911,Evening Post,11)*  
> **A.S. WICKENS** A `whip-behind' was used by children for a swinging ride behind a cart or wagon - perhaps from the driver having to turn around and try to slash them off behind with his whip; perhaps from using the momentum of the cart to get a whip-like release; perhaps from `whipping' (that is, dashing quickly there and away again) in behind the cart for a ride. It was said, e.g.,: `I'll give you a hiding if you whip behind the baker's cart again!' My father remembered this word from when he was a boy in Dunedin in the 1870s to 1880s. *(1870,Personal Communication)* [NOTES: ca1870-1880 Dunedin p.c.]  
> [A note in Blue Domino's column remarks that an elderly man called out `whip behind' when he saw two boys on bicycles having a free tow by hanging on to the tray of a moving truck.] *(Sep 1963,Sports Post)*  
> **SUTTON-SMITH** The coach to Dunedin used to stop at Fagerty's Hotel for watering the horses, which were glad of a spell, and then we got ready to hang on behind. The boys who could not get hold of a strap or something would call out, `Whip behind,' and one ill-natured driver did it, too, as the coach went on. Once one of us tripped, and a heap of boys fell in the wet and mud. Dick and Bob Runciman and my little brother Dick were covered with the slurry from head to foot. We got them cleaned somehow and went on to school. (1868; Green Island, Dunedin) *(1981,History of Children's Play,89)*

### whistleboy

> **BIRD** While calling whistles for a log hauler, the whistleboy - often only 12 or 13 years old - would learn the basic skills for almost all any other bush job. *(1998,Viaducts against the Sky,50)*  
> The man accompanying the logs to the hauler was known as a shoeman and he generally had a whistle boy who stopped the hauler by means of a whistle when the log reached one of the snatch blocks. *(1983,Port Craig,5)*  
> Monty's older brother worked as a whistle boy,.. signalling the hauler driver to slacken or tighten the wire ropes which ran off the winch drums. *(Jan 1992,NZ Geographic,No. 13/119)*  
> **BIRD** "Ho, ho!" called the breaker-out, and the whistleboy gave two claps of the switch in his hand... The job of whistleboy... was to relay instructions from the bush gang to the hauler-driver, out of sight at the machine. *(1998,Viaducts against the Sky,49)*
