### Whitaker's skink
  
noun. [Environment] a threatened skink species found now on just two islands of the Coromandel and in a threatened colony at Pukerua Bay.  
[NOTES: <i>Oligosoma whitakeri</i> aka pre 2009 <i>Cyclodina whitakeri</i>]  
> "The rare Whitaker's skink and the robust skink have been the main focus of the relocations, with the result that the future of these species is probably brighter now than at any time in the past 1000 years," says Towns. *(Jul 2000,NZ Geographic,47 / 78)*  
> Pukerua Bay .. home to five lizard species including the very rare Whitaker's skink <i> Cyclodina whitakeri </i> , a nocturnal species ... *(May 1985,Forest & Bird,23)*  
> **C.M. Miskelly** At first glance the food mass in the weasel's stomach appeared blackish with numerous tiny yellow flecks. This proved to be the remains of a juvenile Whitaker's skink, which made up about 70% of the stomach contents. The rest of the stomach's contents were comprised of the remains of a juvenile brown skink Leiolopisma zelandicum and three tiny invertebrates (a spider, an isopod and a fly) that may have come from the skinks' stomachs. *(1997,Internet - see Notes)* [NOTES: http://www.doc.govt.nz/upload/documents/science-and-technical/casn146.pdf 1997 Department of Conservation, P.O. Box 10-420, Wellington, New Zealand Reference to material in this report should be cited thus: Miskelly, C.M., 1997. Whitaker's skink <i>Cyclodina whitakeri</i> eaten by a weasel <i>Mustela nivalis</i>. Conservation Advisory Science Notes No. 146, Department of Conservation, Wellington.]

### whitau
  
noun.  
> flax - fee'tou [whitau] *(1835,Boultbee Journal,113,1986)*  
> **WHS ROBERTS** The whip was rawhide plaited sinnet, about six feet long, thick at the upper end, where it was fastened to a handle as long as itself, but tapering nearly to a point, to which was attached a narrow strip of rawhide, called `the fall,' finishing off with a twisted `cracker' made of `weetau' or dressed flax in [43] place of whipcord, and was a powerful flagellator in the hands of an experienced `bullock puncher.' *(Sep 19, 1856,Diary)* [NOTES: 1856 Sep 19 W.H.S. Roberts Diary in 1947 Beattie Early Runholding 42-43]  
> **HOOKER** Whitau, <i>Mantell.</i> Same as <i>Muka</i> (dressed flax). *(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),769)*  
> **LINDSAY** In Otago the general name of the [flax] plant, as a whole, is `Korari,' - or as metamorphosed by the settler, pronounced and spelt `Kouradi,' [a-macron] or `Coradi' [a-macron]. The same term is applied in the North Island equally to the whole plant, and to the dried flower-stem when cut for rafts or other purposes; or exclusively to the flower-stalk. In the North [83] Island, the Flax-plant is also known as `Harakeke,' or `Herekiakia,' - its usual name there. ... The lower part of the flower-stem is `Kaikaha;' the outer part of the leaf `Parakoka;' [a1, o-macron] the refuse of the leaf `Hunga-hunga;' the flax, dressed or not, or scraped fibre, manufactured from the leaf, `Whitau,' or `Muka.' *(1868,Contributions to NZ Botany,82)*  
> **BEATTIE** The dressed flax fibre is called whitau, also witau, and at one place it is erroneously written `wituu'. *(1945,Maori Place-Names of Canterbury,65)* [NOTES: c1880 South Island Maori Lists `Wahi Mahika Kai' in 1945 Beattie Maori Place-names of Canterbury 65]  
> With them takiri muka or whitau is pahuhuhu [fn Terms for the preparation of flax.] and they have a slight variation in the technique of kit-making. *(Mar 28, 1936,Na To Hoa Aroha,3/214)* [NOTES: (Ngata)]  
> **BEATTIE** The Maori mats were made of feetow (whitau or dressed flax). ... They were hung over one shoulder and fastened under the other ... *(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,11)*  
> <i>Phormium tenax</i> ... yielded an attractive silken floss (whitau) after patient hours of scraping at the long blades with a shell. *(1971,New Zealand's Heritage,1/277)*  
> **FULLER** Kokopu were sometimes taken on a rod, a small ball of dressed flax-fibre (whitau) attached to the rod by a cord and baited with earthworms. *(1978,Maori Food and Cookery,13)*  
> Several bundles of dressed flax<i> (whitau),</i> in hanks <i>(whenu).</i> *(1897,TrNZI,29/174)*

### white
  
adjective. as a distinguishing epithet in flora and fauna  
[NOTES: see arum, beech, birch, bog-gentian, broom, buttercup, campion, carrot, clematis, clover, couch, crane, cudweed, cushion-celmisia, daisy, mountain; dead-nettle, egret fat hen, flax, fireweed, flax; forget-me-not, shore; fuzzweed, gentian, heron, hinau, kamahi, maire, mangrove, manuka, mapau, maple, marigold, matipo, mignonette, moth, mullein, musk, mustard, nightshade, oxalis, pine, poplar, poroporo, rata; rata, climbing; rock shell, selfheal, shark, shore-gentian, slipper shell, sorrel stonecrop, tea-tree; fern, treefern; tussock, twitch, violet, willow sorrel, tea-tree; fern, tree-fern; violet]

### white
  
adjective. as a distinguishing epithet in names of fish  
[NOTES: see rattail; shark, pointer; warehou see also whitebait, white fillets, whitefish]

### white
  
noun. non-Maori of British or European descent  
See: also white man  
> **McNAB** I do not think either natives or settlers could live any great time in this part [Chalky Inlet] from the myriads of poisonous flies in summer and the cold in winter. We have seen some marks of visitors but whether natives or whites for the bay fishery (I should suppose the latter) I do not know. *(1913,Old Whaling Days,464)* [NOTES: 1838 Dec 10 Capt Balleny Journal <i>Eliza Scott </i>in 1913 McNab Old Whaling Days 464]  
> **MACKIE** The settlers are now busily engaged in the harvest, a few employ Maoris - these earn 3/- per day, the whites 6/-. The whites through the island bear but a small proportion to the aborigines, ... *(Feb 9, 1853,Traveller under Concern,77,1973)*  
> **BEATTIE** The whites would copy the native way of scraping flax for various purposes [c1840], and when their boots gave out would sometimes wear Maori `pararas' or sandals *(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,12)*

### white
  
noun. -  
> **HOOD** The rising generation of `mean whites' in both islands, but especially in the northern one are fearfully profane, and most offensive in their conversation; ... *(1875,Chowbokiana,12)*

### white
  
adjective. non-Maori  
> **HOCKEN** Here [on Stewart Island] was the comparatively large population of about seventy white people, with the same number of natives, inhabiting comfortable cottages, tilling the soil, and having the air of a well-to-do people. *(1898,Contributions to the Early History of New Zealand,53)*

### white
  
adjective. very decent  
See: cf white man  
> `If you want a good electric torch I'll get one from the Ossies; they're white, they are. Of course, they call me a bit of a Socialist.' `I heard you were a Red Fed,' interjected my companion. *(1917,New Zealand At the Front,90)*  
> **HYDE** `And here's the money you lent me.' Olive said curtly: `Well, shake. Wonders will never cease. Anyhow, you're white.' It was an odd expression [to a copper-coloured person] ... *(1938,Nor the Years Condemn,231)*  
> `Course he talks la-di-da, but he's a real white joker once you get to know him.' *(Sep 1852,Landfall,23/206)* [NOTES: (Pearson `Fretful Sleepers')]

### white alley
  
noun.  
> **SUTTON-SMITH** Marbles had a language of its own. There were names for the marbles, the games, and all the various aspects of the play. There were terms for marbles such as `taw' or `shooter', referring to the marbles used to fire with; `dubs', `dates', `stakes', `dukes', or `changers', referring to the marbles put down to be fired at. Then there were the terms referring to particular kinds of marbles: for example, agates, aggies, aggotties, American alleys, blood alleys, bomb-squashers, bonies, bonsers, bottlies, bull's eyes, bum-squashers, cat's eyes, chinkies, connies, duckies, eggies, glassies, Greeks, milkies, molly-bars, mulley-bars [mullibars: large marbles], peelies, peewees, piss-wees, pretties, Scotties, Scottish alleys, smokies, stinkies, stonies, stripies, tom-bobblers, white alleys, and in more recent years ball-bearings, chippies, plastics, and woodies. *(1972,Folkgames of Children,174)*  
> Jem has offered Tom threepence for his bag of white alleys and striped taws and Tom, after chaffering, had consented to trade. *(Nov 3, 1874,Thames Star,2)*  
> The old man fired a book at the tormentor, and, in the excitement, dropped his glass-eye, which the lad picked up and "swopped" for six white alleys. *(Jun 27, 1885,Otago Witness,28)*  
> 'This opened the ball, and at the very start I saw I had a chance for my white alley ...' *(Apr 7, 1894,Southland Times,2)*  
> The other boy had no assets to speak of, but he had very wisely chosen to be born under the Stars and Stripes, where the poor boy with a gnawing ambition gets every show for his white alley. *(Jul 15, 1903,Otago Witness,72)*  
> The other boy had no assets to speak of, but he had very wisely chosen to be born under the Stars and Stripes, where the poor boy with a gnawing ambition gets every show for his white alley. *(Jul 15, 1903,Otago Witness,72)*

### white arum
  
noun. = arum lily  
> <i>white arum [=] arum lily [s] </i> *(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,85)*

### white birch
  
noun. = tawai-rauriki, Nothofagus solandri  
> The crinkly-leaved white birch, cousin to the settlers' New Zealand lilac, had burst into full bloom. *(Nov 23, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,17)*  
> **HOOKER** Fagus <i>Solandri,</i> Hook. fil. ... English name, `White Birch', <i>Bidwill</i>. A very beautiful lofty evergreen tree, attaining 100 feet in deep rich soil ... <i>Old bark </i>black, cracked; young white, smooth, like [English] Birch, which the wood also resembles, being close, tough, white, not durable under exposure ... *(1853,Flora NZ,I Flowering Plants 230)*  
> Red Birch of Otago, Black Birch of Nelson, Beech etc (Fagus Menziesii); Black Birch of Otago (F. fusca) White Birch of Otago (F. Solandri)[get full citation] *(1869,TrNZI,1/32)* [NOTES: (Buchanan Botany Otago)]  
> **KIRK** WHITE BIRCH of Nelson and Otago, BLACK BIRCH of Wellington, Canterbury, and Nelson.- (<i>Fagus solandri</i> and <i>F. cliffortioides</i>) *(1875,Durability NZ Timbers,17)*  
> **HAAST** <i>Fagus Solandri</i>, the white birch of the settlers, hitherto the prevailing tree, now begins to occur more in groves ... *(1879,Geology of the Provinces of Canterbury & Westland,124)*  
> **HAY** The Tawai-rauriki (<i>Fagus Solandri</i>) is the `White Birch' of the settlers. *(1882,Brighter Britain,2/197)*  
> **HECTOR** <i>Nothofagus solandri</i>, Hook. White-birch of Nelson and Otago (from colour of bark), Black-heart Birch of Wellington.--A lofty, beautiful evergreen tree 100 feet high; trunk 4-5 feet in diameter. *(1883,Handbook of New Zealand,125)* [NOTES: [is this mod solandri? note white bark]]  
> <i>[Notho]fagus solandri</i>, Hook. f. ... <i>Myrtilloides cinerascens</i>, Banks & Sol., MS.<i> Cliffortiordes oblongata,</i> Dryander. Entire-leaved Beech. Black Birch of Wellington, Canterbury, and in part of Otago and Southland. White Birch of Nelson, and in part Otago. Black-heart Birch of Otago. White, Black, Red, and Brown Birch of Oxford and Alford Forests. A fine tree, sometimes attaining the height of 100 feet, but usually 60-80 feet, with a trunk from 2 to 4 feet or more in diameter. *(1885,TrNZI,17/301)* [NOTES: (Kirk, NZ Beeches)]  
> **CHEESEMAN** <i>F. Solandri, Hook.f. .</i>.. A lofty forest-tree 40-60 ft. high; ... bark black ... in old trees ... pale and smooth on young ones; ... Leaves ... clothed with an appressed greyish-white pubescence beneath ... usually in hilly or mountain districts. ... <i>Tawhai; Tawhai-rauriki; Black-birch; White-birch</i>. ... Wood pale-red, often streaked with black, not durable ... *(1906,Manual NZ Flora,643)*  
>  *(1918,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,1/307)* [NOTES: (E. Phillips Turner) see cit. 1 b. above.]  
> <i>Fagus cliffortioides</i> Hook. f. .. Mountain beech or birch. <i>[Fagus] fusca</i> Hook. f. .. Tawhai, black-birch.<i> [Fagus] Menziesii</i> Hook. f. .. Silver or red birch. <i>[Fagus] Solandri</i> Hook. f. .. White-birch. *(1918,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,1/144)*  
> White Birch of Otago, Beech, etc. (<i>Fagus Solandri</i>). This has the greatest distribution of the three [Red, Black, White]. Wood white, soft, decays easily; ... *(1869,TrNZI,1/III/32)* [NOTES: (Buchanan Botany Otago)]

### white birch
  
noun. <i>N. cliffortioides</i> mountain beech.  
> **TUCKETT** ... the prevailing species of trees [in Otago] are remo [rimu], totara, and tall manuka, all indicative of inferior soil.... The bush-like woods on the east side are of white birch, likewise no recommendation of the soil, but the chief objection to the district is, that the prairie soil affords scarcely any food for cattle ... it grows only the wiry tree grass [tussock] ... and <i>Phormium tenax</i> [flax], no aniseed, perennial groundsel, or milk thistle, and very little couch grass. *(Aug 16, 1844,Diary)* [NOTES: [check the dates and page nos of these cits]1844 Aug 16 Tuckett Diary in 1898 Hocken Contributions 223-224]  
> **BARNICOAT** ... logs of black and white birch *(1845,Journal,188)* [NOTES: (Nelson)]  
> **HOOKER** Fagus <i>Cliffortioides,</i> Hook. fil. ... HAB. Mountains of the Northern and Middle Islands ... Very similar indeed to <i>F. Solandri,</i> and also called `White Birch', but a more alpine plant. The most alpine tree in New Zealand, covering the tops of the Nelson Mountains (alt. 6000-7000 feet) with a dense scrub ... according to Mr. Bidwill. *(1853,Flora NZ,I Flowering Plants 230)*  
> **HAAST** [?cliffortioides] Several species of Loranthus (misletoe) [sic]) also adorn the woods, growing principally upon the evergreen Fagus, commonly known to the settlers as black and white birch. *(1861,Report on Topographical Exploration of Nelson,146)*  
>  *(1872,Catalogue of the Colonial and Vienna Exhibition)* [NOTES: (And)]  
> ... almost up to the terminal moraine of the Tasman glacier, small groves of the obtuse leafed <i>Fagus Cliffortioides,</i> the White Birch of the settlers, is [sic] found in many localities ... *(1879,Geology of the Provinces of Canterbury & Westland,35)*  
> **KIRK**  *(1889,Forest Flora)* [NOTES: (And)]  
> <i>[Notho]fagus Cliffortioides,</i> Hook. f. ... Mountain beech. Black Birch of portions of Wellington, Canterbury, Otago and Southland. White Birch of Nelson, and in part of Otago and Southland. Barely distinguished by the woodman from Entire-leaved beech. The mountain beech bears a close resemblance to the entire-leaved beech in general appearance, although it never attains the extreme dimensions of that species. *(1885,TrNZI,17/304)* [NOTES: (Kirk, NZ Beeches)]  
> Beech, mountain (white birch) .. Fagus Cliffortioides <i>Ibid.</i> 16 [attrib] 23 Silver beech (white birch) slab. *(1890,PWD Catalogue of Timbers,9)*  
> **HARPER** There is also the rata tree, the prince of firewoods, ... miki-miki, kamahi, or so-called red-birch, white and black birches (which are really beeches), the mountain broom, ribbon wood, broad leaf, totara, and many others, ... and always available in the Ranges [sc. Alps]. *(1896,Pioneer Work in the Alps of NZ,35)*  
> **MATTHEWS** <i>Nothofagus cliffortioides</i> *(1905,Tree Culture in NZ)* [NOTES: (And)]  
> **LAING & BLACKWELL**  *(1940,Plants of NZ,140)*  
> **McLEOD** <i>Birch trees/Beech trees</i>: Bush or forest in the high country consists almost entirely of <i>Nothofagus cliffortioides</i> , the mountain beech. These were always known as birch by the early settlers, and high country men usually call them 'white birch'. *(1970,Glorious Morning,254)*

### white breasted tit
  
noun.  
[NOTES: the North Island tomtit, ngirungiru]  
> **WILLIAMS** <i>Maui-potiki</i> ... [P. toitoi] <i>North Island tit</i>. [so 1957/197 <i>white-breasted North Island tit</i>] *(1917,Dictionary of the Maori Language,229)*  
> <i>Tit Petroica macrocephala</i> (GMELIN, 1789) ... Auckland Island Tit P.m. marrineri</i> (MATHEWS & IREDALE, 1913). ... OTHER NAMES: <i>Ngiru-ngiru, piropiro, South Island tit, Maui-potiki, North Island tit or tomtit, white-breasted tit or tomtit, Snares tit, Snares Island robin. </i> *(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,284)*  
> **WILLIAMS** <i>Maui-potiki</i> ... [P. toitoi] <i>North Island tit. </i>[so 1957/197 <i>white-breasted North Island ti</i>t] *(1917,Dictionary of the Maori Language,229)*  
> <i>miromiro</i>, a bird (Petroeca toitoi), North Island tomtit; white-breasted tit it differs from the South Island form (P. macrocephala) yellow-breasted tit in the colouration of the breast. Whilst miromiro was the name of the North Island bird, both of them bore the name ngirungiru, so that has been adopted for the South Island form, though the South Island Maori would probably give the `ng' sound scurvy treatment and call the bird kirukiru, as he calls ngaio kaio. *(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/154)* [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]  
> **WILLIAMS** <i>Komiromiro = miromiro,</i> n<i>. Petroeca toitoi, white-breasted tit. </i> *(1917,Dictionary of the Maori Language,155)*

### white burn
  
noun. [Rural] a thorough burn of forest plants, debris and forest floor material, resulting in a white ash.  
> **LEVY** A white ash, called a white burn was the objective; a black burn resulted from a less intense fire where the heat was not sufficient to burn the timber, leaf mould, seeds and spores on the forest floor. *(1970,Grasslands of NZ,241)*  
> **WASWO** Reducing all the vegetation to ash was often referred to as a white burn. *(1996,Farming Progress in NZ 1814-1995,16)*  
> **WHITE & HODGSON** The aim was a 'white burn' in which all leaves, twigs, branches and forest floor leaf litter was reduced to ashes. *(1999,NZ Pasture and Crop Science,7)*

### white burn
  
noun.  
See: also white-ash burn  
> **LEVY** A steady, hot fire was desired in order to burn all fallen timber and leaf mould, seeds, and fern spores lying dormant on the forest floor. A white ash, called a <i>white burn</i>, was the objective; a black burn resulted from a less intense fire where the heat was not sufficient to burn the timber, leaf mould, seeds, and spores on the forest floor. *(1951,Grasslands of NZ,241,1970)*

### white climbing rata
  
noun.

> **COCKAYNE** (And) <i> Metrosideros hypericifolia </i> white climbing rata *(1907,AJHR)* [NOTES: Report on a Botanical Survey of Kapiti Island]  
> **COCKAYNE** (And) <i> Metrosideros albiflora </i> white climbing rata *(1908,AJHR,C-14)* [NOTES: Report on a Botanical Survey of Waipoua Kauri Forest]  
> **COCKAYNE** (And) <i>Metrosideros albiflora</i> white climbing-rata *(1919,NZ Plants & Their Story,2nd)*  
> (And) <i> Metrosideros scandens </i> white climbing-rata *(1916,NZ Association of Nurserymen, Trade Register)*  
> **WILAON** <i> Metrosideros [=ironwood] diffusa </i> WHITE CLIMBING RAATAA ... Akatea ... Leafy creeper becoming a high-climbing vine. ... Flowers white, often flushed pink. *(1982,Stewart Island Plants,44)*

### white clover country
  
noun. [Rural] land upon which white clover flourishes as a dominant pasture plant.  
> **LEVY** We would go so far as to say that it is essential on good white clover country that a good strain of white be sown. *(1970,Grasslands of NZ,152)*

### white Continent
  
noun. Antarctica  
> **HYDE** You get the feeling that Antarctica begins there ... [sic] the white Continent, the last of the clean places in the world. Starkie thought it sounded like a lot of Anzac Day dinner. He was getting sullen under the mixture of beer and Scotch. *(1938,Nor the Years Condemn,160)*  
> Lieut. Shackleton's programme expects one party to follow the coastline of the white continent, while another will break due south. *(Sep 21, 1907,Evening Post,16)*  
> On his return from Antarctica, Mr Frank Hurley, the official photographer and cinema expert of the Shackleton Expedition, intends to tour New Zealand with a complete kinematographic story of the crossing of the great white continent. *(Sep 25, 1914,Feilding Star,3)*  
> The most thrilling moments of the expedition will come when attempts are made to explore the great white continent from the air, and particularly when the flight to the Pole is undertaken. *(Mar 16, 1929,Evening Post,10)*

### white crane
  
noun. [obsolete] white heron, kotuku  
[NOTES: (the 1870 citation is a literary transf. into Eng. of a well-known Maori proverb (W.150; Neil Grove <i>Nga Peepeha a nga Tuupuna</i> (1984) no. 671): He kootuku rerenga tahi i te tau, used of a distinguished visitor who comes as rarely a the white heron),]  
> There is also a very fine specimen of the white crane, or kotuku, a bird that is now so seldom seen that "rare as a kotuku" has passed into a proverb with the Maoris in the North. *(Jan 16, 1890,Otago Witness,18)*  
> After some manoeuvring, I shot a white crane in one of the lagoons about Timaru ... *(Mar 16, 1849,Torlesse Papers,68,1958)*  
> **SHORTLAND** If the feathers are those of the bird called <i>huia</i>, it is a sign the child will be a girl; if those of the <i>kotuku</i> (a white crane), the dream prognosticates a male child. *(1856,Traditions & Superstitions,143)*  
> **THOMSON** Three of the <i>Heron</i> family live on the land. The Matuku, or bittern, has the cry of a bull. The Kotuku, or white crane, the noblest bird of this family, is abundant in the Middle Island, but is rarely seen north of Wanganui in the North island. *(1859,Story of New Zealand, The,1/27)*  
> **HAAST** Amongst them we may assign the first place to the white crane (Herodias flavirostris). *(1861,Report on Topographical Exploration of Nelson,136)*  
> **BUTLER** I must not omit to mention the white crane, a very beautiful bird, with immense wings, of purest white; and the swamp hen, with a tail which it is constantly bobbing up and down like the wood hen; ... *(1863,First Year in Canterbury Settlement,ix/136)*  
> **BARRINGTON** Shot a white crane, four feet high ... [ed fn] White heron <i>(Egretta alba). </i> *(Mar 23, 1864,Diary)* [NOTES: 1864 Mar 23 Barrington Diary in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 403]  
> The noble white crane and blue heron are occasionally found ... *(1868,Province of Otago in NZ, The,31)*  
> Formerly the White Crane(<i>Herodias flavirostris</i>), the Bittern (<i>Botaurus poicilopterus</i>) ... were found on both [Chatham] islands. *(1869,TrNZI,1/178)* [NOTES: (Travers)]  
> The stately White Crane (<i>Ardea flavirostris</i>) takes a prominent place in this section. *(1869,TrNZI,1/iii/16)* [NOTES: (Buller Ornithology)]  
> Those less common birds, the great White Crane, the Avocet, and Spoonbill Duck were seen at rarer intervals. *(1870,TrNZI,2/48)* [NOTES: (Potts Birds)]  
> A gentleman who can propose to pay off Britain's national debt ... is as rare a stranger as the `white crane of the Maoris'. *(Dec 16, 1870,Evening Post,2)*  
> **WILLIAMS** <i>Kootuku</i>, n. <i>white crane</i>; a bird. *(1871,Dictionary of the NZ Language,65)*  
> **BULLER** ARDEA SYRMATOPHORA {EGRETTA}. (WHITE HERON.) ... <i>Native name</i>, - Kotuku; `White Crane' of the Colonists. [227] The White Heron occurs so sparingly in most parts of New Zealand, that `rare as the Kotuku' has passed into a proverb among the Maoris; . *(1873,Birds of New Zealand,226,1888 2nd edn 2/124)*  
> **HAAST** Thousands ... of water-fowl were here congregated, amongst which the spotless plumage of the White crane was very conspicuous ... *(1879,Geology of the Provinces of Canterbury & Westland,19)*  
> **DOMETT** There, as the shy <i>white crane</i>, so rarely seen,/ Stands ... <i>Ibid.</i> 1/306 White Crane (<i>Kotuku</i>) .... Ardeidae; Herodias flavirostris (?) *(1883,Ranolf and Amohia,2/279)*  
> 69. <i>Ardea alba</i>, L. White heron, White crane, Kotuku. *(1885,NZ Journal of Science,556)* [NOTES: (T.H. Potts)]  
> **WILSON** We had come off the usual road, as B. wished to show us ... the country round Lake Brunner (`Kotuku waka oka' - home of the white crane). *(1894,In the Land of the Tui,92)* [NOTES: 1890 Mar 4 in 1894 Wilson Land of the Tui 92]  
> **WILLIAMS** <i>Kotuku, ... white crane. </i> *(1892,Dictionary of the Maori Language,76)*  
> **PASCOE** The White Crane [The White Heron, <i>Egretta alba</i>] *(1957,Mr Explorer Douglas)* [NOTES: c1899 Douglas Birds of South Westland in 1957 Pascoe Mr Explorer Douglas]  
> **TREGEAR** If the feathers seen in the dream were those of the white crane (<i>kotuku</i>) the child would be a boy; ... <i>Ibid.</i> 180 The white crane or heron (<i>kotuku</i>: Ardea egretta) was kept for the sake of its feathers, which were plucked every five or six months. *(1904,Maori Race, The,40)*  
> **WILLIAMS** <i>Kotuku, ... white heron.</i> *(1917,Dictionary of the Maori Language,175)*  
> **NEAVE** Just as we were passing ... lake [Ohau in 1858], we saw what I never saw again ... - it was a beautiful white crane. [1980 ed note: What was seen would be a white heron from the West Coast. A crane is a light greyish colour.] *(1980,Land of Munros, The,65)* [NOTES: 1917 James Strachan Experiences (Hocken L.) in 1980 Neave The Land of Munros 65]  
> The rare white heron - why will some people persist in describing it as a `crane' - seems to have been given a new lease of life in New Zealand. *(Dec 1, 1919,Quick March,33)*  
> **WILY & MAUNSELL** ... but that is as rare a sight as the white crane, for it requires absolutely perfect atmospheric conditions. *(1938,Robert Maunsell,47)*  
> **BEATTIE** I never actually saw the bittern but I heard its whoop ..., and I saw a white crane at taieri Lake. *(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,121)*  
> **BRERETON** In the earliest days [in Ngatimoti, Nelson, c1860s-1870s] a few white cranes were seen and some were shot, ... *(1947,No Roll of Drums,18)*  
> **VON HAAST** In bird-life Haast recorded as interesting features, an unsuccessful attack by three sparrow-hawks upon a white crane fishing; ... *(1948,Julius Von Haast,106)*  
> **GILLESPIE** White herons have been reported from various parts of South Canterbury for many years and were often referred to as `white cranes'. *(1958,South Canterbury,7,1971)*

### White Ferns
  
noun. the NZ international women's cricket team  
> Ten points for originality to whoever had the bright idea of naming our women's cricket team the White Ferns. It can't have been too hard. The netball teams has dibs on the Silver Ferns and the rugby team has claimed the Black Ferns. Guess that only left one option, really. It's certainly not the most menacing of names. In keeping with the Black Caps, perhaps the side should have been named the Black Cats. At least it would conjure up images of claws and fangs, and not pretty native shrubs. [Rachel Smalley] *(Aug 24, 2000,Evening Post,29)*

(c) Other common names: Silver trumpeter, Silver strip (smoked), Elephants. (d) Principal localities where taken: [Wellington and South Island]. ... This fish rarely appears in commerce in other than fillet form and trade name of White Fillets is applied. *(1970,Nomenclature of NZ Fish,22)*

### white flight
  
noun.  
> A clear frontrunner for each and any marketing, public relations or packaging award in the `best socio-intellectual catch-phrase of `86' category: `White flight.' Think about it, talk it out, see and hear how it rolls off your tongue. Doesn't matter what it means or involves or entails - the two-word phrase is the attention-grabbing winner of the year. With such curt alliteration, it doesn't matter that what's in contention is actually a complex educational, socio-economic and racial issue. It has miraculously been reduced to the limits of the number of letters an <i>Evening Post</i> headline writer can squeeze into a front-page banner, which number also equates to the newspaper's collective IQ. So what the hell is `white flight' anyway? According to race relations conciliator Wally Hirsch, it's that startling new phenomenon of honky parents taking their kids away from schools whose rolls have significant numbers of different-tinted folk. -->1986 May 23 Dominion 14 Sir, - Much has been said about the `white flight' from Maori schools. I assure you this is not new. When we lived near Rotorua in the 1960s, people of Pakeha descent would pay to send their children miles to school, rather than send them to schools with a predominantly Maori roll. *(May 23, 1986,National Business Review,19)* [NOTES: (Warren Mayne)]  
> In a <i>Sunday Star</i> special report, Mrs Russell and Mr Shallard talk about `white flight' - pakeha parents avoiding schools with high Maori and Pacific Island rolls or removing their children from such schools in favour of ones with predominantly Pakeha rolls. *(Jun 15, 1986,Sunday Star Times,A1)*  
> The Race Relations Conciliator ... has aroused much controversy since he coined the phrase `white flight' to describe parents of European - as opposed to the offensively racist and discriminatory term Pakeha - ancestry moving their children to schools where due emphasis is placed on values the majority of New Zealanders still adhere to. *(Oct 4, 1986,Listener (New Zealand Listener),8)*  
> The parents caught up in the `white flight' phenomenon have been taken unawares by the quiet revolution among a significant group of Pakeha who have steadily embraced the concept of biculturalism. *(Sep 1986,Metro,68)*

### white flowered rata
  
noun.  
[NOTES: aka white flowering rata, white rata, white climbing rata, et al]  
> **FEATON** METROSIDEROS ALBIFLORA. (<i>Banks & Sol.</i>) ... [160] The `WHITE-FLOWERING RATA'. This handsome climber is indigenous to ... the Bay of Islands and the East Coast, but is not abundant. *(1889,Art Album NZ Flora,159)*  
> **LAING & BLACKWELL** (And) <i>Metrosideros albiflora</i> white-flowered rata *(1906,Plants of NZ)*  
> **BROOKER** <i>Metrosideros albiflora</i> Sol. ex Gaertn.  Common name: <i>White-flowered rata Maori</i> name: Akatea *(1981,NZ Medicinal Plants,73)*

### white grub
  
noun.  
See: grass grub  
> There are twenty-eight native species belonging to the genus <i>Odontria,</i> but all observers agree that the principal grass grub is <i>Odontria zealandica.</i> In the larval stage this is known as `grass grub,' `white grub,' or `curl grub,' and the adult stage is known as `grass grub beetle,' `brown beetle.' or `turnip fly.' *(1943,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,23/306A)*  
> All the attention of agriculturists is now turned to the destruction of the white grub or worm that is neither more nor less than the larva of the cockchafer, that great favourite of schoolboys, but in reality one of the most mischievous insects of the temperate zone, and capable of committing as much havoc as the locust in warm climates. *(Feb 28, 1867,Bruce Herald,7)*  
> .. I wonder if any of the professors could give us some practical information as to the habits of the notorious "white grub" which infests and destroys grass pastures, lawns, strawberry plantations, &c., so that it could be destroyed. *(May 15, 1882,Otago Daily Times,4)*  
> The cure also acts admirably against the white grubs that destroy the roots of grass on lawns, and has been very successfully tried upon some valuable lawns. *(Jan 7, 1892,Otago Witness,5)*  
> Probably the warm days account for the beetle of the "white grub" (Odontua striata, is it not?) being found on the wing in numbers. *(Aug 4, 1909,Otago Witness,85)*  
> The grass-grubs are the larvae of the cockchafer beeter, a Cawthron Institute bulletin points out, and are sometimes known as white grubs;  these larvae are very easily recognised by the characteristic manner in which they lie doubled up in the ground. *(Dec 18, 1936,Evening Post,5)*

### white Gurkha
  
noun. a New Zealand soldier at Gallipoli  
> The Tommies have christened my men [on Gallipoli] the `White Gurkhas'. We are very proud of the soubriquet and mean to live up to it. *(May 12, 1915,Great Adventure,47,1988)* [NOTES: (Malone)]  
> **SHADBOLT** It was somewhere around then [May 1915 after the Daisy Patch] that New Zealanders got the name of `the white Gurkhas'. *(1988,Voices of Gallipoli,38)*

### white hen
  
noun.  
> **TAYLOR** Caught a bird of the Maori hen species, with a bill six inches long, black and white feathers, ; we called them white hens or long-bills. {Note. Not precisely identifiable, but may be the Pied Oystercatcher <i>(Haematopus finschi).</i> *(1959,Early Travellers,404)* [NOTES: 1864 Mar 29 Barrington Diary in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 404]

### white heron
  
noun. [Environment]  
See: kotuku.  
[NOTES: See also crane]  
> White herons begin nesting in September and are preened and ready for gawpers by November. *(Aug 30, 1999,Evening Post,12)*  
> **HULME & MORRISON** The white heron, kotuku, is not a rare bird on a world-wide scale. *(1989,Homeplaces,17)*  
> The Hat was lying in the bath, and a white heron landed right beside him. *(Jan 6, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),14)*  
> Lieut [sic] Pickersgill ... shot a White Hern which answers exactly with Mr Pennants discription of the White Herns that either now are or were formerly in England. [ed. fn] kotuku <i> Egretta alba modesta </i> (Gray) The European form is <i> E. alba alba </i> Linn. *(Apr 25, 1773,Cook's Journals,2/126,1961)* [NOTES: The </i> hern</i> of Cook is a dialectal variant of heron: cf Tennyson's `The Brook': I come from haunts of coot and hern]  
> **FORSTER** We found likewise a white heron but amazingly shy.  <i>Ibid.</i> 266 The Capt & Gentlemen returned late from a shooting party & brought a large white Heron  (<i> Ardea alba </i>), a white & brown Gull [267] (<i> Larus naevius </i>). *(Apr 6, 1773,Resolution Journal of J.R. Forster, The,2/247)*  
> **FORSTER** The rest of this day [24 April 1773] was spent in shooting, and among the different birds killed was a white heron (<i> ardea alba </i>), common to Europe. *(1777,Voyage Round the World,1/177)*  
> **NICHOLAS** There was shot in this bay [Dusky, by Cook's party] a white heron, that answered exactly to the description given by Pennant, in his British Zoology, of a bird formerly found in England. *(1817,New Zealand (various authors/titles),2/256)*  
> **POLACK** <i> Cormorants </i>, and a <i> white heron </i>, are found; said to have formerly existed in England, according to Pennart. *(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/307)*  
> **TAYLOR** Shot a fine <i> koatuku </i>, or white heron. *(1959,Early Travellers,219)* [NOTES: 1846 Heaphy Expedition to Kawatiri in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 219]  
> **HOWARD** A stately white heron shot by the boy Wright. *(1940,Rakiura,391)* [NOTES: 1850 May Acheron Journal in 1940 Howard Rakiura 391]  
> **HUTTON** 70. ARDEA ALBA. L.White Heron. Kotuku. <i> A. flavirostris.</i> WAGL. *(1871,Birds of New Zealand,27)*  
> **BULLER** ARDEA SYRMATOPHORA {EGRETTA}. (WHITE HERON.) ...  <i> Native name </i>, - Kotuku; `White Crane' of the Colonists. [227] The White Heron occurs so sparingly in most parts of New Zealand, that `rare as the Kotuku' has passed into a proverb among the Maoris. *(1873,Birds of New Zealand,226,1888 2nd edn 2/124)*  
> **POTTS** ... the white heron (often called the white crane) the kotuku of the Maoris, enjoys so extensive a range... *(1882,Out in the Open,2)*  
> <i> Ardea alba </i>, L. White heron, White crane, Kotuku. *(Nov 1885,NZ Journal of Science,69)* [NOTES: (T.H. Potts)]  
> **TREGEAR** The white crane or heron (<i> kotuku </i>: Ardea egretta) was kept for the sake of its feathers, which were plucked every five or six months. *(1904,Maori Race, The,180)*  
> **COWAN** The naturalist will find much interest in the [Okarito] lagoon, for in its reedy recesses there is a colony of that extremely rare and beautiful bird the <i> kotuku </i> or white heron. *(1926,Travel in NZ,(Vol II South Island) 90)*  
> **POWELL** WHITE HERON (<i> Casmerodius albus), Kotuku of the Maoris, is a stately white bird larger than the reef heron. </i> *(1947,Native Animals of NZ,83)*  
> **McLINTOCK** White heron .. .. kotuku .. .. <i> Egretta alba </i> *(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-12)*  
> **WILLIAMS** <i> Kootuku, n. 1. Egretta alba modesta, white heron. </i> *(1975,Dictionary of the Maori Language,150)*  
> 92. <i> Egretta alba modesta </i> (Gray, 1831) <i> White  Heron (Kotuku)</i> Australia to India, widespread ... *(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,33,1980)*  
> <i> White Heron Egretta alba modesta </i> (GRAY, 1831) ... OTHER NAMES: <i> Eastern great  white egret, great white heron, white egret,  large egret, kotuku. </i> *(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,131)*  
> A lovely white heron appeared, flying slowly close overhead. *(Jul 22, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,17)*  
> & sometimes there's the white heron / knocking about *(Jun 14, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),64)*

### white joker
  
noun. = white man (sense 2)  
> Men are in two classes, the `white jokers' and the `bastards'. *(Sep 1852,Landfall,23/220)* [NOTES: 1952 Sep Landfall 23 (Pearson `Fretful Sleepers') 220]

### white kowhai
  
noun.  
> The white kowhai, or kaka beak, has attracted the attention of an American visitor. *(Jan 22, 1924,Otago Witness,6)*

### white lady
  
noun. meths as a drink  
[NOTES: cf. white light]  
> Abbreviated local rhyming slang is also used. `I'd love an aristotle of alison' means `I'd love a bottle of bourbon' - bourbon rhyming with Alison Durbin. [a NZ singer of ?1960s] Meths is blue or white lady, and steam is a mixture of meths and sherry. *(Feb 14, 1988,Dominion Sunday Times,19)*

### white lake
  
noun.  
> The gas escaping through the water of the [Ohaewai] pools ... is partially oxidized, liberating sulphur, which imparts a milky-white colour to the pools, locally known as `white lakes'. *(1906,TrNZI,38/29)*

### white light
  
noun. meths as a drink  
> **HYDE** [Maoris] drink a lot of metha, don't they. Great names they've got for it - Phar Lap, and Johnny Gee, and White Light. There's one old Maori down here who went blind, drinking the metha. *(1938,Nor the Years Condemn,190)*

### white lightning
  
noun. hokonui  
> **WILSON** ... I imagine he provided them with little money, unless he paid the grocery bill when he'd made a horse trade or sold some white lightning. *(1984,South Pacific Street,9)*

### white maire
  
noun. <i>Nestegis lanceolata</i>  
[NOTES: [1919 July 29] Ver.(WB). = Vernacular names used by settlers supplied by W.Best, Otaki (And) <i>Olea lanceolata </i>]  
> **HAY** The Maire (<i>Santalum Cunninghamii</i>) is not a large tree, but the wood is extremely hard, heavy, and finely grained. ... Bushmen call it `Black Maire', to distinguish it from the following:- The Maire-Tawhake (<i>Eugenia Maire</i>), or `White Maire'. The Maire-aunui (<i>Olea Cunninghamii</i>), which ... is a much bigger tree than the maire, but does not yield such valuable timber. *(1882,Brighter Britain,2/193)*  
> [Native name] Maire .. [Settlers' name] White-maire .. [Family] Jasmineae *(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,121)* [NOTES: 1886 T.Kirk in 1902 Settler's Handbook of NZ 121]  
> **KIRK** <i>Olea lanceolata </i> *(1889,Forest Flora)* [NOTES: (And)]  
> Maire, white .. Olea lanceolata .. Timber very even in grain, hard, and shrinks little; used for wood engraving, machine-bearings, fencing-posts, rails, &c. *(1890,PWD Catalogue of Timbers,11)*  
> [Native name] Maire .. [Settlers' name] White-maire .. [Family] Jasmineae *(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,121)* [NOTES: 1886 T.Kirk in 1902 Settler's Handbook of NZ 121]  
> **CHEESEMAN** <i>O[lea] lanceolata, Hook.f. </i>... North Cape to Cook Strait ... Nelson ... <i>Maire; White-maire.</i> ... Very close to [<i>O. Cunninghamii</i>, Black-maire], from which it differs in the smaller size ... The wood is very similar ... *(1906,Manual NZ Flora,438)*  
> **MATTHEWS** <i>Olea lanceolata </i> white-maire *(1905,Tree Culture in NZ)* [NOTES: (And)]  
> **CHEESEMAN** <i>O[lea] lanceolata, Hook.f. </i>... North Cape to Cook Strait ... Nelson ... <i>Maire; White-maire</i>. ... Very close to [<i>O. Cunninghamii</i>, Black-maire], from which it differs in the smaller size ... The wood is very similar ... *(1906,Manual NZ Flora,438)*  
> **LAING & BLACKWELL** <i>Olea lanceolata (The White Maire)</i>. Smaller in all its parts than <i>O. Cunninghamii</i>. Bark white. *(1907,Plants of NZ,334,1940 p. 355)*  
> <i>Olea lanceolata </i> *(1908,AJHR,C-14)* [NOTES: 1908 Cockayne Report on a Botanical Survey of Waipoua Kauri Forest(And)]  
> <i>Olea lanceolata </i> *(1909,AJHR,C-11)* [NOTES: Turner Botanical Examination of the Higher Waimarino District]  
> The white maire (? <i>Olea lanceolata</i>) is here [among Tuhoe] known as <i>maire-rau-nui</i> and <i>maire-roro. </i> *(1910,TrNZI,42/477)*  
> <i>Olea lanceolata </i> *(1917,Duncan & Davies' Catalogue)* [NOTES: (And)]  
> **COCKAYNE** <i>Olea lanceolata </i> white maire *(1924,Cultivation of NZ Plants, The)* [NOTES: (And)]  
> **LAING & BLACKWELL** <i>O.lanceolata </i>(The White Maire). ... Bark white. ... Timber similar to that of the Maire. *(1940,Plants of NZ,355)*  
> **MARTIN** 2. White maire (<i>Olea lanceolata </i>) bark whitish; leaves narrow, opposite, ... (<i>N. lanceolata</i>). *(1961,Flora NZ,164)*  
> Oleaceae <i>Nestegis lanceolata</i> white maire *(1975,Tane,21/8)* [NOTES: (Wright Vegetation Mt Maungatawhiri)]  
> **MOORE & IRWIN** `The four New Zealand species were long placed with the true olives in <i>Olea</i>; more recently they fell temporarily into <i>Gymnelaea </i>but now are all attributed to <i>Nestegis, ... NESTEGIS CUNNINGHAMII, black maire</i>. A tall, stout-trunked tree of North Island mixed forests, recorded also from Nelson and marlborough but now common in only a few districts. The young sapling has longer and narrower leaves as has its nearest relative, the white maire (<i>N. lanceolata</i>). *(1978,Oxford Book of NZ Plants,118)*  
> **DAWSON** Three of the four species of <i>Nestegis</i> or `native olives' have narrow adult leaves. In the case of two species, black maire (<i>N. cunninghamii</i>) and white maire (<i>N. lanceolata</i>), the juveniles are much narrower than the adults ... *(1988,Forest Vines to Snow Tussocks,42)*  
> White maire slab. *(1890,PWD Catalogue of Timbers,163)*

### white man
  
noun. a non-Maori of European descent; often used to translate Maori Pakeha cf. white n.  
[NOTES: incl whiteman, (whitemen)]  
> white man - ton|g|ata bulla (Bay of Islands - tongata pa'hkihow) [tangata pora (tangata pakeha)] *(1835,Boultbee Journal,109,1986)* [NOTES: >c1826-1827 list of Maori words in c1835 Boultbee Journal (1986) 109]  
> **EARLE** The white taboo'd day [sc. Sunday], when the packeahs (or white men) put on clean clothes and leave off work. *(1832,Narrative of a Nine Months Residence in NZ,146)*  
> **HENRY WISHART** He then demanded a white man to be left at Waiderippa until the vessels return, as a hostage for the safe performance of my word. *(Apr 2, 1836,Journal)* [NOTES: 1836 Apr 2 Capt Henry Wishart in 1913 McNab Old Whaling Days 144]  
> **SARAH MATHEW** As it came alongside we perceived that it contained several white men and a number of natives. [cf contrrast with native] *(Apr 29, 1840,Journal)* [NOTES: 1840 Apr 29 Sarah Mathew Journal in 1940 Founding of NZ 132]  
> **HOCKEN** We were informed that about seventy or eighty white men live on Stewart's Island, and about as many natives. *(1898,Contributions to the Early History of New Zealand,254)* [NOTES: 1844 Monro Notes of a Journey (Nelson<i> Examiner </i>[used fairly often in Monro's account of Southland] July 20 et seq.) in 1898 Hocken Contributions 254]  
> **TUCKETT** There were only one native man (deformed in his feet) here - a number of women, all kept by or keeping <i>white</i> men as they <i>are called. </i> *(May 10, 1844,Diary)* [NOTES: 1844 May 10 Tuckett Diary in 1898 Hocken Contributions 219]  
> **ROCHFORT** On going down to the beach, some Maories* came ashore in a canoe. They were about building a market-place to trade with the white man. <i>Ibid.</i> 22 The house was what is called a toi-toi whare; the toi-toi is a reed which the natives use for the walls and roofs of their huts; these reeds are plaited together with flax; ... The New Zealanders will build a house in this manner for a white man for five pounds. [fn] Whare is the native name for house. *(1853,Adventures of a Surveyor,14)*  
> **HOCKEN** They [the South Island Maoris] consented to the terms, and on the 31st of July the deed was read to them - ... that they must respect the white man's land, and that the white man would not touch the land reserved for them. *(1898,Contributions to the Early History of New Zealand,57)*  
> **BEASLEY** The chief `tapued' (or to use the white man's pronunciation of the word `tabooed') ... *(1936,Pioneering Days,27)*  
> **HOWARD** Mutton-birding is the only modern survival of native enterprise, the only phase of primitive economic development which has withstood the destructive competition ... introduced by the white man. *(1940,Rakiura,210)*  
> LATE intelligence from the South reports that the Natives are bent upon refusing either mail, ministera, or whitemen to pass, in spite of Wi Kingi's orders to the contrary. *(Mar 1, 1862,Taranaki Herald,2)*

### white man
  
noun. a good fellow, a dinkum joker, a thoroughly acceptable fellow; a person who always comes through with the goods cf white aj, white joker.  
> **BARLOW** With a couple of good dogs and a `white man' (as a good fellow is called out here) for a companion, what more enjoyable than a day after the long tails [sc Chinese pheasants]. *(1888,Kaipara and Waitemata Echo,192)*  
> **McKEE WRIGHT** He was a whiteman through and through. *(1897,Station Ballads,44)*  
> **BEATTIE** My great friend ... was `a white man'. His way back hotel was a home ... *(1913,Trade Hunting,[16])*  
> **PFAFF** On our way down we met a man in rags ... He went back with us ... He said we were `white men' for feeding him so well. *(1914,Diggers' Story, The,66)*  
> **THORNTON** He was - to use a colonialism - `a white man'. *(1916,Wowser, The,45)*  
> `White man - First to Last' is evidently a suitable epitaph for the late Corporal A.C.Sumner *(Apr 11, 1921,Quick March,23)*

### white man's grass
  
noun.  
See: grass, white man's grass

### white man's PT
  
noun.  
[NOTES: same meaning as Maori PT?]  
> Back to the only position they know, / To what Maoris call - white man's PT. *(Jun 28, 1943,NZEF Times,III/105 : 5)*

### white manuka
  
noun. <i>Leptospermum ericoides</i>, kanuka, white tea-tree  
[NOTES: ca1935 Marlborough White manuka or tree manuka used to distinguish the species from the more common (red) manuka]  
> <i>Leptospermum ericoides</i> white manuka *(1877,TrNZI,9)* [NOTES: (And)]  
> [in Plant, Tree file] White manuka L ericoides *(1887,Handbook NZ Mines,28)* [NOTES: 1883 Hector in 1887 Hbk NZ Mines 28]  
>  *(1887,TrNZI)* [NOTES: (And.)]  
> **SUTHERLAND** The call of a Californian quail passing on the alert to his scattered tribe came from a shelter-clump of white manuka a hundred yards down the hillside. *(1953,Gold of Noon, The,41,1963)*  
> **WILLIAMS** <i>Kanuka</i>, n. <i>Leptospermum ericoides, white manuka. </i> *(1957,Dictionary of the Maori Language,94)*  
> The insect is found associated with both red manuka (<i>Leptospermum scoparium</i>) and white manuka or kanuka (<i>Leptospermum ericoides</i>). *(Oct 1959,Tararua Tramper,7)*  
> **BRAMBLEY** ... a half-dozen magpies whirled up the slope to settle in a big white manuka ... *(1966,Sea-cockies of the Manukau,27)*  
> <i>white manuka [=] kanuka</i> [s] *(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,85)*  
> **BROOKER et al.** <i>Leptospermum scoparium</i> J.R.& G.Forst. Common names: <i>Tea tree, red manuka</i> Maori names: <i>Manuka, kahikatoa Leptospermum ericoides</i> A.Rich. Common names: <i>Tree manuka, white manuka</i> Maori name: <i>Kanuka </i> 3. (occas.) L. ericoides, =kanuka, =tree manuka *(1981,NZ Medicinal Plants,71)*  
> Whilst one need not take exception to the pakeha speaking of those two forms as "red tea-tree" and white tea-tree," it is quite a different matter when he speaks of "red manuka" and "white manuka."   There is not, properly speaking, such a tree as white manuka. *(Sep 3, 1931,Evening Post,12)*

### white Maori
  
noun. scheelite or lime tungstate  
> [Scheelite] generally occurs as a coarse white heavy sand, difficult to pan off when washing gold, and is called `White Maori' by the miners. *(1868,Province of Otago in NZ, The,39)*  
> **BAKER** The following New Zealand terms, recorded in the closing twenty years of the last century, are also worthy of comment: ... <i>white Maori,</i> tungstate of lime (New Zealand miners' use);... *(1941,NZ Slang,46)*  
> **PARTRIDGE** <i>White Maori.</i> Tungstate of lime: New Zealand miners': since ca. 1875. B., 1941. Ex appearance. *(1949,Dictionary of Slang Addenda,1222)*  
> **McGILL** <i>white Maori</i> tungstate of lime, among miners, c.1875. *(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,122)*  
> **A CITIZEN** `Tungstate of lime occurs plentifully in the Wakatipu district, where from its weight and colour it is called <i>White Maori</i> by the miners.' *(1883,Illustrated Guide to Dunedin,169)*  
> **MORRIS** <i>White Maori, n. </i>New Zealand miners' name for a stone. See quotation. *(1898,Austral English,286)*  
> **McKAY (1897)** In the face of this can be seen a stratumof the cement-stone, "Chinaman" or "whiteMaori," as boulders of it are calledindifferently by the miners in differentparts. *(1894,Report on the Older Auriferous Drifts of C.Otago,56)*

### white Maori
  
noun. a Pakeha Maori, a European who lives as a Maori, or with Maoris (I have also heard the term used patronisingly or disparagingly of a Maori who, in the judgement of the Pakeha speaker, apes the European in attitudes or habits)  
[NOTES: ca1930 Marlborough W.H.B. Orsman)]  
> **McGILL** <i>white Maori </i>... Maori who has adopted Pakeha name and/or ways, as quoted in New Zealand Oral History Unit study of Martinborough. *(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,122)*

### white mapau
  
noun. <i>P. eugenioides</i>, lemonwood, tarata  
See: also maple  
> <i>Pittosporum eugenoides</i> white mapau *(1872,Catalogue of the Colonial and Vienna Exhibition)* [NOTES: (And)]  
> **KIRK** <i>Pittosporum eugenoides</i> white mapau *(1889,Forest Flora)* [NOTES: (And)]  
> **THOMSON** <i>Pittosporum eugenoides</i> white mapau *(1909,NZ Naturalist's Calendar)* [NOTES: (And)]  
> **LAING & BLACKWELL** [<i>Pittosporum eugenoides</i>] it is often cultivated for its beauty, and is sometimes - though not so often as <i>P.</i> [195] <i>tenuifolium</i> - used to form an ornamental hedge. It is known by a variety of names amongst the colonists, such as <i>Mapau, White Mapau</i>, and even <i>Maple</i>, and <i>Lemon-tree</i>. The Maori name seems to have been <i>Tarata</i>, but it is also called the <i>Mapau</i>. (ii) (? Otago obsolete) <i>Carpodetus serratus</i>, WHITE MAPAU, =PUTAPUTAWETA (So Williams Dict) *(1907,Plants of NZ,194,1940 p. 203)*  
> White Mapau, or Piripiri-whata (Carpodetus serratus), an ornamental shrub-tree with mottled-green leaves, and large cymose panicles of white flowers. *(1868,TrNZI,37)* [NOTES: (Buchanan, Botany Otago.)]  
> MAPAU FAMILY. White Mapau; Tarata (<i>Pittosporum eugenioides</i>). <i>Ibid.</i> 37 White Mapau, or Piripiriwhata (<i>Carpodetus serratus</i>). An ornamental shrub-tree, with mottled-green leaves, and large cymose panicles of white flowers. *(1869,TrNZI,1/III/33)* [NOTES: (Buchanan Botany Otago)]  
> <i>Carpodetus serratus</i> white mapau *(1875,Durability NZ Timbers)* [NOTES: (And)]  
> <i>Carpodetus serratus</i> white mapau *(1877,TrNZI,9/)*  
> **HECTOR** <i>Carpodetus serratus</i>, Forst. Tawiri, White Mapau, White-birch (of Auckland). --A small tree, 10-30 feet high, trunk unusually slender. *(1883,Handbook of New Zealand,132)*  
> <i>Carpodetus serratus</i> white mapau *(1889,Forest Flora)* [NOTES: (And)]  
> ... in the neighbourhood of Dunedin: - ... White mapau - <i>Pittosporum eugenioides</i>; Black Mapau - <i>Pittosporum tenuifolium</i>; ... *(1891,NZ Journal of Science,1/71)*  
> Tawiri-kohu-kohu, or white-mapau (<i>Carpodetus serratus</i) *(1892,NZ Official Handbook,150)*

### white maple
  
noun.  
[NOTES: [a1927] Ver.(A). = Vernacular names supplied by B.C. Aston (And) Pittosporum eugenioides white maple; [1919 July 29] Ver.(WB). = Vernacular names used by settlers supplied by W.Best, Otaki (And) Carpodetus serratus white maple]  
> **KIRK** <i>Carpodetus serratus</i> white maple *(1889,Forest Flora)* [NOTES: (And)]

### white marigold
  
noun. <i>Senecio Lyallii</i>  
> <i>Senecio Lyallii</i> white marigold *(1885,NZ Country Journal)* [NOTES: (And)]

### white matipo
  
noun. tarata  
> [Native name] Kowhiwhi .. [Settlers' name] White-matipo .. [Family] Pittosporeae *(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,121)* [NOTES: 1886 T.Kirk in 1902 Settler's Handbook of NZ 121]  
> **STUDHOLME** In this bush were ... Ohau, (panax or five-finger), ini-ini (whitey wood); konini, white and red matipo; koromiko and other shrubs. *(1940,Te Waimate,246,1954)*

### white matipo
  
noun.  
> <i>Carpodetus serratus </i> *(1872,Catalogue of the Colonial and Vienna Exhibition)* [NOTES: (And)]

### white mistletoe
  
noun. <i>Tupeia antarctica</i>  
> White mistletoe, or <i>Tupeia antarctica</i>  has strongholds around Rotorua, Kaikoura, Banks Peninsula and the Otago Peninsula. *(Sep 2002,NZ Geographic,No. 59/90)*

### white moth
  
noun. an earlier common name (f. (white) cabbage moth) for what is now universally known a <i>white butterfly </i>  
> **G W TURNER** <i>White moth</i> was usual, but called <i>white butterfly</i> in 1932. *(1933,Diary)* [NOTES: Dannevirke]

### white mud
  
noun. snow  
> I get ticked off for romanticising by farm-raised friends who can't look at paddocks without seeing thistles and who call snow "white mud". *(Sep 2003,North & South,10)*

### White New Zealand policy
  
noun.  
[NOTES: incl jocular use in soap advert]  
> **GRANT** A door closing on an Indian has a notice`WHITE NEW ZEALAND POLICY'. *(1980,Unauthorised Version,128)* [NOTES: 1917 Glover cartoon in Truth `The Hindoo Peril' in 1980 Grant Unauthorised Version 128]  
> In the 1920s New Zealanders' fears that the superior white races would be swamped by inferior coloured races led to the White New Zealand policy and anti-Chinese feeling. *(Apr 1987,NZ Journal of History,142)* [NOTES: (Boyd)]  
> New Zealanders might almost as reasonably contend for the adoption of a White New Zealand policy, in regard to the islands now within the jurisdiction of the colony, as Australians for a White Australia. *(Jan 14, 1904,Ashburton Guardian,2)*  
> Between the arguments for and against the White Australia (and White New Zealand) policy, which Dr. Thwing summarises in astonishingly new and commendably simple words, considering the magnitude of the question, the writer asks:-  What method can be adopted which shall worthily promote the highest aims of those who demand that these islands shall be maintained as white populations, and also shall secure the purposes of those who believe that the world interests require a less exclusive policy? *(Sep 22, 1923,Evening Post,19)*  
> It is pleasing to note that in those days we were producing the soap needed to maintain a "White New Zealand" policy, only a few fancy kinds being imported;  and the local candlemakers were letting their light so shine before men that importation was steadily declining. *(Oct 2, 1930,Evening Post,19)*

### white one
  
exclamation. good!  
> <i>white one</i>: good! *(Feb 1981,Avondale College Slang Words in Use)* [NOTES: (Goldie Brown Collection)]

### white pine
  
noun. usually kahikatea  
[NOTES: usually Kahikatea, <i>Dacrycarpus dacrydioides</i>; also used in reference to <i>Manoao colensoi</i> formerly <i>Dacrydium colensoi</i>, silver pine; and <i>Leipdothamnus intermedius</i> formerly <i>D intermedium</i>, the yellow pine or yellow silver pine, occasionally refers to kauri. [<i>Podocarpus dacridioides</i>, kahikatea, swamp-pine, a timber tree with whiteish wood, and its wood or timber white pine the usual SI name for the tree and its timber up to c1970s, though it is probably being replaced, esp. as a tree-name name, by kahikatea, a name not unknown fairly early in the south esp. in Nelson and Marlborough in the NI, white-pine often linked with kahikatea esp. by some early writers. Polack, for example uses both terms [check???]; (In New Zealand) - <i>P. dacryoides</i>, A. Rich.; Maori name, <i>Kahikatea</i> (q.v.). [white pine, kahikatea] ca1953 R. Mason The only name used on the West Coast: fide J.H. Cooney, Forst ranger, who says `They do not know what you mean by `kaik' here [in Westport].' White P. - -->(In Australia) - <i>Frenela robusta</i>, A. Cunn. <i>F. robusta</i> (var. <i>microcarpa</i>), A. Cunn.]]  
> I am informed the price of white pine plank there is 16s. per 100 feet. *(Jul 5, 1845,New Zealand Spectator & Cook's Strait Guardian,3)*  
> IN order to Encourage Cash Purchases, the undersigned will SELL FIRST-CLASS RIMU at 10/- per 100 Feet; WHITE PINE from 9/- to 10/- for CASH ONLY. *(Aug 10, 1871,Nelson Evening Mail,2)*  
> Q. Are there many harbours there [on the west side of New Zealand]? A. Several ... All covered with wood Q. Of what sort? A. Of white and red pine and a wood called `black oak'. Q. Is it of the species called `kaikatua'? A. It is. Q. Did you see any of the species called `cowrie'? A. I did not. <i>Ibid.</i> 1/560 Q. Whereabouts do the red and white pine trees grow? A. The white pine grows in wet swampy places. The red pine grows on the sides of hills. Q. Is the red pine like the cowrie? A. It is the same grain, but not the same color. *(May 1821,Evidence of McDonald before Commissioner Bigge)* [NOTES: 1821 May Evidence of McDonald before Commissioner Bigge in 1908 McNab Historical Records 1/559]  
> **YATE** It [kahikatea] is, what has commonly been designated, the White Pine: but it is of so soft and spongy a nature, as to rot in a few months, if exposed to the weather. *(1835,New Zealand (various authors/titles),40)*  
> **POLACK** Few of the Kauri pine spars were procured by these vessels, their lading consisting of the [389] inferior white pine, called Kahikatea [a2,e-acute]; this injured the name of yellow pine, ... <i>Ibid.</i> 2/390 <i>The Kahikatea</i> [a2,e-acute] (<i>treniperus Novae Zelandicae</i>) g. coniferae, is very similar in outward appearance to the Kouri, ... The name by which it is distinguished, is that of white pine; ... *(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),2/388)*  
> A rival to this tree [the totara] is found in the Kahikatea, commonly called white pine, ... *(1841,Establishment of New Plymouth Settlement)* [NOTES: 1841 Establishment of New Plymouth Colony in 1940 Establishment of New Plymouth 80]  
> **HEAPHY** The wood most used in house-building is the <i>Kahikatea</i> (white pine), which is much esteemed for the ease with which it is worked, ... Next to the Kauri, the various sorts of red and white pine are of much . ... the wood most used in house-building is the <i>Kahikatea</i> (white pine). *(1842,Narrative of a Residence in  Various Parts of NZ,41)*  
> **WAKEFIELD** 1. Kahikatea, (<i>Dacrydium excelsum</i>.) This tree flourishes most in low, swampy, or alluvial soils. ... It is commonly called the <i>white pine</i>; and its wood is of much the same character in all the central and southernpart of New Zealand. *(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,140)*  
> Walked about it [sc. Peel Forest], fine totaras, white and red pines: ... *(Mar 28, 1849,Torlesse Papers,70,1958)*  
> **TORLESSE** Kaikatea, (white pine), mahi, black pine, remu, (red pine) *(1851,Spring in the Canterbury Settlement, A,6)*  
> The timber in [the groves] is chiefly Kaikatea, (white pine), Mahi, (black pine), Remu, (red pine), Pokaka, Kowai, and Totara. ... That in the other forest [ie. not Harewood or Alford] is principally tawai, (black birch), the best timber for boat and ship building, but sometimes unsound: ... *(Jun 28, 1851,Lyttelton Times,6)*  
> Totaras, white and black pines, koas etc *(1851,Otago Journal,8/126,1852)*  
> **TAYLOR** white-pine, P. dacrydioides - kahikatea, kahika, koroi.  ... This tree is generally called white pine, from the colour of its wood. *(1855,Te Ika a Maui,439)*  
> **THOMSON** <i>Valuable ... furniture planks are sawn from the Rimu, Kakikatae, or white pine (Dacrydium excelsum), </i>Matai, Mairi, and Manuka<i> (Leptospermum scoparium). </i> *(1859,Story of New Zealand, The,1/20,1974)*  
> **PRENTICE** Black, white and red pines, totara .... *(1870,Tale of NZ,33)* [NOTES: (Otago)]  
> [White pine] timber is known in all the provinces except Otago by the native name of `kahikatea'. I think we should adopt it also, not only on account of its being more euphonious, but for the reason that so many timbers in other parts of the world are called white pine. *(1876,TrNZI,9/160)* [NOTES: (Blair, Otago)]  
> [Native name] Kahikatea .. [Settlers' name] White-pine .. [Family] Coniferae *(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,121)* [NOTES: 1886 T.Kirk in 1902 Settler's Handbook of NZ 121]  
> The timber on the western side consists of red- and white-pine, matai (or black-pine), totara, kawhaka (or cedar), rata, and occasional silver-pine, besides black- and red-birch (<i>Fagus fusca</i>). *(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,19)*  
> Kahikatea (white-pine) .. Podocarpus dacrydioides .. Timber white, compact, straight in grain, and of even texture; ... *(1890,PWD Catalogue of Timbers,10)*  
> [The men at the timber-yard] had taught him ... how to tell rimu from matai, totara from kauri and white pine. *(1980,Some Other Country,147)* [NOTES: 1970 Middleton `The Loners' in 1984 Some Other Country 147]  
> Kahikatea (white pine) slab. *(1890,PWD Catalogue of Timbers,164)*  
> **POLACK** ... the most superior [canoes] are to be found at Wairoa, (Hawkes' Bay) the tribes at that place employ the <i>totarra</i> or red pine, the <i>kouri</i> or white pine being rarely found beyond the Thames (Horake). *(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,1/227)*  
> White Pine, or Kahikatea (<i>Podocarpus dacrydioides</i>). This is a straight, narrow, sometimes conical tree, growing on wet flats; wood of little value. <i>Ibid.</i> 32 A large round-headed tree, also called White Pine in Otago, and Silver Pine in Nelson, is not uncommon near Dunedin; it is probable that the male and female plants differ in their leaves, similar to the Kahikatea; the timber is valuable for boat-building. This plant is more like a Dacrydium than a Podocarpus. Flowers and fruit not seen. <i>Ibid.</i> 49 White Pine. Podocarpus dacrydioides, A. Rich. *(1869,TrNZI,1/iii/31)*  
> **HECTOR** A variety of this tree, [kahikatea, white pine] known as yellow-pine, is largely sawn in Nelson, and considered to be a durable building timber *(1883,Handbook of New Zealand,124)*  
> **LAING & BLACKWELL** <i>Podocarpus dacrydioides [(The Kahikatea or White Pine)]</i>. native name, Kahikatea. The following, probably fanciful, derivation has been given of this name: <i>tea, whitish,</i> from the colour of the wood, and <i>kahika, an ancient chief. </i> *(1907,Plants of NZ,70,1940, p. 75)*  
> Says a simple country paper:- `The borer, which is so destructive to white pine, is not at all particular what he bores through. In the Southland `Daily News' office a sheet of lead in one of the walls was found simply riddled by the pest.' *(May 13, 1911,NZ Truth,307/1/3)*  
> The chief indigenous trees of commercial value are: Kauri (<i>Agathis Australis</i>), Kahikatea or white pine (Podocarpus dacrydioides).. northern and southern rata (<i>Metrosideros robusta</i> and <i>Metrosideros lucida</i>). *(1936,Agricultural Organization in NZ,578)*  
> **HARVEY** Under our house was a beaut basement where Dad put all his tools and things and there was a big pussy willow ... with a green seat underneath made out of white pine. *(1964,Any Old Dollars Mister?,1)*  
> <i>Dacrydium Colensoi</i> and <i>D. intermedium,</i> are abundant in certain localities and yield the `white pine' of the saw mills; the true white pine (Podocarpus dacrydioides does not appear to occur on the island.)[check locality] *(1885,TrNZI,17/216)* [NOTES: (Kirk)]  
> The trees in the large pine forest are mostly small,and consist principally of the white pine (kahikatea), the rimu, and the matai, with a few of the tawa, of which shingles are made. *(Aug 13, 1842,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,91)*  
> A series of similar plains, though of a smaller size, reach for about twenty miles down the coast, intersected by streams and valleys of white pine, in two of which, the Waitakare and the Waitohi, I discovered seams of coal . . . *(Jan 27, 1860,Wellington Independent,5)*  
> The kahikatea, or white pine, as the sawyers call it, is an inferior New Zealand timber, and if for  short time exposed to the weather, easily rots.  It might, however, do well for the inside work of houses. *(Sep 12, 1840,New Zealand Gazette & Wellington Spectator,3)*  
> **Sewell** Black pine - white pine - and some Totara, but that is scarce. *(1853,Journal,1/320,1980)*  
> The vessel is to be 58 feet long, 19 feet 11 inches extreme breadth of beam.  The deck is to be of white pine. *(Oct 8, 1853,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,3)*  
> Half the timber exported in 1921 was white pine for the Australian market. *(Jun 15, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,14)*  
> Sale by Auction. A quantity of white pine deals of good quality. *(Apr 18, 1840,New Zealand Gazette & Wellington Spectator,1)*

### white rag
  
noun. a white (Sunday) shirt  
> **THORNTON** I rigged up the sack [shirt] to save my only remaining `white rag', as men here call them, for Sunday. *(1916,Wowser, The,89)*

### white rata
  
noun. <i>Metrosideros albiflora</i>  
[NOTES: see also white rata, white flowering rata, white flowered rata, and climbing rata.  <i>Metrosideros</i> spp., esp. <i>M. perforata</i> (in SI <i>M. diffusa</i>), a vine with small white flowers; =white (flowering) rata.]  
> The angular regularity of the altar railings was entirely disguised with a wreath of white rata and ivy leaves, whose sombre tint was relieved with the brilliancy [sic] of roses and gladiolas [sic]. *(Dec 26, 1884,Taranaki Herald,2)*  
> " .. On the shores of Te Anau, which I believe the most beautiful and grandest lake in the world, the manuka and white rata attract moths very notably." *(Feb 26, 1890,Hawke's Bay Herald,3)*  
> **HETLEY** (And) <i>Metrosideros albiflora</i> white rata *(1888,Native Flowers of NZ)*  
> **HUDSON** The best blossoms for attracting insects in New Zealand are those of the white rata, which blooms in the forest from February till April,... <i>Metrosideros scandens. </i> *(1892,Elementary Manual of NZ Entomology,13)*  
> **MARTIN** ...(<i>M[etrosideros] hypericifolia</i>), the only climbing rata found in Otago , where it is known as the WHITE RATA,  a name more commonly applied in the North Island to <i>M. scandens </i> *(1929,Flora NZ,148)*

### white rock shell
  
noun.  
[NOTES: cf. dark rock shell]  
> **POWELL** WHITE ROCK SHELL (<i>Neothais scalaris</i>). Hopetea of the Maoris. A thick, coarse spirally ridged white shell up to 3 a inches in height. It is common amongst rocks at low tide, both in harbours and on the open coast. The species is widespread in New Zealand and occurs in Tasmania and Southern Australia also. *(1947,Native Animals of NZ,28)*  
> **McLINTOCK** White rock shell .. .. hopetea .. .. <i>Neothais scalaris </i> *(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-12)*

### white shark
  
noun. great white shark  
See: also white pointer  
> The specimen, from an examination of the teeth, must have belonged to the genus <i>Squalus</i>; described by Cuvier under the term <i>Carcharias</i>, and known to practical men as the great white shark. *(Sep 6, 1843,New Zealand Gazette & Wellington Spectator,2)*  
> Oil is obtained from their bodies; the Chinese make a highly-prized gelatinous soap from the fins of the white shark, and its liver and flesh are eaten by the natives of some of the Pacific islands. *(Jun 8, 1870,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,3)*  
> <i>Carcharadon carcharias</i>. On account of the pure white colouring of its under-surface, this species is usually referred to as `white pointer' or `white shark.' *(1924,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,6/269)* [NOTES: (Phillipps)]  
> **HUTTON** CARCHARODON RONDELETII. Mull. White Shark. *(1872,Fishes NZ,125)*  
> **SHERRIN** <i>Carcharodon Rondeletti</i> .. White shark. *(1886,Handbook Fishes of NZ,115)*  
> White shark (<i>Carcharoclon rondeletii</i>) *(1890,TrNZI,22/375)*  
> ... I felt confident that it was an unusually large specimen of the White Shark (<i>Carcharodon rondeletti</i>), which visits our coasts every summer. *(1891,TrNZI,23/126)*  
> White shark (<i>Carcharoclon rondeletii</i>) *(1890,TrNZI,22/375)* [NOTES: (G.M. Thomson)]  
> <i>Carcharodon carcharias</i>: White shark, white pointer. *(1936,Handbook for New Zealand, The,71)*  
> **POWELL** WHITE SHARK (<i>Carcharodon albimors</i>), Mango tuatini of the Maoris, is a large, heavy-bodied shark....This voracious shark occurs in Australian waters also, where its name `White Death' indicates that it is a dangerous species. *(1947,Native Animals of NZ,60)*  
> **GRAHAM** White Shark <i>Carcharodon albimors</i> (Whitley) *(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,398)*  
> **McLINTOCK** Shark, white.. .. mango-taniwha, mango tuatini .. .. <i>Carcharodon carcharias </i> *(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712)*  
> **DOOGUE & MORELAND** WHITE SHARK ... <i>Other names: Carcharodon carcharias</i>; great white shark, white pointer, man-eater; mango-tuatini (Maori). *(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,185)*  
> **BRAMBLEY** Among the various species [of shark] we have landed, the seven-gilled, or tuatira, and the white-pointer have one similarity. ... The seven-gillers we catch all the year. *(1966,Sea-cockies of the Manukau,44)*  
> **MORELAND** WHITESHARK [<i>Carcharodon carcharias</i>] ... White pointer, great white shark, and maneater, are widely used alternative names, Mango-tuatini is Maori and not in regular use. *(1967,Marine Fishes,12)*  
> **PAUL et al.** [index to common names 283-293] shark, white 138-43 *(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography)*

### white sickness
  
noun.  
> **LAWSON** [The prospector c1870s] has the `white sickness'. *(1951,Gold in their Hearts,127)*

### white skin
  
noun. rare ?literary or transl. of Pakeha  
> **FEATON** Directly after they had fired, the Maoris commenced shouting to the white skins to come on. *(1879,Waikato War,48)*

### white slipper shell
  
noun.  
> **POWELL** WHITE SLIPPER SHELL (<i>Zeacrypta monoxyla</i>). A limpet-shaped shell, up to 1 1/2 inches long, with a curious internal shelf. *(1947,Native Animals of NZ,26)*

### White Sunday
  
noun. a Samoan Festival transferred to New Zealand  
> To Samoans, yesterday was Lotu Tamaiti. Others know it as White Sunday, when Samoans dress in white for church and children lead the ceremony. *(Oct 10, 2005,NZ Herald,A1)*  
> The Quitline, a free caller-support service for people wanting to quite smoking, has a new advertising campaign focussing on Pacific people with images from White Sunday. The campaign has one clear message: stop smoking if you want to be there for your children and grandchildren at their White Sunday. *(2005,Spacifik,74,2005)*  
> Ruth Caughy shares a service to celebrate White Sunday which is a special service for people from Samoa to be held on Sunday, 13 October 2002. *(2005,Internet - see Notes)* [NOTES: www.presbyterian.org.nz]  
> White Sunday .. falls on the second Sunday each October, where church services honour children - who often wear white clothes on the day - with singing, performing skits or readings from the Bible. Tofete suggested the idea of White Sunday, a core childhood experience for so may Samoans where they either enjoy or dread all the attention ... *(Apr 29, 2009,Dominion Post,D2)*  
> Mention White Sunday to almost any Samoan and a smile will cross their face as they recall some favourite memories... It's held once a year on the second Sunday of October and is sometimes called Children's Sunday - it's a church service especially for children and they perform the service as well... I remember back then, my mum driving around Auckland looking for the best white dresses for me and my sisters for White Sunday... In the scheme of things, White Sunday - Le Aso Sa Tamaiti has come to signify many things throughout my childhood years. *(Sep 2004,Wellington Family Times,Spring/ 6)*

### white tea
  
noun.  
> **MISS G HALL** This ... is new to me. I heard it for the first time used by a farming family on the Taeri Plains outside Dunedin. It means tea (i.e., the 5.30 p.m. meal) consisting of tea, bread and butter, and scones; ... a meal where nothing is cooked [and served hot]. *(1951,Personal Communication)*

### white tea-tree
  
noun.  
[NOTES: incl white ti-tree]  
> [Three Kings] is clothed principally with white tea-tree - manuka rauriki (Leptospermum ericoides) ... *(1930,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,11/152)* [NOTES: (Fraser)]  
> Whilst one need not take exception to the pakeha speaking of those two forms as "red tea-tree" and white tea-tree," it is quite a different matter when he speaks of "red manuka" and "white manuka."   There is not, properly speaking, such a tree as white manuka. *(Sep 3, 1931,Evening Post,12)*  
> After various experiments, they have succeeded in utilising the white tea-tree (manukau) [<i>sic</i>] for the purpose of coach shafts, and have found that it will bear a strain of 75 per cent. greater than the best gum timber sent from Australia. *(Apr 28, 1871,Daily Southern Cross,3)*  
> But at last as the morning wore away they grew tired of the flax swamps and the willows, and even of the pretty white ti-tri and sweetbriar that grew along the bank, and though they had eaten an alarming number of ginger-nuts, they wanted to get ashore and have lunch. *(May 16, 1895,Otago Witness,45)*  
> They had white tea-tree, a wood that was better than hickory. *(Mar 19, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,31)*  
> Tenders for firewood from Messrs G. Dunton, A. E. Shepherd, B. Killen, Dalbeth and Rea were opened, the last being accepted for 80 tons of white teatree at 6s 3d; the tender for puriri at 6s 6d was deferred, as was also till next meeting a proposal to get a small sample lot. *(Oct 16, 1901,Bay of Plenty Times,2)*

### white tern
  
noun. [Environment]  
[NOTES: (Raoul and Kermadecs)]  
> On Saturday last Mr Boyd received in good order the following stuffed birds and animals:-  White tern, black-headed tern, red-throated diver, kestrel hawks ... *(Feb 16, 1910,Wanganui Chronicle,8)*  
> The sooty tern, little grey noddy, and white tern, which all breed at the Kermadec Islands, have wandered to New Zealand on one or two occasions, while the noddy, which is not uncommon at the Kermadecs, has also twice paid New Zealand a visit. *(Oct 22, 1927,Evening Post,17)*  
> Four species of terns frequent the group for breeding purposes, the sooty tern, white-capped noddy, white tern, and grey noddy. *(Apr 14, 1934,Evening Post,11)*  
> Little birds abounded in the bush, including redbreasts, a bird like a tiny sparrow, but darker in colour with a white head, and small parakeets with blue wings and tail and big scarlet beaks, and also the white tern. *(Aug 25, 1944,Evening Post,4)* [NOTES: Norfolk Is. ref]  
> **FALLA et al.** WHITE TERN <i>Gygis alba Other names</i>: White Noddy, Love Tern, Fairy Tern *(1966,Birds of New Zealand,166)*  
> <i>209. Gygis alba [candida</i> Gmelin, 1789] Tropical and subtropical seas, including Norfolk and Kermadec Islands, breeding on Raoul Island ... *(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,56,1980)*

noun. <i>Cyathea dealbata</i>, ponga.
