> (And) <i>Cyathea dealbata</i> *(Jul 29, 1919,Vernacular Names)*  
> Cyathea dealbata, the white tree fern, is harder than the medullaris or black. *(Jun 15, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,60)*

### white tussock
  
noun.  
> The land ploughed was old lea, which was partly covered with white tussock, but notwithstanding this there was no sign of grass when the plough had done its work. *(Jun 20, 1890,Mataura Ensign,4)*  
> White tussock with plenty of clover and trefoil and some native bush. *(Aug 2, 1919,NZ Truth,3)*  
> In place of white tussock and sheep run we now see proverbial smiling homesteads and cultivated fields, and other signs of progress resulting from close settlement. *(Feb 4, 1903,Otago Witness,16)*  
> The hills afford good rough pasture for sheep, being pretty well covered over with white tussock grass, which shows a warmer, drier soil, likely to produce abundantly more native grass that the colder snow tussock country. *(Dec 23, 1887,Otago Witness,36)*  
> TENDERS will be received by the undersigned, till 25th August, for the Ploughing of 200 acres of white Tussock Land, Furrow 4a  inches by 10 inches. *(Aug 18, 1875,Southland Times,3)*

### white warehou
  
noun.  
> Black cardinalfish, frostfish, ghost shark, ribaldo, rubyfish, sea perch, trumpeter, white warehou and yellow-eyed mullet have now been included... Other reductions to catch limits are to specific stocks of grey mullet, barracouta and oreo dory. *(Sep 12, 1998,Dominion,2)*  
> **AYLING** <i> WHITE WAREHOU Seriolella caerulea </i> ... It is distinguished ... by its uniform grey-white body colour. *(1982,Collins Guide,301,1984)*  
> [species name] white warehou .. [scientific name] <i> Seriolella caerulea </i>.. [other name(s)] -- *(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters)*  
> **PAUL** <i> White warehou Seriolella caerulea </i> A deepwater warehou that also occurs off southern Australia and southern South America. *(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,138)*  
> The trawlers, Dong Wons 513, 520, 521 and 522, were seized in March 1988. Fisheries officers suspected that the four trawlers had caught the quota species silver warehou and mislabelled it white warehou, a non-quota species. *(Aug 13, 1990,Dominion,8)*

### white-ant
  
verb.  
> **SOLJAK** Following are examples of colloquialisms common to New Zealand and Australian English, with their English or American equivalent ... <i>white ant: </i>to undermine; e.g.<i> to white ant the Labor movement </i> *(1946,New Zealand (various authors/titles),115)*  
> The sea is slow and untaught/ With no thought to white-ant/ In hate or elation plant or plantation. *(Feb 20, 1953,Listener (New Zealand Listener),7)* [NOTES: (D. Glover)]  
> <i>to white ant </i> *(Feb 1981,Avondale College Slang Words in Use)* [NOTES: (Goldie Brown Collection)]

### white-ash burn
  
noun.  
> **LEVY** It was not so difficult to replace the forest with grass where the climate made intense white-ash burns possible after the felling of the forest. ... But in higher rain-fall belts, when poor burns (black burns) only occur, much forest litter remained unconsumed ... *(1951,Grasslands of NZ,87,1970)*

### whitebait
  
noun. the fry of <i>Galaxias</i> and <i>Retropinna</i> spp. (esp. <i>R. richardsonii</i>), bait, inanga, inaka (cf (s)cent, silvery smelt; gutty bait) also called bait, inanga the young of a group of small fishes, in New Zealand especially galaxias, regarded as a table delicacy.  
[NOTES: incl fig (hasn't got the guts of a whitebait)]  
> The shortjawed kokopu is one of five native fish species commonly referred to as whitebait when they are juveniles migrating back to fresh water. *(Aug 28, 2008,Dominion Post,B5)*  
> Denis Glover told him it [sc. MonteHolcroft's book "Carapace"] reminded himof "one small whitebait struggling to getup the Niagara Falls." *(1992,North & South,62)*  
> Several varieties of smelt (<i>Retropinna</i>) called by the Natives `inanga', ...; bullheads of different sorts, and a slimy fish the `kokopu' (<i>Galaxias fasciatus</i>) the young of which are supposed to be the New Zealand `whitebait' are widely distributed ... *(1894,NZ Official Yearbook,431)*  
> **MORRIS** <i>Whitebait, n</i>. a fish; not, as in England, the fry of the herring and sprat, but in Victoria, <i>Engraulis antarcticus</i>, Cast ln.; and in New Zealand, the young fry of <i>Galaxias attenuatus,</i> Jenyns (<i>Inanga</i>, q.v.). The young of the <i>New Zealand Smelt</i> (q.v.), <i>Retropinna richardsonii</i>, Gill, are also called <i>Whitebait</i>, both in New Zealand and in Tasmania. *(1898,Austral English,510)*  
> <i>White.bait</i>. (hw&imacr.t.b&amacr.t.),<i> n. (Zoo.l.) [Add.] (c)</i> A name for the inanga. See INANGA. *(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2034)*  
> **PASCOE** whitebait [get citation] *(1957,Mr Explorer Douglas,280)* [NOTES: c1899 Douglas Birds of South Westland in 1957 Pascoe Mr Explorer Douglas 280 ...]  
> **DRUMMOND & HUTTON** The waters of New Zealand abound with fishes, ..., from the shark to the whitebait. <i>Ibid.</i> 75 Whitebait (which are known to the Maoris as inanga) and smelt go to the sea to spawn in April, ... *(1902,Nature in NZ,71)*  
> The question of the true nature of the so-called `New Zealand whitebait' has been .... fully worked out ... *(1903,TrNZI,35/312)*  
> **TREGEAR** A small fish (<i>inanga</i>), resembling the minnow, swarmed in tidal streams ... They were eaten and pressed into a solid mass, and are now called whitebait by the settlers. *(1904,Maori Race, The,108)*  
> <i>WHITEBAIT (fried)</i> ... flour ... shake the whitebait in it ... Then fry ... Do not cook too much or they will be tough ... Serve with <i>brown bread and butter ... slices of lemon </i> *(1910,Colonial Everyday Cookery,108)* [NOTES: c1910-1914 Colonial Everyday Cookery 108]  
> RETROPINNA RETROPINNA. <i>Smelt</i>. The larval smelt, which has a strong cucumber-smell, is rarely sold as `whitebait.' Canning-factories at Hokitika do not purchase any fish under the term `whitebait' other than the larval <i>Galaxias attenuatus</i>. Odd smelts may occasionally be detected among whitebait exposed for sale in Wellington... GALAXIAS ATTENUATUS. <i>Minnow</i>. All whitebait examined from Auckland were found to be referable to this species. Whitebait were exposed for sale in Auckland during August, September, and October. *(May 1922,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,V/94)* [NOTES: Phillipps and Hodgkinson Further Notes on Edible Fishes]  
> **SPEIGHT** The whitebait runs begin definitely in August and continue through the two following months; but in November the shoals begin to include young smelts and bullies <i>(Gobiomorphus) </i>which prey actively upon the whitebait, the bullies being particularly voracious. *(1927,Natural History of Canterbury,196)* [NOTES: Archey `Native Fishes of Canterbury]  
> <i> i[i]nanga</i>, a fresh-water fish (Retropinna richardsoni), whitebait and Galaxias attenuatus), whitebait. Once netted by the ton; now becoming scarce, being devoured by introduced fish as well as human beings. *(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/149)* [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]  
> <i>whitebait</i>: small edible fish. *(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,176)*  
> **GRAHAM** Whitebait (Minnow) <i>Austrocobitis attenuatus</i> (Jenyns) (Syn. <i>Galaxias campbelli</i> Sauvage) *(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,400)* [NOTES: 1955 Whitley Namelist in 1956 Graham NZ Fishes 400]  
> **GRAHAM** MINNOW or WHITEBAIT (INANGA) <i>Austrocobitis attenuatus</i> (Jenyns) ... For a long time it has been known to the Maori and to Europeans that our New Zealand Whitebait is the young of a native freshwater fish commonly known as Minnow, or Inanga or Hiwi to the Maori, and erroneously called a Trout. ... The early settlers called it Maori Trout, Native Trout, Rock Trout and Minnow. But it is not a Trout. Nor is it a Minnow, through it resembles the English freshwater Minnow. While the name Trout is not correct, it is and will always be known as such by farmers and naturalists. In the Bay of Plenty, Whitebait is known to the Maori as Porohoe. *(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,118)*  
> **McLINTOCK** Whitebait .. .. inanga .. .. <i>Galaxias Retopinna </i> *(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712)*  
> **NATUSCH** Best known to most of us as whitebait are immature [208] galaxiids - usually <i>Galaxias attenuatus</i>, called (when grown) inanga in the north, inaka in the south. *(1967,Animals of NZ, The,207)*  
> **DENNIS** The juveniles of five species of galaxias make up the whitebait catch: inanga<i> (Galaxias maculatus), </i>koaro <i>(G. brevipinnis)</i>, banded kokopu <i>(G. fasciatus)</i>, giant kokopu<i> (G. argenteus)</i> and short-jawed kokopu <i>(G. postvectis). </i> *(1981,Paparoas Guide,187)*  
> **AYLING** The whitebait species that develop in the sea are: <i>Galaxias argenteus ..., G. fasciatus ..., G. postvectis ..., G. brevipinnis ..., </i>and <i>G. maculatus </i>... An adult koaro, <i>Galaxias brevipinnis</i> is illustrated here but the whitebait of all five species are similar to this in shape ... *(1982,Collins Guide,109,1984)*  
> **PAUL** In New Zealand the term whitebait is usually applied to juvenile galaxids as they enter rivers from the sea in early spring. *(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,48)*  
> I venture to settle ... the vexed question, `What is Whitebait?' ... English Whitebait ... has been looked upon as the young of the Sprat, the Shad, and the Herring. ... The New Zealand Whitebait has no affinity with the English fish, whose name it bears ... *(1870,TrNZI,2/84)* [NOTES: (Powell)]  
> THE NEW ZEALAND MINNOW. NOTES ON THE LIFE-HISTORY OF THE NEW ZEALAND MINNOW (THE ADULT WHITEBAIT), <i>GALAXIAS ATTENUATUS. </i> *(1924,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,7/117)* [NOTES: (Phillipps)]  
> **PHILLIPPS** Gilbert Whitley in July, 1935, notes that the first naturalist to discover the extraordinary breeding habits of the New Zealand whitebait was D.H. McKenzie whose account in <i>The New Zealand Illustrated Magazine</i> (vol. 10, pt. 2, May, 1904, pp. 122-26, with 4 figs) is most informative. *(1940,Fishes NZ,29)*  
> **GRAHAM** MINNOW or WHITEBAIT (INANGA) <i>Austrocobitis attenuatus</i> (Jenyns) ... For a long time it has been known to the Maori and to Europeans that our New Zealand Whitebait is the young of a native freshwater fish commonly known as Minnow, or Inanga or Hiwi to the Maori, and erroneously called a Trout. ... The early settlers called it Maori Trout, Native Trout, Rock Trout and Minnow. But it is not a Trout. Nor is it a Minnow, through it resembles the English freshwater Minnow. While the name Trout is not correct, it is and will always be known as such by farmers and naturalists. In the Bay of Plenty, Whitebait is known to the Maori as Porohoe. *(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,118)*  
> **WILSON** SECOND-CLASS WHITEBAIT These are the young of the smelt (<i>Retropinna retropinna</i>) which are caught from wharves mainly - especially Auckland's Devonport Wharf - and sold as whitebait. There are no regulations governing them as they are not regarded as being `true' whitebait. *(1981,Fisherman's Bible,288)*  
> **IHIMAERA & LONG** the whitebait with moonlight eyes / go running up the river *(1982,Into the World of Light,259)*  
> During his visits to New Zealand in the 1960s and 70s, English actor and raconteur Robert Morley used to insist that his whitebait be filleted and have their eyes cut out before being made into fritters. *(Aug 20, 2003,Capital Times,18)*  
> **McDOWALL** W e're having an argument at work about whitebait? Are they a separate kind of fish or the young of something else? The boss reckons they're baby inanga. *(1984,NZ Whitebait Book, The,5)*  
> In each city, town and village, real estate agents spawn like whitebait. *(Nov 6, 2004,Dominion Post,B4)*  
> **HULME & MORRISON** Not to mention the whitebait called kokopara, that somehow transmogrified to be the cockabully... *(1989,Hold This Land,10)*  
> **HULME & MORRISON** Whitebait in pristine condition arebeautiful. *(1989,Homeplaces,104)*  
> New Zealand whitebait consists of the juveniles of five species of galaxiid -inanga, koaro, banded kokopu, shortjaw kokopu and giant kokopu... *(2006,National Geographic Magazine,77/31)*  
> A number of the "white bait" variety of bookmaker did a big business.. at Taranaki [sic] recent meeting. *(Mar 12, 1904,NZ Observer,15)*  
> "Have you ever wondered what becomes of a whitebait when its life isn't frittered away?" *(Nov 11, 2003,Dominion Post,A18)*  
> It must be just great weather for the frost fish ... whatever the whitebait think about it. *(Jul 3, 1937,Evening Post,8)*  
> Continuing his speech, Mr. Sample said the British people had a desperate struggle ahead of them today to defend their liberty, and anyone who would not fight such a challenge had not "the guts of a whitebait." *(Apr 17, 1940,Evening Post,6)*  
> **ELDRED-GRIGG** They [sc. Natives] looked very ugly and peaceable, with a rude sort of basket made of flax fibres, or buckets filled with whitebait, which they wanted us to buy. *(1980,Southern Gentry,63)*  
> Also the well-known and delicious whitebait, the inanga - which I believe to be the larval stage of the common eel - cannot ascend to the lakes; but the name "inanga" is given locally to a tiny fish that is said to be the young of another fish, the "kokopu"... It enjoys a name, <i>Galaxias attenuatus</i>. *(Mar 20, 1921,Blackwood's Magazine,368)*  
> I was surprised to see in a bush stream shoals of whitebait, making their way up. *(Dec 7, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,17)*  
> **KENNEALLY** When you heard the call "Fresh Foxton Whitebait" - it was out onto the street with an empty jug. *(1981,Johnsonville Yesterday,25)*  
> We can get her a job in N.Z., filleting"whitebait". *(Nov 29, 1916,Christchurch Girls' High School,I/7 : 158)*  
> The forerunners of that great piscatorial army - whitebait - have made their appearance in the river. *(Aug 10, 1901,West Coast Times,4)*  
>  *(1840,Journal of Ensign Best,222,1966)*  
> The Natives were employed catching White Bait which the[y] took in nets in great numbers [at Waikanae]. *(Oct 24, 1840,Journal of Ensign Best,254,1966)*  
> **SELWYN** Here we [33] found the natives engaged in cooking white bait (Inanga), a small fish the size of a minnow, caught in great abundance in Taupo and Rotorua Lakes. *(Oct 30, 1843,Journal)* [NOTES: 1843 Oct 30 Selwyn Journal in 1845 NZ Part II (Church in the Colonies VII) 32-33]  
> **SELWYN** ... a large supply of the `white bait' of the lake [Taupo] already mentioned. *(Nov 3, 1843,Journal)* [NOTES: 1843 Nov 3 Selwyn Journal in 1845 NZ Part II (Church in the Colonies VII) 40]  
> **SHORTLAND** The fish were excellent, having somewhat the flavour and size of white bait. *(Jan 19, 1844,Southern Districts of NZ,228)* [NOTES: 1844 Jan 19 in 1851 Shortland Southern Districts of NZ 228]  
> **SHORTLAND** The fish were excellent, having somewhat the flavour and size of white bait. *(Jan 19, 1844,Southern Districts of NZ,228)* [NOTES: 1844 Jan 19 in 1851 Shortland Southern Districts of NZ 228]  
> **WAKEFIELD** This fish is called hinanaga [check] and resembles Blackwall white-bait in size and flavour. Its colour is a pinkish white, spotted with black. *(1845,Adventure in NZ,2/100)*  
> **TAYLOR** Aperahama gave ... us a capital breakfast of potatoes and whitebait, brought dry from Araura *(1959,Early Travellers,224)*  
> **WAKEFIELD** There are scarcely any fresh-water fish; but <i>Eels</i> of the finest kind abound in the swamps, lagoons, and rivers; a small fish very like <i>Whitebait </i>is taken in most of the rivers, and exists in the fresh-water lakes in such quantities as to form a principal article of the food of the natives inhabiting the country near the Taupo and other lakes. *(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,161)*  
> **POWER** There is a kind of white-bait in most of the rivers in the spring months, quite equal to their Blackwall congeners. *(1849,Sketches in NZ,78)*  
> **GREENWOOD** The lake abounds with ... small fish like white bait, with which we made most luxurious fries. *(1850,Journey to Taupo,50)*  
> **HURSTHOUSE** These, with the smelt, lamprey, whitebait ... are all the fish ... *(1857,New Zealand (various authors/titles),1/122)*  
> **BRUNNER** In October and November commences the fishing season here [in Paroa, West Coast] - the mutta [fn. mata, a variety of the inanga], or white-bait entering the rivers ...in great quantity. *(1857,Great Journey,55,1952)*  
> **SMITH** This morning, we breakfasted off the whitebait or <i>inanga</i>, which is so common in Taupo; and it is very nice fried. *(Jan 18, 1858,Notes of a Journey)* [NOTES: 1858 Jan 18 Smith Notes of a Journey in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 365]  
> **THOMSON** In the lakes of New Zealand are a large number of delicate fish, not unlike white-bait, called Inanga ... *(1859,Story of New Zealand, The,1/30)*  
> **BARKER** Our first stage was to Kaiapoi ... where we had a good luncheon of whitebait ... *(1865,Station Life in NZ,24,1870)*  
> **MONEY** The afternoon of the 1st July, 1861, found me enjoying a white-bait dinner at Blackwall, ... *(1871,Knocking About in NZ,1)*  
> **HECTOR** At certain seasons the young fry [of <i>Galaxias attenuatus</i>] swarms in incredible numbers, and forms the Whitebait of New Zealand, but it is a very poor substitute for the little Herring that is so well known at Greenwich by that name. *(1872,Edible Fishes,129)*  
> [Maori or Settlers' Name] Whitebait .. [Scientific Name] <i>Retropinna richardsoni </i> *(1978,TrNZI,10/326)* [NOTES: (P. Thomson, Otago)]  
> **FEATON** shoals of whitebait *(1879,Waikato War,10)* [NOTES: (Griffiths Collection)]  
> In the freshwater streams are eels, lampreys, and whitebait; ... *(1882,Brighter Britain,2/231)*  
> **DOMETT** That he the first might touch and taste,/ In flax-wov'n basket for a dish,/ A dainty pile of delicate fish/ .../ Like<i> whitebait</i> some; .../ <i>Ibid.</i> 1/305 Whitebait ..... Eleotris basalis. - Abundant. *(1883,Ranolf and Amohia,1/183)*  
> **SHERRIN** SMELT (<i>Retropinna Richardsoni</i>). ... Common in rivers and lakes. In the Waikato they go down to the sea to spawn in April, and the young fish return in October, when, together with the young of <i>Galaxias attenuatus</i>, they a called whitebait. <i>Ibid.</i> 141 WHITEBAIT, or MINNOW (<i>Galaxias attenuatus</i>). ... At Taupo Lake and other places in the interior, small fish, which the Maoris collectively term inanga, but which are chiefly of the species now referred to, ... *(1886,Handbook Fishes of NZ,139)*

### whitebait
  
adjective.  
[NOTES: attributive use; various collocations]  
> The ABC Quick Lunch Cafe.. at this time of year serves succulent whitebait sandwiches on white bread for $5.50. *(Oct 21, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),94)*  
> The whitebait net was made of coarse cotton, with a frame of supplejack tied with flax. *(Nov 6, 1982,Listener (New Zealand Listener),131)*  
> He [sc. Robert Alan Lucas] invented the whitebait pie. *(Dec 1998,Mana,9)*  
> I found myself in the ABC Quick Lunch Cafe in Tainui St, Greymouth, wolfing down a whitebait sandwich for $1.50. *(Jan 13, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),94)*  
> The beginning of summer was equated with the "Whitebait Man". His long drawn-out call of "fresh whi-te-ba-it" would echo through the streets while he scooped two shillings' worth of the tiny fish from an open kerosene tin. *(Jul 26, 2000,Evening Post,23)*  
> At Greymouth evidence was also heard from the Hokitika whitebait fishermen. *(Jun 11, 1871,Evening Post,15)*  
> This lure he finds quite as killing as the deadly whitebait minnow. *(Jun 23, 1899,Auckland Weekly News,Suppl. 3)*  
> Whitebait science could improve the odds in the Wellington region of netting the wriggling delcacy... "We would like people who fish for whitebait to be involved in raising the productivity of whitebait spawning." *(Nov 24, 2001,Evening Post,1)*

### whitebait
  
verb. to fish for whitebait  
> It appears that the Chinese went whitebaiting about 5 am and the river rose too quickly to allow them to gain the high ground. *(Oct 22, 1897,Grey River Argus,2)*  
> He was whitebaiting when he lost his footing, and was carried away by the current. *(Oct 22, 1904,Grey River Argus,5)*  
> **McGILL** ... - once a whale was washed up on the beach, down by The Cut, where Dad whitebaited, successfully. *(1980,Ghost Towns of NZ,238)*

### whitebait
  
noun. an inconsequential person  
> **HYDE** `That Dickie, do you think I listen to him? That little whitebait.' *(1938,Nor the Years Condemn,185)*

### whitebait camp
  
noun.  
> `We've always lived on the road up to now. In vans or whitebait camps. We do a bit of gold now and again. And Robin runs a good `possum line. *(Jul 14, 1986,NZ Woman's Weekly,12)*

### whitebait eyes
  
noun. tired eyes  
> **McDOWALL** At the beginning of the season catchers suffer from 'whitebait eyes' brought about by the strain of peering at running water for long periods. *(1984,NZ Whitebait Book, The,163)*

### whitebait fishing
  
noun.  
[NOTES: incl attrib as in 'whitebait fishing season']  
> The secretary was instructed to write to the Superintendent, asking him to extend the protection on the river Avon, as far as New Brighton, such protection not to include whitebait fishing with ordinary whitebait nets. *(Sep 30, 1871,Star, The,2)*  
> That whitebait fishing be allowed during the months of August, September and October. *(May 14, 1889,West Coast Times,4)*  
> This afternoon the police were informed that two young men named Waldron, who were whitebait fishing on the river Avon, had found the body of a child wrapped in some clothing floating nearly opposite the residence of Mr Pannell, at Avonside. *(Aug 11, 1892,Star, The,3)*  
> The whitebait fishing season in the Waimakariri and Ashley Rivers is being well maintained. *(Dec 12, 1906,Otago Witness,12)* [NOTES: attrib]  
> Whitebait fishing is a most important industry, and from an economic point of view a very valuable national asset. *(Aug 6, 1927,Evening Post,11)*  
> As regards the woman who went whitebait fishing and had to take her baby with her, there is another side to that story. *(Nov 18, 1930,Evening Post,11)*  
> The prohibition of whitebait fishing in the Grey River, on the Greymouth side, from the railway station to the lagoon, is being urged by the Health Department, and it is understood that the Chief Inspector of Fisheries, Wellington, is being asked to take action, states a Greymouth correspondent. *(Aug 16, 1941,Evening Post,8)*  
> COOKING DEMONSTRATION .. "Queen Cakes, Apple Cheese Tartlets, Whitebait Fritters, Tongue Tart, Cornflour Sponge." *(Sep 9, 1922,NZ Truth,2)*  
> WHITEBAIT FRITTERS .. 3d. *(Aug 14, 1934,Evening Post,15)*  
> He has been appointed by the Government to investigate the circumstances in respect of whitebait fishing at Temuka. *(Oct 13, 1911,Ashburton Guardian,5)*

### whitebait fritter
  
noun.  
> If it must be done, eating a whitebait fritter is at least a privilege that should be earned  - not bought. *(Aug 28, 2008,Dominion Post,B5)*  
> <i>WHITEBAIT FRITTERS </i>... flour ... egg ... milk ... Drop a small quantity at a time into plenty of boiling fat, and fry ... *(1906,Tetaka Kai: A Book of Well Tried Recipes,8)*  
> The crispy whitebait fritters in thin white bread and butter at Mokau were eaten in a tea-room where plastic flowers framed the view up the Taranaki coast to the cloud-covered mountain. *(Jul 6, 2003,Sunday (Sunday Star Times Magazine),16)*  
> ...she managed to find me a plate of fresh cooked whitebait fritters... *(Aug 2004,Photographer's Mail, The,13 (8)/8)*  
> **VIRTUE** Parnell cooked whitebait fritters. *(1991,Always the Islands of Memory,134)*  
> The Te Kuiti Muster, they called it; a market day accompanied by the Te Kuiti & Districts Pipe Band, at which whitebait fritter sandwiches in white bread could be had for $2 each, and two groups of hangi and boil-up sellers eyed each other warily from opposite sides of the same intersection. *(Jun 2004,North & South,76)*  
> To compensate there is the classic West Coast whitebait fritter. *(Nov 2003,AA Directions,No. 58/69)*  
> It is the highest altitude at whichwhitebait fritters have been cooked in analpine camp. *(1940,Canterbury Mountaineer, The,IX/18)*  
> COOKING DEMONSTRATION .. "Queen Cakes, Apple Cheese Tartlets, Whitebait Fritters, Tongue Tart, Cornflour Sponge." *(Sep 9, 1922,NZ Truth,2)*  
> WHITEBAIT FRITTERS .. 3d. *(Aug 14, 1934,Evening Post,15)*

### whitebait net
  
noun. fabric with which to make a net for catching whitebait

> PROCURE YOUR WHITEBAIT NET FROM THE FARMERS'. *(Sep 17, 1943,Ellesmere Guardian,5)*  
> WHITEBAIT NET, 2/11 yard.   In Green and White, 80-in. *(Sep 3, 1929,Otautau Standard & Wallace County Chronicle,1)*  
> Whitebait Net, the very best value, at 1s and 1s 3d. *(Aug 26, 1910,Marlborough Express,5)*

### whitebait net
  
noun. a mesh net for catching whitebait  
> Fragment of a whitebait-net made of flax. *(1897,TrNZI,29/175)*  
> **SEDDON** ... the equipment for the expedition in the form of large whitebait nets and kerosene tins with handles are found and the family goes fishing. *(1968,Seddons, The,20)*  
> Mrs Wi Wilson has a piece of polished greenstone, a shark's tooth earring, and a whitebait net, whilst Mesdames Hope Nihini and Fini send flax baskets, greenstone brooch, and a small greenstone mere. *(Apr 22, 1882,Colonist (Nelson),2)*  
> At the weekend several large parties from Wanganui set off gaily with whitebait nets and tins ... *(Nov 16, 1937,Evening Post,8)*  
> Flourishing a grey blanket .. Mr. Semple vehemently criticised the article for its poorness of quality.   "It would make a good whitebait net for Chinese," he said. *(Sep 11, 1935,Evening Post,7)*

> Fishing in the rivers Avon, Heathcote, Cust .. is strictly prohibited, unless for whitebait with whitebait nets ... *(Jan 31, 1877,Star, The,3)*

### whitebait pattie
  
noun. a fritter or pattie made with whitebait  
> Greg turned up as we were sitting down to a feed of buttered white bread and whitebait patties (they are rarely called fritters on the Coast), which Dave had just cooked according to his mum's recipe. *(Nov 30, 2004,Dominion Post,E8)*  
> Whitebait patties were always on the menu of any social gathering at that time of the year, be it wedding, funeral, send-off or welcome home. *(Nov 6, 1982,Listener (New Zealand Listener),131)*

### whitebait run
  
noun.  
> The "whitebait run" is becoming a real headache for the pilots. *(Nov 12, 1947,NZ Observer,5)*  
> Whitebait runs are a bonus for trout, and as these fish are the juvenile form of larger fish that live in swamps and marshes, sometimes many kilometres from the sea, they have to run a gauntlet of trout, herons and shags before they reach the safety of their dark and mysterious homes. *(Aug 21, 2004,Dominion Post,E14)*

### whitebait season
  
noun.  
[NOTES: This is now from Sept. 1 to Nov. 15 inclusive every year.]  
> In the Grey during the whitebait season (September and October) the birds are very numerous ... *(1889,TrNZI,21/215)*  
> During the last whitebait season, Mr. James King, secretary of the Westland Acclimatisation Society, obtained a quantity of whitebait ... *(1903,TrNZI,35/310)*  
> **HULME** Last year, I missed the whitebait season. *(1986,Te Kaihau,38)*  
> The forgotten coast - except during the whitebait season, which is lousy at the moment, and in March, when Hokitika hosts its famous Wild Food Festival. *(Oct 21, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),94)*  
> Although it was the first day of the whitebait season, the conditions in Wairarapa prevented anyone going fishing at Lake Ferry... *(Sep 2001,NZ Geographic,No. 53/8)*  
> Mr W. KELLER begs to inform his friends and supporters that during the Whitebait season a Counter Lunch will be provided FREE OF CHARGE. *(Sep 28, 1866,West Coast Times,3)*  
> Mr C. Sibree, of Westport, during the last whitebait season, experimented on preserving the fish in cans. *(May 10, 1876,Nelson Evening Mail,2)*  
> The Board of Education forwarded a copy of a letter sent by the Rev F. R. Inwood, on behalf of the Burwood School Committee, drawing attention to the bad language used on the public roads near the Burwood School during the whitebait season. *(Sep 27, 1899,Star, The,2)*  
> The Westport whitebait season this year is a long way the best that has been known at Westport for many year [sic]. *(Dec 7, 1921,Grey River Argus,3)*  
> The Westport News says - Roughly, the whitebait season recently closed yielded only about aGBP100 to Westport, as compared with aGBP2000 to aGBP2500 in former seasons. *(Jan 17, 1921,Ohinemuri Gazette,2)*  
> Although the 1939-40 whitebait season in the North Island has been open since July 1 there has been so far a practically complete absence of supplies to the Wellington market. *(Jul 13, 1939,Evening Post,10)*  
> The whitebait season in the Buller district has been more prolonged than for many years, states the "Press." *(Nov 19, 1940,Evening Post,6)*

### whitebait trench
  
noun.  
> **WATSON** ... they would go possum-trapping, ... or maybe even get a whitebait trench down on the West Coast, or do a ringbolt on [16] a ship somewhere *(1965,Stand in the Rain,15)*

### whitebaiter
  
noun. a fisher of whitebait  
> A Westport whitebaiter a few days ago caught a 12 lb trout in his hand net. *(Nov 19, 1898,Grey River Argus,2)*  
> ".. There's just so many whitebaiters coming to the Coast," said Jim Bushby [sic], president of the West Coast Whitebaiters Association. *(Aug 15, 2007,NZ Herald,A2)*  
> **THOMPSON** Watching the whitebaiters at work when the Grey was quiet, their fine lace nets moving like reconnoitring gliders, then quivering up in an agitation of silver. *(1980,All My Lives,33)*  
> WHITEBAITER DIES [heading]... A man had been whitebaiting ... when he collapsed. *(Oct 9, 1982,Press, The,5)*  
> Two whitebaiters have told the police that they thought they saw a body floating down the river on Wednesday afternoon. *(Oct 20, 1986,Evening Post,1)*  
> A quick phone around some of Wellington's fish suppliers revealed that this season's whitebait is likely to sell for around $100 a kilogram excluding GST (some may consider this fair as it must be hard for a whitebaiter to put bait on all those little hooks). *(Aug 20, 2003,Capital Times,18)*  
> A Whitebaiter Found Dead. - An old Maori chief named Henare Kahu was found dead at the fishing grounds near the mouth of the Opihi on Sunday morning, where he was camped "whitebaiting." [sic] *(Dec 12, 1906,Otago Witness,59)*  
> He hasn't got the guts of a whitebaiteror If his brains were gunpowder the blastwouldn't raise his hat. *(Jul 28, 1984,Listener (New Zealand Listener),32)*  
> ...the ambulance was hijacked by a stoned whitebaiter from the Coast while slowing at the Colombo St lights. *(Apr 3, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),46)* [NOTES: Owen Marshall]  
> Whitebaiters have gathered for their annual expeditions to the mouth and, please God, a good full billy. *(Aug 20, 2005,Press, The,A9)*  
> Whitebaiters are up early and stand in patient wait for their tiny delicacy... The only sound is a distant buzzing of an outboard motor on a boat ferrying a whitebaiter to a remote bend that will give him first crack at the incoming fish. *(Sep 10, 2002,Dominion Post,D3)*  
> [Caption] Whitebaiters fish in the Hutt River mouth... The yearly upstream run from the sea is a hit-or-miss affair at the best of times, and increasingly for whitebaiters over the last decade, more hit than miss. *(Nov 24, 2001,Evening Post,1)*  
> Whitebaiters have to work quickly - as soon as a shoal enters, the net is lifted or the fish will quickly swim out again. *(Sep 19, 2007,Viva,38)*  
> A cribbage match between the Fire Brigade and whitebaiters was played at the Fire Brigade Station on Thursday evening. *(Oct 19, 1894,Star, The,28)*  
> Whitebaiters have been out on two or three occasions during the week, but the weather was evidently too rough and cold. *(Sep 19, 1900,Hawera & Normanby Star,4)*  
> Two whitebaiters who went to Wellington to interview the Minister of Marine have reported to their fellows at Kaiapoi that the use of a net with an orifice area of seven square feet will be allowed. *(Aug 27, 1910,Evening Post,9)*  
> Are whitebait fish? adks an exchange.   According to a statement made at the whitebaiters' meeting at Hokitika on Saturday night the railway authorities say, No! *(Jul 15, 1932,Evening Post,6)*  
> For three days in succession whitebaiters at Westport have had exceptionally heavy catches, one party of three netting on Monday and Tuesday more than 60 kerosene tins full, states a Press Association message. *(Oct 26, 1944,Evening Post,9)*  
> The Whitebaiter's ball: not exactly what you'd call a debutantes' ball. Well maybe a sort of blokes debutantes ball. *(2001,Good Bastards: the larrikin's guide to success,240)*  
> At the estuaries of the Hutt River, the Otaki and the Waikanae, trout are a pest to whitebaiters. *(Aug 21, 2004,Dominion Post,E14)*  
> Downstream from the Burn, it was a short journey to the mouth, past whitebaiters' stands built precariously on the riverbank and looking as though at any moment the pressure of swamp and forest behind them would force them into the water. *(Jan 11, 2003,Dominion Post,F18)*  
> **BEARDSLEY** On the far bank you could see the sunflash from time to time on the shimmeringnets of the whitebaiters. *(1984,Blackball 08,43)*

### whitebaiter
  
noun. a Maori activist  
> **McGILL** <i> whitebaiter</i> Maori activist; of academic origin, pun on popular seasonal fry fishers. *(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,122)*

### whitebait-fish
  
verb.  
> **McLAGLAN** Near her was an old girl, also whitebait-fishing, who, in true West Coast style, told the story of her ups and downs. *(1965,Stethoscope & Saddlebags,179)*

### whitebaiting
  
noun. fishing for whitebait, the juvenile forms of five native fish species.  
> Whitebaiting appears to be the chief pastime in town now. Some good hauls have been made. *(Oct 19, 1900,Wanganui Herald,2)*  
> **SLATTER** Mick .. casually talked about whitebaiting on the Coast. *(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,145)*  
> **PEARSON** In a few months .. he was going whitebaiting in one of the rivers of South Westland .. He would make big money there. *(1963,Coal Flat,42)*  
> CHRISTMAS SPORTS .. Eel-catching, white-baiting &c, &c *(Dec 22, 1868,Grey River Argus,3)*  
> Whitebaiting has long been an important tradition in New Zealand, for Maori and Pakeha alike. *(Aug 28, 2008,Dominion Post,B5)*  
> Whitebaiting meant a lull in the normal routine of the farm, when our old "Tin Lizzie" truck was loaded with gear to transport us to the mouth of the Whataroa River. *(Nov 6, 1982,Listener (New Zealand Listener),131)*  
> Dad and I have to go to a reception at Government House tonight for the Duke and Duchess of Kent. It is a real pest as the tide is good for whitebaiting tomorrow and how on earth can we get up at the crack of dawn and go, on top of the party? *(Mar 9, 2000,Evening Post,7)*  
> On the day that I was to be born, a Saturday, my father was tempted to go whitebaiting in one of the not very distant rivers .. and my mother had no reason to detain him. *(Feb 20, 2000,Sunday Star Times,F2)*  
> A diminutive member of the finny tribe sailed out of the milk jug into the tea cup.   Do milk men ever go whitebaiting? *(Sep 27, 1880,Star, The,3)*  
> The accused asked for a remand in order that he might bring witnesses to prove that he had been whitebaiting at Kaiapoi. *(Nov 5, 1894,Star, The,3)*  
> Kahu had been camped at the Opihi during the past few days "whitebaiting," and after a successful day visited Temuka on Saturday evening, disposing of his stock. *(Dec 4, 1906,Ashburton Guardian,3)*  
> Brookes admitted having just bought the liquor, and said that he was going whitebaiting, and wanted the "shandygaff" for refreshment. *(Oct 31, 1911,Marlborough Express,5)*  
> [It's been] the worst [season] I can remember in 40 years of whitebaiting. *(Nov 24, 2001,Evening Post,1)*  
> Mr. Percy Walton, who was whitebaiting near the old engine shed, on the Blaketown side of the river, fell into the water and was drowned. *(Oct 25, 1920,Grey River Argus,5)*  
> While whitebaiting, Mrs. Munro, aged 46, was drowned this morning the the Wairoa River ... *(Aug 19, 1931,Evening Post,8)*  
> The body was found by lads returning from whitebaiting. *(Oct 21, 1940,Evening Post,14)*

noun. [Rural]

> **WILLIAMS** <i>Aroarotea, n. white-breasted shag.</i> [so 1917/20] *(1892,Dictionary of the NZ Language,11)*

### white-capped albatross
  
noun. [Environment]

> .. the annual incidental catch in recent years has been dominated by 5 taxa: white-capped albatross, sooty shearwater, white-chinned petrel, Buller's albatross, and Salvin's albatross. *(Feb 2007,Niwa Update,23/1)*

> **FORSTER** We saw immense flocks of blackbanded Petrels, common & black Shearwaters, ...[ed. fn] White-chinned petrel. Forster calls this species `black petrels or shearwaters'. *(Feb 11, 1773,Resolution Journal of J.R. Forster, The,2/225,1982)*

noun.

This name was derived from the enormous boulders lodged in the creek flats, as large as the rooms of a house, which the miners called whitecombers. *(1975,Eight Daughters,21)*

### white-eye
  
noun.  
[NOTES: aka waxeye, wax-eye, wax eye, silvereye, silver-eye, silver eye, white eye]  
> A notable exception is the silver-eye,white-eye or blight-bird (zosterops)which was blown across the Tasman Sea bya storm some fifty or sixty years ago. *(Jun 18, 1904,Press, The,8)*  
> Zosterops dorsalis. White eye. I did not see this bird, but Mr. Allom informed me that it had been on the island for the last four years. *(1869,TrNZI,1/160)* [NOTES: (Hutton Birds Great Barrier)]  
> **HUTTON** 14.ZOSTEROPS LATERALIS. Lath. White-eye. Wax-eye. Blight-bird. Tauhou. *(1971,Birds of New Zealand,6)*  
> ZOSTEROPS LATERALIS, Lath. - White-eye, Blight Bird. *(1886,TrNZI,18/125)* [NOTES: (Hamilton)]  
> **BULLER** By the settlers it [sc <i>Zosterops</i>] has been variously designated as Ring-eye, Wax-eye, White-eye, or Silver-eye, in allusion to the beautiful circlet of satiny-white feathers which surrounds the eyes; and quite as commonly as the `Blight-bird', or `Winter Migrant'. *(1888,Birds of New Zealand,1/82)*  
> **MORRIS** White-eye, n. <i>another name for the bird called variously Silver-Eye, Wax-Eye, Blight-Bird,</i> etc., <i>Zosterops</i> (q.v.). *(1898,Austral English,510)*  
> The case of the white-eye (<i>Zosterops caerulescens</i>) ... is rather different. *(1901,TrNZI,33/254)*  
> The little green white-eyes, or blight birds, are moving from place to place ... *(Oct 1904,NZ Illustrated Magazine,43)*  
> **CHEESEMAN** The fruits [of <i>Pisonia Brunoniana</i>, Parapara] are so excessively viscid that small birds, such as the white-eye (<i>Zosterops</i>) ..., are often caught and glued down by the feathers, and fail to free themselves. *(1906,Manual NZ Flora,575)*  
> **40/489** Coming to our little white-eye, or tauhou, `the stranger', who came from Australia in 1856 ... is now as rare as he was common. *(1908,TrNZI)*  
> **STUDHOLME** The White-eye has a pretty little song, ... *(1940,Te Waimate,230,1954)*  
> **RICHARDS** <i> Zosterops (ring eye) cerulescens</i> ... The white-eye feeds on insects and fruit besides honey. ... [74] White-eye, Blight Bird. POTEREKAI. *(1952,Chatham Islands,73)*  
> Christmas Island also has a number of land birds indigenous to the island, one of them the little white-eye (<i>Zosterops natalis</i>) being pleasantly familiar as it is identical in appearance and habits with the New Zealand white-eye. *(Apr 1952,NZ Geographer,25)* [NOTES: (Ellis `Christmas Island')]  
> <i>white-eye</i> See <i>blight-bird</i>.) [13th edition expands to: One of the New Zealand names for a small bird. It is an Australian bird but established itself in New Zealand about a hundred years ago. (see<i> blight-bird.</i>)] *(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),443)*  
> **FALLA et al.** SILVEREYE <i>Zosterops lateralis Other names</i>: Tauhou, Waxeye, White-eye, Blightbird, Blightie. *(1966,Birds of New Zealand,223)*  
> **NATUSCH** Family ZOSTEROPIDAE (silvereyes). <i>Zosterops lateralis</i> ... , called ring-eye in the south, wax-eye, white-eye, silvereye, blightie, etc., elsewhere, is probably in actual fact a blown-away Australian. Not seen here before 1886, it is certainly plentiful ... *(1967,Animals of NZ, The,282)*  
> <i>Silvereye Zosterops lateralis lateralis </i> (LATHAM, 1801) ... OTHER NAMES: <i>White-eye, waxeye, blightbird, tauhou. </i> *(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,288)*  
> <i>white-eye NZ = silver-eye. </i> *(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,880)* [NOTES: [cf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict. 1345; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 546]]  
> Throughout the winter months in most parts of New Zealand White-eyes are found in flocks of thirty to a hundred birds. *(1944,Korero (AEWS Background Bulletin),2(22) : 20)*  
> It is called the "White eye" or Tyasterop dorsalis. *(Jul 6, 1867,Taranaki Herald,3)*  
> Whiteheads, rifleman wren, and white-eye were also very numerous. *(Apr 12, 1929,Evening Post,8)*  
> They must be flocks of wax-eyes, or white-eyes, making for somewhere in the darkness. *(Jun 8, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,15)*  
> We have a flock of lively tauhou, the white-eye or silver-eye..... pegging away at the scraps in tins hung on a tree for them. *(Jul 1937,NZ Railways Magazine,25)*  
> The hammock style of nest made by the white-eye.. is found fairly often.. in  manuka. *(Mar 4, 1924,Otago Witness,7)*

### white-faced heron
  
noun. Ardea novaehollandiae novaehollandiae ,  occas. also called blue heron and (poss. erron. for white-faced) white-fronted heron, A grey-blue heron with a white face and throat. Self-introduced (?c. date) from Australia, now the commonest heron in NZ.  
[NOTES: also called matuku(-moana)]  
> <i> Ardea novae-hollandiae </i>, Lath. White-fronted heron, Matuku. It is far from common in this district. *(1885,NZ Journal of Science,557)* [NOTES: (T.H. Potts)]  
> 90. <i> Ardea novaehollandiae [novaehollandiae] </i>(Latham, 1790)  <i> White-faced Heron *(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,34,1980)*  
> **SANSOM** On the estuary white-faced herons feed with an occasional visit ...of the kotuku or white herons, distinguished and welcome visitors *(1970,Stewart Islanders,143)*  
> **WILLIAMS** Matuku, n. ... 3. Notophoyx novaehollandiae, white-faced  heron. Called also <i> matuku-moana. </i> *(1971,Dictionary of the Maori Language,195)*  
> **SANSOM** White-faced herons, the river's [Heron River] namesakes, and the occasional white egret, ... sit on the out-thrust snags to become double-herons, their reflections perfect. *(1982,In the Grip of an Island,139)*  
> **McGILL** Present local authority plans ... could destroy the breeding grounds for ... the rarer bittern, godwit, white-faced heron, shag, ... and greenshank. *(1985,G'day Country,77)*  
> </i> White-faced Heron Ardea novaehollandiae  novaehollandiae <i> LATHAM, 1790 ... OTHER NAME:  </i> Blue crane. ... Throughout New Zealand in wetland  habitats; ... Still expanding north and east from Australia. <i> *(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,129)*  
> **KINSKY & ROBERTSON** </i> White-faced Heron Ardea novaehollandiae <i> Family: ARDEIDAE  Native: Self-introduced from Australia, rare till 1940s. ... - most common heron, widespread throughout New Zealand. *(1987,Handbook NZ Birds,23)*  
> The white-faced heron is another self-introduced bird which has taken a liking to wet places in town and country. *(May 2002,Forest & Bird,304/27)*  
> **Moon** White-faced herons are now by far the commonest of the heron species. *(2002,Birds of New Zealand,80)*  
> The white-faced heron is another self-introduced bird which has taken a liking to wet places in town and country. *(May 2002,Forest & Bird,No. 304/27)*

### white-faced storm petrel
  
noun. <i>Pelagodroma marina</i>, the "Jesus bird" or "J.C. bird"  
> <i>69. Pelagodroma marina maoriana </i>Mathews, 1912 <i>White-faced Storm Petrel </i> *(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,29,1980)*  
> **FALLA et al.** WHITE-FACED STORM PETREL <i>Pelagodroma marina Other names</i>: Takahikare-moana, Skipjack, Jesus Christ Bird (shortened to `J.C. Bird'). *(1966,Birds of New Zealand,55)*  
> **SALE** The white-faced storm petrel ... is the most common of its kind around the New Zealand shores. *(1982,Four Plays,37)*  
> Essentially a bird of the open ocean, the New Zealand white-faced storm petrel is sometimes seen in coastal waters during the breeding season, from August to March. ...<i> New Zealand White-faced Storm Petrel P[elagodroma] m[arina] maoriana </i>MATHEWS, 1912. *(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,104)*  
> Several species of petrel can be found here [on Rangatira Island] - one rare and others in large numbers, such as the noisy sooty shearwater, the fairy-like white-faced storm petrel and the clownesque broad-billed prion. *(Aug 2001,NZ Geographic,No. 52/86)*  
> In summer, once a breeze gets up, the common white-faced storm petrel <i>Pelagodroma marina</i> can often been seen pattering acorss the surface of the outer gulf with wings held up - a practice that has earned storm petrels the irreverent title of "birds" or "J.C. Birds" from seafarers. *(Jul 2004,NZ Geographic,No. 68/11)*

noun.

### whitefish
  
noun.  
> **SORENSON** <i>MONKFISH </i>(a) Scientific name: (i) <i>Geniagnus monopterygius</i> (ii)<i> Kathetostoma giganteum.</i> (b) Suggested commercial name[s]: (both) Whitefish. (c) Other common names: (both) Monkfish, Monks, Stargazer, Flathead (also applied at times to export gurnard); Churchills, Toebiters, Bulldogs, Spotted Stargazer, Maori Chief (Dunedin); Muddies. (d) Principal localities where taken: Napier, Cook Strait, Nelson, Banks Peninsula, Bluff-Stewart Island. ... Monkfish is a general term for species of the Families Uranoscopidae and Leptoscopidiae. *(1970,Nomenclature of NZ Fish,37)*  
> **WILSON** WHITEFISH (<i>Coregonus albus</i>). A species known in UK as `Freshwater Herring' which was introduced to N.Z. by the Government in 1900. Millions of ova were brought to New Zealand but attempts to establish the species were a complete failure. None is known to exist today. *(1981,Fisherman's Bible)*  
> [species name] stargazer .. [scientific name] <i>Kathetostoma giganteum</i> .. [other name(s)] monkfish, boofs, bulldogs, churchills, flathead monk, giant stargazer, monks, muddies, spotted stargazer, toebiters, whitefish, sandcod *(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters)*  
> **PAUL et al.** [index to common names 283-293] whitefish 1822 *(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography)*

### white-flippered penguin
  
noun. [Environment] a subspecies of the little blue penguin, found on Banks Peninsular and Motunau Island  
See: little blue penguin  
[NOTES: <i>Eudyptula minor albosignata</i> also silver penguin]  
> The white-flippered penguin is a subspecies of the little blue penguin. *(Nov 2003,NZ Geographic,No. 66/88)*  
> A penguin belonging to the smallest class, and said to be known onlly [sic] from Banks Peninsular, [sic] is the White-flippered Penguin, which is similar in size, shape, and plumage to the Little Blue Penguin, the only difference being that the wings are widely bordered with white, with a more or less distinct white patch near the middle of the rear margin. *(Apr 14, 1920,Grey River Argus,6)*  
> <i>Eudyptula albosignata</i> Finsch. Silver Penguin; White-flippered Penguin. Oliver found this species breeding in fair numbers at a South Island locality in 1907. *(1923,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,6/74)* [NOTES: Myers Endemic Birds]  
> Canterbury has its own distinctive penguin, the white-flippered penguin, which is a local variant of the Little [sic] blue or fairy penguin found only in New Zealand and Australia. It is slightly heavier than the northern Little blue penguin, and has a distinctive white band along both edges of the flippers. They breed only on Banks Peninsula, Motunau Island and the neighbouring coasts. *(Aug 24, 1988,Press, The,21)*  
> The endangered white-flippered penguin was there hundreds of years before he was a twinkle in his dad's eye and thus has more historical right to breed at Boulder Bay than the Jamieson family. *(Feb 16, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),9)*

### whitefooted Auckland Island paua
  
noun.  
> Talbot Murray was to study and collect specimens of the small, whitefooted Auckland Island paua... It was possible through careful selective interbreeding of the blackfooted with the whitefooted sub-Antarctic paua, to give a fast-growing, large, white-footed shellfish more widely acceptable in the Orient. *(Aug 13, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),53)*

### white-fronted tern
  
noun. [Environment] a common coastal tern, Sterna striata, with a long black crown and white underparts (also called black cap, grenadier, kahawai bird, tara).  
> I recently watched.. a flock of white-fronted terns on the Kartigi Beach in Otago. *(Nov 16, 1938,Auckland Weekly News,60)*  
> He appends the following list of birds to be found on the Little Barrier in support of his statement:-  Stitch-bird, parson-bird, bell-bird .. white-fronted tern, rainbird, shear-water, sombre do., Cook's petrel ... *(Nov 16, 1892,Colonist (Nelson),3)*  
> They were in full breeding plumage, and were associating with a large breeding colony of white-fronted terns. *(Oct 22, 1927,Evening Post,17)*  
> **BULLER** STERNA FRONTALIS. (WHITE-FRONTED TERN.) ... <i>Native name.</i>- Tara; `Sea-swallow' of the Colonists. *(1873,Birds of New Zealand,281,1888 2nd edn 2/68)*  
> **POWELL** WHITE FRONTED TERN (<i>Sterna striata</i>), Tara of the Maoris, is abundant all around the New Zealand coasts and occurs in Tasmania and Eastern Australia as well. This is the graceful little `Kahawai-bird' or `Sea-swallow,' which pursues schools of small surface fish, wheeling, darting and dipping to the water, in its energetic quest of food. Since the Kahawai fish is predaceous on the small school fish also, the tern is an excellent indicator of the presence of Kahawai. *(1947,Native Animals of NZ,80)*  
> **DEMPSEY** ... a pair of black oystercatchers and a small colony of white-fronted terns. *(1964,Little World of Stewart island,118)*  
> **McLINTOCK** Tern, white-fronted .. .. tara .. ..<i>Sterna striata </i> *(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712)*  
> **FALLA et al.** WHITE-FRONTED TERN <i>Sterna striata Other names</i>: Sea-swallow, Kahawai Bird, Tara, Blackcap, Grenadier, Swallowtail, Noddy (Chathams), Tikkitak (Stewart Is.) *(1966,Birds of New Zealand,164)*

(WHITE-HEADED STILT.) ...<i> Native name</i>.- Tutumata {and Tuturipourewa}; Torea (of Arawa tribe). ...The White-headed Stilt, which appears to be also widely distributed over ... ### Whitestone City
  
noun. Oamaru

> Views of Oamaru, the "white-stone city," with its splendid architecture and beautiful public gardens, and a panoramic view of Greymouth, show New Zealand towns of very different features. *(Sep 3, 1901,Star, The,2)*

### whitetail
  
noun. short for white-tailed spider  
> Experts say last winter's lack of rain has led to more of the white-tailed spiders scuttling around homes in the greater Wellington region this summer... Newlands resident Valerie Burney has caught four white-tails in her house over summer... Dunedin's National Poison Centre has received more calls from Wellington region residents seeking advice after being bitten by white-tails. *(Apr 3, 2000,Evening Post,1)*  
> ...spider expert Phil Sirvid said the trans-Tasman stowaway had been living here for more than 100 years. But people had only become aware of white-tails in recent times, their fear of its venom fuelled by horror stories of bites turning flesh into blackened and rotting tissue, salvageable only by surgery or amputation. *(Oct 23, 2001,Dominion,10)*  
> If a person reacts to the bite, they will have the symptoms for good... This feature of the whitetail bite is said to be its worst. *(May 12, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),12)* [NOTES: Originally from Christchurch Mail, 22/03/01]

### white-throated shag
  
noun. [Environment] little (pied) shag, a smallish variety, found throughout New Zealand  
> **HAAST** Of birds still belonging to the <i>Avifauna</i> of New Zealand, the following species were represented :- <i>Graculus punctatus</i>, spotted shag; <i>Eudyptula undina</i>, small blue penguin; <i>Anas superciliosa</i>, grey duck; <i>Graculus carbo</i>, black shag; <i>Graculus varius</i>, pied shag; <i>Graculus brevirostris</i>, white throated shag; <i>Ossifraga gigantea,</i> nelly; <i>Apteryx australis</i>, large kiwi, <i>Nestor meridionalis,</i> kaka; <i>Stringops habroptilus,</i> kakapo, and some other undetermined species. *(1879,Geology of the Provinces of Canterbury & Westland,417)*  
> There are thirteen species [of shag] in New Zealand <i>Phalacrocorax novae-hollandiae</i> (Black shag), <i>P. melanoleucus</i> (Frilled Shag), <i>P. brevirostris</i> (White-throated Shag), <i>P. varius</i> Pied shag, and <i>P. punctatus</i> (Spotted Shag), which I have often found in the inland bays, rivers, and lakes. *(1886,TrNZI,18/100)* [NOTES: (Reischek)]  
> **FALLA et al.** LITTLE SHAG <i>Phalacrocorax melanoleucos brevirostris Maori name</i>: Kawaupaka. The name White-throated Shag is also in general use for the most abundant phase of a rather variable species which, at its other extreme, is identical with the Little Pied Shag of Australia. However, as intermediates occur, and the Little Pied form is relatively uncommon, it seems more convenient to treat the New Zealand population as a variable one under a distinctive name. *(1966,Birds of New Zealand,67)*  
> **HUTTON** 152. GRACULUS BREVIROSTRIS. Gould. White-throated Shag. *(1871,Birds of New Zealand,51)*  
> **BULLER** PHALCROCORAX BREVIROSTRIS. (WHITE-THROATED SHAG.) ... <i>Native name</i>.- Kawau-paka. ... The White-throated Shag, which appears to be confined to New Zealand and the Chatham Islands, frequents the freshwater rivers and lagoons in all parts of the country. *(1873,Birds of New Zealand,330,1888 2nd edn 2/168)*  
> WHITE-THROATED SHAG (<i>Microcarbo melanaleucos</i>). *(1925,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,8/208)*  
> **NATUSCH** The white-throated [shag] <i>P[halacrocorax] melanoleucos brevirostris</i> ... sometimes called little pied, nests in the same time way as the pied, all round our shores. Increasingly common from Auckland southward to Otago. *(1967,Animals of NZ, The,260)*  
> <i>Little Pied Cormorant Phalacrocorax melanoleucos </i>(VIEILLOT, 1817) ... OTHER NAMES: <i>Kawaupaka, white-throated shag, frilled shag, little river shag, little cormorant, little shag. </i> *(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,117)*  
> PHALACROCORAX BREVIROSTRIS, <i>Gould.</i> - White-throated Shag. *(1886,TrNZI,18/128)* [NOTES: (Hamilton)]  
> <i>Phalacrocorax brevirostris</i>, Gould. (The White-throated Shag.) *(1892,TrNZI,24/84)* [NOTES: (Buller)]

> **COWAN** Here and there a little watercourse ... arboured over by a matted roofing of <i> kotukutuku </i> , ... and <i> mahoe </i>  or whitewood and glossy-green <i> karaka. </i> *(1926,Travel in NZ,(Vol II South Island) 32)*  
> **COWAN** Here and there a little watercourse ... arboured over by a matted roofing of <i> kotukutuku </i> , ... and <i> mahoe </i>  or whitewood and glossy-green <i> karaka. </i> *(1926,Travel in NZ,(Vol II South Island) 32)*

noun. <i>Melycitus ramiflorus</i>, mahoe  
[NOTES: a1926 W. Best (Otaki) vernac. (And.) Juniper descended, we would-be Nimrods tramped homeward ... emptying our rifles into a whitey-wood stump. [a1927] Ver. = Vernacular names heard by J.C. Andersen from sawyers, settlers, etc. (And) (Stony Bay) - ca1935 Marlborough (HWO) <i>Whiteywood</i> was the usual term, though <i>mahoe</i> was known and used as a boat-name. 	dhurley		8/7/2003	0	1	0]

> **LAING & BLACKWELL** Though so much smaller than many of the forest trees, the mahoe is a noticeable object in the bush, as its straight, thin trunks are often covered with a white fungoid growth, which is like a coating of whitewash. *(1940,Plants of NZ,279)*  
> **ANDERSEN** <i>ma[a]hoe</i>, a tree (Melicytus ramiflorus), whitey-wood: hinahina in many parts of Newzealand. *(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/144)* [NOTES: Maori Words in English]  
> In the old days fungus was plentiful, thriving on the dead trunks of Whitey Wood (Mahoe) trees. *(1940,Tales of Pioneer Women,91,1988)*  
> **STUDHOLME** In this bush were ... Ohau, (panax or five-finger), ini-ini (whitey wood); konini, white and red matipo; koromiko and other shrubs. *(1940,Te Waimate,246,1954)*  
> **LAING & BLACKWELL** Another common European name for [Mahoe] is <i>Whitey-wood.</i> *(1940,Plants of NZ,280)*  
> **RICHARDS** <i>Melicytus ... ramiflorus</i> ... Whitey-wood, INA-INA (pale). *(1952,Chatham Islands,64)*  
> **CAMPBELL** MAHOE (Whitey wood) <i>Melicytus ramiflorus/ </i> *(1955,By Reef and Range,23)*  
> **PEARSON** <i>Whiteywood</i>: The <i>maahoe</i>, a small tree. *(1963,Coal Flat,421)*  
> **McLINTOCK** Whiteywood .. .. mahoe .. .. <i>Melicytus ramiflorus </i> *(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712)*  
> **WOOLLASTON** Two were whiteywoods ... if I had known their Maori name ... <i>mahoe ... </I? *(1980,Sage Tea,43)*  
> <i>whitey-wood n. NZ</i> = MAHOE. *(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,880)* [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]  
> Cabbage trees, rata, mahoe (whiteywood), makomako (wineberry), karamu, kowhai and grasses, are among the species being added to the hillside... *(Oct 2003,Footnotes,No. 20/7)*

### whiti
  
noun. [Maori] a species of earthworm  
> **BURTON** [Earthworms] were apparently steamed in bowls of hot water for  several hours ... Two varieties, known to the Maori as <i>kurekure</i> and <i>whiti</i>, were reserved for the chiefs or as food for the dying. *(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,10)*

### whitie
  
noun. a kind of marble  
> **KEITH & MAIN** Marbles were the currency of childhood and there were an astonishing number of names for them, some referring to the material or colour, others to value or function in the game. There were agates, aggies, American alleys, blood alleys, bomb-squashers, bonies, bonsers, bottlies, bull's eyes, bum-squashers, cats-eye, [sic] chinkies, connies, duckies, eggies, glassies, Greeks, milkies, mollies, peelies, peewees, pisswees, pretties, Scotties, smokies, stinkies, stonies, stripies, tom-bobblers and whities. The march of technology added ball bearings - ballies or steelies - which were unbeatable when it came to smashing an opponent's miserable glassie. World War II[roman] brought `clayeys' often imperfectly spherical they were pathetic terracotta substitutes for real marbles. *(1984,NZ Yesterdays,288)*

### whiting
  
noun.  
> **YATE** Those saltwater fish] most plentiful, and of greatest note, are, soles, mackarel, cod-fish, a species of salmon, whiting, snapper, mullet, bream, skate, gurnards, and a few smaller kinds, some not near so large as a sprat; with an abundance of crayfish, oysters, shrimps, prawns, muscles, and cockles. *(1835,New Zealand (various authors/titles),71)*  
> **HODGSKIN** Gurnets are plentiful everywhere, ... Whiting, bream, and snappers are abundant, with several kinds of skate. *(1841,Narrative of Eight Months Sojourn in NZ, A,34)*  
> **DRURY** FISH [MET WITH IN PELORUS]. Whiting (or resembling the European Whiting). Kahawi. Flat fish - Patiki, plentiful ... *(1855,Sailing Directions,68)*  
> **MORRIS** <i>Whiting, n. </i>Four species of the fish of the genus <i>Sillago </i>are called <i>Whiting</i> in Australia (see quotation). The <i>New Zealand Whiting </i>is <i>Pseudophycis breviusculus</i>, Richards., and the <i>Rock-Whiting</i> of New South Wales is <i>Odax semifaciatus</i>, Cuv. and Val., and <i>O. richardsonii</i>, Gunth; called also <i>Stranger</i> (q.v.). *(1898,Austral English,511)*  
> **PAUL et al.** [index to common names 283-293] whiting 833, 847-59 *(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography)*

### whiting
  
noun.  
> <i>Merluccius Gayi </i>... Hake, Whiting *(1911,Records of the Canterbury Museum,1/175)* [NOTES: Waite]  
> Whiting. <i>Merluccius gayi ... </i> *(1918,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,1/270)* [NOTES: (Phillipps)]  
> MERLUCCIUS GAYI (Guichenot). <i>Whiting.</i> Sold as `haddock' in Dunedin, but not common. Odd examples have been taken as far north as Hawke Bay. Distribution: Unknown north of East Cape. *(Jun 1921,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,IV/120)* [NOTES: Phillipps Notes on the Edible Fishes]  
> **SPEIGHT** The latter is caught plentifully by line and trawl, but is not highly regarded as a food fish, while its close relative the Hake or Whiting <i>(Merluccius gayi)</i>, is much less common but is not more eagerly sought after on that account. *(1927,Natural History of Canterbury,200)* [NOTES: 1927 Archey `Native Fishes of Canterbury' in Speight et al. Natural History of Canterbury 200]  
> **PHILLIPPS** <i>Merluccius gayi</i> (Guichenot). WHITING. *(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,23,1971)*  
> <i>Merluccius gayi</i> (Guichenot). Hake (whiting). ... Vernacular: The name whiting, though more properly reserved for <i>Gadus</i>, is firmly rooted in Otago and Canterbury. <i>Merluccius</i> in England and America is called hake. *(1938,TrRSNZ,68/404)* [NOTES: Graham Fishes of Otago Harbour]  
> <i>Merluccius gayi:</i> The `whiting' of Wellington fishmongers, called English hake by fishermen. *(1936,Handbook for New Zealand, The,71)*  
> **DOOGUE & MORELAND** ENGLISH HAKE ... <i>Other names: Merluccius australis</i>; whiting. This is a true hake, very similar to the North Sea species. *(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,209)*  
> **MORELAND** ENGLISH HAKE [<i> Merluccius australis </i> ] ... An alternative name is whiting. *(1967,Marine Fishes,52)*  
> **SORENSON** <i> HAKE </i> (a) Scientific name: <i> Merluccius australis </i> . (b) Suggested commercial name[s]: Hake. (c) Other common names: Whiting; English Hake. (d) Principal localities where taken: Taken only rarely nowdays off Wellington in deep water; ... Whatever the position was years ago it is a fact that this fish is rare today. The position is further complicated by the almost indiscriminate use of the name hake, by fishermen, [for] other species of fish. ... The name Hake shold not be applied to either Southern kingfish or the Whiptail although there can be little, if any, objection to the commercial name of Blue Hake for the latter species. *(1970,Nomenclature of NZ Fish,28)*

### whiting
  
noun.  
> Hoki - alias whiting or whiptail - is ideally suited to the catering and restaurant trade. *(May 1983,NZ Clubman,24)*  
> Hoki - alias whiting or whiptail - is ideally suited to the catering and restaurant trade. *(May 1983,NZ Clubman,24)*  
> **McL;INTOCK** Whiting, hake .. .. kehe .. .. <i> Merluccius australis </i> *(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712)*  
> **PAUL** <i> Hoki Macruronus novaezelandiae </i>  ... Alternative names include blue hake, whiting (wrongly), and whiptail. Also occurs off southern Australia ... *(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,61)*

### whiting
  
noun.  
> [species name] whiting .. [scientific name] -- [other name(s)] usually gemfish [Rexea solandri] but may be other species particularly small hake *(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters)*

### whiting
  
noun.  
> The Whiting, <i> Pseudophycis breviusculus </i> , is got occasionally as is also the Haddock, <i> Gadus australis. </i> *(1879,TrNZI,11/381)* [NOTES: (P. Thomson, Otago)]  
> <i> Pseudophycis breviusculus </i> , Richardson. Occasionally taken, and locally known as `whiting'. *(1906,TrNZI,38/551)* [NOTES: (Thomson, Otago)]

### whiting
  
noun.  
> **SHERRIN** WHITING (<i> Pseudophycis breviusculus </i> ). Another fish of the cod kind, which has a deeper body than the <i> Lotella rhacinus </i> , and more delicate flesh, resembling that of the whiting. It is abundant on the west coast of Otago, where specimens are caught with the hook, in 15 fathoms water, at the entrance to Dusky Bay, weighing about 5lb. *(1886,Handbook Fishes of NZ,17)*  
> **PARROTT** Hake <i>Merlangius australis</i> Whiting; Haddock *(1957,Sea Anglers' Fishes of NZ,169)*  
> **GRAHAM** Whiting <i> Merlangius australis </i>  (Hutton) *(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,403)* [NOTES: 1955 Whitley Namelist in 1956 Graham NZ Fishes 403]  
> **GRAHAM** HAKE or WHITING <i> Merlangius australis </i> (Hutton) Hake is [<i> ibid.</i>  166] known in Christchurch, and by some in Otago, as Whiting, but it is not a true Whiting. In Napier, Wellington and Marlborough the Southern Kingfish (<i> Rexea solandr </i> i) is caught and sold as Hake. *(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,164,2nd)*

### whiting
  
noun.  
> **WILSON** SOUTHERN BLUE WHITING <i> Micromesistius australia </i> . A commercially important inhabitant of our southern seas which generally measures between 30 and 50 cm. *(1981,Fisherman's Bible,201)*  
> **AYLING** <i> SOUTHERN BLUE WHITING Micromesistius australis </i> ... The southern blue whiting is very similar to the blue whiting from the North Atlantic and grows to a similar size. *(1982,Collins Guide,149,1984)*  
> **PAUL** <i> Southern blue whiting Micromesistius australis </i>  ... A Southern Hemisphere species, known from south-eastern New Zealand and southern South America ... *(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,56)*  
> [species name] southern blue whiting .. [scientific name] <i> Micromesistius australis ..</i>  [other name(s)] southern poutassou *(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters)*  
> **E.J. WAKEFIELD**  *(Apr 16, 1840,Diary)*

### whizz waggon
  
noun. [informal] a motor car  
> ... some miscreant had tampered with the doctor's whizz waggon. *(Nov 11, 1916,Hutt Valley Independent,4)*

### whizziness
  
noun.  
> But there's a whole generation of management and academic disciples of public sector whizziness out there who urgently need to be sent on a course somewhere to have it all knocked out of them. *(Aug 7, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),13)*

### who goes round my stone wall
  
phrase.  
> **SUTTON-SMITH** There are games recorded in this period for which there is no record before 1900 though they may well have existed under other names. <i>West Coast:</i> Dunk and Davey, Spang Weazling; <i>Wellington</i>: Who Goes Round My Stone Wall, Beg o' My Neighbor, Bellahonie, Run a Mile, On and Off the Bridge, Here Come Two Nuns, Colours, Isaac and Rebecca. Scrag, Diabolo, Bumble Puppy, Homai, Fives; <i>Auckland:</i> My Fair Young Lady, Rushes and Reeds, Surrender the Tower, Walls, Ball Hopscotch, ball games, Solomon Says; <i>Hawkes Bay:</i> Wolf Wolf Go Home, Homai; Canterbury: Poor Old Tom, Rats and Rabbits and Rips, Steps, Skin the Snake, Steal a Peg, Toodle-em-Buck, The Gag; <i>Golden Bay</i>: Who's That Walking Round My House Tonight, I've Come to See Poor Mary Jane, Steps and Stairs, Creeping, Branding, O'Leary; <i>Otago:</i> Sir Roger de Coverley, Who's That Knocking at My Door. *(1981,History of Children's Play,231)*

### whodackie
  
noun. thing, thingummy  
See: hoodackie  
> **COWLEY** I never seen half those whodackies in the shops. *(1979,Growing Season, The,188)*  
> Harder to read is the intensity scale on the Richter whodackey, because those affected, and the publishing community generally, seem divided on both cause and effect. *(Apr 27, 1992,Listener (New Zealand Listener),55)*

### whole box and dice, the
  
phrase. everything; `the whole works'  
See: cf. whole shooting-box, the  
[NOTES: check SND; ca1940 Havelock; cf. also the whole kit and cabood; ca1946 R. Mason, who suggests it may arise from shopkeeper's idiom, one out of the box in contrast with one on the shelf, shop-soiled: cf (one) out of the drawer]  
> **CRUMP** He must have used about ten plugs [of gelinite]. Blew the whole box and dice to bits. *(1962,One of Us,169)*  
> **McCALLUM** Put the whitebait into the pan ... Heave the whole box and dice onto the brown paper, add pepper and salt ... and serve. *(1963,Half-Gallon Jar,52)*  
> **SYDER & HODGETTS** Everything in that connection, every person in that group. Often derogatory. `They are all scroungers, the whole box and dice of them.' *(1974,Australian & NZ English [TS],1098)*  
> **McGILL** <i>box</i>, in phr.<i> the whole box and dice</i> everything; c.1930. ANZ. <i>- in a box</i> confused state.<i> [Baker] </i> *(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,20)*

### whole pitch and toss and caboodle, the
  
phrase. variation on the whole (kit and) caboodle  
> **HENDERSON** How does the whole entire pitch and toss and caboodle of education fling such a distant person into the land of logs? *(1983,Down from Marble Mountain,103)*

### whole quid, the
  
phrase.  
> **MANTELL** `Yes, Kylie. Y'know, woman's angle. Does she think it's the whole quid or a put on?' `She says real - ...' *(1980,Murder or Three, A,41)*

### whole shooting-box, the
  
phrase. the whole box of tricks, the works; a NZ variant of <i>the whole shoot</i>(1884) or WWI 1915 <i>the whole shooting match</i>  
[NOTES: ca1941 St Patrick's College, Silverstream 1952 June 7 R. Mason letter (Wellington) -->The whole shooting box: used 1944 Canterbury]

### wholesale licence
  
noun. a liquor licence permitting the sale of alcoholic liquor to any person in quantities of not less than 2 gallons (to 1967) or 8 litres (1967-89) for off-premises consumption  
[NOTES: Jim writes: Now subsumed under off-licence. Thsi special meaning of <i>wholesale</i> is not recorded in any dictionary I have seen and I am not aware of any extra-NZ currency. Most "wholesalers" were principally - or exclusively - "retailers".]

### wholesaler
  
noun.  
See: wholesale licence

### whooer
  
noun. variant of hooer q.v.  
> **IRELAND** He yelled at the dogs to 'Get away back, you mongrel whooers' *(1998,Under the Bridge & Over the Moon,91)*

### whoop
  
verb. often constr with ALONG, to speed, to go fast also constr. with other preps <i>away, in, out</i>, etc., and with<i> it,</i> in senses of depart or arrive hurriedly (cf HOOP) P: <i>hoop it</i> 2. to run away: obs 1900: suggests a derivation from <i>hop it;</i> ADD <i>whoopityscoot, lickety-split </i>  
[NOTES: [??TRANSFER TO HOOP: ?f. hop it (as P); or f. whoop it up `enliven' a a party; or ?bowl one's hoop] ca1938 popular among Marlborough boys]  
> **COOZE** A car whooped along in a cloud of dust. <i>Ibid.</i>62  A bus which whoops along ... *(1944,Kiwis in the Pacific,29)*  
> Over 3,000 people paid for admission, and the Waipawa exhibtion  [sic] is whooping along as merrily as a circum run on the deadhead system. *(Feb 1, 1888,Observer (Auckland),9)*  
> If somebody sent aGBP1000 to Miss Statham to whoop along those funds we'd be awfully surprised - but glad. *(Apr 15, 1916,Observer (Auckland),16)*

### whooptee bang
  
phrase.  
> **McGILL** <i>whooptee bang/whooptybang </i> making whoopee or merrymaking; common in Northland. [Jim Henderson] *(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,123)*

### whopcacker
  
noun. anything excellent of its kind  
See: also wopcacker, woopcacker  
> **TURNER** </i>Yeah, he's a woopcacker all right.) A hard shot only needs to be a little unusual to be a woopcacker, though <i>woopcacker </i>seems to apply to things as well as people. The word illustrates the lack of standardization in these seldom written words: I would have spelt it <i>wopcacker</i>. Baker records the word as whopcacker but gives a slightly variant meaning, as `very good', `bonzer', `snitcher', `rumptydooler', and the many synonyms of these words. *(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,121)* [NOTES: (quoting 1959 Gordon Slatter <i>A Gun in My Hand:]  
> **McGILL** <i>whopcracker </i>extravagantly approved person or thing; extension probably of `whop' or `wop', to excel; eg `Couldn't have come at a better time. Thanks, mate. You're a whopcracker!' NZA. *(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,123)*  
> **Baker** Of children's terms.. we may note snitcher, snitch, whopcacker, bottler and snozzler, descriptive of something superlative or excellent, both as nouns and adjectives. *(1941,NZ Slang,51)*

### Whopper Chopper
  
noun. annual Wellington seaside event  
> [Hdg] I've got a whopper chopper... While the ever-growing Cuba Street Carnival is taking a large proportion of his time [Chris Morley-Hall] continues to organise the Whopper Chopper Extravaganzas, that he started with local cafe owner Matt Wilson, as well as running performance agency Gondwana Productions. *(Feb 6, 2002,Capital Times,5)*  
> The other crazy competitions are designed to appeal to a kid's sense of the fantastical and there are some things at Whopper Chopper that <i>only</i> kids can do... *(Feb 13, 2002,Capital Times,16)*  
> Organiser Chris Morely-Hall is hoping to continue the modern tradition of live music on Wellington beaches with the WhopperChopper sound system - DJspowered bicycles. *(Dec 29, 2004,Capital Times,p3)*  
> Wellington's Whopper Chopper could soon be a nationwide event. The seaside extravaganzas have been running for the past three years in Wellington... *(Aug 4, 2002,Capital Times,3)*  
> These days the Whopper Chopper seaside extravaganza is billed as the "greatest show on earth"... *(Jan 15, 2004,Wellington Contact,16)*  
> Whopper Chopper is coming home to Frank Kitts park this summer... "El Capitan", (organiser and co-founder) Chris Morley-Hall first launched the free extravaganza in Wellington in 2000 as a celebration of its beautiful coastline and creative talents. *(Jan 14, 2004,Capital Times,12)*  
> This is a fabulous smash of a ballet that combines all ages and tastes into a truly Aotearoa whopper-chopper. *(Feb 4, 2003,Dominion Post,B7)*  
> The idea for Whopper Chopper, now in its fourth year, came through Morley-Hall's life experiences... "We have a strong core of people who come back each year and know exactly what Whopper Chopper is all about," he says. *(Jan 15, 2002,Capital Times,11)*

phrase.  
> **SUTTON-SMITH** There are games recorded in this period for which there is no record before 1900 though they may well have existed under other names. <i>West Coast:</i> Dunk and Davey, Spang Weazling; <i>Wellington</i>: Who Goes Round My Stone Wall, Beg o' My Neighbor, Bellahonie, Run a Mile, On and Off the Bridge, Here Come Two Nuns, Colours, Isaac and Rebecca. Scrag, Diabolo, Bumble Puppy, Homai, Fives; <i>Auckland:</i> My Fair Young Lady, Rushes and Reeds, Surrender the Tower, Walls, Ball Hopscotch, ball games, Solomon Says; <i>Hawkes Bay:</i> Wolf Wolf Go Home, Homai; Canterbury: Poor Old Tom, Rats and Rabbits and Rips, Steps, Skin the Snake, Steal a Peg, Toodle-em-Buck, The Gag; <i>Golden Bay</i>: Who's That Walking Round My House Tonight, I've Come to See Poor Mary Jane, Steps and Stairs, Creeping, Branding, O'Leary; <i>Otago:</i> Sir Roger de Coverley, Who's That Knocking at My Door. *(1981,History of Children's Play,231)*

phrase.

Scrag, Diabolo, Bumble Puppy, Homai, Fives; <i>Auckland:</i> My Fair Young Lady, Rushes and Reeds, Surrender the Tower, Walls, Ball Hopscotch, ball games, Solomon Says; <i>Hawkes Bay:</i> Wolf Wolf Go Home, Homai; Canterbury: Poor Old Tom, Rats and Rabbits and Rips, Steps, Skin the Snake, Steal a Peg, Toodle-em-Buck, The Gag; <i>Golden Bay</i>: Who's That Walking Round My House Tonight, I've Come to See Poor Mary Jane, Steps and Stairs, Creeping, Branding, O'Leary; <i>Otago:</i> Sir Roger de Coverley, Who's That Knocking at My Door. *(1981,History of Children's Play,231)*

### whou/whow
  
noun.  
See: whao

### whyenee
  
noun.  
See: wahine

### Whykickamoocow
  
noun.
