### wi
  
noun. [Maori]  
See: also wiwi  
> **LEE & KENDALL** W|i, s. A small rush. *(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,227)*  
> **HOOKER** Juncus <i>maritimus,</i> Lam. ... Nat. name, `Wi' or `Wi-Wi' (for the whole genus), <i>Colenso. </i> *(1853,Flora NZ,I Flowering Plants 262)*  
> **HOOKER** Whi, <i>Col[enso]. Juncus maritimus. </i> *(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),769)*  
> **NORRIS** They crossed the great Tokoroa Plains [in 1883], thickly covered with `wi' and white grass, offering good feed for sheep and cattle, and travelled over the partly formed road leading to the Maori settlement of Maungaiti. *(1964,Settlers in Depression,76)*  
> **BROOKER et al.** <i>Poa caespitosa</i> Spreng. Common name:<i> Tussock grass </i> Maori name: Wi *(1981,NZ Medicinal Plants,59)*  
> There is very little growth in the grass all this distance, except the wi, which has found its way through, and is quite green... The plains are quite level, nothing growing on them except stunted tea-tree and wi-grass. *(Apr 9, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,7)*  
> Maori names for tussock varied from region to region. In one southern dialect, <i>wi</i> was used for silver tussock (further north the same word was applied to rushes) and <i>patiti</i> seemed to be a general term for tall tussocks, with <i>maania</i> an alternative or the word for red tussock in particular. *(Nov 2003,NZ Geographic,No. 66/40)*

### WIA
  
abbreviation. Wellington International Airport  
> Wellington International Airport's (WIA) aeronautical revenue is set to soar by 60 per cent to $40 million a year, according to leading sharebrokers... The report is expected to have more impact on Auckland International Airport than WIA. *(Jul 3, 2002,Evening Post,21)*

### wick
  
noun. a dull, stupid person, a twerp  
[NOTES: ca1940 Wanganui Collegiate (p.c. N.C.C. Small) -->ca1951 Wellington Aw, he's a wick - a wet as anything.]

### wick
  
noun. a penis  
[NOTES: [P at get on one's wick equates it with <i>Hampton Wick</i> = prick] esp. in (mainly schoolboys', and esp. in imper.) FLICK ONE'S WICK, (to) hurry up!; wake up! --Possibly derives from a fuller threat `I'll flick your wick for you if you don't hurry up, wake yourself up, get out of my way, etc.', from the painful prank of flicking a boy on the point of the penis with a wet towel]

### wickedie
  
noun. a baddie; the bad character in a film, etc  
[NOTES: ca1930 L. Cleveland p.c. Children at the pictures used to yell: Here come the wickedies.]

### widdygiddy
  
verb.  
> **McGILL** <i>widdygiddy</i> to behave in a flighty manner; another of the `widdy' words Stevan Eldred-Grigg came across in researching pre-WWII Christchurch working class families. Partridge identifies `widdy' as either a widow or the gallows in dialect and low colloquial C19, the latter Scots, possibly a pun on `widdy', a halter. however, the juvenile or even baby talk of fanciful adults seems more likely. *(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,123)*

### widdykikoo
  
verb.  
> **McGILL** <i>widdykikoo</i> to kiss and canoodle and generally carry on, says Stevan Eldred-Grigg of what he assumes came into Christchurch working class English as dialect word from elsewhere, or was coined. *(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,123)*

### wide comb
  
noun.  
> We worked for a contractor in the south-east who had just weathered a storm of controversy over the wide combs he and his shearers wanted to use to help them shear more sheep each day, and so earn more money, against the wishes of a strong union. *(Mar 1988,More Magazine,32)*

### wide gear
  
noun.  
> The wide gear was finally legalised in that area of South Australia the year before we arrived and all this affected me because it served to illustrate just how traditional things were and how hard changes came in the shearing world, even in the 1980s. *(Mar 1988,More Magazine,32)*

### wideawake
  
noun. the sooty tern  
> **FALLA et al.** SOOTY TERN<i> Sterna fuscata Other name:</i> Wideawake. *(1966,Birds of New Zealand,165)*

### Wideawake
  
noun. the nickname given by Maoris to Colonel William Wakefield, the NZ Company's Wellington agent: an alteration of or play on <i>Wake</i>field, with reference to his headgear, and possibly to the fact that he was an alert or devious businessman  
> This is Wide Awake's ship [sc <i>Tory</i>] come to watch his son. *(1840,NZ Journal,24/298)* [NOTES: (E.J. Wakefield)]  
> **E.J. WAKEFIELD**  *(Apr 16, 1840,Diary)* [NOTES: (MS: ATB) passim]  
> Colonel Wakefiield ..was not idle;  "Wideawake" the Maoris called him and wideawake they found him. *(Aug 21, 1890,Otago Witness,32)*  
> ".. Some evil disposed person or persons have industriously circulated it among the natives here that Wideawake (meaning yourself) is going to bring up soldiers and volunteers to kill the Maories [sic] ..." *(Jan 11, 1861,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,4)*

### wideawake
  
noun. a soft felt hat with a broad brim and a low flat crown, the common headgear of early settlers and pioneers(cf BUSH-HAT)  
> **BARKER** I must say, [the bushmen] were the most picturesque of the party, being ... dressed in red flannel shirts and leathern knickerbockers and gaiters; they ... wore `diggers' hats,' a head-dress of American origin - a sort of wide-awake made of plush, capable of being crushed into any shape, and very becoming. *(1867,Station Life in NZ,182,1870)*  
> **MONEY** In a place [sc. the diggings] where open shirts, moleskin trousers tucked into long boots, crimson sashes tied round the waist, and tall American wide-awakes, were the prevailing attire, ... *(1871,Knocking About in NZ,18)*  
> **SENIOR** The general [Maori] costume ... was to put on a belltopper hat, garibaldi shirt, paper collar, and necktie, a pilot jacket ... but French caps, deerstalkers, and wide-awakes were to be seen. *(1880,Travel & Trout,196)*  
> **MANSFIELD** Wisps of white hair straggled from under his wideawake - his moustache and eyebrows were called white -[551 ed. note] A `wideawake' ... is a broad-brimmed hat in the Australian style; ... *(1912,Stories,109,1984 Alpers)*  
> **LUSK** The Lady [Mrs Warrington] looking remarkably well in her [riding] habit and wideawake cap. *(Mar 29, 1851,Auckland Journals,70)*  
> Those were the early, happy, goldfield days when the pioneer miner appeared in his blue short, moleskin trousers, wide-awake hat, and nugget boots. *(Mar 11, 1924,Otago Witness,60)*

### wideawake
  
adjective.  
> **YOUNG** Missionaries in their `bush' costume, consisting of a `wide-awake' hat, *(1854,Southern World,162)*  
> **BAINES** <i>Bush fashion.</i> [page heading] ... He [the Northland sawmiller] wore what is called a `blue shirt', that is, a sailor's shirt of serge, fitting very closely, and a pair of moleskin trousers, very much torn ... from walking through harsh fern and `tea-tree' scrub, ... and a wideawake hat ... *(1874,Edward Crewe,39)*  
> **HAY** Six flannel shirts, ... one pair of leggings, a wide-awake hat, and a few odds and ends. *(1882,Brighter Britain,1/68)*  
> **GREEN** ... in walked an elderly gentleman in a wideawake hat, black coat, neat spring-side boots, and, for luggage, an umbrella. *(1883,High Alps of NZ,279)*  
> **REEVES** The Australian coo-ee, the Australian buck-jumping horse, the Australian stockwhip and wideawake hat came into New Zealand pastoral life. *(1898,Long White Cloud, The,242)* [NOTES: [In Canterbury c. 1850]]

### wideawake tern
  
noun. [Environment]  
> WIDEAWAKE TERN (<i>Sterna fuliginosa</i>, Gml.). *(1891,TrNZI,23/221)*  
> <i>Sooty Tern Sterna fuscata</i> (LINNAEUS, 1766) NATIVE OTHER NAMES: <i>Wideawake tern, whale bird, egg bird</i>. ... The sooty tern is the most oceanic of all terns; it ranges over most tropical and sub-tropical seas and comes to land only to breed. *(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,233)*

### Widerossa
  
noun.  
[NOTES: see Wydrop]  
> **SHORTLAND** I was informed by the natives that there is an extensive and beautiful valley at Widerossa, or Paliser Bay; ... *(Oct 10, 1840,Letter)* [NOTES: 1840 Oct 10 Shortland to Hobson in GBPP House of Commons 1841 (No.311) 120]

### widgeon
  
noun.  
> Now ... we watch the vistors ... (amongst them, the Black Widgeon and variegated Shoveller were rarely to be seen) .<i>Ibid.</i> 73  FULIGULA NOVAE ZELANDIAE, Gml. Papango. Black Widgeon. In the hill-country, a few years since, this was sufficiently common; a small tarn, near Lake Coleridge, yet retains the name of Widgeon Lake, ... *(1870,TrNZI,2/48)* [NOTES: (Potts Birds)]

### widgeon
  
noun.  
> **HEAPHY** The Paradise and common duck, teal, widgeon, water-hen, and diver; are all found in great numbers on the rivers.; ... *(1842,Narrative of a Residence in  Various Parts of NZ,47)*  
> **PHILLIPS** ... - got to `Widgeon Lake' (a good sized Lagoon at the head of Lake Coleridge) ... Seal then went and killed 6 more widgeon & some Red-bills - *(Dec 29, 1856,Journal Kept at Rockwood and the Point,61)*  
> Grey ducks, Paradise ducks, (which are rather a species of geese) teal, and widgeon frequent the streams. ... Blue mountain-duck is found in the more elevated regions. *(1868,Province of Otago in NZ, The,31)*  
> FULIGULA NOVAE ZEALANDIAE. (NEW-ZEALAND SCAUP.) ... <i> Native names.</i>  Papango, Tetepango, Matapouri, Titiporangi, and Raipo; `Black Teal' and `Widgeon' of the Colonists. *(1873,Birds of New Zealand,1888 2nd edn 2/273)*  
> **HAY** The species we have identified in the Kaipara and Hokianga are - the Putangi, or `paradise-duck' (<i> Casarca variegata </i> ); the grey duck, or Parera (<i> Anas superciliosa </i> ); the brown duck, an allied species or variety of the last; the Papanga, or `teal,' or `widgeon' (<i> Fuligula N.Z </i> .), and some other varieties that may be imported birds, or crosses, or other native species. *(1882,Brighter Britain,2/225)*  
> **WILLIAMS** <i> papango </i> , n. <i> widgeon. </i> *(1892,Dictionary of the NZ Language,124)*

### widgeon
  
noun.  
> **FALLA et al.** BANDED RAIL <i> Rallus philippensis other names </i> : Landrail, Striped Rail, Mohopereru, Mioweka. (The old sealers' name for the Macquarie Island race was Wigeon!) *(1966,Birds of New Zealand,104)*

### widgie
  
noun.  
> **GEE** This was.. because the badmintonassociation headquarters opposite herhome was staffed by "bodgies andwidgies." "So bad is their behaviour thatrecently I have had to erect a six-footfence costing sixty pounds along the sideof my house to stop them making loveunder my pine trees". *(1959,Our Mabel,187)*  
> **CROWTHER** During the early months of this year [1956] much publicity was given in the Christchurch newspapers to the fact that large numbers of young people were to be seen, especially at the weekends, in the central areas of the city. These young people congregated in the streets and milk-bars. Many of them had motor-cycles ... At various times these young people were referred to as `Teddy Boys', `Milk Bar Cowboys' and `Bodgies and Widgies'...  [2] Bodgies and Widgies are members of a closely knit sex cult. This cult is centred and operates actively in Australia (and especially in Sydney). As far as is known, the cult itself does not operate in Christchurch, though [3] many boys and girls imitate its fashions and share with its adherents an interest in jive. The dress of these youngsters resembles that of the Teddy Boy and Girl, but is not so immaculate; hair is worn long and brushed back, without the characteristic Teddy Boy tuft; behaviour tends to be more boisterous and `attention provoking'. In this report such phrases as `Teddy Boy style' or `Bodgie type' are used purely as descriptive terms, to indicate certain fashions of dress which show these overseas influences. *(1956,Street Society in Christchurch,1)*  
> **DUCKWORTH** The Rosemarie [milk-bar]? That ghastly place. You'll get all the cowboys and widgies on Saturday. *(1963,Barbarous Tongue, A,43)*  
> **McLINTOCK** Australian importations include . . . <i>bodgies</i> and <i>widgies</i> for young male and female delinquents. *(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,2/680)* [NOTES: Wall NZ Speech]  
> <i>widgie n. NZ & Austral. colloq. </i>female bodgie. [orig. unkn.] *(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,882)* [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]  
> **McGILL** <i>widgie</i> female bodgie, defined <i>Sunday Chronicle</i> of 6 January 1952: `girl with horse-tail hair-do and bobby socks, also playing juke-box in milk-bar'; possibly a little wilder this side of the Tasman, where bodgies and widgies were regarded as juvenile delinquents. *(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,123)*  
> **MANNING** The terms "Bodgie" and "Widgie" arepeculiar to the middle 20th Century, butthe characteristics are not... In theunder-privileged masses the counterpartsof Bodgies were variously styled as'yahoos', 'larrikins', 'hooligans' and'yobs'. The 'playboy' of upper classsociety is the "Bodgie" of the poor. *(1958,Bodgie, The,7)*  
> One of the first things that emerged inMr Manning's study was that neitherbodgies nor widgies lack intelligence butthere was widespread resentment at thelack of freedom in school and education. *(Aug 8, 1958,Listener (New Zealand Listener),8)*  
> On Labour weekend the island was overrunwith hundreds of boozing, cursing,bottle-smashing bodgies and widgies...The bodgies favour blue jeans, thewidgies black sweaters and tight blackshorts. *(Nov 2, 1955,NZ Truth,1)*  
> [Hdg] S.M. Refers to Waiheke Bodgie-Widgie Trouble. *(Nov 16, 1955,NZ Truth,5)*

### widgie
  
noun.  
> **CROWTHER** Very few girls in Widgie clothes (tight black skirts, white, high-collared shirts) were seen, but there were large numbers of girls of `Widgie-type'. *(1956,Street Society in Christchurch,5)*  
> The girls were all 'widgie' members of Elbe's Milkbar gang, meeting boy 'bodgies', listening to music, and sipping milkshakes. *(Jul 13, 2000,City Voice,3)*

### widowhood
  
noun. when pigeons are prepared for a race by enforced solitude  
> In what handlers call "widowhood", a bird is typically deprived of its mate for a few months and then placed with the mate a few hours before the race. The hapless racer may even be shown a rival being placed with its lover before it is whisked away to be basketed. *(Jun 11, 1990,Listener (New Zealand Listener),19)*

### widowmaker
  
noun. a branch which remains caught up in surrounding trees when a tree is felled and apt to fall later (cf SAILER) spg also widow-maker  
[NOTES: 1953 Dec Term used by bushmen in film made for the NZ Forest Service -->W3 Something dangerous ... to workmen; spec. a loose limb hanging in or falling from a tree in logging;]

### widowmaker
  
noun. flax clump that falls and kills  
> We wander past 350-year old rimu and fallen 'widow-makers' - huge clumps of flax known to fall from the canopies and kill loggers - as a fat kereru (wood pigeon) flaps heavily by. *(Jul 10, 2005,Sunday Star Times,442)* [NOTES: page number can't be right]

### widowmaker
  
noun. a large astelia plant species growing on tree branches [that can suddenly fall when waterlogged]  
> BLAKE Trappers, Dogs 'n' Deer 32 It was the type of bush that I loved though, podocarp - broadleaf rain forest with tall rimu, matai and miro trees forming a canopy over the mixed broadleaf undergrowth and huge astelias, or 'widowmakers' as they are called, hanging treacherously from the upper limbs of the tall timber. *(1999)*  
> ibid. 263 Glossary Widow maker: large astelia that grows on branches of trees and is inclined to fall when waterlogged. *(1999)*

### widowmaker
  
noun. [early bush felling] a branch that falls unexpectedly.  
> WASWO Farming Progress in NZ 11 Many bushmen were killed or injured by falling trees and branches. Branches that came down unexpectedly were called 'widowmakers.' *(1996)*

### wife
  
noun. a patronising jocular, but often derogatory, male usage, the def. art. replacing the possessivemy; possibly the middle-class equivalent of <i>the old woman </i>  
> Will you come in and the wife will make you a cup of tea. *(1900,NZ Illustrated Magazine,2/519)*  
> The wife got a kit of potatoes and called all the pigs. The cowboys let the pigs out of a yard and they all ran after the wife who ran towards the beach where there were strong yards and into these yards ran the pigs. *(Feb 18, 1906,Chudleigh Diary,431,1950)*  
> **MANSFIELD**  *(1923,Poems (various authors))*  
> **GUTHRIE** He wrenched Gertrude out into the open. `Meet,' said George, `the wife!' *(1935,Little Country, The,321,1937)*  
> **HYDE**  *(1936,Checkmate To Your King)*  
> **GUTHRIE** Betty told the wife the other day .. *(1947,Big Game,54)*  
> **BALLANTYNE** You weren't too keen on old Ma Jacobsen, ... or on the wife's friend Bella Simpson, ... *(1948,Cunninghams, The,20,1970)*  
> **BALLANTYNE** You weren't too keen on old Ma Jacobsen, ... or on the wife's friend Bella Simpson, ... *(1948,Cunninghams, The,20,1970)*  
> **BALLANTYNE** You weren't too keen on old Ma Jacobsen, ... or on the wife's friend Bella Simpson, ... *(1948,Cunninghams, The,20,1970)*  
> **G.E.BELL** [N.Z. usage includes] `the wife', instead of `my wife' (you never hear `the husband' which must have a psychological significance!) *(Nov 25, 1957,Letter)* [NOTES: Dr. G.E. Bell letter (Wellington)]  
> **SLATTER** `How'd you get away from the farm? I suppose you left the wife up there and you're down on the scoot.' *(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,42)*  
> **BACON** `Crickey! I nearly forgot!' said Alf Saunders. `The wife gave me a few pikelets for supper. She thought you would've been too busy to do any cooking yet.' *(1963,In the Sticks,18)* [NOTES: [check spg `crickey'???]]  
> **SLATTER** I work flat to the boards, never even cough till ten, slogging me guts out to buy luxuries for the wife and she goes butchers because me home-brew stained her posh water set. *(1968,Pagan Game, The,150)*  
> **ANDERSON** James laid a hand on his shoulder: `Come on in. The wife will be pleased to see you.' *(1972,Letter from James,159)*  
> **FERNANDEZ** `The wife's brother is up from the South.' *(1973,Tussock Fever,71)*  
> **WILSON** `Going to see the wife, Dick?' Bruce asked. *(1976,Pacific Star,129)*  
> **LELAND** <i>the:</i> is often used instead of MY. `The wife gave me a right bollicking when I came in late last night.' *(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,103)*

### wife
  
noun.  
> **SLATTER** Conformity was the [University hostel] House Code. A room mate was always a wife, trousers were always reamers, old boy was always pronounced air bair, the board at the bottom of the bed was the power board, a shower was a soak, all other hostels were inhabited by morons, freshers answered the phone. *(1968,Pagan Game, The,84)*  
> **TURNER** It is becoming difficult to point out that men living together in university hostels where rooms were shared could some years ago use colloquially the term <i>wife </i>for a room-mate without any hint of homosexual interests at all. *(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,126)*

### wifebeater
  
noun. [informal] a singlet  
> ..buy $10 worth of liquor, get the free white singlet colloquially known as a wifebeater. *(Dec 18, 2005,Sunday Star Times,C1)*

### wife-beater
  
noun. an early volunteer for the First Echelon in WW2  
See: debt-dodger  
> **ST BRUNO** Certain near-sober First Echelon wife-beaters and debt-dodgers. *(1944,Desert Daze,(foreword))*  
> **ST BRUNO**  *(1946,Maleesh George,84 (et passim))*  
> Their lot was made no easier by the knowledge that, in some unthinking quarters back in New Zealand they had been dubbed `wife-beaters and debt-dodgers'. *(Sep 26, 1979,Evening Post,2)* [NOTES: [Arthur Helm, writing on a First Echelon reunion]]  
> **Sinclair & Harrex** The troops left home in three 'Echelons'. The first went to Egypt. As eager volunteers, they were labelled 'the wife beaters and debt dodgers'. *(1978,Looking Back,215)*  
> I went away with the "wife-beaters" and "debt-dodgers". The people who went in the Second Echelon were known as "Cook's Tourists." *(1961,NZ Parliamentary Debates,326:122)*

### wig
  
verb. to clip wool from a sheep's head, esp. around the eyes  
See: also wink, eye-wig  
>  *(1933,Press, The)* [NOTES: Acland]  
> Lambs ... while ewes are being shorn ... are being wigged or eye-clipped ... Through eye clipping, wool blindness is avoided; a wool-blind sheep is seriously hampered on run country in its search for feed and consequently does not thrive. *(Oct 16, 1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,349)*  
> <i>wig ... NZ = eye-wig. </i> *(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,882)* [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

### wig
  
noun. = topknot (often pl.)  
> **FERNANDEZ** `What's 2nd Pcs H?' `Grade 2, pieces - that's skirtings - hogget wool.' `And Bls E?' `That's belly wool. E grade bellies.' `Lox?' `Oh, that's floor sweepings.' `What about Lbs Wigs?' `Lamb top-knots - off the head.' *(1973,Tussock Fever,166)*

### wig
  
noun. an old male seal  
See: also clapmatch  
> **McNAB** Q. Is any oil obtained from the seals ...? A. There is. A pup seal will give about 2 gallons more or less. A wig, that is an old male seal, will yield 5 or 6 gallons. *(1908,Historical Records,1/559)* [NOTES: 1821 May Mr McDonald's Evidence to Commissioner Bigge's Inquiry in 1908 McNab Historical Records 1/559]  
> The Maoris speak of them [seals] as much larger than the wigs, as they call the full-grown Brown Seal of our coasts. *(1870,TrNZI,2/30)*  
> **DOUGALL** When full grown ... each male [sea lion], or `old wig' as the sailors call him, will weigh about seven ... hundredweight .. *(1888,Far South,10)*  
> **HOWARD** Incoming skins are sorted to arbitrary standards - wigs (old males), clap-matches (females), bulls, yearlings, grey or silver pups (under one year), black pups. If the cargo is intended for the European or American market, the skins are removed from the animal with an inch of blubber adhering, and in the final simple treatment are washed, heavily slated, and then folded or `booked' into rectangular shape for packing. Skins destined for China, however, are treated with more preliminary care. They are taken as cleanly as possible, and after careful washing are sent to the `beamer' to be thoroughly scraped free of the last remaining particles of flesh and blubber. *(1940,Rakiura,40)*  
> **HALL-JONES** The bull seals (sea-lions, or wigs) come ashore in spring and take up their stance on the rocks. *(1945,Historical Southland,28)*  
> **BEATTIE**  *(1950,Far-Famed Fiordland,24)*  
> **SANSOM** Stretched out on the rocks, relaxed or asleep, only one watchful old wig had his head raised and on the alert. *(1982,In the Grip of an Island,92)*  
> <i>Wigging: </i>Shearing wool from the head of sheep. Normally carried out at crutching. The trade term for this wool is eye clips. Also known as topknots or wigs. *(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,61)*  
> <i>Wigging: </i>Shearing wool from the head of sheep. Normally carried out at crutching. The trade term for this wool is eye clips. Also known as topknots or wigs. *(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,61)*

### wigeon
  
noun.  
See: widgeon

### wigged
  
adjective. [of a sheep ] with eye-wool or head-wool removed.  
> J of Ag Oct 15. 366 Experiments with wigged and unwigged woolly-faced sheep have shown that the production differences are an inherent character of open and woolly-faced sheep. *(1962)*

### wigging
  
noun. the removing of head-wool around a sheep's eyes.  
See: eye-wigging, eye-clipping  
> NZ Farmer Aug 10. 95 There is no wigging or eye clipping, wool-blindness is a thing of the past. *(1972)*  
> J of Ag June 49 The job may be small, such as ring crutching, but those heavy sheep have to be handled. Ideal for full crutch, ringing and wigging, dagging or sale crutch of prime lambs. *(1982)*

### wigging
  
noun. an area of wool cut from a sheep's head in the process of wigging or clipping.  
> HENDERSON Wool and Wool Classing 34 Wool of this kind, shorn from the face and sometimes from behind the ears at the same time as crutching is carried out, is called 'eye-clips' or 'wiggings' and because of its great softness it is particularly valued for certain types of woollen fabrics. *(1965)*

### wigging
  
noun.  
> <i>Wigging</i>: Shearing wool from the head of sheep. Normally carried out at crutching. The trade term for this wool is eye clips. Also known as topknots or wigs. *(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,61)*

### wiggle stick
  
noun.  
> If they [sc. whitebait] try to by-pass the daunting array of nets and screens, Daryl gets busy with his wiggle stick. The pole with a flag on the end, also known as a swiggle stick or a diddle dasher, comes in handy for herding fish in the right direction. *(Oct 15, 1989,Dominion Sunday Times,13)*

### wiggle-room
  
noun.  
> If you have a financial crisis, you  can sell the house or trade down. But that's not a lot of wiggle-room and it certainly won't enhance your capital position. *(Dec 14, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),17)*

### wiggy
  
noun. a weed  
> <i>wiggy [=] wire vine [s] </i> *(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,86)*

### wiggy
  
noun. a shrubby coprosma, often known as miki-mik or mingimingi.  
> MARTIN Flora NZ 215 A majority of small bushy shrubs with twiggy branches and small opposite leaves are Coprosmas, colloquially known as 'wiggy bushes' or 'micki-miks' this name being a corruption of the Southern Maori miki-miki. *(1961)*  
> LOUGHNAN Glossary 91 wiggy bush: another term for a miki-mik, a coprosma. Perhaps the word is a mutilation, not of the Maori name mingimingi, but of twiggy bush. *(1981)*

### wiggy
  
noun. a twiggy bush.  
> STACEY Tales of a Tussock Jumper 19 Eels, flounder and watercress were always welcome additions to our diet and a sedge-like wiggy used to provide berries which we called sweet pies. *(1996)*  
> ibid. 126 As I rode by in the spring the snow had all gone and the tops of the wiggy were level with me. [wiggy bush] *(1996)*

### wiggybush
  
noun. <i>Muehlenbeckia</i> spp., esp. <i>M. complexa </i>  
[NOTES: [a1927] Ver.(A). = Vernacular names supplied by B.C. Aston (And) <i>Muchlenbeckia</i> sp, wiggybush]  
> **LAING & BLACKWELL** It is the straggling unattractive <i>Muhlenbeckia complexa.</i> That name does not appeal and shall not be the popular name! I [sc. Johannes Andersen] have heard one which I omitted from my list, [<i>Popular Names</i>] `wiggy-bush.' This describes its habit very well, and may be adopted; ... *(1940,Plants of NZ,160)* [NOTES: [from Andersen]]  
> <i>wiggy bush [=] wire vine [s] </i> *(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,86)*  
> **MARTIN** A majority of small bushy shrubs with twiggy branches and small opposite leaves are Coprosmas, colloquially known as `wiggy bushes' or `miki-miks', this name being a corruption of the Southern Maori miki-miki. *(1929,Flora NZ,215,1961)*

### wig-out
  
noun.  
> You can easily imagine how having one more media celebrity lawyer mealy-mouthing on about what a beast Dalziel was - when again, Dalziel had not made the primary decision - .. would have caused wig-out. *(Mar 6, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),15)*

### wigwam
  
noun.  
> ... he brought us to the front of a small wigwam or <i>whare ... </i> *(1867,Rambles with a Philosopher,47)* [NOTES: Griffiths Collection]  
> The result of that evidence is to show that one of the Surveyors of the New Zealand Company erected a Hut near Cook's Straits, with various signals, for the purposes of his survey - that the lands on which these erections were made was in the possession of the Chiefs and their Tribes, and have been so from time immemorial - that they claimed to be the proprietors as well as the occupants of the soil - that the hut was a mere wigwam, rudely put together of wood and rushes cut from the same land ... *(Sep 21, 1844,Daily Southern Cross,2)*  
> **BLAIR** As he reached his wigwam near the lonely Bell hill. *(1889,Lay of the Old Identities, The,48)*

### wigwam for a goose's bridle
  
phrase.  
[NOTES: (ca1938 W.H. Orsman, WINGWAM ... as a reply to an inquisitive child) (alteration of Brit. dialect whim-wham for...)]  
> Mum was an old Scot and had a great deal of homespun philosophy. If I asked what something was she would often tell me `It's a wigwam for a goose's bridle.' *(Nov 14, 1989,Evening Post,6)* [NOTES: (de Cleene)]  
> **LELAND** <i>wigwam for a goose's bridle:</i> an old unusual and uncommon Southland term for something that is totally useless. `That's about as much use as a wigwam for a goose's bridle'. *(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,112)*  
> **FRAME** Those answers were as meaningless as the teasing answer people gave when you asked them what they were making: `A wigwam for a goose's bridle.' Fortunately, Poppy had all the information I wanted. *(1982,To the Is-Land,80)*  
> **McGILL** <i>wigwam for a goose's bridle</i> evasive and/or dismissive answer to question about what one is doing; variant of `whim-wham to bridle a goose', a `whim-wham' being any fanciful abject; eg `What's that thing you're making?' `Oh, just a wigwam for a goose's bridle.' NZA. *(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,123)*  
> **GIFKINS** The people at 195 were full of sayings. ... `What's that?' I would enquire. `A wigwam for a goose's bridle,' he would reply. *(1988,Through the Looking Glass,94)* [NOTES: (Cornish)]

### wika
  
noun.  
See: weka

### Wilbur
  
noun. an American soldier, a GI (cf WW1 SAMMY) ? Italy when in contact with US 5th Army  
> `Then you've got to remember the Wilburs,' I said. `All hands to the PX for an extra ration of coca-cola.' *(1945,Sixes & Sevens,7)*  
> (gloss) <i>Wilbur</i>: New Zealand for American. *(1946,Johnny Enzed in Italy)*

### wild
  
adjective. as a distinguishing epithet in floral and faunal names see ANTIRRHINUM, BASIL, bee, birch, cabbage, calceolaria, carrot, cattle, celery, chamomile, cherry, cineraria, clematis, cress, daisy, dog, flax, fuschsia, garlic, geranium, ginger, gladiolus, gooseberry; grass, oat heliotrope, hog, honey linseed, mangrove, marjoram, mignonette, millet, mustard, oat, onion, parsley, parsnip, passionflower, passion vine, pig, pigeon, pineapple, portulaca, radish, rubarb, rice, sage, serradella, spaniard, strawberry, tare,(s), terasel, thyme, tobacco, turnip, vetch, zinnia (see also maori, native) (b) for close combinations see also wild irishman, ??wild spaniard

### wild as a hatter
  
phrase. very angry, furious  
> **DAVIDSON** The doctor was as wild as a hatter. *(1889,Stories of NZ Life,13)*

### wild as a meat axe
  
phrase. very wild  
> **SANSOM** Although sold to him as `bullocks broken in to the harness' they were `as wild as meat-axes'. *(1970,Stewart Islanders,145)*

### wild as buck rats
  
phrase.  
> **SUTHERLAND** A dozen dogs got after them but the sheep were as wild as buck-rats and wouldn't be headed. *(1953,Golden Bush, The,154,1963)*

### wild birch
  
noun. ? <i>Gaultheria</i> spp.; snowberry cf false beech  
> **SUTTON-SMITH** We sucked honey from various spring flowers, but the most prized was a low-growing and a very prickly native heather with flowers about half or three quarters of an inch long that grew on the stony plain. There were masses of it which we called rice-plant because its masses of sweet, juicy white berries spread out near the ground resembled polished rice in appearance. A plant that we called `wild birch' (because its leaves resembled birch leaves, I suppose) had red astringent berries that we enjoyed. It grew among the manuka scrub. ... (1900; Lower Moutere) *(1981,History of Children's Play,115)*

### wild cabbage
  
noun.  
> **SAVAGE**  *(1807,New Zealand (various authors/titles),57)*  
> **BIDWILL** The natives had a large copper mowrie to cook the pork they had brought with them, and for the first time I saw them eat the common milk-[52]thistle. They cook it as constantly as the leaves of the wild turnip and cabbage, and seem to relish it just as much. It is so precisely like the English milk-thistle that I am almost inclined to think it must have come with the potatoes. *(1841,Rambles in NZ,51,1952)*  
> **ANGAS** The whole island was thickly overrun with wild cabbage, now in full blossom; .. *(1847,Savage Life,1/278)*  
> **WAKEFIELD** There are also several <i>grasses</i>, as well as <i>wild carrots, turnips</i>, and <i>cabbages</i>, whose origin, from their similarity to ours, may be attributed to the visits of some European shipping. *(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,150)*  
> Saw the hut of old E.Turu, where we pulled up and were glad to feast on fern root and wild cabbage, [69] fresh and preserved. *(Mar 18, 1849,Torlesse Papers,68,1958)*  
> ... it feeds on the leaves of the Kowai (<i>Edwardsia microphylla</i>) or on wild cabbage. *(1869,TrNZI,1/3/14)* [NOTES: (Buller Ornithology)]  
> **WILLIAMS** <i>Haaria</i>, n<i>. wild cabbage. </i> *(1871,Dictionary of the Maori Language,20,Also 1892. 22)*  
> **BULLER** I have sometimes found its crop distended with the ripe pulpy seed of the tataramoa (<i>Rubus australis</i>);.. and it is said to feed also on the leaves of the thistle and wild cabbage. *(1873,Birds of New Zealand,1888 2nd edn I/3)*  
> **LADY MARTIN** Sometimes the Maoris would bring a basket of wild cabbage, or some large water-melons, ... *(1884,Our Maoris,42)*  
> **HILGENDORF** WILD CABBAGE (<i>Brassica oleracea</i>), often called Maori cabbage, is a rape-like annual found on sea cliffs in both islands. *(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,93,1967)*  
> **WILLIAMS** <i>Niko, ... Wild cabbage, Brassica</i> sp. (mod.) *(1957,Dictionary of the Maori Language,222)*  
> <i>wild cabbage</i> [s] <i>Brassica oleracea </i>L. *(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,86)*  
> **STARK** KAPETI (Wild Cabbage) <i>Brassica oleracea </i> This is doubtless a garden escape plant *(1979,Maori Herbal Remedies,28)*  
> **BROOKER et al.** <i>Brassica oleracea</i> L. Common name: <i>Wild cabbage </i>Maori name: <i>Kapeti </i> *(1981,NZ Medicinal Plants,47)*  
> **WILSON** WILD CABBAGE (<i>*Brassica oleracea</i>) differs [from WILD TURNIP] in having the leaves all blue-green, fleshy and hairless ... *(1982,Stewart Island Plants,140)*

### wild carrot
  
noun. <i>Daucus brachiatus</i> Wild carrot  
> **WAKEFIELD** There are also several <i>grasses</i>, as well as <i>wild carrots, turnips</i>, and <i>cabbages,</i> whose origin, from their similarity to ours, may be attributed to the visits of some European shipping: and a <i>sowthistle </i>grows very luxuriantly wherever the forest has been recently felled, affording nourishing fodder for pigs, horses, and cattle. *(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,150)*  
> **R. TAYLOR** The chief plants I noticed were a beautiful everlasting, the harebell, 2 kinds of gentian, a pretty speedwell, an epacris and several other pretty flowers, a wild carrot also abounded. *(Jan 30, 1853,Diary)* [NOTES: 1853 Jan 30 R. Taylor Diary in 1966 Mead Richard Taylor 192]  
> <i>Daucus brachiatus</i> Wild carrot *(1870,TrNZI,2/125)* [NOTES: (Armstrong Botany Canterbury)]  
> The soil [on Campbell Island] is very wet ... the surface between the tussocks (Patiti) carpeted with beautiful mosses ... The M'Quarrie cabbage, cotton plant (I have an idea that good serviceable paper might be made from the latter), and wild carrot grow abundantly ...The inevitable Piri-piri appears everywhere. *(1870,TrNZI,2/177)* [NOTES: (Hector Outlying Islands)]  
> **COCKAYNE** (And) *(1915,AJHR,c-6)* [NOTES: Notes on the Plant Covering of Kennedy's Bush ... Canterbury]  
> **HILGENDORF** WILD CARROT (<i>Daucus carota</i>) is common in fields and waste places in scattered localities. It is annual or biennial, with a distinct white tap root only faintly suggestive of its cultivated descendant. It grows 1 to 2 feet high, and the leaves and flowers are like those of an ordinary carrot. *(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,132,1967)*  
> <i>carrot, wild [s] Daucus carota</i> L. <i>Ibid.</i> 86 <i>wild carrot [s] Daucus carota</i> L. *(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,12)*  
> **TAYLOR** WILD CARROT (<i>Daucus carota</i>) ... has a smaller root and more hair than the garden carrot. ... introduced from Eurasia, is very common on roadsides. ... *(1981,Weeds of Roadsides,169)*  
> **ELL** WILD CARROT. This is a common wild plant of the roadside, growing to a metre in height. At first glance its flower looks like the yarrow but its foliage is quite different. ... Wild carrot takes its name from the similar look of its leaves to those of the garden carrot. *(1983,Wildflowers and Weeds,22)*  
> Another common umbelliferous weed, often seen on dry roadside verges, is the wild carrot (daucus carota). *(Jan 15, 2005,Dominion Post,E13)*

### wild cat
  
noun. a (? main-trunk) night freight train  
> **LYON** I dressed, caught a horse and was on my way to the railway, twenty miles distant, and boarded a `wild cat,' as they called a night freight train. ... our train pulled into a station under Mt. Ruapehu. ... *(1952,Faring South,150)*

### Wild Cats and Tigers' Union
  
noun. the Women's Christian Temperance Union  
> **McGILL** <i>Wild Cats and Tigers' Union</i> the New Zealand Women's Christian Temperance Union, acronymically so, c.1950. *(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,123)*

### wild cattle
  
noun. [used elsewhere, but prominent in N.Z bush country] cattle beasts that breed and roam in back country and bush areas.  
> KETTLE Diary March 24 in Hall-Jones (1944) Kelly of Inverkelly 59 We saw a wild ox but it took to the bush before we could get within gun-shot of it. The large forest on our right is said to abound with wild cattle which strayed from the Bluff some years ago. *(1852)*  
> Haldon Station Diary Aug 12 in Vance (1965) High Endeavour 252 Frasers tackled the wild cattle and got them all over except about six. *(1864)*  
> BARRER Brave Days 215 There were herds of wild cattle in the bush, which George Wratt and William Robinson were engaed in killing off, and over which there was much excitement. *(1939)*  
> NEWTON High Country Journey 146 The bushbound country beyond the run boundaries and the headworks of the Ant and Esk, were also a great possie for wild cattle. *(1952)*  
> RICHARDS A Pioneer's Life 49 Before releasing the wild cattle from the yards, in preparation for the 40-mile trip they were given a good 'smoking' to subdue them. *(1989)*  
> HINDMARSH Come Be a Pioneer 97 .. we snared quite an assortment of dodgers - even half a dozen wild cattle caught napping well out from the bush edge. *(1997)*

### wild cattle
  
noun. unearmarked or unbranded stock originally from farm or run escapes [AND 1801].  
> **BARLOW** There are great numbers of wild cattle in the back country [of Kaipara]. *(1888,Kaipara and Waitemata Echo,124)*  
> Saw 3 wild cattle - 2 cows, 1 bullock- on high bank of R. Coldstream. *(Dec 30, 1848,Torlesse Papers,45,1958)*  
> **WAKEFIELD** It has already been observed, that there are some <i>wild cattle</i> on [170] the island of Kapiti. *(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,169)*  
> **HAAST** In the open country near Mount Murchison ... we met with numerous tracks of wild cattle, which probably came down in former years from the Roto-iti Valley ... *(1861,Report on Topographical Exploration of Nelson,136)*  
> **BAINES** In the course of the day we saw many wild cattle, of which, at the time I write ... there were upwards of a thousand head on the [Barrier] island. *(1874,Edward Crewe,152)*  
> **PYKE** Never was he happier than ... when, as at mustering [57] seasons, madly galloping over the plains, he chased the wild cattle, waking the echoes with the crack of the long stockman's whip, which none could more dexterously [sic] flourish than he. *(1873,Wild Will Enderby,2/3/56,1889, 1974)*  
> **HAY** Wild cattle abound there [Hokianga], possibly in hundreds; and the Maoris make a good thing by hunting them for their hides. *(1882,Brighter Britain,1/212)*  
> **AYSON** Minor adventures in rounding up wild cattle in swamp country running up from the lake on the north side of Taratu also befell Thomas. Single-handed, he one day made a very satisfactory round-up ... *(1937,Thomas,44)*  
> **HALL-JONES** They [sc. early runholders] had good sport together [108] among the native game and the wild cattle and the hunting of the kuri. *(1945,Historical Southland,107)*  
> **NEWTON** ... I had a go at the wild cattle at Lake Coleridge. These cattle, all cleanskins, were mainly in the bush-bound Harper country, ... *(1971,Ten Thousand Dogs,74)*  
> **BARKER** [Hdg]<i>WILD CATTLE HUNTING IN THE KOWAI BUSH. </i> *(1867,Station Life in NZ,175,1870)*  
> There are numbers of wild cattle and wild pigs running in the bush. *(Feb 26, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,8)*

### wild celery
  
noun. <i>Apium prostratum </i>, aka Maori or native celery  
> My valued weed is wild celery, also known as soup celery or smallage. *(Dec 24, 2004,Dominion Post,C9)*  
> Among the herbs may be mentioned the wild celery <i>(Apium prostratum). </i> *(1911,TrNZI,43/200)*  
> Also growing in some damp and coastal places is introduced wild celery (<i>Apium graveolens</i>), which is an indigenous plant in Europe and parts of Asia and is probably the plant from which modern cultivated celery has been derived. *(May 29, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),41)*

### wild cherry
  
noun.  
> <i>wild cherry [s] Prunus avium</i> L. *(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,86)*

### wild colonial boy
  
phrase.  
> **SLATTER** Ingenuity the war correspondents called it. Like the bloke who looted a three-toned horn from an Ite garage and fitted it to his stores truck. And sounded it majestically through the narrow streets of the Castelraimondo. Toodle-ay-eh. A wild colonial boy. The irrepressible she'll-be-right New Zealander. *(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,148)*

### wild dog
  
noun.  
> **MACKIE** A few miles further brought us to a shepherd's hut; we found the shepherd ... unwell, having met with a serious accident whilst hunting wild dogs. These dogs are of English origin, they are often met with and are destructive to the sheep ... *(Mar 11, 1853,Traveller under Concern,86,1973)*  
> **FITTON** NATIVE DOGS. [heading] ... at all events the renegade curs, which now haunt the unfrequented parts of the mountains and the stony river beds, and are dignified by the name of `Wild Dogs' by the English settlers, are evidently the neglected produce of some European mongrels ... *(1856,New Zealand (various authors/titles),5)*  
> Wild dogs among the Eden sheep.  4. working dog [check working: ?move here],  for kinds of working dogs: see backing-, eye-, heading-, huntaway, ?leading, stopping-, sunday-dog. 5. In combin. [?move here dog-hunting, dog money, dog-tax dog-worrying. See also dog-skin, dog-trial, dog tucker. *(Mar 12, 1859,Puketoi Station Diary)*  
> When I left school wild dogs were a great deal in evidence. *(Nov 2, 1920,Otago Witness,54)*

### wild duck
  
noun.  
> The wild duck (anas superciliosa).. is also numerous. *(Nov 5, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,7)*

### wild flooding
  
noun. a method of surface irrigation (in areas where contour is too difficult for border-dyking or border-ditching, but where ditches are stopped up in high areas to allow flooding over low-lying areas)  
> The water for irrigation is stored in the Manorburn dam at the northeast end of the valley and irrigation is carried out on the contour system - mainly 'wild flooding.' *(Aug 13, 1942,NZ Farmer,5)*  
> There are a number of methods of irrigating land, of which the following are the principal:- 1. Border dyke. 2 Border ditch. 3. Contour. 4. Wild flooding. 5. Furrow. 6. Basin-check. *(Jul 16, 1945,NZ Journal of Agriculture,25)*  
> Wild flooding is not as haphazard as its name suggests. The water is discharged from the head-race at a high spot, and the irrigator directs it with a shovel to different parts of the area through small channels and stop-banks until the whole area is covered. *(Jul 16, 1945,NZ Journal of Agriculture,26)*  
> **RIDDOLLS** Wild flooding. Sometimes the land has little general slope but the surface is so irregular that neither border dyke nor border ditch irrigation is practicable. In these circumstances the only practicable surface irrigation method is wild flooding. *(1958,Farm Engineering,192)*

### wild food
  
noun. food or game that is not farmed  
> Collecting wild food from the sea, cooking and eating it, is remembered with affection by many of the people who responded to our call for food memoirs. *(Oct 20, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),42)*  
> Each year the Monteith's Brewing Company stages a winter competition aimed at unearthing the best 'wild food' and Monteith's beer matches in restaurants and cafes across the country. *(Jul 2001,Cuisine,61)*  
> I'm always ready for a new challenge, such as Monteith's Wild Food matching has tossed at us... The guts of the whole "promo" is that restaurants and cafes must come up with two different courses, dishing up "wild foods" that are not farmed, but that measure up to health regs. *(Jul 7, 2001,Dominion Weekend Magazine,6)*  
> Petit Lyon owner-chef Kent Baddeley asked himself this question and his witty reinvention of Kiwiana won him the Monteith's Wild Food Challenge in Wellington last night... Baddeley's "pie and gravy" comprised chunks of venison, wild duck and black swan, served in a tall potato-topped pie with game gravy, matched with Monteith's Celtic Red Beer. *(Jul 12, 2001,Evening Post,2)*  
> Tonight sees the much anticipated results of this year's Monteith's Wild Food Challenge. Sixteen restaurants have created a special menu of wild (and for some) wonderful fare ... think very wild, as in jellyfish & prawns with spicy Asian dressing, seared rabbit livers, braised  kidneys, fried sweetbreads & poached tongues, or perhaps a galantine of wild birds. *(Jul 11, 2001,Capital Times,17)*  
> He wanted to promote the use of indigenous product and, with Monteith's position as a beer that had its roots in the West Coast - an area associated with wild foods - he found a match made in heaven. *(Jun 5, 2003,Dominion Post,D1)*  
> Wild food can come from sea, rivers or lakes, the sky or the countryside. *(Jun 22, 2004,Independent Herald (Wellington),8)*

### Wild Food Festival
  
noun. an annual West Coast celebration of wild game and food held at Hokitika  
> The forgotten coast - except during the whitebait season, which is lousy at the moment, and in March, when Hokitika hosts its famous Wild Food Festival. *(Oct 21, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),94)*  
> Hokitika.. is having its 13th Wildfoods Festival... Festival organiser Mike Keenan says the great thing about Wildfoods is that everybody gets a piece of the pie... Wildfooders seem to be tribal by nature and come dressed in matching Hawaiian shorts of T-shirts with club insignia such as "Booze hags, pissed since 1976" scrawled across the bosom. *(Mar 13, 2002,Evening Post,22)*  
> The film [sc. <i>Magik & Rose</i>] is set in Hokitika during the Wild Food Festival, where the shoestring crew made good use of a free cast of thousands. *(Jul 27, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),40)*  
> Entertainment has always been a key feature of the event, with clowns, folk singers.. dance raves and the Wildfoods Dance combining to belie Hokitika's otherwise sleepy reputation. *(Mar 3, 2002,Sunday Star Times,F5)*

### wild ginger
  
noun. One of two <i>Hedychium</i> spp., <i>gardnerianum</i> (kahili) and <i>flavescens</i> (yellow).  
[NOTES: Jim Cameron writes: Name and plant known to me from 1950s. Now a serious pest plant. <i>Introduced Wildflowers, New Zealand Weeds</i>, Gordon Ell, Bush Press, 1983; <i>Native Forest Restoration</i>, Tim Porteous, QEII National Trust., 1993. NSOD and Collins record wild ginger as <i>NAmer</i> but with reference to a wholly different plant, <i>Avarum canadense</i>.]  
> <i>ginger, wild [s] Hedychium gardnerianum</i> Roscoe <i>Hedychium oblongum</i> K. Schumann <i>Ibid.</i> 86 <i>wild ginger [s] Hedychium spp. </i> *(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,29)*  
> [Hdg] Bacteria could beat wild ginger pest... Wild ginger was introduced in New Zealand as an ornamental plant in the 1940s and is now a serious weed in native bush, especially in the North Island. *(Nov 29, 2003,Dominion Post,A17)*

noun.

### wild honey
  
noun.  
[NOTES: [cf Campbell's Poem]]  
> **BARKER** ... the mistress of this charming `bush-hut' [previously `a picturesque gable-ended little cottage surrounded by a rustic fence] insisted on our having some hot coffee and scones and wild honey .. *(1867,Station Life in NZ,181,1870)*  
> **HAWTHORNE** Fruit groves abound, and wild honey is found in every forest. *(1869,Dark Chapter from NZ History,4)*  
> Some four or five bushmen had proceeded to Kerikeri ... for the purpose of collectingwild honey. *(1894,TrNZI,26/221)*  
> **HYDE** ... and perhaps the heavy sweetness of wild honey: ... *(1934,Journalese,109)*  
> **RICHARDS** We always had plenty of wild honey. The bees would build their hives in hollow trees. *(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,27)*

### wild Irishman
  
noun.  
> **LINDSAY** In this condition [<i> Rubus australis </i> , Forst.] forms one of the most ... troublesome `Lawyers,' or `Supple-Jacks,' of the forest. It is known to the settler also as the `Wild Irishman,' and to the North Island Maori as `Tataramoa'. [fn] A term defined by Dieffenbach as `Thorns, Blackberry,' - a description at least partly applicable, and with greater propriety, to <i> Discaria Toumatou. </i> *(1868,Contributions to NZ Botany,54)* [NOTES: (Lindsay has confused <i> Lawyer </i>  (? and Supplejack) with Matagouri or Wild Irishman]  
> (And) <i> Rubus australis </i>  wild Irishman *(1916,Dunedin Field Club Plant Catalogues)*

### wild Irishman
  
noun. <i>Discaria toumatou</i>, also Irishman, = matagouri (tumatakuru), pl. often </i> wild Irishmen <i>  the literal base of the name is not clear, but can perhaps be compared with SCOTSMAN (Scotch thistle), and (WILD) SPANIARD, with a play on the plant's thorny intractability and the difficulties it makes for humans and the term </i>wild Irish</i>, acording to OED, the less civilised Irish (the <i>  puri Hibernici </i> ), with possible suggestions of <i>blackthorn </i> , a shillelagh wood. <i> Wild Irishman </i>  is prob. the original, with <i> Irishman </i>  the derivative  
[NOTES: Andersen 1926 (Popular Names) notes that Shortland in 1851 Southern Districts does not mention the plant by the name <i> Irishman </i> , though he describes it on his traverse of South Canterbury. -->cf the following `literal' use (possibly a back reference to the plant): -->1850 WJD to 1921 NZJSc (And. 17 entries)]  
>  *(Jan 1856,Edinburgh New Philosophical Journal)* [NOTES: 1856 Tancred Notes on the Natural History of ... Canterbury (Edinburgh New Philos. Journal (NS) Jan 1856) (And)]  
>  *(1871,TrNZI,3/)*  
>  *(1896,Dunedin Field Club Plant Catalogues)*  
> **HUDSON**  *(1898,NZ Moths & Butterflies)*  
> **DENDY**  *(1900,Canterbury Old & New)*  
> **COCKAYNE**  *(1907,AJHR)* [NOTES: Report on a Botanical Survey of Kapiti Island]  
> <i>  Discaria toumatou </i>  wild-irishman *(1914,TrNZI,46/)*  
> (And). <i> Discaria toumatou </i>  wild-irishman *(1921,NZ Journal of Science & Technology)*  
> <i> wild-irishman:</i>  spiny bush. *(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,176)*  
> <i> wild irishman n </i> . see tumata Kuru. *(1966,Contemp. Dict. Supplement (Whitcombe's),628)*  
> <i> wild Irishman.</i>  See <i> matagouri. </i> *(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),22)*  
> [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; cf. 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 302] *(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,882)*

### wild Irishman
  
noun. Discaria toumatou, a thorny shrub also known as tumatakuru or matagouri.  
> The universal cooking pot was the camp oven..; a good, quick fire underneath, and solid live embers of wild Irishman firewood on the lid above. *(1988,Tales of Pioneer Women,255)* [NOTES: Written of 1940]  
> The <i> Phoebe Dunbar </i>  attempted to leave for Wellington ... Joe (wild Irishman) assaulting people, and not taken up. E.J.W[akefield]. writing on subject to Wellington. *(Nov 10, 1850,Torlesse Papers,176,1958)*  
> **WELD** ... we had remarked little or no wood [near the Awatere] but gigantic `wild Irishmen' here as large as hawthorns, ... *(Jan 1851,Letter)* [NOTES: 1851 Jan Weld Letter in 1851 Feb 21 NZGG 32]  
> **HALL-JONES** ... overrun with a scrub called Tomataguru [ ] by the natives, or `wild Irishman' by the colonists. It is full of prickles and is difficult to penetrate ... *(1971,Mr Surveyor Thomson,59)* [NOTES: (Griffiths Collection)1850s in 1971 Hall-Jones Mr Surveyor Thomson 59]  
> **SEWELL** The only thing to boil your kettle with is wild Irishman, a miserable apology for a Shrub. *(1856,Journal of Henry Sewell,2/216,1980)*  
> ... moved on to the end of Lake Coleridge where they camped ... sheltered from wind behind a thicket of Wild Irishman, Bush Lawyer &c. *(Dec 29, 1856,Journal Kept at Rockwood and the Point,61)*  
> **THOMSON** The country is much over-run with a scrub called Tomataguru by the natives or Wild Irishman by the colonists. *(1857,Survey of Otago,310)*  
> **BUTLER** ... the sluggish creeks and swampy ground thickly overgrown with Irishman, snow grass and Spaniard ... which extend on either side of the river for half a mile or more. ... I will now return to the subject of wild Irishman and wild Spaniard. The former is a thorny tree growing with ungainly unmanageable boughs sometimes as large as our own hawthorn trees *(Jun 1860,Forest Creek MS,40,1960)*  
> **HAAST** ... after scrambling through a dense scrub of manuka and wild irishman, [we] reached a spur running up from the mountain in a westerly direction. <i>Ibid. </i> 6 [pl -men] [We had] to scramble through a dense growth of wild irishmen, lawyers, and other prickly plants, which horribly retarded our progress. *(1861,Report on Topographical Exploration of Nelson,4)*  
> **HARPER**  *(Jul 20, 1861,Letters from Canterbury, NZ,56,1914)*  
> **HAAST** After crossing a small stream ... we arrived at such an impenetrable thicket of `Wild Irishman' and `Spaniards', that ... we had at last to give up the attempt with ... hands and faces covered with blood. *(1948,Julius Von Haast,210)* [NOTES: 1862 Mar 31 Von Haast in 1948 Von Haast Julius Von Haast 210]  
> **WALKER** The diggings [at Dunstan] extend 80 miles one way and fifteen another. If you want to boil a pot you have to hunt about for dead `Wild Irishmen' a nasty prickly little shrub. The high lands abounded with `Spaniards' I suppose from their cut-throat propensities. A kind of very thick grass, grows in a little lump. *(Jan 7, 1863,Journal and Letters (Walker))* [NOTES: (Dunstan Diggings)]  
> **BARKER** Nothing seems [25] so wonderful to me as the utter treelessness of the vast Canterbury plains; occasionally you pass a few Ti-ti palms (ordinarily called cabbage-trees), or a large prickly bush which goes by the name of `wild Irishman,' but for miles and miles you see nothing but ... undulating downs of yellow tussocks, the tall native grass. *(1865,Station Life in NZ,24,1870)*  
> **HOOKER** Wild Irishman. <i> Discaria Toumatou.</i> *(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),769)*  
> **HOCHSTETTER**  *(1867,New Zealand (Hochstetter),1867)* [NOTES: 1867 von Hochstetter NZ (list of trees from Taylor 1848)]  
> ... perfectly treeless plains, where nothing woody ... grows loftier than a `Wild Irishman' <i> (Discaria Toumatou,</i>  Hook.,f.) ... *(1869,TrNZI,1/III/8)* [NOTES: (Monro)]  
> **PYKE** NOTE. - As I have no desire to mystify readers unacquainted with New Zealand flora, I append a brief description of the shrubs mentioned:- <i> Kowhai </i>  - A papilonaceous flowering tree, of the <i> acacia </i>  tribe. [17] <i> Bush-lawyer </i> - Native name, Tataramoa - A disagreeable bramble, very tenacious of grasp. <i> Koromiko </i>  - A white flowering Veronica. <i> Tutu </i>  - A berry-bearing, glossy-leaved plant, deadly to man and to all animals - except goats. <i> Tumata-kuru </i>  - Better known as `Wild Irishman' - a thorny plant, very difficult to handle. *(1873,Wild Will Enderby,1/iii/16-17,(1889,1974))*  
> **JOLLIE** ... the thick Wild Irishman Scrub. *(1878,Reminiscences,33)*  
> **HAAST** After ten miles travelling ... amongst dense scrub, mostly consisting of <i> Discaria toumatoo </i> , the Wild Irishman of the settlers, the junction ... was reached. <i>Ibid.</i>40 [pl -men] ... a dense vegetation of Wild Irishmen and Speargrasses ... made travelling very laborious. <i>Ibid.</i> 124 [spg hyphen] Our road lay now ... across a grassy flat, mostly overgrown with the same dense vegetation met with on the banks of all our river-flats, namely the spiny Wild-Irishman, and the bayonet-like Spaniard <i> (Aciphylla Lyallii)</i> *(1879,Geology of the Provinces of Canterbury & Westland,4)*  
> **HECTOR** <i> Discaria toumatou </i> , Raoul. Tumatakuru, Wild Irishman.--A bush or small tree with spreading branches; if properly trained would form a handsome headge that would be stronger than whitehorn. *(1883,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),131)*  
> **PYKE** As the only `trees' to be found on the Tuapeka goldfields were Te-matau Kauri (better known as `Wild Irishmen') and Tutu bushes, of not very exuberant growth, the law in this respect went ahead of the facts. *(1887,History of the Early Gold Discoveries  in Otago,61,1962)*  
> **FEATON** DISCARIA TOUMATOU (<i> Raoul </i> .) ... The `TOUMATOU'.- ... The plant is very generally known throughout the Colony as `The Wild Irishman'. In the Middle Island, more particularly towards Otago, it is called `Native Thorn'. *(1889,Art Album NZ Flora,93)*  
> **MORRIS** <i> Wild Irishman, a spiny New Zealand shrub, Discaria toumatou </i> , Raoul, <i> N.O. Rhamnea </i> . The Maori name is <i> Tumata-Kuru </i>  (q.v.). 1867. F. Hochstetter, `New Zealand,' p. 133: *(1898,Austral English,511)*  
> ... tumatakuru, a small thorny scrub, one of the few deciduous plants of New Zealand, which Europeans nick-named `Wild Irishman', ... speargrass, or `Spaniard', as the stronger variety was called, ... *(1914,History of North Otago,4,1978)* [NOTES: 1906 W.H.S.Roberts in 1914 History of North Otago (1978) 4]  
> **LAING & BLACKWELL** <i> Discaria toumatou (The Wild Irishman). ...</i>  Maori name <i> Tumatakuru. </i> *(1907,Plants of NZ,239,1940, p. 252)*  
> **THOMPSON** WILD IRISHMAN.* [title] [*fn] Another name for Matagauri. *(1909,Ballads about Business,73)*  
> **COCKAYNE** (And) <i> Discaria toumatou </i>  wild-irishman *(1910,NZ Plants & Their Story)*  
> (And) <i> Discaria toumatou </i>  wild-irishman *(1911,AJHR,C-13)*  
> **COCKAYNE** (And) <i> Discaria toumatou </i>  wild-irishman *(1919,NZ Plants & Their Story,2nd)*  
> The wild-irishman (<i> Discaria toumatou </i> ), too, attained a greater stature and was more abundant than now. *(1922,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,IV/282)* [NOTES: (Bathgate, Otago)]  
> **BATHGATE** ... I saw that the supposed bushranger was a compact bush of matakoura or wild Irishman. *(1925,Random Recollections,27)*  
> [Fantastic plant-names] show the imagination running riot; they seem appropriate though often it is impossible to give any definite reason for the name. No trace has been found for the name `wild-irishman' as applied to Discaria toumatou. Many old settlers have been questioned without result, or with such barren result as the following: One being asked, `Why was the wild-irishman called the wild-irishman?' answered `I suppose because it is like a wild Irishman.' `What characteristics have they in common?' `Well, I-I-I really can't say.' And so it is; it is a name that everyone understands, but no one can explain. Whilst it is often written with a capital, it is now as often written with a small letter, `irishman'. *(1926,TrNZI,663ff)* [NOTES: Andersen Popular names]  
> **BEATTIE** There was then plenty of the shrub called matagouri or `Wild Irishman' and also abundance of speargrass of two kinds. <i> wild Irishman NZ </i>  = MATAGOURI. *(1947,Pioneer Recollections,47,1956)*  
> **HAAST** On March 12 [1867] they travelled up the river [Rakaia], ... making their way through dense Wild Irishmen until they reached the junction of the two main branches. *(1848,Julius Von Haast,176)*  
> **PARTRIDGE** <i> Wild irishman, the.</i>  2. The tumatakuru (a New Zealand plant): New Zealand coll.: C. 19-20. B., 1941. *(1949,Dictionary of Slang Addenda,1223)*  
> **RICHARDS** <i> Discaria ... Toumatu </i>  [sic] ... Wild Irishman. MATAGOWRI, TUMATA-KURI [sic] ... *(1952,Chatham Islands,54)*  
> **MALING** Matagouri or Wild Irishman, and speargrass or Spaniard. *(1958,Torlesse Papers,74fn,1958)*  
> <i>  Irishman, wild </i>  A common New Zealand shrub of the buckthorn family, also called `matagouri' (<i> ma|-ta-go|-ry </i> ). The real Maori name is `tumata-kuru', of which `matagouri' is an incorrect form. *(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),224)*  
> **MCLINTOCK** Wild Irishman (matagouri, matagowry) .. .. tumatakuru .. .. <i> Discaria toumatou </i> *(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-12)*  
> <i> wild Irishman [=] matagouri [s] </i> *(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,86)*  
> **OGONOWSKA-COATES** <i> Wild Irishman. </i> Matagouri; native, spikey, very hardy. *(1987,Boards, Blades & Barebellies,98)*  
> **McGILL** <i>  wild Irishman </i> a thorny shrub, aka matagouri, from Maori `tumatakuru' or speargrass. *(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,124)*  
> SEWELL Journal Jan in McIntyre (1980) 216 The only thing to boil your kettle with is Wild Irishman, a miserable apology for a shrub. *(1856)*  
> THOMSON in Hamilton (1952) History Of Northern Southland 9 Much of the country bears fine grass but is overrun with a scrub called tomataguru by the natives or Wild Irishman by the colonists ... *(1857)*  
> in BUTLER (1863) First Year 34 There was a good deal, too, of a very uncomfortable prickly shrub, which they call Irishman ... *(1860)*  
> BARKER Station Amusements in NZ 35 Especially detrimental to riding habits were wild Irishmen ... *(1873)*  
> ibid.44 From time to time we fell into and over Spaniards, and what was left of our clothes and our flesh the wild Irishmen devoured. *(1873)*  
> STRONACH Musterer on Molesworth 38 Our camp was in a small bay surrounded by .. very big matagouris or Irishman ... [Irishman] *(1953)*  
> "If you want to boil a pot you have to hunt about for dead 'wild-Irishman,' a nasty prickly little shrub. *(Jul 27, 1939,NZ Centennial News,22)* [NOTES: Quote from WALKER 1862/63]  
> The flats are covered with long fine grass, with wild Irishman shrubs of an enormous size growing plentifully scattered about. *(Mar 22, 1873,Auckland Weekly News,20)*  
> Descending into the valley, the travelling became rough;  rocks, spear-grass, and the prickly plant called "Wild Irishman," everywhere abounding. *(Aug 3, 1850,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,91)*  
> **MORELAND** Here and there ragged pines, secured a foothold on some knoll, and manuka and 'wild Irishman' (that very objectionable thorny scrub) caught at us with Leafless branches as we passed... [Note.] <i>Discaria toumatou</i>. *(1911,Through South Westland,86)*  
> In a little time hollows among the "wild Irishman" bushes were pointed out to the credulous as Maori ovens. *(Dec 7, 1897,Otago Witness,50)*  
> The scrub was of the thorny kind  That some dead wag who often ran  Against it, cursing, left behind  His name for it - "Wild Irishman." *(Nov 25, 1897,Otago Witness,47)*

### wild lands
  
noun.  
> The merino occupies and thrives on the wild lands of the colony, from the snow-line to the border of the plains, ... *(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,92)*

noun.

Muehlenbeckia australis ... grape weed ... Muehlenbeckia complexa ... wirebush *(1947,At Omatua's Fireside,127)*

### wild mint
  
noun.

### wild mushroom
  
noun.

### wild mustard
  
noun.  
> **HILGENDORF** WILD TURNIP ... (c) <i>Brassica nigra</i> (WILD MUSTARD) with short hairy pods. *(1967,Weeds of New Zealand,98,1926)*  
> <i>mustard, wild [=] hedge mustard: wild turnip</i> [s] *(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,51)*  
> **HILGENDORF** HEDGE MUSTARD (<i>Sisymbrium officinale</i>) is often called wild mustard, or tumbling mustard. ... It is abundant in gardens, cultivated fields, and waste places in both islands. *(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,90,1967)*  
> **HILGENDORF** WILD TURNIP (<i>Sinapsis arvensis</i>) [1967, no species given] is also commonly called charlock and yellow weed, and even wild mustard. It is exceedingly common on rich agricultural lands, at any rate in Canterbury and Otago, where masses of it often rival gorse in brilliancy. *(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,90,1967)*  
> <i>wild mustard [=] hedge mustard: wild turnip</i> [s] *(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,86)*

noun.

### wild parsley
  
noun. hemlock  
> **HILGENDORF** HEMLOCK (<i>Conium maculatum</i>) is loosely called wild carrot or wild parsley in certain districts. It is a biennial found in waste places and rich grasslands in scattered localities in both islands. ... Hemlock has been famous from ancient times for the poison extracted from its roots and unripe seeds. *(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,130,1967)*

### wild parsley
  
noun. edible weed  
> **TAYLOR** WILD PARSLEY (<i>Petroselinum crispum</i>) Biennial grown for culinary use, introduced from Europe by ... Cook, widely naturalised, and common in limited areas e.g. parts of North Canterbury. *(1981,Weeds of Roadsides,171)*  
> **TAYLOR** WILD PARSLEY (<i>Petroselinum crispum</i>) Biennial grown for culinary use, introduced from Europe by ... Cook, widely naturalised, and common in limited areas e.g. parts of North Canterbury. *(1981,Weeds of Roadsides,171)*  
> <i>wild parsley</i> [s] <i>Petroselinum crispum</i> (Mill.) Nym. <i>Ibid.</i> 56 <i>parsley, wild</i> [s] <i>Petroselinum crispum</i> (Mill.) Nym. *(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,86)*  
> **NICHOLAS** The herbaceous productions indigenous to the country are, wild celery, canary grass, wild parsley, plantain grass, a species of ray-grass, the <i>ensata</i> or flag, the phormium tenax or flax-plant, and a species of the fern. *(1817,New Zealand (various authors/titles),2/249)*  
> **RHODES** There are a few stunted trees and a little wild parsley and other herbage on the most elevated part. *(1836,Whaling Journal,36,1954)* [NOTES: [on Macauley Island in Kermadec Group]]  
> **HEAPHY** We ... lived upon ... <i>toretore</i> or sea anemonies, making the latter into what we termed a `soup', flavoured with salt water and the wild parsley which grows among the rocks of that [West] coast. *(1846,Notes of an Expedition to Kawatiri and Araura)* [NOTES: 1846 Heaphy Exped. to Kawatiri in 1959 Taylor Early Travs. 244]  
> **WALLACE** The BLUE-TUSSOCK (Triticum scabrum R. Br.), a grass of superior 9 quality, has been mostly eaten out of the South Island of New Zealand. ... Along with it a BARLEY GRASS [? <i>Panicum</i> sp.] and WILD PARSLEY [bold], both superior feeding value, have also practically disappeared from the natural pastures ... *(1891,Rural Economy of Aust. & NZ,296)*  
> **HARPER** The kihi-ki (<i>Freycinetia Banksii</i>) which grows on the lower hills near the sea, wild parsley (<i>Angelica geniculata</i>), spinach (<i>Tetragonia expansa</i>), ... are also eatable. *(1896,Pioneer Work in the Alps of NZ,33)*  
> We baked mamaku, and lived on that and toritori or sea anemonies, making the latter into what we termed "soup", flavoured with salt water and the wild parsley which grows among the rocks of the coast. *(Oct 17, 1846,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,130)*  
> Still they had variety - mutton-birds, mutton-fish, seabirds' eggs, limpets, periwinkles, rock cod, and a kind of wild parsley for salad. *(Dec 15, 1883,Otago Witness,14)*  
> This plant is very common all over New Zealand, even to Stewart Island, and is known as wild parsley nowadays. *(Aug 25, 1892,Otago Witness,43)*  
> We may not obtain, or, if obtained, we cannot grow, some of the best tonics; but wild parsley is native, therefore can be raised. *(May 19, 1866,Daily Southern Cross,5)*

noun.

> Sow thistle is extremely abundant; wild parsnips are also common. *(Apr 16, 1842,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,23)*  
> **HILGENDORF** WILD PARSNIP ([<i>Pastinaca sativa</i>]) is similar to the cultivated form except in its smaller root. It is found in scattered localities, but is *(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,132)*  
> <i>parsnip, wild</i> [s] <i>Pastinaca sativa</i> L. <i>Ibid.</i> 86 <i>wild parsnip</i> [s] <i>Pastinaca sativa </i>L. *(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,56)*  
> **TAYLOR** WILD PARSNIP (<i>Pastinaca sativa</i>) Biennial, native to Eurasia, very similar to the garden parsnip. *(1981,Weeds of Roadsides,171)*  
> **WILSON** <i>Pastinaca sativa </i>WILD PARSNIP ... Erect, aromatic, biennial herb. Root edible in the first year of growth ... *(1982,Stewart Island Plants,188)*

### wild pig
  
noun. a feral pig  
> **IHIMAERA & LONG (ed.)** where the wild pig roamed and rooted / They've measured the land into precise sections / Worth 3 000 dollars (or to sound better / For the prospective buyer, 1 500 pounds). *(1982,Into the World of Light,103)*  
> The hunters caught poaka (wild pigs), parera (grey duck), putakitaki (paradise duck), kereru or kuku (pigeon), and hurepo (bittern). *(1988,Tales of Pioneer Women,249)* [NOTES: Written of 1940]  
> **SHAW** She immediately set to work, cooking for us some wild pig, which was near at hand. *(1858,Gallop to the Antipodes, A,136)*  
> Most of the time they lived on native pigeon and wild pig. *(Dec 2001,NZ Memories,No. 27/39)*  
> They might even tell you about the survey team attacked by a wild pig up in the Waimapihi reserve a few years ago. *(Oct 20, 2001,Evening Post,25)*  
> Next came wild pigs, erect crests - tusks. *(Dec 17, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,8)*  
> The man's point [sc. in a lawsuit] was whether hunting wild pigs was part of the farm work in that district, where wild pigs are plentiful and very destructive. *(Jan 11, 1923,Dominion,3)*  
> **WOODHOUSE** He [sc. Mr Buchanan] remembered walking along a track made by wild pigs though the tall fern and flax to the site of the present homestead. *(1982,Blue Cliffs,7)*  
> In a moment the party surprised, and were surprised by, a drove of wild pigs. *(Jul 1863,Southern Monthly Magazine,1(5) : 263)*  
> The scene which followed in the Hun positions resembled a wild pig hunt. *(Aug 2, 1918,Chronicles NZEF,V/49 : 12)*  
> **64** Now there's the trig station, there's the trampers' hut; perhaps a hermit lives up there among the wild pigs. *(1988,Carpathians, The)*  
> Harry and I at underbush all day. We cleared round a rimu that had been much frequented by wild pigs ... *(Nov 30, 1853,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/136)* [NOTES: (A.S. Atkinson)]  
> **BARKER** Late in spring ... we were all returning from a great pig-hunting expedition, when I saw one of the party coming ... with a small ... wild pig under each arm ... *(1867,Station Life in NZ,106,1870)*  
> **NESFIELD** The New Zealand wild pig, if young and fat, ... is very delicious. *(1881,Chequered Career,37)*  
> **DRURY** Wild pigs were plentiful in the bush. *(1855,Sailing Directions,68)*  
> **FROBISHER** A passer-by gave them intelligence of some wild pigs having been sighted shortly before on the spur above the road. *(1893,Sketches of Gossipton,42)*  
> An up-river Wanganui farmer reported the loss of a foal which was eaten by wild pigs. *(Jan 10, 1934,Feilding Star,4)*

### wild pig
  
noun. a descendant of domestic pigs released by Europeans since Cook's first voyage.  
See: also bush pig, cooker  
> On Good Friday four residents of Cust went into the Wharfdale part of the back country in quest of wild pigs, intending to return on Monday. They succeeded in capturing over twenty, and had some good sport. *(Apr 23, 1895,Star, The,3)*  
> The rabbits were not so plentiful as we expected, but we bagged about 40 skins, and had the good luck also to kill seven wild pigs - four young ones, two old sows, and a good sized boar. *(Sep 24, 1881,Otago Witness,13)*  
> We had seen to day [sic] many traces of natives, a foot-path, branches which had been broken by them about six months ago, and we had sure indications that many wild pigs live in the bush. *(Sep 12, 1840,New Zealand Gazette & Wellington Spectator,3)*  
> **CAMPBELL** ... the boar flashed off, running `level' as a wild pig does, down the far slope. *(1955,By Reef and Range,7)*  
> **WAKEFIELD** [The wild hog, which is caught with dogs, and afterwards domesticated and home-fed, partakes a good deal of the Chinese breed about the head, and frequently grows to a great size.] *(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,167)*  
> **MACKIE** The supply of wild pigs scattered all over the islands are quite a boon for which the colonists are indebted to Captn Cook. *(Mar 14, 1853,Traveller under Concern,87,1973)*  
> Abundance of wild pigs. *(Jan 5, 1849,Torlesse Papers,46,1958)*  
> **FITTON** The wild pigs ... are descended from runaway tame pigs; or from those turned loose from vessels visiting New Zealand. *(1856,New Zealand (various authors/titles),266)*  
> **HAAST** It is well known to the settlers of the province how abundant [136] wild pigs are on the eastern side of the central chain, ... In order to show how abundant these wild pigs are in some parts of the country, I may mention ... that a party of three men killed ... no less than 25,000 under a contract ... *(1861,Report on Topographical Exploration of Nelson,135)*  
> **LINDSAY** Hence a `Spear-grass Scrub' on the hill ranges is frequently most formidable ... to men and horses, though it forms one of the favourite hunting grounds of the Wild Pig. In the Matukituki Valley, `Bayonette-grass' forms an undergrowth in a dense scrub of prickly thorn ... *(1868,Contributions to NZ Botany,49)*  
> **BAINES** ... nominally we were supposed to be going on a pig-hunting expedition ... not to return minus a back-load each of wild-pig. *(1874,Edward Crewe,228)*  
> **ADAM** The passengers (newly arrived) [c1850] now began to crowd round the pilot, asking many questions about the natives, wild pigs, and the country. *(1876,Twenty-Five Years in NZ,25)*  
> **FEATON** ... their gullies swarmed with wild pig and not a few cattle. *(1879,Waikato War,10)*  
> **HAY** But it was out of the question to fence in the grass, so we had to take what chance there was ... of the damage wild pigs might do to the young turf, or of dingoes. <i>Ibid.</i> 2/323 The New Zealand wild pig of the present day is the descendant of animals introduced by Captain Cook and other early voyagers from the old countries. *(1882,Brighter Britain,2/237)*  
> Wild Pigs - a legacy from Captain Cook - Pigeons, and other birds abounded in its food-supplying cover; ... <i>Ibid.</i> 163 Eels, Birds of all sorts, Fernroot, and wild Pigs were to be found in profusion in the `Creek', the `Bush', or on the Mountain-side; ... *(1911,Piraki Log,155)*  
> **AYSON** My flock was the dry flock wethers. One day I got notice to ... put my flock of 3,000 sheep away back on the hill country of `Lochindorb', near the head of the Owaka river. It was very much infested with wild dogs and wild pigs, ... but it was very good sheep country. *(1937,Pioneering in Otago,94)*  
> **BURDON** In the unlikely event of their surmounting all these dangers, they would find the country useless for pasture and inhabited by nothing but wild pigs and wild dogs. *(1938,High Country,53)*  
> **BEATTIE** Wild pigs were a great nuisance; the runs were overrun with them, and they started to eat the lambs. *(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,99)*

### wild pig snout
  
noun. [Rural] a token of a wild pig for which a bounty is paid.  
> Up to the 30th November 1925, the Forest Service, in its capacity as organizing and controlling agent, collected and paid out the bonus upon 17.227 wild pig snouts in the Wellington conservation region. Arrangements have been made whereby all persons who presented wild pig snouts for counting and payment before the 30th November 1925 .. the system of granting a bonus for the destruction of wild pigs was extended to cover a portion of the Opotoki County, with the result that 2,026 wild pig snouts were destroyed in that district. *(Oct 20, 1926,NZ Journal of Agriculture,251)*

### wild pigeon
  
noun. [Environment]  
See: pigeon, kereru  
> In autumn wild pigeons become extremely fat by feeding on the berries if the Miro, Tawa, Hinau, &c.; but when they feed on the leaves of the Kawhia they become so poor as to be uneatable. *(Nov 11, 1859,Daily Southern Cross,3)*  
> He added that wild pigeons were not protected, and apparently it was the intention of the Government to make the embargo permanent. *(May 19, 1919,Poverty Bay Herald,6)*  
> WANTED KNOWN - Wild Pigeons for Sale. - THELWALL AND STAPLES have for sale 500 pairs of WILD PIGEONS, 2s per pair, Cash. *(Jul 23, 1886,Poverty Bay Herald,3)*  
> Wild Pigeons 2s a pair, Wild Ducks 4s a pair. *(Sep 1, 1925,Otautau Standard & Wallace County Chronicle,3)*  
> **SAM STEPHENS** Wild pigeon. *(1841,Letters)*  
> **WILLIAMS** The Wild Pigeon of New Zealand reigns the undisputed king of beauty amongst this feathery tribe, this handsome bird known by the name of Ruckupa [Kereru, <i>Hemiphaga novaseelandiae</i>] ... *(1844,NZ Journal,112,1956)*  
> **FEATON** The aril, or fruit [of the kohekohe], is eaten by the wild pigeon, (<i>Carpophaga Novae Zelandiae</i>), with avidity. *(1889,Art Album NZ Flora,84)*  
> **WHITE** Our Wild Pigeon is a very handsome bird with coloured plumage and white breast, two or three sizes larger than the English Wood Pigeon. *(1905,My NZ Garden,73)*  
> **PFAFF** Wild pigeons were very plentiful, and to the men in the bush were their mainstay for meat at first. *(1914,Diggers' Story, The,109)*  
> **BARKER** Overhead the trees were alive with flocks of wild pigeons, ka-kas, parroquets, and other birds ... *(1867,Station Life in NZ,181,1870)*  
> Many birds lead a wandering life, and remain in one place only so long as they find plenty of food; as, the wild pigeon, the heron, wild geese, and wild ducks. *(Apr 14, 1877,Otago Witness,19)*

### wild pork
  
noun. pork from wild pigs

> The crowd drift into a tent for beer and a delicious hangi of potatoes, chicken, pumpkin, kumara and wild pork. *(Jul 17, 1993,Listener (New Zealand Listener),26)*

noun. .

### wild sheep
  
noun. an unfarmed sheep that roams and breeds in the wild [ shedding wool naturally, often carrying disease and leading astray farmed sheep.]

> GUTHRIE-SMITH Tutira III (1999) 221 We lived on wild pig, wild beef shot on Kaiwaka, and the fat wild sheep and double-fleecers that could be raked in from river cliffs ... *(1921)*  
> WHYTE The Sheep-dog 52 ... although with a larger flock of wild sheep on a steep hillside turning in the corners becomes an urgent necessity. *(1927)*  
> MCRAE By the Braes of Balquether 74 In the later 1920s wild sheep were such a problem that a bounty of ten shillings a head was offered. *(1963)*  
> SINCLAIR Boot in the Stirrup 52 For the benefit of those who've never seen a wild sheep, they are a breed apart, not tame sheep termed hermit. *(1973)*
