> ibid. 149 We were shooting and skinning goats and wild sheep and catching the roughies and shearing them. *(1996)*  
> KERR High Times in the High Country 81 Duncraig's big fleecers shed wool like wild sheep. *(2000)*

### wild Spaniard
  
noun. (Aciphylla spp.) <i> wild </i> perhaps from analogy with wild Irishman  
See: Spaniard, bloody Spaniard, giant Spaniard; cf. wild Irishman  
> **BUTL;ER** I will now return to wild Spaniard - Irishman was a nuisance, but Spaniard is simply detestable - he is sometimes called spear grass. *(1860,Forest Creek MS,41,1960)*  
> **HOOKER** Wild Spaniard. <i>Aciphylla. </i> *(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),769)*  
> **LINDSAY** The larger species [of <i>Aciphylla</i>] are familiar to the settlers as `Spear-grass', or `Bayonette-grass', `Wild' or `Bloody-Spaniard', [<i>bloody</i> =?] in allusion to their very rigid, strong, poniard-like, sharp-pointed leaves ... Hence a `Spear-grass Scrub' on the hill ranges is frequently most formidable ... to men and horses, though it forms one of the favourite hunting grounds of the Wild Pig. In the Matukituki Valley, `Bayonette-grass' forms an undergrowth in a dense scrub of prickly thorn ... *(1868,Contributions to NZ Botany,49)*  
> **McGILL** <i>wild Spaniard</i> or simply `Spaniard', speargrass, a sub-alpine herb with leaves like a Spanish dagger and a smell like celery and turpentine. *(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,124)*  
> Introduced mammals such as sheep, deer and hares began to graze their way up, feeding on the more palatable plants, pulling out and trampling tussocks and herbs, and leaving behind a higher concentration of less edible spoecies like <i>Dracophyllum</i> and wild Spaniard. *(Nov 2003,NZ Geographic,No. 66/32)*  
> The plains are covered with grasses, the roots of which are gathered into tufts or tussocks, intermixed with the Bayonet grass or `Wild Spaniard' (<i> Aciphylla Colensoi</i>), Toumatakuru (<i> Discaria Toumatou </i>), and a great variety of the New Zealand brooms (<i> Carmichaelia </i>), ... *(1869,TrNZI,1/3/5)* [NOTES: (Hector)]  
> **LAING & BLACKWELL** <i> Aciphylla squarrosa (The Spaniard)</i> ... Maori name <i> Kuri-Kuri </i>; Colonists' local] name, <i> Spaniard </i> or <i> Wild Spaniard.</i> [check whether this or foll. cit. to be retained???] [321] <i> Aciphylla squarrosa (The Spaniard) </i>... Maori name [so 1907 edition] <i> Kuri-Kuri;</i> local name, <i> Spaniard </i> or <i> Wild Spaniard.</i> *(1907,Plants of NZ,320,1940, p. 339)*  
> **HOCHSTETTER**  *(1867,New Zealand (various authors/titles))* [NOTES: (list of trees from Taylor 1848) (And)]  
> **HOOKER** <i> Aciphylla </i> sp. wild spaniard *(1867,Handbook NZ Flora)* [NOTES: (And)]  
> **DENDY** <i> Aciphylla squarrosa </i> wild spaniard *(1900,Canterbury Old & New)* [NOTES: (And)]  
> <i> Aciphylla squarrosa </i> wild spaniard *(1916,Dunedin Field Club Plant Catalogues)* [NOTES: (And)]  
> **HILGENDORF** FAMILY UMBELLIFERAE. The family of carrots, parsnips, etc., as well as of the striking native known as Spanish dagger or [wild] Spaniard. *(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,129,1967)*  
> <i> Spaniard, wild [=] blue Spaniard giant Spaniard Spaniard taramea </i> [s] ... 87 <i>wild Spaniard [=] Spaniard </i> [s] *(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,72)*  
> **DENNIS** One of the most striking of alpine plants is the spikey [sic] spaniard, of which the fiercest in this range is the wild spaniard (<i> Aciphylla colensoi</i>) (speargrass). *(1981,Paparoas Guide,165)*  
> The gradual replacement of the spaniard (<i> Aciphylla colensoi </i>, Hook. f.) by self-sown pasturage-plants is most remarkable. ... The common spear-grass (<i> A. squarrosa </i>, Forst.) is often displaced in the same way. *(1895,TrNZI,28/19)*  
> <i> Span&#769;.iard </i> (sp&abreve.n&#769;.y&etilde.rd),<i> n. [Add.] 2. (Bot.) </i> The spear grass of New Zealand <i>(Aciphylla Colensoi).<i> *(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2031)*  
> <i> Aciphylla squarrosa </i> wild spaniard *(Jan 1856,Edinburgh New Philosophical Journal,(NS) Jan 1856)* [NOTES: Tancred Notes on the Natural History of ... Canterbury (And)]  
> **LINDSAY** The larger species [of <i> Aciphylla </i>] are familiar to the settlers as `Spear-grass', or `Bayonette-grass', `Wild' or `Bloody-Spaniard', [bloody =?] in allusion to their very rigid, strong, poniard-like, sharp-pointed leaves ... Hence a `Spear-grass Scrub' on the hill ranges is frequently most formidable ... to men and horses, though it forms one of the favourite hunting grounds of the Wild Pig. In the Matukituki Valley, `Bayonette-grass' forms an undergrowth in a dense scrub of prickly thorn ... <i> Aciphylla squarrosa </i> wild spaniard *(1868,Contributions to NZ Botany,49)*  
> The light fawn-coloured moth noticed by Mr Bathgate only where spear-grass abounded, I have no doubt is identical with that found on these plains, and which I have myself bred from the larva taken out of the heart of "Wild Spaniard" (aciphylla squarrosa) on which it was feeding. *(Oct 31, 1871,Star, The,3)*  
> Then, again, specimens of the ligusticum, a member of the carrot family, will be noted, and if one is not careful the spines of the wild Spaniard will remind one to be watchful. *(Dec 26, 1925,Evening Post,9)*

### wild spinach
  
noun. = New Zealand spinach  
> **BULLER** In some localities the [Little White Terns'] nests ... were placed in the thick masses of wild spinach or in the midst of `sand-fire'. *(1888,Birds of New Zealand,2/76)*  
> **BURTON** A voyager on Cook's third voyage in <i>Discovery </i> in 1772, Dr Sparrman, notes that `the green stuffs which were cooked with pease and broth or used as salads, consisted of wild celery (<i>Apium prostratum </i>), wild spinach (<i>Tetragonia expansa </i>) and Cook's scurvy grass (<i>Lepidum oleraceum </i>).' The last mentioned, known as <i> nau </i> by the Maori, has nowadays been virtually eaten out of existence by sheep and cattle. *(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,16)*

### wild teasel
  
noun. <i>Dispsacus fullonum</i>  
> <i>teasel, wild</i> [s] <i>Dipsacus fullonum </i>L. <i>Ibid.</i> 87 <i>wild teasel </i>[s] <i>Dipsacus fullonum</i> L. *(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,77)*  
> **TAYLOR** WILD TEASEL (<i>Dispsacus fullonum</i>) ... is native to Eurasia and locally plentiful in a few districts through New Zealand. *(1981,Weeds of Lawns,134)*

### wild tobacco
  
noun.  
> <i>tobacco, wild</i> [s] <i>Nicotiana rustica </i>L. <i>Ibid.</i> 87 <i>wild tobacco</i> [s] <i>Nicotiana rustica</i> L. *(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,79)*

### wild tobacco
  
noun.
